CULTURE POTAGERE (PETITE ET GRANDE CULTURE) 1.M PAR DYBOWSKI PROFESSEUR A L'INSTITUT NATIONAL AGRONOMIQUE A ANCIEN MAITRE DE CONFÉRENCES D'HORTICULTURE L'ÉCOLE NATIONALE D'AGRICULTURE DE GRIGNON Avec 115 figures dans le texte. DEUXIÈME ÉDITION REVUE ET CORRIGÉE PARIS G. MASSON, ÉDITEUR 120, BOULEVARD SAINT-GERMAIN 1895 r. .. .. .. ... .. D. . . . . - ". AI . ...."  The book I am presenting to growers today grew out of the lessons I was asked to give at the École Nationale d'Agriculture in Grignon. It seems to me to fill a gap and to meet a real need; indeed, for some years now, there has been a great deal of concern about the powerful help that agriculture can find in vegetable production. A large number of brochures and special notes have been written on this important subject; vegetable horticulture, under the welcome influence of these writings and thanks to the support of Horticultural Societies and the exhibitions they organize every year, has entered an era of new prosperity. It was useful to bring together the documents published to date and to show the progress made; this is what I have tried to do. For the crop descriptions, I've adopted alphabetical order, which facilitates research. In addition, I have designated plants by their most commonly used names, so as to make this book accessible to everyone; it is aimed at the amateur as well as the professional gardener and farmer. I have endeavored to show all the practices in use, stripping them of all bias. Finally, wherever possible, I have highlighted the advantages of large-scale cultivation and commercial seed production. It remains for me to thank all those who, breaking with the blind routine that made horticultural processes a kind of mystery, have not hesitated to reveal the secrets of their cultivation for the publicity of this book. The progress to which they have thus contributed will be their best reward. Finally, I would like to express my most sincere thanks to Messrs. de Vilmorin, who have so graciously made available to us a number of their fine photographs of the best vegetable varieties. JEAN DYBOWSKI. The new edition has been completely revised. Indeed, while the general principles remain the same, the methods are being perfected and it is important to record these improvements. As commodity prices change, we have had to revise and establish them as they are today. Where appropriate, we have included notes on vegetable growing in the French colonies we visited ourselves. Finally, we have added a new crop to the already long list of plants enumerated above, that of the Epiaires à chapelets (Crosnes), which should henceforth be considered as a common vegetable. J. D. . . U . . - VEGETABLE GROWING FOREWORD Vegetable growing encompasses all forms of vegetable production, whatever their point of view. The amateur, the owner, or simply the momentary holder of the smallest piece of land, thinks, if he has a practical mind, not only of obtaining from his garden some pleasant flowers, but also vegetables which, for him, will have this inappreciable value of having come under his eyes, under his hand, by his own care. He sowed them himself, he cared for them, and it's only natural that he should appreciate the value of his efforts in producing them. The amateur is a powerful helper of horticulture as a whole. At first, he cultivates his own little patch of land, giving it assiduous but often inexperienced care. Gradually, these first attempts, in which he was partly successful, gave him a taste for a closer look at things; he picked up special publications, studied them and DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. studies them, comments on them. He became very strong, he knew the trade; he knew that the land can produce a lot when you help it a little. As a result, not content with following the beaten path, he soon began experimenting with new methods, or even cultivating plants that had not been used much or at all until then. He studied these innovations, saw what could be gained from them, and finally formulated a clear-cut approach to their use. From now on, he has rendered the art of horticulture an immense service; for the professional grower cannot, not even the right to spend his time experimenting, he must follow a clear path and only commit his capital wisely. The amateur has prepared the way, and the grower will follow it if it proves to be the right one. From this point of view, and without even taking into account the service he renders to himself, the amateur's role is of inestimable importance. He is the researcher, the often happy introducer of new plants, which the grower will then exploit, when trials have shown them to offer some serious advantages. The amateur will find in our book precise information on what has been done; he will deduce from it what remains to be done. Professional growers, those who produce vegetables exclusively, belong to two very different categories: the market gardener on the one hand, and the actual grower on the other. The market-gardener, this artist of cultivation, cultivates a small area of land; but thanks to the powerful machinery at his disposal, the relatively considerable capital he puts at his disposal, and above all his understanding of cultivation and his constant hard work, he knows how to extract from the soil all that it can yield. It's from his garden, or factory as it were, that all those remarkable vegetables emerge which, most often produced in or near Paris, are consumed all over Europe. The annual export figure for market garden vegetables outside the frontier reaches the enormous sum of several million. For the market gardener, there is no season; activity reigns in his garden at all times, and fresh vegetables are constantly being produced. But how much care and resources he needs! Manure and water are the most active. Manure, of which he uses a few hundred cubic meters every year, is not only used as fertilizer; on the contrary, that's only its secondary role. Its primary use is to produce a certain amount of artificial heat through fermentation, which is why market gardeners use it in the construction of their beds. Placed in regular heaps, suitably packed, then watered, it ferments, and this fermentation, depending on the size of the mass, produces a variable amount of heat, which the market gardener can foresee in advance. To store this heat, he uses cloches or frames fitted with chests. No sooner has one crop been completed than another is planted in its place, and often several crops are planted simultaneously on the same plot of land. It is only under these conditions that he can make the soil produce eight or ten crops in the course of a year (fig. 1). Water is one of his most powerful aids; he waters constantly, and when the summer heat comes on, he pours it on in torrents. It must -. I . there must always be a close relationship between the quantity of manure used and the quantity of water poured into the watering= View of a market garden in Paris. -I 3 wise. Fertilizer can only be assimilated if it is soluble, so no matter how much you smoke, if you leave the soil to drought, the manure will remain inactive. Conversely, if you water the soil, you are obliged to smoke abundantly, as this would quickly exhaust the soil, rendering its nutrients soluble. It is generally thought and said that vegetables watered and then smoked abundantly lose their flavor and nutritive properties. This is an error that a simple examination of the facts will invalidate. In fact, we know that in plant tissue, made up of vessels, fibers and cells, it is almost exclusively in these latter elements that the principles useful for nutrition develop. However, whenever plants are pushed to great and rapid development, cellular production is exaggerated. The ratio of fibers to cells becomes lower, and the organoleptic properties of the plant are correspondingly enhanced. What market gardeners produce is therefore well made, and it's with good reason that their products always have a higher value on the market. In more ways than one, they are our masters in vegetable growing, and there's a lot to take from them. However, they have everything to gain by reading special books, because while it's good to follow the path laid out, we must also be wary of falling into a rut. Experimental and scientific methods are especially valuable when applied by skilled practitioners. Once again, strength lies in umon. But the market gardener, because of the environment in which he finds himself, the expensive rent he has to pay, the equipment he has to match, can only grow plants that are demanding even if they have to be, but in any case evolve rapidly. He has the right to grow early crops. But it's not enough to live on early vegetables, which are often the privilege of the rich, and you have to produce large vegetables for everyday consumption. It's the farmer's job to produce them. The farmer farms the plains around the big cities; he smokes his fields, but most of the time he doesn't water them, relying on the rains to help his crops. The expanse of land over which he cultivates makes watering by hand impossible; under certain conditions, however, he makes use of the water he distributes through canals, by means of which he irrigates his fields. This is the case of farmers who use sewage water, which has given the best results wherever it has been used. The farmer obtains a considerable yield from the soil, less than that obtained by market gardeners, but his cultivation costs are lower and his profit is still sufficient to support him and his family. Finally, there's the agrieulteur, owner or farmer, who always grows vegetables in a more or less extensive kitchen garden, to provide food for his staff. In many circumstances, he can and must go even further. These days, people complain bitterly about the difficult situation facing agri-business. Its products have to contend with those imported from abroad, which are produced more cheaply and sold more cheaply. Certainly, in many circumstances, the farmer's situation is made difficult; but isn't it also sometimes his fault? Why, where economic circumstances mean that cereal production is no longer sufficient, does he insist on growing cereals exclusively? Wouldn't it be better to know how to modify his cultivation system, adapting it to the requirements of the moment? Certainly, in the present state of affairs, industrial crops are a powerful auxiliary, and among them, vegetable crops are among those that give the most assured results. They have the invaluable advantage over tree crops of paying for themselves in the short term, and therefore only committing capital for a very limited time. With fruit trees, you have to wait and take many risks; with vegetables, you harvest a few months after sowing. People object to the difficulty of outlets and transport. This reason is of little value. Fruits are more difficult to transport than vegetables, yet huge quantities of various fruits are exported every year. What's more, vegetables come in many forms; it's simply a question of knowing how to choose them to suit the circumstances. In the vicinity of large cities, all vegetables are good to grow, as transport is short; further afield, the choice will naturally fall on those that offer greater resistance to wilting. If, finally, means of transport become difficult, value will be concentrated in a small volume, and seed production will become an important industry. There is room everywhere for industrial vegetable growing; by spreading it, we'll be showing patriotism by improving people's diet, which in many respects still leaves much to be desired. Often, a farmer's vegetable production can even become a real market garden. The main cost of a market gardener's crop is the purchase of manure, which serves more as heating than fertilizer. He buys this fresh manure, and sells it once it has lost its ability to ferment and therefore produce heat; but there's a huge difference between the purchase price and the selling price; this difference is roughly equal to a third of the total value, and simply represents the cost of the heat used. Now, the farmer is a manure producer; if, instead of grinding this manure, he used it in the beds and then returned it to the field, he would save the price of the heat, which would cost him absolutely nothing. In fact, recent experiments have clearly shown that manure used in layers remains as rich as if it had been placed in millstones, as the plants do not draw on the manure in the layer, and evaporation is reduced as a result of covering with frames. We hope that these considerations will encourage farmers to embark resolutely on the path of vegetable production, in which they will find many advantages. With this in mind, we have endeavored to show the costs involved in each crop, as well as the benefits they are likely to bring. But while it was useful to give precise information, it could be dangerous to generalize; so we have endeavored to cite particular cases and have indicated the localities where these results have been obtained. We hope to see more of these success stories, and will be happy to record any that are brought to our attention. It is a plant of great antiquity and extremely widespread use; as a result, it is hardly possible to give a precise indication of its place of origin, for the reason that, cultivated in all gardens, it may have escaped and been taken as indigenous where it was only sub-spontaneous. It has been collected in a state that is, all indications suggest, truly wild in south-west Siberia, in Soongarie. This plant, which has tunnelled bulbs branching out into numerous cloves which, in practice, are called pods, has long, narrow leaves. These have sheaths which, when joined together, form a kind of cylinder on top of the bulb. From the center of these leaves rises a spike bearing flowers enclosed in a membranous spathe when young. In cultivated garlic, these flowers are usually replaced by bulbous buds. Uses. - Garlic's fiery, highly aromatic flavor makes it a widely used condiment plant, with a large place in the cuisine of southern countries. Cultivation. - Garlic is grown in all vegetable gardens, over widely varying areas, depending on consumption needs. In Brittany, in the Bay of Mont-Saint-Michel, the plant is grown over often considerable areas; but whether in kitchen gardens or in large-scale production, cultivation methods are exactly the same. Moderately fertile clay-silica soils are particularly suitable for this plant, which fears fresh fertilizers and too much water 121 Fig. 2 - Ordinary garlic. in the soil. In Brittany, the soil to be used for this crop is ploughed; then, after harrowing and rolling, it is planted. This is done using the pods that make up the garlic bulb (fig. 2). Most often, the pods that could not be sold because of their defective condition are used for this purpose. This is a bad practice, as it leads to the use of heads that have not been well received and which only produce inferior quality products; on the contrary, it is advisable to reserve very good heads for multiplication, as the yield obtained will be more abundant. Planting is usually done in February or March; in the south of France, it can be done in autumn. Pods are transplanted either with a finger or with a planter, at Om, 10 on the rows, the rows being Om, 15 apart. In regions where this crop is grown extensively, a line of parsnips is usually sown every meter, to shelter it from the sun. During the summer, parsnips are weeded once or twice if the land is grassed. Harvesting is done by hand in July. Yields vary greatly from one year to the next, as garlic often rots, reducing the harvest by varying proportions. In good years, we harvest up to 5,000 kilograms per hectare; this yield is reduced by a third when the season has been unfavorable. The selling price also varies considerably: when demand is high and the produce is of good quality, it can fetch up to 100 francs per 100 kilograms; but it drops to 25 francs for inferior products. Diseases and insect pests. - Garlic is attacked by the garlic moth, which does not cause much damage. However, we have much more to fear from a disease which, in practice, is called grease and which consists of the rotting of the bulb. This disease seems to be bacillary in origin. It is difficult to prevent. However, by cultivating in healthy soil, by using only healthy pods for planting, and by not letting the crop return too often to the same spot, garlic can be preserved to a certain extent from the disease that ravages it ? 3 2Js-3 ARTICHAUT According to recent research, the Artichoke does not appear to be a botanical species, as Linnaeus believed, but a derivation of the wild cardoon that grows spontaneously throughout the Mediterranean region. As a result, its real name would be Cynara cardunculus, variety satira (Moris). Artichoke is a perennial plant with a large underground rhizome. Each year, it produces leaves 1 m to 1.50 m long, whitish-green on top and turning woolly-white underneath due to the abundant hairs covering them. These leaves are deeply incised in adult plants, much less so or not at all in young individuals. In the wild type, the lobes are terminated by vulnating prickles. In the center of the leaves, a robust branching system rises in spring, bearing a few lateral flower heads and one terminal one. These flower heads become very large in cultivated artichokes, and form the edible part of the plant. The flowers are azure-blue and give rise to dark-brown-striped, grey fruits (achenes). Seeds are germinative for five years. Uses. - Artichokes are eaten for their inflorescence receptacle, which becomes fleshy and highly developed in cultivated varieties. The bases of the bracts are also eaten. In some cases, the leaves are blanched and the main vein eaten, as with cardoons. Varieties. - There are many cultivated varieties of this plant, but only three or four are commonly grown: GROS VERT DE LAON. - Vigorous, hardy plant. This is the most highly regarded variety for large-scale cultivation. It is recommended for the very strong development of its apples (flower heads) and the abundance of the flesh of its receptacle. The scales (bracts) are green, pointed and divergent, giving the apples a spiky appearance (fig. 3). ARTICHAUT CAMUS DE BRETAGNE. - The apples are broad and short, with brownish-green or purplish scales that form a ball-shaped head. This variety is recommended for its earliness. Large quantities are consumed in Paris, coming from Brittany and Anjou. In the south of France, the green and vtolet de Provence varieties are particularly popular, as is the white Artichoke in Roussillon and Algeria. - Artichoke is an undemanding plant, which can be grown in any kind of soil; however, it is only in rich, moist soils that it produces good yields. In rich but insufficiently moist soils, its growth stops early, and there is no reason to count on prolonged production, which, on the contrary, always takes place when the soil has sufficient moisture. Siliceous-clay and clay-limestone soils suit it very well; but we must never lose sight of the twofold requirement of Artichoke, which sums up the whole cultivation process, and which relates to the fertilization and watering it needs for the time it needs to vegetate. However, humidity must not be too staFig. 3 - Artichoke gros vert de Laon. gnant, as this would make it extremely difficult to keep artichokes over the winter, which they would have difficulty surviving in such conditions. In practice, artichokes are rarely propagated from seed, which has the double disadvantage of giving slow and often random results. Many of the young artichokes produced from seed tend to revert to the primitive type, and need to be carefully selected. The most widely used method is to remove branches from rhizomes in spring and plant them as cuttings; this procedure gives more satisfactory results than sowing; but as, in many circumstances, we do not have a sufficient number of old plants to be able to obtain cuttings in large quantities, we are obliged to resort to sowing, which should therefore be examined. Sowing. - There are three different ways to sow seedlings, which we will describe in order of merit, if you want to establish a large plantation, or if the frosts of a harsh winter have destroyed the old plants. The best method, insofar as it gives the quickest results, consists of building a warm layer of potting soil in March, in which Artichoke seeds are sown in rays spaced Om, 08 apart. The seed is covered with a centimetre of potting soil, and the soil is drenched to hasten germination, which takes about ten days. When the plants have emerged and produced their first leaf, they are thinned out to leave a distance of a few centimetres between the rows, leaving around 250 plants per frame. Their development is encouraged by frequent watering. Towards the end of April, young Artichoke plants already have three or four well-developed leaves. We'll have got them used to the action of the outside air by lifting the trays every day, eventually removing them completely. Now that the plants have become robust, it's time to start planting them. Once the soil has been smoked, it must be ploughed deeply with a plough for large-scale planting, or with a spade for small-scale cultivation. Then, after harrowing, lines of Om, 80 apart are drawn on the surface, along which the planting will take place. Once the soil is ready, the Artichoke plants, which have been raised under a frame, are carefully removed to avoid dislodging them. However, as the plant is already equipped with a long pivot that digs deep into the soil, it should be cut to give the plant lateral roots which, by increasing its roots, will hasten the development and flowering of the Artichoke. Planting should be done after transporting the plant in baskets, taking the utmost care. Planting with a planter has the disadvantage of breaking the root ball, so it is best done by hand, at Om, 80 on the rows. After planting, watering is necessary to facilitate recovery. Another, simpler method is to sow the seeds in the ground in April. Seeds should be sown very lightly, or better still, one by one at around Om, 10 in all directions. The seeds should be sown very lightly, or even better, one by one, at about Om, 10 in all directions, with a basin to hasten emergence, which, despite this precaution, usually takes around twenty days. When the plant is sufficiently strong, it is transplanted into place, taking care, as before, to pinch the pivot and to transplant into a root ball if possible. Artichokes propagated in this way rarely flower in the first year, whereas they do in the previous system. In order to avoid transplanting, which, as we have just seen, is not without its difficulties, we sometimes sow directly in situ. This is done by sowing five or six seeds in potted soil every Om, 80. This sowing is done in April, and in May, when the plants have emerged, only the most vigorous are left and the others pulled out. The advantage of this method is that it requires little labor, but the disadvantage is that it doesn't produce any produce in the first year. Whenever possible, therefore, given the need for sowing, it is advisable to give preference to layer propagation. Whichever sowing method you choose, you will almost invariably see a certain number of seedlings covered with prickles. These plants should be rejected, as they represent degenerate plants, and the choice should always be made for plants whose leaf blades are only slightly divided and devoid of prickles. Whether or not the plants have produced flowers in autumn, they should be protected from frost when the first cold spells arrive. To do this, around November, the tips of the large leaves are cut off, the remaining ones are tied together with straw, and the plant is mounded with soil taken from around it (fig. 4). Leave things as they are until the cold sets in. When the thermometer falls to 5 or 6 degrees below zero, it becomes necessary to shelter the part of the leaves that emerges above the mound of earth. This is usually done using DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 2 either leaves or litter spread over the artichokes. During the winter, if the weather turns milder, it's a good idea to uncover them, even if it means covering them again if the cold comes back. In February, if there's no longer a risk of severe frosts, the bedding can be removed for good, so that it can be used again the following year. If, on the other hand, leaves have been used, they should be spreadFig. 4. - Artichoke, butted. in the plantation to be buried during ploughing. It is estimated that about 12 cubic meters of either leaves or litter are needed to shelter the artichokes planted in one hectare. At the end of February, or in the first days of March, the artichokes are started, and a fertilizer is spread on the soil, consisting of slurry or half-decomposed farm or horse manure, at a rate of around 25,000 kilograms per hectare. This fertilizer is ploughed into the soil, which can be done with a plough in large-scale cultivation, on condition that the work is finished with a hoe around the artichoke vines. Eyeletting. - About a month after ploughing, when the artichokes have regained their growth and formed strong tufts of green, they should be subjected to an operation known as "œilletonnage". If you then examine what has become of an Artichoke plant, you'll see that the main stem, having ceased to lengthen, has produced offshoots from the underground rhizome; it's to these offshoots that we give the name "œilleton" in practice. If they were all left on the stump, the result would be as many aerial branches producing flower heads; but these, being in very large numbers on the same plant, would be small in volume and therefore of almost no value. The aim of "œilletonnage" is to remove all these extra branches, leaving only the two most vigorous; the result is well-developed artichoke heads. This operation, which, as we have said, is carried out in the first days of April, needs to be carried out with great care, as it determines the quality of the harvest. When the time comes to do this, uncover the Artichoke's stump with a spade; then, with the help of a wooden spatula, remove all the weaker eyelets, leaving only the two strongest; these should be chosen not only from among the best available, but also from among those which are the lowest on the rhizome. All the eyelets removed should take with them a piece of the rhizome on which they originated, but without damaging it; the operation is completed by smoothing out the wounds made on the stump, which is then covered again with soil. The detached eyelets are used to propagate the artichoke. This method of propagation offers a number of advantages over sowing: in addition to being quicker than the latter, in that it usually produces results in the first year, it faithfully reproduces previously cultivated varieties; whereas with sowing, as we have seen, there is always the risk of a certain number of plants reverting to the wild type. What's more, it's much more economical, since, as we shall see, it requires no special care. The eyelets used to propagate Artichoke will be chosen from all those, often eight or ten in number, that are removed from each strain. Those with a stub or stump fragment will be chosen, and in all cases will have a small, well-constituted stem bearing at least three or four leaves (fig. 5); the others will be rejected as too weak and offering little guarantee of recovery. The strongest plants are not the best; they are often not even used, as they have the disadvantage of flowering too early. Before planting, the eyelets should be prepared. To do this, flatten the cut part with a serpette and shorten the roots, which are often either too long or too badly damaged during uprooting. Milletons are widely available on the market. Whether you buy them from horticulturists, or find them on the market, their price generally varies from 2.50 to 4 francs a cent. They should not be too wilted, as this would make them difficult or even impossible to plant again. Carnations are planted in ploughed and smoked land. The lines are spaced at Om, 80 or 1 metre, depending on the fertility of the soil; for it is clear that when the soil is very rich, artichokes grow vigorously, and it is therefore necessary to leave a greater distance between them than in the opposite case. At the same distance as between the rows, the eyelets are planted two by two, leaving a distance of about Om.25 between them. In this way, you can be sure that the plantation will be well stocked. If possible, water each transplanted eyelet, otherwise choose a foggy day for planting. Recovery is assured when planting is done with care, using vigorous carnations which have not been allowed to wither and which have a heel already bearing a few roots. For small-scale cultivation, we recommend planting a number of carnations in pots of Om, 10 and placing them on a warm bed. In this way, the plant picks up very quickly, and when it emits new leaves, it is removed from the pot and placed in position, thus obtaining a faster product than with direct planting in place. As soon as the plants start to grow, they are hoeed twice during the summer. Around August, or early September, a few Artichoke heads appear, which can be harvested and fetch a premium price on the market at this time of year. Artichokes grown from carnations should be treated in the same way as those grown from seed in autumn. All branches with heads should be cut off flush with the ground. Winter will be spent in the conditions we have indicated, and when spring comes, we'll start and grommet. In the south of France, it's best to carry out eyeletting in autumn, as it helps the plants to recover, and produces vigorous plants the following spring, which will certainly flower during the summer. It has also been recommended (1) to use branches as seedlings, which, (1) Daurel, Bull. de la Soc. des agric. de France. having borne heads, are cut close to the ground; once in place, they are said to regrow vigorously and form a robust clump. In the Midi region, Artichokes don't need to be covered; they're simply butterflied, which means they spend the winter more easily than in the Paris climate, where they often have to choose between freezing if they're uncovered too much, or rotting if the covering is too thick. Second year of cultivation. - Regardless of the cultivation method used, and whether or not the plants have produced in the autumn following planting, the second year will produce an abundant harvest. In our climate, flower heads begin to appear in June. Each retained eyelet will usually produce one main artichoke or head and two or three secondary ones, known as fins. Each clump, as we've said, is usually made up of two feet, to which two ceilletons have been retained, which means that a similar clump is capable of producing four heads and eight fins. But it's not uncommon for one or even two of the eyelets to be missing, which can cut production in half. Harvesting takes place when the flower heads are fully developed, but well before they are ready to flower, which is indicated by the fact that the top bracts open to reveal the purplish center bracts. The fins should be harvested relatively younger than the heads, as they are usually eaten raw, and must be very tender for this purpose. When harvesting is complete, the branches are cut off at ground level and all dry leaves removed; the ground is then completely cleared and given a good hoeing to loosen the surface of the soil that has been trodden underfoot during harvesting. It doesn't take long for the leaves to grow back abundantly, and in autumn they are treated as described above. An artichoke plantation can produce abundant harvests for four years. It is therefore advisable to establish a new plantation in the third year, in order to obtain a sustained yield. Growing costs. - To estimate the cost of growing artichokes, we need to take into account the first year, when yields are usually very low, and planting costs relatively high. We'll do this by assuming that, as is generally the case, carnations have been used for planting. Their usual price, when we are obliged to buy them, is 3 to 4 francs per hundred. As mentioned above, artichokes are covered over winter either with litter or leaves. The latter is generally used when the plants are close to woods. In Chambourcy, for example, which is very close to the Saint-Germain forest, artichokes were widely cultivated, as the right to collect leaves was formerly granted for a fee of O fr. 70 centimes for a one-horse carriage, i.e. 2 cubic meters. Today, this price has risen to 8 francs for the same quantity: artichoke cultivation has therefore declined in importance in this locality. Litter is even more expensive, costing 5 francs per cubic meter; it has the advantage of not rotting completely the first year, and can be used again the following year by mixing it with half new litter. Here are the first-year cultivation costs for a plantation in the Oise region: Rent for one hectare of land........... 250 fr. Plowing and harrowing..................... Manure: 25,000 kilos at 10 p. 1000...... Plant purchase; 15,000 at 3 50 p. 100.... Planting.................... ......... Three hoeings at 30 fr..................... Bedding............................ . . . .. Ridging and covering................... Harvesting and packing..................... 100 250 175 30 90 60 50 25 Total....... 1030 fr. For the second year, costs are reduced by the purchase price of eyelets and planting, but increased by the cost of eyeletting and extra work for the harvest, which is becoming more abundant. Rent................................... 250 fr. Fertilization................................. 250 Plowing.................................. Three hoeings............................ Carnation............................ 30 90 50 Harvesting and packing..................... 100 Bedding................................... Ridging and covering................... 30 50 Total............ 850 fr. To establish the average cost of cultivation over the four years of the plantation, multiply the total cost of the second year by three, add the cost of the first year and divide by four, giving an average annual cost of 895 francs. As for the harvest, it is, as we said, low in the first year, and we can only harvest a number of heads equal to a quarter of the planted plants, i.e. around 3,700. These autumn artichokes sell well, with an average price of 15 francs per hundred. In the second year, with only two heads per plant and four fins, this constitutes a minimum which is almost always exceeded. An average price of 10 francs per hundred is accepted for heads, of which 20,000 per hectare are harvested in a plantation planted at Om, 80 in all directions. Fins are usually only worth 3 francs per cent. First-year harvest: 3,700 heads at 15 fr. per cent............. 555 fr. Second-year harvest: 20,000 heads at 10 fr. per cent............ 2,000 40,000 fins at 3 fr. per cent......... 1,200 Second-year product... 3,200 Multiplying this product by three, the number of harvest years, then adding the first year's product and dividing by four, we get an average annual income of 2,538. If we subtract the average annual costs, we're left with a sum of 1,643 francs. This income, which is still quite high, as we can see, was much higher a few years ago; for the same costs, we obtained infinitely higher selling prices. This drop in value was due to the enormous quantity of artichokes whose markets were flooded by the large areas now devoted to this crop around Paris, in Brittany, Poitou and the South of France. M. d'André, professor of agriculture in the Pyrénées-Orientales region, estimates that 4 or 5 thousand hectares of land around Perpignan are devoted to the cultivation of early artichokes. In the south of France, this crop is advantageous, as the early products obtained, as well as the late harvests that can be made in autumn, fetch a relatively high price. According to M. Daurel, the first artichokes to be harvested in the Médoc marshes and on the Gironde islands sell wholesale for 4, then 3 francs a dozen. Under these conditions, the crop becomes one of the most lucrative. For some years now, artichokes have been consumed almost continuously throughout the year in Paris and other major cities, with winter and early spring production supplied by Algeria. In the vicinity of Algiers and Philippeville, artichokes are widely grown, and under these conditions are very profitable. If growers took a little more care in harvesting and packing the produce, it would arrive fresher and sell for even higher prices. Seed carriers. - To obtain seeds that will give good results, in spring, select the plants with the most developed heads (fig. 6). Remove the fins, leaving only the main head. When flowering is over, it is a good idea to cover the heads with bells placed at the end of a stile, as we recommend for lettuces. Diseases. - Artichokes are affected by a disease caused by a parasitic fungus (Ramularia cynaræ, Sacc), which, as M. Prillieux has pointed out, causes considerable damage to crops in the south of France. The only way to combat it is to prevent propagation by burning leaves that become covered with rust spots. Asparagus (Asparagus officinalis, Linnaeus) is a plant that grows spontaneously in dunes and, in general, in siliceous soils. Its main root obliterates early on, so that a large number of tracer roots develop, most of which are adventitious in origin and grow from a short, stubby rhizome; this plant is known as a Fig. 7 - Asparagus claw. claw (fig. 7). From this claw, erect, highly branched annual branches grow each spring, bearing small scale-shaped leaves with multiple buds in their axils, which develop into cylindrical twigs, about 1 to 2 centimetres long, forming a sort of small bundle, most of which are sterile. It's these branches that, in common parlance, are improperly referred to as leaves. A number of these branches end in flowers, which are female on some plants and male on others, as asparagus being a dioecious plant. The female flowers are followed by red berries with three chambers, each containing two seeds, up to a maximum of six, but some of these seeds often abort and the number is reduced accordingly. The seed is black and shiny, and remains germinative for five years. Uses. - Branches emerging from the soil, called thurtons, are eaten either white, when the asparagus has been covered with soil, or green, in which case they are known as pea asparagus, because they are eaten cut into small, pea-like pieces. Varieties. - Among the very many varieties, most of which take their name from the locality where they are grown, only three should be singled out as truly distinct types: HOLLAND ASPARAGUS. - The shoots of this variety are large and rounded, and are tinted pink or purplish-red when exposed to light. ASPERGE HATIVE D'ARGENTEUIL. - The tips of the shoots are slightly pointed and the scales are tightly pressed together. The thurions are much larger than in the previous variety. This is the earliest of all Asparagus varieties (lig. 8). ASPERGE TARDIVE D'ARGENTEUIL. - The scales are less compressed and open slightly. This variety continues to produce when the previous one is almost exhausted. Sowing. - In practice, asparagus is propagated exclusively by seed, and sowing must be carried out with particular care to ensure the grower Fig. 8. The soil in which the seed is sown must be, as far as possible, healthy, light and rich in humus, which Asparagus is very keen on. It has been argued that it is better to sow in poor soil, on the grounds that plants placed in well-smoked soil will suddenly sprout new growth. This reasoning does not stand up to serious examination of the facts, and there is no more reason to sow Asparagus in lean soil to see it regrow later than there would be to entrust a child to a dry nursemaid, on the pretext of seeing it regrow quickly when its diet becomes better. What is gained in youth is well gained, and the effects will be constantly felt. Once the soil has been chosen, we spread a layer of decomposed manure on its surface, the thickness of which varies according to the richness of the soil. During this operation, which is usually carried out in March, any stones that may be found should be removed from the soil. The soil should be as loose and clean as possible. After ploughing, if the soil is cloddy, it is harrowed vigorously with a hooked-tine fork, and the preparation is completed with a rake to make the surface absolutely even. Once the soil has been loosened, lines are drawn on its surface, using a radiator and a chalk line, about Om, 12 to Om, 15 centimeters apart. These rays should be about Om, 03 deep; every five lines, a path should be left to allow maintenance work to be carried out without walking between the lines. Sowing takes place in the first fortnight of April, but can be extended to the end of the month if the season is poor. The seed is spread by hand, so that it is 2 or 3 centimetres apart in the radius. The seed is covered with a light raking motion, which causes the soil to fall back into the rays; the ground is now more or less level again, and the lines are barely visible on the surface. The whole area is then covered with an abundant mulch, for which we use with advantage the manure coming out of the beds or mushroom wheels; in all cases this will be highly decomposed cheyal manure. Emergence usually takes place about a month after sowing. During this time, care must be taken to ensure that the soil is not overgrown with weeds, and that it does not dry out; both these problems can be prevented by hand weeding and watering where necessary. When the young plant emerges, it appears as a small green filament that pierces the soil, covered with a few small scales from the axils of which the small sterile branches will emerge. If the seed has been a good one, the lines will soon turn green as all the young asparagus grows. About three weeks after emergence, the plants are thinned out to leave a distance of Om.08 to Om.10 between each plant. To do this, you need to take advantage of damp weather, or wet the soil beforehand. Then, armed with a small wooden spatula, you remove the plants that are too close together. This should not be done haphazardly, and only the weakest plants should be removed. As a result of this thinning, about 75 to 90 plants per square metre remain. Throughout the summer, the only care to be taken will be DYBOWSKI. - Cultivation, 2nd ed. a repeated watering and weeding, to keep the soil free of weeds. In autumn, usually towards the end of October, the stems, which have reached a height of around Om, 50 to Om, 60, turn yellow, then dry out. The time has come to cut them off with pruning shears at around Om, 05 above ground level. No care is needed throughout the winter. The plant, prepared as described above, is ready for use the following spring. It has often been suggested that only seedlings that have spent two years in the nursery should be used. This practice has the disadvantage that, by leaving the plants in place for so long, they get in each other's way and only produce weakened individuals. One-year-old seedlings will always be the best when grown with the necessary care. The so-called eighteen-month plant was also advocated, i.e. sown in July, the young asparagus plants were not put in place until the spring of the second year, but under these conditions, the individuals are too weak to survive the first winter without danger. This system has been completely abandoned by skilled growers, because, although it requires more time, it gives less good results than the one which consists in using the one-year old plant raised with care. Plant selection. - When it's time to plant, the plants raised in the nursery should be carefully removed. This is done either with a flat-tined fork, or with a flat-tined hook. There is a choice to be made between claws, not all of which are equally suitable for use. Preference should be given to those with large, vigorous buds and abundant, healthy roots. Even with severe sorting, rarely more than a tenth of a claw falls to waste in a nursery that has been well cared for. There's no harm in pulling them out a few days before planting; the claws don't suffer at all from being out of the ground, as long as they're placed in a cool, slightly damp place like a cellar, for example. Some growers even claim that recovery is better when the plant has dried out a little in the air. Choice of soil. - Asparagus is not very demanding in terms of the mineralogical nature of the soil; it is more demanding in terms of its physical qualities. The most important thing to ask of a plot of land to be planted with asparagus is that it be healthy, i.e. free of stagnant moisture. Soils of any kind in which water can be found, either permanently or only during the rainy season, at a shallow depth below the surface, should under no circumstances be used for this crop, unless drained first. Stagnant humidity should be avoided, as any soil that retains excessive moisture by its very nature is unsuitable for growing asparagus. It is for this reason that essentially clayey soils are unsuitable, unless their condition is modified by energetic amendments. With this restriction in mind, it can be said that any soil is almost equally suitable for Asparagus. The very fine crops grown at Argenteuil are grown in limestone and clay-limestone soils; around Orléans and Bordeaux, remarkable harvests are made in siliceous sands; finally, we could mention the crops grown at Pont-Chartrain in peat bogs reclaimed by drainage, which give excellent results. There is therefore no need to worry about the nature of the elements making up the soil to be used; but it is important that the layer of arable soil be of sufficient thickness. This depth needs to be at least Om, 30, because of the cultivation work the soil will have to undergo. Soil preparation. Fertilization. - Once the site has been chosen for the asparagus plantation, it's a good idea to plough the land, either by hand or with a plough, depending on the size of the plot, in the autumn before planting. This first ploughing will loosen the soil and rid it of any weeds. If the soil is stony, this first ploughing should be used to remove the stones. As soon as the hard frosts have passed, which is usually towards the end of January, the soil should be fertilized and ploughed or spaded in. It is important that this first manuring is abundant; almost all fertilizers are good for Asparagus cultivation; however, those to be given preference are either city sludge and sweepings, which are used exclusively in Argenteuil cultivation, or decomposed farmyard manure or horse manure. In all cases, manuring should be carried out at a rate of approximately 1 cubic meter per are, or 60,000 kilograms per hectare. The fertilized and ploughed land must now be prepared for planting. To do this, furrows of around Om.50 wide and Om.10 to Om.15 deep are dug into the soil, either with a spade or hoe, or with a plough if the cultivation is large. Between each small pit, a free space of around Om.70 is left, over which the soil from the digging will be thrown. As a result of this operation, the ground will present longitudinal furrows, called trenches, alternating with ados. The middle of one trench should be 1.20 m from the middle of the neighboring trench. These distances are by no means absolute, and can be varied in one direction or the other without great inconvenience. In Thourotte in the Oise department, for example, there are some very fine plantations planted with lines only 1.10 m apart; in Argenteuil, on the other hand, the space left free between each of them is 1.40 m. The most important point is not to give the trenches a false appearance. The most important thing is to ensure that the trenches are no deeper than indicated above. The bottom of each trench should be levelled with a rake if the work has been carried out with a spade or hoe; stones, roots or other foreign bodies which might interfere with planting should be removed. Planting. - Once the soil has been prepared in advance, as mentioned above, planting should be carried out in March or April, either with the seedlings removed from the nursery, or with purchased claws. In all cases, only those that are of good appearance and guaranteed to come to the right place should be used. When planting, start by drawing a line down the middle of each trench, to ensure the planting is as regular as possible. On these lines, the claws are planted at a distance of around Om.90 to 1 metre. It is not necessary to increase the distances any further, as this would excessively reduce yields per hectare; but it would be disadvantageous to reduce them much, as the plants would then get in each other's way and produce inferior products. Moreover, we must not lose sight of the fact that planting is done for a large number of years and that, as a result, the clumps, which are constantly increasing in volume, will later leave very little free space between them. From the distances indicated above, it follows that there will be around 9,000 plants per hectare; which means that, if you make your own plants under the conditions we have indicated, you will need to give the nursery a surface area of 120 square meters to be able to plant one hectare. To proceed with the planting, the soil is lightly dug at the spot where the claws are to be placed, taking care to arrange the bottom of this little hollow, which is about Om, 20 in diameter, in the shape of a small mound; the claw is placed on its top, and the roots are spread out in the hole, which, about Om, 05 deep, is filled with potting soil either from old layers, or from city sludge passed through the clay. This method of planting means that each claw placed on the soil is covered with compost, then with soil, which together form a thickness of around Om, 08. In careful plantings, it is advisable to cover the entire bottom of the trench with a layer of mulch, but this care can be neglected without great damage. Once planted, the spot occupied by each seedling should be marked with a stick, which should be driven in obliquely, avoiding touching the roots; in this way, the seedling will be protected when weeding takes place. Throughout the summer (fig. 9), the soil in the trenches is kept perfectly clean by repeated Fig. 9 - Asparagus, first year of planting. hoeing and weeding. During the first two years of planting, it is customary to use the teenagers by planting them with vegetables of all kinds, such as potatoes, beans, cabbage, etc. Quite frequently, we even plant in the trenches between the Asparagus; there is no great disadvantage to this, provided we are very sober and plant, for example, only one cabbage between each Asparagus plant in the rows. In autumn, the stems of young asparagus turn yellow and dry out; they are then cut, and the best thing to do is to burn them on the spot and spread the ashes around the plantation. By November, the ground should be completely clear, and only the fragments of stems left above ground indicate where the claws have been. At this point, it's a good idea to have some decomposed manure available, and to spread a layer of about ? or 3 centimetres over the entire surface of the trenches. This manure is only intended to act as a fertilizer, not as protection against the cold, which Asparagus do not fear. The winter thus passes without the Asparagus requiring any cultivation care. Second year of cultivation. - In the spring of the second year, the first operation to be carried out is to plant new asparagus claws where it has been noticed that those of the previous year have not grown. These locations must have been marked with a stick the previous year. At the beginning of April, or in the course of that month, young asparagus plants appear; it is then advisable to use the hoe to bring back a little soil from the ados on each plant, in order to consolidate them, and in this way cover the manure deposited the previous autumn. Intercropping will be possible again this year without any inconvenience. By May or June, Asparagus stems will have developed considerably, forming compact tufts of green. In this state, Asparagus are very susceptible to the wind, and if left to their own devices, there is a great risk that a certain number of stems will not only break off lengthwise, but more seriously, become detached from the claw, causing great damage to it. To avoid the serious inconvenience of detachment, which weakens the claws and often breaks the buds that are to develop later, it is a good idea to support the stems. In Argenteuil, we don't hesitate to use stilts, despite the expense involved, against which we tie the asparagus stems; we take care to drive the stilts obliquely at Om, 40 from the foot of the asparagus so as not to injure the roots; the stilts are driven in on the opposite side to the direction of the prevailing winds (lig. 10). With this method, Asparagus is absolutely consolidated by simple tying with a few strands of rye straw. In many localities, we are content to gather all the stems of a single clump and bind them together; this is better than leaving the asparagus to its own devices; but we must avoid binding too tightly, otherwise the branches caught in the center of the clump will turn yellow and dry out, which has the disadvantage of weakening the claws. Finally, pinching above the twentieth lateral branch has been recommended, and even practiced in some localities. In this way, asparagus is less exposed to the wind and more resistant to it, firstly because it is less elevated, and secondly because pinching makes the stem grow fatter and more resistant. The only drawback to this method, used by Mr. Parent, a grower in Rueil, is that it eliminates a certain quantity of the asparagus's green organs, thereby reducing its aerial nutrition. It has been claimed that this removal was compensated for by the number of asparagus fruits that T'on supprimait at the same time, fruits whose production always exhausts the plant that bears them. This is a mistake, which direct observation of the facts suffices to establish; in fact, fruits are only ever produced by the lower branches; by removing the top, we do not u= "M - "-- Ca -- Fig. 10. - Asparagus with a stake. reduce the number. With this restriction in mind, the pinching method should be considered advantageous wherever cultivation is carried out on a large scale, and where the expenditure on stakes would be too considerable; where cultivation is carried out with a plough, the use of stakes is made impossible, as they would hinder the free operation of ploughing implements. Stilts will continue to be used to advantage in mam cultivation. In the autumn of the second year, care is absolutely the same as in the first year. It goes without saying that throughout the summer, the soil is kept absolutely clean by repeated hoeing, more or less often, depending on how clean the asparagus is. At the end of October, the stems are cut back to a few centimetres above ground level, then incinerated, and the soil that has been brought back on top of the manure is thrown back onto the asparagus. The soil from the trenches, like that from the teens, is smoked again, either using sludge or decomposed horse manure. In the Oise region, either dried blood or guanos is used successfully, and the winter passes without any need for care. Third year. - First harvest. - Finally comes the spring of the third year, i.e. two full years have elapsed since planting. If the plant has been properly cared for, as we have indicated, and especially if fertilization has not been spared, and if the plant is of good quality, harvesting can begin at this time (1). To this end, towards the end of March, under the cli(1) A friend of ours who has followed step by step our planting instructions showed us a second-year plantation already producing thumb-sized asparagus that we were tempted to harvest. This planting was made with one-year-old seedlings. Asparagus should be buttered, generally two to three weeks before the normal harvesting time, so that when they are covered with soil and protected from light, they remain white for a certain length of time. Butting. - This operation is performed in different ways, depending on the type of cultivation adopted. In large-scale plough cultivation, asparagus is ridged using this instrument. To do this, the horse passes over the ados, and the mouldboard throws some of the earth from this ados onto the trench in which the Asparagus located to the left of the started ados are planted. At the end of the row, the plough picks up the same spur again, discharging the soil onto the right-hand trench. Two turns on the same spur are needed to load Om's asparagus trench with around 25 tonnes of soil. Once the first spur has been cleared, we move on to the next, and the end result is that each Asparagus trench is covered with a continuous mound of soil Om, 25 high. Once this work has been done with the plough, the whole is levelled out with a hoe, to break up the clods and create a perfectly loosened soil. In more carefully cultivated fields, a completely different method is used, which is infinitely preferable to the first. It only has the disadvantage of being more labor-intensive; but skilled growers, such as Mr Lesluin in Thourotte, do not hesitate to follow this procedure, even on a sixteen-hectare crop such as his. This process consists of using the hoe to bring the soil back to the spot occupied by each asparagus plant, so as to form a sort of vast molehill, Om.50 in base and Om.25 in height; this process has numerous advantages, because, in addition to avoiding the risk of injuring the asparagus roots, it will result in an advantageous change in the harvesting system. Harvesting the produce. - In the continuous line ridging system, harvesting is carried out using special instruments. The most convenient is a kind of gouge following a steel rod, which is fitted into a handle (fig. 11). As the asparagus grows and begins to appear above ground, the gouge is pushed in along the asparagus until the claw is reached, as can be felt by the opposing resistance; then, with a rocking movement, the asparagus is lifted off. This operation works well when the asparagus has grown straight; but if it is curved, it becomes impossible to follow it underground with the help of the gouge, which often cuts it at random and at varying heights. The cores thus left on the claw often end up rotting, and eventually Fig. 11. - Asparagus gouge. damaging the stump. Avoid replacing the gouge with a knife, whatever its shape, as the tip will inevitably injure the stump, or the Asparagus will not be cut in its entire length, and we have seen that it is important to remove it completely. When harvesting Asparagus using the molehill system used in Argenteuil, the soil is infinitely looser than when ploughing, so the soil is spread by hand until the stump is reached; then, weighing it down with the thumb, the stump is removed completely. This method produces asparagus of full length, without ever damaging the claw. The disadvantage of this harvesting system is that it takes much longer than the previous one. In the first year of harvest, which is, as we said, the third year of planting, you need to be sober and content yourself with cutting only two or three asparagus shoots per plant, otherwise you run the risk of tiring out the young stock and compromising the harvest in subsequent years. Bunching. - Asparagus harvested in baskets is, as soon as it is cut, taken to a cellar or storeroom, where, covered with grass and straw, it will keep for one or two days without damage. When a sufficient quantity has been harvested, the asparagus is sorted into three categories: the beautiful ones, which include all those larger than an inch, the medium-sized ones and the small ones. Sometimes deformed asparagus is also separated out; it is sold to herbalists for the preparation of diuretic medicines. To make bundles of equal size, molds are used, consisting of hollowed-out boards in the shape of the bundle, joined together by crosspieces (fig. 12). Molds for small and medium-sized Asparagus are usually Om, 12 in diameter; those for large Asparagus Om, 18, so that small bunches are set at around 2 kilogr, and large ones at 5. When making the bundle, care is taken to place the best ones around the edge and the smallest ones in the center. The bundle, once made, is tied with two wicker strands previously soaked in water (fig. 8). Autumn start-up. - In the autumn, when the dried-out stems have been cut and burned, any mounds that have been made for the sole purpose of growing white asparagus to the desired length should be removed. In plough cultivation, asparagus rows are ploughed over with the ploughing tool. The Fig. 12 - Asparagus bundling machine. mouldboard thus removes part of the soil; the rest is removed during the second round. This operation must be carried out very carefully, to avoid damaging the claw. It's better to leave too much soil, even if it means finishing the job with a hoe. In the molehill system, the soil is removed with a hoe or a hoeing tool; after this operation, smoking is carried out as in previous years. Fourth and subsequent years of cultivation. - In the spring of the fourth year, ridging is carried out as described above. The harvest is now complete. In most cases, growers remove everything that grows; on the other hand, those who are concerned about the future of their asparagus plants, take care to leave one or two beautiful asparagus plants to develop completely in the spring, in order to feed the stump from the aerial organs; this is a wise measure, the practice of which cannot be too highly recommended. In any case, harvesting should never be overly prolonged; it usually ceases towards the end of June, on St. John's Day (June 24), as growers say in their precise instructions. From now on, cultivation care will be identical every year, and can easily be summarized as follows: in spring, ridging, then harvesting until the end of June. In summer, staking or pinching of stems and maintenance hoeing. In autumn, stems are cut and incinerated, then pruned and fertilized. With proper care, the product remains strong for 15 to 18 years, more beautiful in the early years, more abundant in later years. Yields. - We have borrowed the following figures on yield and cultivation costs for one hectare of asparagus from a five-hectare ploughed crop grown by Mr. Broutin du Pavillon, a farmer in Fourneau (Loiret). Mr. Broutin estimates them as follows: Opening the row with a plow, one day of one horse and one man....... Line drawing, 2 man-days.... Purchase of 7,000 clusters at 70 francs per thousand. Planting, 10 man-days......... Miscellaneous expenses............................. 15 francs. . 490 35 53 Total............. 600 francs. According to Mr. Broutin, these expenses are for 20 years of cultivation, and the costs to be borne each year can be broken down as follows, for one hectare. Breakdown of planting costs per year. Land rent per hectare............... Six plows for relining, unlining and cleaning at 20 francs................. . . ... . ..... ... Two ways by hand at 25 francs........ One way for hand ridging..... Annual fertilization........................ Picking....................... ....... Bundling................................ Transport to the hall..................... 30 francs. 60 120 50 35 250 400 100 125 Selling expenses at 5%............... 350 Amortization of equipment.............. Miscellaneous expenses............................. 20 60 Total............... 1600 francs. Mr. Broutin's average product is half a bunch per clump, i.e. for 7,000 clumps, 3,500 bunches at 2 francs each, 7,000. After deducting costs of 1,600 francs, this leaves a sum of 5,400 francs, which represents Mr. Broutin's annual net profit from one hectare of asparagus cultivation. In the light of this information, which is not lacking in interest, because it shows the costs involved in planting with a plough, the production figure, and consequently the figure representing net profits, seems a little high to us, especially if we compare it with the following yields, which relate to a crop grown in the Oise region, and which, on the other hand, seem a little low to us. However, we didn't want to change anything, leaving the authors DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 4 the responsibility for the production figures they accused us of. At M. Lesluin's Montmacq site, planting is carried out with extreme care and covers an area of 16 hectares. It is planted in rows spaced m 10 apart, along which the vines are only planted at Om 90. Most of the maintenance work is done by hand, and the asparagus is ridged in molehills. Harvesting takes place only in the fourth year, and every year at harvest time, a few vigorous shoots are kept to keep the vegetation in good condition. Mr. Lesluin estimates planting and maintenance costs at 4,000 francs for the first few years of non-revenue, for a one-hectare crop. He also assumes that the duration of the harvest years is only thirteen years, and that each of these years must therefore bear the amortization of the first years' costs. Hence he establishes the annual costs as follows: Amortization of initial installation costs............................... 300 francs. Rent........................ . . . . . . . . . . . . 125 Labor costs.................. 245 Fertilizers................................. 104 Harvesting and bundling..................... 352 Miscellaneous costs............................ 200 Total costs......... 1326 francs. It can be seen that the total of these costs is significantly lower than that of Mr. Broutin's crops, which might at first sight appear abnormal, since the advantage would remain with hand cultivation. The reason for this difference is that, instead of using decomposed manure, Mr. Lesluin uses guano and dried blood, with which he is very satisfied. What's more, neither transport nor sales costs are taken into account in this Oise crop, as the produce is sold locally. The savings achieved in this way amount to 622 francs, which should be deducted from the costs indicated by Mr. Broutin. Mr. Lesluin says he only harvests 2,065 bunches per hectare at the very low price of 1fr,50 to 1fr,60, which gives a total product of 3,200 francs. Deducting the cost of cultivation from this sum, a net profit of 1,874 francs remains. It can be deduced from these figures that yields can be extremely variable, depending on whether we operate in more or less fertile conditions. In any case, however, asparagus cultivation, whose cash yield has fallen significantly in recent years as the crop has become more widespread in France, is capable of producing relatively high profits, approaching 2,000 francs per hectare per year in less favorable conditions. What should encourage farmers to take up this type of production is the ease with which it can be cultivated, which for the most part can be done with a plough. Forced cultivation on site. - Market gardeners in Paris only grow asparagus for the production of early vegetables. Their cultivation is quite special, and in no way corresponds to that carried out in the open air. Asparagus is planted in a sort of trench, only a few centimeters deep (around 5 to 6) and 1.10 meters wide. In each trench, market gardeners usually plant four rows of claws, leaving a space of Om, 25 between each row. Asparagus is planted at Om, 40. Horses are fertilized abundantly with decomposed horse manure, and between the rows we grow a few vegetables that don't take up much space: lettuces or radishes, for example. In autumn, the stalks are cut and the plants are fertilized again. In autumn of the second year, i.e. around eighteen months after planting, forcing begins. Asparagus plants are rarely left to rest for another year; however, forcing them only in the third year would result in more vigorous claws. Asparagus heating begins in November, shortly after the summer stems have dried out, and continues successively until the end of January. The first step is to install the boxes on the row of asparagus to be heated. These boxes are 1.30 m wide, so each trench is completely covered. Once the rows of boxes are in place, we fill them up to Om.10 of their upper edge with soil from the ados that we've dug; we add a wheelbarrow of potting soil per frame to make the soil lighter. The result of this work is that between each row of chests there is a path of Om, 50 which has been dug to provide the soil for the Asparagus. In these paths, layers of horse manure are established, either entirely fresh, or mixed in part with annealed manure, i.e. ground during the summer. The layer is laid around the trunks, filling the paths completely and rising to the top edge of the trunks, so as to surround them on all sides. This layer needs to be watered and compacted to allow fermentation to take place. After a few days, the layer heats up and the temperature of the soil inside the coffers gradually rises. To keep the temperature at a constant maximum, every 15 to 20 days some of the manure is removed and replaced by new manure, which is mixed with the rest of the old manure. During this Fig. 13 - Forcing Asparagus on site. time, the frames, which have been placed on top of the trunks from the start, are covered with straw mats, which are double-layered overnight (fig. 13). After about three weeks of this heating, the asparagus can already be seen emerging from the soil. To give the Asparagus tips a little color, which makes them much more prized, the mats are removed for a few hours during the day if the weather is fine; otherwise, the Asparagus remains white. Once harvesting has begun, it continues for twenty to twenty-two days, during which time care is taken to keep the temperature constantly high by shuffling the layers established in the paths. The harvested asparagus is put into bunches of Om, 12 in diameter, and we reckon that we can harvest, during the whole forcing period, about three quarters of a bunch per frame. The price of these bunches varies greatly according to the season, and especially according to the mildness of the winter. It's not uncommon to see these bunches selling for 25 to 30 francs in the depths of winter. Once the harvest is over, the boxes and frames are removed from the asparagus and transferred to others, and forcing continues throughout the winter. Usually, two asparagus squares are grown at the same time, and forced alternately, one year, the other the following year, so that each square rests for a year, during which time the outdoor harvest is all that's needed. However, some market gardeners don't hesitate to force the same asparagus every year. If they take care to smoke abundantly, they can produce a fine crop for ten years. Asparagus is a costly crop, requiring large quantities of manure to be constantly replenished. Once the manure has been used up, it can no longer be used by market gardeners, who sell it on to growers as fertilizer. The farmer would therefore find it advantageous to grow forced asparagus; the manure costs him nothing in fact, since he returns it to the fields after using its heat, without it having lost any of its richness, as analyses have shown. The intercropping done in the first years of planting is continued every year during spring and autumn. Forcing out old claws. - When an asparagus plantation is exhausted and about to be destroyed, it's a good idea to extract a last product by heating the old claws. This is done by building a warm layer Om, 50 cm high over the winter, filling it with potting soil and covering it with boxes and frames. Claws are planted very close together in the potting soil, almost touching each other, and covered with Om, 15 of potting soil. Asparagus comes out of the ground after ten or twelve days, and the harvest can last for almost three weeks. But Asparagus harvested in this way are infinitely less beautiful than those produced by the first method; moreover, as the claws are lost after this type of forcing, it can only be applied to plants that no longer have any value as regular production. Harvesting the seed. - To harvest good Asparagus seed, it is essential not to use just any plant; on the contrary, they must be chosen with the greatest care. The best seedbeds are young plants, four to seven years old, producing only good Asparagus and in fairly large numbers. On the contrary, avoid harvesting asparagus from plants that produce many shoots, but of small diameter, or from those that produce good quality asparagus, but in too small numbers. Harvesting takes place in October, when the berries have turned absolutely red. The berries are removed by hand, then crushed in a bowl of water. The pulp is removed by decanting, and the result is completely clean, shiny black seeds, which are left to dry in the shade on a cloth. Asparagus seeds retain their ability to germinate for five years. Insect pests. - Asparagus is attacked almost exclusively by a single type of insect: the leaf beetle, of which there are two species, the asparagus leaf beetle and the twelve-spotted leaf beetle. These insects only wreak havoc when they are larvae, which cover themselves with their greenish excrement, no doubt to be less conspicuous. In this state, they gnaw the stems and all the small twigs. It is quite difficult to guard against their ravages, and the means of destruction available are imperfect. In Argenteuil, a wide-mouthed container of water is carried to the foot of the attacked plants, or a jug of water with a wide funnel; the branches laden with locusts are shaken off and fall into the water, which is easy to destroy. Eggplant (Solanum Melongena, Linné) is native to eastern Asia, from where it has been transported to Europe and the new continent. This plant has stiff, upright branches bearing alternate, oblong leaves, frequently with prickles on the veins. The flowers, usually solitary, are light purple. They give rise to an often very large fruit (berry), induced by the calyx, which is accentuated and frequently covered with prickles. Seeds are depressed and yellowish. They retain their germinative capacity for five or six years. Uses. - The fruit is widely consumed, especially in southern France. In northern France, on the other hand, and even in Paris, they are much less widely used. They are eaten cooked and prepared in a variety of ways. Varieties. - LONG PURPLE EGGPLANT. - This variety is characterized by black-purple fruits, often no less than Om, 18 to Om, 20 by Ou, 09 in diameter, and slightly swollen towards the tip. The flesh is firm; the seeds are not very numerous, which is an advantage from a culinary point of view. This variety is especially recommended for the South of France, where it is almost exclusively grown, as it is a late bloomer and does not do well in the Paris climate. It is very fertile; in the Midi, it bears up to twenty fruits. (fig. 14). For market gardening around Paris, Fig. 15 - Long purple eggplant. an early sub-variety is preferred, whose cultivation, by virtue of its earliness, is more successful. ROUND PURPLE EGGPLANT. - The fruit is rounded and very large, often reaching the volume of two fists combined. The plant is much less fertile than the previous one; like the latter, the fruit is purple, but a little lighter (fig. 15). This variety should not be confused with the round, purple Aubergine de Chine, which looks very much like it, but which is easy to distinguish, for the reason that the fruit remains green wherever the calyx covers it, which is not the case for the varieties mentioned above. It is important not to confuse them, as Chinese Aubergines are pungent and not recommended for use. Fig. 15 - Round purple eggplant. Cultivation in the Paris climate. - Market gardeners have been growing this crop for a long time, but they are beginning to abandon it because of the competition they face from the Midi, where this plant comes much more easily. In the provinces and in farm kitchen gardens, where supplies of produce from the Midi are often difficult to come by, this crop still has its raison d'être; here's how it should be grown. As early as the end of January, a layer of horse manure capable of giving 20 degrees of heat is built, i.e. about Om.50 high, and fitted with large stoves. The chests inside are filled with Om, 15 of potting soil, and as soon as the fire has gone out and the temperature, after having risen for a few days to its maximum, has become constant, we sow the Aubergine seeds, covering them lightly with potting soil. The frame is kept tightly closed and covered with straw mats, which are doubled if cold weather is expected. After about eight days, the seeds germinate; from then on, remove the mats during the day and leave them on the frames only at night. Three weeks to a month after sowing, the plants have five or six leaves; it's time to transplant them. A new layer is prepared, less thick than the previous one, as the weather becomes less cold, and the young Aubergines are transplanted with the planter, spacing them out by about Om, 08 to Om, 10 in all directions, which amounts to about 200 per frame. After transplanting, we water with a fine apple to encourage the plants to take root; then we close the trays and cover them with straw mats for twenty-four hours, to prevent the plants from wilting. On the following days, the mats are removed and the trays covered only at night. Five or six days after transplanting, you can see that the plants have recovered, as they start to put out new leaves. From now on, whenever the weather permits, we'll give them air by lifting the trays on wedges for a few hours during the day. This procedure produces vigorous, highly resistant plants, whereas not giving them air would have seen them wither away. Finally, about a month after transplanting, the seedling must be planted permanently. This is done either on a new layer built for the purpose, Om.30 thick, or on layers previously used for early crops. In all cases, four plants are usually planted per frame, reserving the remaining space for lettuces and radishes. The plants should be planted in rootballs and watered thoroughly. To encourage recovery, close the frames and cover them with straw mats. Once the plants have taken root, air them and get them used to being aerated as much as possible, until the frames are completely removed in mid-May. Eggplants need to be pruned; the main purpose of this pruning is to remove the twigs that develop at the base of the stem, as these would usually remain unproductive and unnecessarily exhaust the plant. Then, when the Aubergines have flowered, we pinch off the ends of the branches to encourage the development of the fruits, which we leave six to ten in number, depending on the vigour of the plant. If the plants are too weak, it's a good idea to tie them up with a stake. Harvesting usually begins towards the end of June and lasts until September. Fruits should be harvested before they are fully ripe and when they are a beautiful purple color. Their color fades as ripening approaches.
