Even in the Paris climate, cultivation can be carried out in a simpler way. We've found it very successful, but it produces later-ripening plants. This second method consists in sowing the seeds towards the end of March, on a warm bed, transplanting the seedlings also on a bed in April, then, as they gradually become accustomed to the air, planting them out definitively towards the end of May, simply in the ground, with good exposure if possible. To do this, the ground is abundantly smoked, and the Aubergines are planted at Om, 60 in all directions. Water well, and repeat frequently during the summer. Care should also be taken to cover the soil with thick mulch to keep it cool. Under these conditions, harvesting generally doesn't start until August, and lasts until the frosts. Growing in the Midi. - This is the region where eggplant cultivation and use are, by far, the most widespread. They are grown over very large areas, both for local consumption and for export. Sowing takes place at the beginning of March, on a bed and under a frame. When the plant has three or four leaves, it's a good idea to sow it again under a frame, as we've already indicated for cultivation in the Paris climate; But this procedure is flawed, as it leaves a weak plant with insufficiently developed roots, and a delicate plant that has not been gradually acclimatized to the action of the outside air. So we transplant it, which is easy since it can grow 200 feet in a frame, and we'll have a vigorous, well-built plant that we've had time to get used to.

make it hardy by giving it air every day by lifting the frame. Planting is definitely done around the middle of May. The soil in which this crop is to be grown must be specially prepared. Abundant manuring, consisting of farmyard manure or nitrogenous chemical fertilizers, is necessary to ensure the plant's proper development. In large-scale cultivation, ploughing is carried out in such a way as to create ados planks, one meter wide; each plank is separated from its neighbor by a sort of gutter, which is used to irrigate the crop. After ploughing, the soil is prepared by double harrowing. When the ground is well prepared, we plant, as we said, in May. A line is drawn down the middle of each bed, along which the eggplants are planted, keeping a distance of Om.10 on the line between each one. This planting must be done with great care; to facilitate recovery, the transplanted plants are lifted into a clod using a trowel, and transported to the field in flat baskets, in which they are arranged side by side. In this way, the clod of soil that accompanies the roots is not broken, and recovery is now assured. After planting, if the weather is dry, the soil is irrigated by running water into the gutters; this operation is repeated four or five times during the summer, and the soil is kept perfectly clean by hoeing, which also has the advantage of preventing the soil from drying out. Two hoeings are usually necessary. Harvesting begins in the month of Quin and continues until late summer. With this cultivation method, one plant of Aubergine is capable of producing up to twenty to twenty-five beautiful fruits. These are sometimes consumed in the vicinity of the production site, and sometimes shipped in crates or baskets to Paris or other major cities. Growing costs and yields. - Throughout the Midi region and in Algeria, where irrigation is possible, Aubergine cultivation is lucrative, although fairly labor-intensive. Expenses for a one-hectare crop can be calculated as follows: Rent for irrigable land.............. 300 francs. 40,000 kilos of manure at 5fr. per 10,000 kilos... 200 Plowing and harrowing................... 100 Raising of transplanted seedlings on bed... . 625 Planting; 30 days at 3 francs each . Two hoeing operations............................ 90 70 Irrigation costs........................ 100 Harvesting costs............... ......... 75 Total expenses........... 1560 francs. Eggplant plants being planted at Om, 40 on the rows and the rows being I meter apart, one hectare contains 25,000. Assuming an average of 10 fruits per plant, which is almost a minimum for the Midi, we obtain 250,000 fruits. These usually sold from 2 francs up to 3 francs per 100. Taking the minimum of 2 francs again, we obtain a yield of 5,000 francs, from which, after reducing cultivation costs, we are left with a net profit per hectare of 3,440 francs. To this sum should be added the yield that can be obtained by intercropping when Aubergines are not yet fully developed. These can consist of beans or lettuce, and provide an appreciable ancillary product. In the Congo, Dahomey, etc., our varieties do very well, but there are also local forms, often with yellow fruit, used by the natives, which are of very inferior quality. Seeds. - To harvest good quality seeds, as soon as the first fruits have formed, select those that appear to be the best and leave only two or three on the plant, which will be marked and will not produce any more. Fruit maturity is indicated by their paler color. They are left until they become soft, at which point they are split and the seeds extracted, dried in the shade after being washed free of the accompanying pulp. DYSOWSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. - Beet (Betta vulgaris, Linnaeus) is a plant that has been cultivated since antiquity. It grows wild throughout the Mediterranean region. It is characterized by a fleshy root, highly variable in shape, size and color. The very short stem above this taproot bears oblong, long-stalked leaves with red tints that vary in intensity in horticultural varieties. During the second year, the beet plant bears a sturdy floral branching, 1.50 m high. The flowers are greenish, with a faint appearance; they are grouped in sessile contracted cymes. The fruits that follow them are joined together by the receptacles. As a result, what is commonly known as a beet seed is a collection of fruits; this explains why, when one of these so-called seeds is sown, several plants usually sprout. Uses. - Beets are grown in vegetable gardens, and their roots are eaten either as a salad dressing, or even as a dish. Beet is widely used in Northern Europe, where it is used in the preparation of a variety of very tasty dishes. S Varieties. - Through cultivation, beet has undergone numerous variations based on shape, root coloration, earliness and nutritional value. In vegetable growing, the preferred varieties are those with deep red roots. They are moderately sweet, firm-fleshed and very low in fibro-vascular tissue, the abundance of which would make them inedible. BEET BIG RED. - The cylindrical root measures Om.10 in diameter and Om.35 in length. The dark red flesh is of excellent quality. This is the horticultural variety with the highest yield. A large part of the root is above ground (fig. 16). BETTErave Rouge NaINE. - This is a small, dark-red variety. Its roots, which are completely buried, are of good quality; their small size enables them to be sown in very close rows. TOADSTOOL BEET. - It is characterized by its root bark, which is all chapped and therefore resembles that of a black radish. The conical root is of good quality and intensely red in color, although the leaves are almost completely green. BETTeRaVe PlaTE DE BASSaNo - The root, whose diameter reaches Om, 15 to Om, 20, is flattened like a flat turnip; it is buried only by its lower part. Its red flesh, washed with white, is generally appreciated. EGYPTIAN FLAT BEET. - The flat root of this variety lies entirely on the ground: its dimensions are small, not exceeding Om, 12 in diameter, but its sparse foliage means it can be grown very close together. It is a very early variety, Fig. 17 - Flat beet from Egypt. particularly suitable for cultivation on beds (fig. 17). Cultivation. - Beet likes deeply loosened soil with a high proportion of fertilizer; loose, loamy soil is ideal. When grown in the field, they are sown in soil that has just been smoked. Seedlings are sown in rows spaced Om, 30 to Om, 50 apart, depending on whether more or less vigorous varieties are used. Broadcast sowing is also possible, but has the disadvantage of not allowing the cultivation methods required by this plant during its growth to be carried out with equal ease. Sow from the beginning of March to the end of May. Early varieties will be sown first, and will produce their products during the summer; on the other hand, those intended to provide food during the winter are sown in April, and large-growing varieties are chosen for this sowing. When the young plants have taken on two or three leaves, the first hoeing is carried out, which consists of passing the hoe between the rows to remove weeds and loosen the soil, and also on the rows to light the plant. However, we don't remove all the excess plants from the rows; on the contrary, we leave about twice as much as should be left permanently, i.e. during this first cultivation method, we leave a space of Om.10 to Om.15 between the plants in the rows. As soon as the beets have five or six well-developed leaves, we hoe them again, and remove any plants left over for possible accidental destruction. Finally, in the course of the summer, a third hoeing is useful to keep the soil free of weeds. It has often been suggested that beet should be sown in a nursery, either on a bed or in the ground, and then transplanted in situ when the root has grown to the size of a pen pipe. In this process, great care must be taken to ensure that the end of the root is not bent when it is transplanted into the soil, and to achieve this, long planters are needed, with which deep holes can be made. It is necessary to water several times to encourage recovery. Apart from the disadvantage of being more labor-intensive than ordinary cultivation, this method also has the more serious drawback of producing inferior quality produce. As a result of transplanting, there is a lull in vegetation, during which the fibrous tissue develops very abundantly, and the roots then become hard and stringy. Harvesting begins in June for early-sown varieties, and lasts until October. At this time, all beets should be uprooted and taken down to the cay, where they can generally be stored with the greatest of ease until spring, if care has been taken when uprooting to remove the leaves along with the plant collar. Beets are sold at Paris markets after being baked, either in a bakery oven or in water; but the first of these two processes produces results far preferable to the second. If you are in a hurry to grow beets in the spring to replace those stored in the cellar, you can sow them in the months of March and April. Under these conditions, it's a good idea to sow in the nursery, then transplant into the frames, at Om, 30 in all directions. Transplanting here has no great disadvantages, as it doesn't stop vegetation, as it picks up very quickly. As soon as the cold is over, the frames are removed and the beets are left to complete their development. If you use Egyptian beet, you'll be able to harvest it after two and a half months of vegetation. Growing for seed. - To harvest good seeds, it's best to select the best-formed roots at the time of harvesting. Only the leaves are removed, but care must be taken not to damage the terminal bud that gives rise to the inflorescence. They are preserved by covering them with sand in the cellar. In April, they are planted at Om, 80 in all directions, and when the beet comes into bloom, they are staked. It's a good idea to pinch the tips of flowering shoots to hasten fruit ripening. They are harvested around September. In the Bay of Mont-Saint-Michel, we grow Egyptian flat beet and red beet for seed. This crop is grown in a special way that deserves to be mentioned. In August, 20 kilograms of beets are broadcast over an area of 40 ares. This nursery will provide the seedlings needed to plant one hectare. Planting is done in March using a planter. The rows are spaced at Om, 70 and the plants are planted at Om, 50 along the rows. During the summer, one or two hoeing operations are carried out, followed by ridging using a horse-drawn ridging machine. This last operation should be carried out when the beets start to go to seed. Harvesting is done with a sickle in August; the seeds are threshed with a threshing machine or a flail after being placed in javelins, and then passed through a tarare and a sieve. These cleaning operations are not without their difficulties. The yield is around 800 to 1000 kilos per hectare, with prices ranging from 80 to 100 francs per 100 kilos. Cardoon (Cynara cardunculus, Linnaeus) appears, as we have said, to be a single plant with artichoke, which is merely a cultivated variety. It grows wild throughout the Mediterranean region. It is characterized by large, white, tomentose, pennatipartite leaves that can reach or even exceed Im, 50 in length. The stem carrying them is short and stocky, and forms an underground rhizome which, together with the main vein of the leaf, forms the edible parts of the plant. From this rhizome rises a robust branching every year, ending in voluminous flower heads containing azure-blue flowers in an involucre of sterile bracts, which give rise to oblong, grey, dark-brown flagellated fruits (achenes). The seeds retain their germinative capacity for seven years. In its spontaneous state, the plant bears vulnating prickles at the end of its leaf lobes and on all its organs. These have disappeared with cultivation, but only in certain varieties. Uses. - The main veins of the leaves, previously whitened by deprivation of light, are consumed in various preparations, together with 73 the underground rhizomes stripped of their bark. Varieties. - They are distinguished by the presence or absence of prickles on the leaves, and by the state of the rib or main vein of the leaves, which is sometimes filled with cellular, sapid tissue, and sometimes, on the contrary, hollow throughout the central part. CARDON DE TOURS. - This is one of the varieties with the least development, but its ribs are very full, and for this reason, it is widely grown, particularly around Tours and Paris. It is furnished with very vulnating prickles. CARDON PLEIN INERME. - The name of this variety contains a double promise which the plant does not fulfill: the ribs are not absolutely full and are, on the other hand, provided with prickles, which are rare it is true, but which are enough to make the plant unwieldy. This cardoon is larger than the previous one (fig. 18). SPANISH CARDOON. - A large variety with slightly hollow ribs, but inert. Much grown in the South of France. CARDON PUVIS. - An absolutely inert variety with hollow ribs, widely grown around Lyon. Cultivation. - Although Cardon is a relatively hardy perennial that survives winters more easily than artichokes, it can only be propagated from seed. There are two different methods of sowing Cardon, both of which have been used successfully. The first is to sow Cardon in a nursery and transplant it, as is done by market gardeners near Paris; the second is to sow it in the field, which is the preferred method in the south of France. If you adopt the method of replanting R° ~~ ~ 21 Fig. 18 - Cardon plein inerme. Cardoons, which has the advantage not only of saving time - which, incidentally, is not very useful, as these plants have plenty of time to come to fruition - but above all of occupying the soil for only a short time, a warm layer is built in mid-April; it is on this layer and under a frame that the seeds are sown in small rays, which are covered with Om, 02 soil. Germination takes place after eight days, and the young plant appears with two thick, spatula-shaped cotyledons. As soon as the young plant has produced two leaves in addition to these two cotyledons, it's time to transplant it. A number of cups equal to the number of Cardoon plants to be planted are then placed on a bed which need not be new. These cups should be about Om, 09 in diameter; when they are arranged side by side on the bed, they are filled with a mixture of garden soil and potting compost. Then transplant a plant into the center of each cup, taking care not to bend the root, but to leave it straight, as the success of the plantation depends on it. Immediately after transplanting, water with apples, then replace the frames, which are covered on the first day with straw mats to prevent the plants from wilting. Towards the beginning of May, it's time to start planting; in any case, the best time to do this is when the roots, which have elongated, begin to run around the inner surface of the flowerpot rim. Cardoon should be planted in fresh, substantial, moist soil of any kind. Clay-limestone soils suit it well, but it also thrives in siliceous soils, provided these are fresh and rich in fertilizer. The soil is prepared by deep ploughing, followed by harrowing; lines are then drawn on the ground, I meter apart, and on these lines, I meter apart, a spade is used to open a pot, which is then filled with a good shovelful of used potting soil. The Cardoons are planted one by one in this potting soil, I meter apart in all directions. The young plants are unstuck by turning the bucket upside down on your hand and giving it a sharp tug with an instrument of some kind, such as a planter. The clod of earth contained in the pot then emerges whole, without breaking, and is carefully planted in the potting soil. Immediately after planting, each plant should be watered, which will help it to take root again. A simpler method, but with much less certain results, is to sow in a frame, then transplant directly into the pots. This procedure is faulty; the recovery is poor, so a certain number of plants are lost, and those that remain have difficulty recovering, even if they are watered frequently. Finally, in many cultivations, seedlings are sown on the spot in pots. These seedlings can be sown from April onwards, and in the Midi region, where this procedure is most widely used, successive sowings are carried out so that not all the produce reaches full development at the same time. When the cardoons have emerged, only one is left per planting, the most vigorous. In this region, cardoons are grown on ridged ground, which is irrigated at least once a month. Whatever the cultivation method adopted, there is one thing that is constant: vegetation is very slow during the first two or three months of planting, then quickly takes off, and the plants, which until then had been weak, grow to such a size that the soil becomes completely flat. It follows from this observation that it is useful to plant intercrops to occupy the soil during the months of May, June and July; for example, beans, lettuce or spring cabbage can be grown between the lines of cardoons, which will not be in the way, and in this way an additional harvest will be obtained from the soil, which will help to pay for the cultivation costs. Cultivation methods are of little importance, and are limited to a few hoeings in summer to rid the soil of weeds, keep it clean and prevent it from drying out. As we have said, cardoons can only be eaten once they have been blanched, i.e. etiolated by the deprivation of light. The processes used to obtain this result are varied; we'll just mention the main ones. In the Paris climate, bleaching begins towards the end of August, and continues into September. It is important, whatever the suiyi process, to blanch this vegetable only as and when it needs to be consumed, as the plant does not tolerate this condition well, and there is a risk that it will rot if it is not consumed when it is white. Market gardeners around Paris carry out this operation in September; they begin by binding the cardoons with rye straw previously soaked in water; but as most cardoons are thorny and therefore not only unpleasant, but often dangerous to handle, they are forced to resort to a stratagem. The trick is to tie two sturdy sticks together about a third of the way up, using a rope about 80cm long. The first passes the two sticks under the base of the leaves, close to the ground; the second grabs the two longer ends, crosses them over themselves, and the Cardon leaves are thus caught between the rope and the two sticks. The two men then gradually raise this sort of collar and bind the Cardoons with two or three ties, enough to hold the leaves in place. Care must be taken not to tie the leaves too tightly, as this would cause them to rot if air were prevented. Once tied, the cardoon is surrounded by half a bundle of straw, which is held in place with ties. Fifteen to twenty days after this procedure, the cardoon is ready for consumption. Many growers, instead of stuffing, simply bind with three ties, then butter strongly with soil taken from around the foot. This more economical method is less successful than the previous one. In the Centre and Midi regions of France, Cardons are blanched by tying them with straw as before, then uprooting them, leaving a clod of earth around the roots. In the driest soil possible, a gauge about Om.50 deep by Im.50 wide is opened, the Cardons are placed close together and then covered with litter. Finally, sometimes, in these same regions, the Cardoons are bedded and covered with soil. This procedure has the serious disadvantage of requiring washing later on, which damages the Cardoons. When the cardoons are to be taken to market, they are trimmed by removing useless or rotting leaves, then cutting off all the rootlets so as to leave only the pivot. By leaving them in rootballs, they can be stored for a month or two in dry cellars, and only gradually released for consumption or sale. The cultivation of cardoons, too little used up to now, nevertheless offers serious advantages. The average price of a blanched cardoon varies between O fr. 50 and 0 fr. 75, and can even reach I franc when the plants are very beautiful. Stuffing is certainly costly, but the selling price is such that labor costs cannot be neglected. Growing for seed. - To produce Cardoon seed, select the best-quality plants with full ribs at the time of autumn stuffing. These plants are left in place, untied. As the frosts approach, cut off about a third of the length of the leaves, then mound the plants as you would an artichoke. In the spring, the ground around the plant is ploughed. It won't be long before a vigorous stem rises from the center of the leaves, soon to bear a number of flower heads. Remove all the smaller ones, leaving just two or three. After flowering, it is necessary to protect the seeds from rain, which would spoil them, and from birds such as goldfinches, which are fond of them. This is achieved either by bending the stalks so as to turn them towards the ground, or by covering them with a cloche, after having united the two or three flower heads into a bundle, using the same procedure as that employed for Lettuces (see this article). The same plants can be left in place, and the seed harvested for several years in succession. Seeds retain their germinative power better when left in the flower heads than when separated from them. Carrot (Daucus carota, Linné) is a plant native to Europe, where it is still found in the wild, scattered along roadsides or in fields with damp soil. Very convincing experiments, which clearly link our cultivated carrots to the wild type, were carried out in 1830 by M. de Vilmorin. Seeds harvested from plants growing spontaneously were sown in good garden soil. After a few generations, the root became absolutely fleshy in some of the young individuals; some were white and others colored yellow and red. The carrot is a biennial plant, with the edible part mainly made up of the fleshy taproot, followed by a thickened stem with compound, sheathing leaves. In the spring of the second year, from the center of these leaves rises the aerial branching ending in umbels composed of dirty-white flowers, followed by fruits which are paired (diakenes) when young, but separate when mature. They are covered with ribs fitted with hook-shaped extensions, so that these fruits, which in practical language are called seeds, hang DYBONSKI. - Cultivation, 2nd ed. one on top of the other, forming a sort of little ball. They cannot be sown in this state, and it is essential, before entrusting them to the soil, to subject them to a preliminary preparation, which consists of rubbing them between the hands, after having mixed them with fine sand. The seed becomes smooth or marbled, and is ready for sowing. It retains its germinative capacity for four to five years, but it's a good idea not to mix seeds of different ages together, as they take longer to germinate the older they get. For early crops, where it's important not to lose time, it's best to use seeds that are two or three years old. Uses. - Carrots are eaten in a variety of ways. They are a necessary condiment in many soups, and in some regions they are used to make jam. The fruit is used to make liqueurs, thanks to its aromatic properties, which are due to the presence of scented glands. Fruits intended for this use must not be marbled. Varieties. - Taken as a whole, there are many varieties of carrot, but many of these are used as fodder, and therefore do not concern us here. Carrots come in a wide variety of colors and shapes. Red carrots, to the almost general exclusion of all others, are the only ones grown for direct consumption and, above all, for sale. CARROT GRELOT. - This variety is the only one that should be used, not only for early cultivation, but even for spring sowing. It is characterized, as its name suggests, by an almost globular root (fig. 19), whose diameter is roughly equal to its length. It is a beautiful red color and extremely early. This variety is a perfected version of the Short Dutch Carrot. HOLLAND SEMI-LONG CARROT. - This variety, generally known as the Croissy Carrot, from which Crécy has been corrupted, is widely cultivated in the Paris area, where it enjoys special favor. Its red root is Om, 10 to Om, 14 long. CARROT DEMI-LONGUE NANTAISE. - This is a very good variety, resembling the previous one on the whole, but its root is more cylindrical and produces slightly earlier. Its cultivation is to be recommended. HOLLAND LONG CARROT. - Its root, much longer than that of the Fig. 19. - Grelot carrot. preceding varieties, resembles them in shape and color. It produces produce later in the season, but is also more abundant and has a remarkable shelf life (fig. 20). In the south of France, in addition to the above varieties, the following are still grown: Longue de Toulouse (fig. 21), and several yellow carrots that are only suitable for this region because of their low resistance to cold. Outdoor cultivation. - Carrots thrive best in deep, cool, light soil; cool siliceous and silico-clay soils are ideal Fig. 20 - Long Dutch carrot. Fig. 21 - Long carrot from Toulouse. in a very special way. In order to produce good results, they must be abundantly supplied with decomposed fertilizers, without which carrot roots will only grow small. Carrots like deeply loosened soils, but not too much lifted, as they would then dry out easily; so, after deep ploughing, the soil is trodden down, either with the feet in the vegetable garden, or with a roller in the field. In healthy, well-exposed vegetable gardens, sowing can begin as early as February. They are then planted in "costières", i.e. in beds sheltered from the north by a wall or palisade. Sowing is broadcast at a rate of 50 grams per acre of marbled seed. Once the seed has been spread as evenly as possible, it is lightly buried with a rake. If you're in a hurry to harvest an abundance of carrots, it's a good idea to let them occupy the land on their own; however, in the usual practice, as soon as the soil has been covered with a thin, even layer of top soil after harrowing with a rake, you plant a few rows of lettuce, which will be harvested long before the carrots have fully developed. Sometimes, romaine is planted and covered with cloches, so that two seasons' worth of carrots can be harvested from the same bed: the first will come from the carrots sheltered by the cloches, while the second will come from the soil left in the open air. In any case, as soon as the lettuce or romaine has been harvested, it's a good idea to replenish the base of the carrots that have become a little etiolated by the lettuce leaves, by covering them with potting soil spread by hand between the plants in a layer Om, 015 thick. After these initial sowings, for which we prefer to use Carrot grelot, we successively sow others, more or less frequently as required, until the end of July. This cultivation is no longer done in costires, but in full squares, using either the variety we have just mentioned, or rather, for the latest sowings, all the half-long or even long varieties. Growers in Croissy and neighbouring areas produce carrots specifically for sale in autumn and winter. They almost exclusively use half-long varieties. Their seedlings are sown in rows Om, 20 apart, using 30 grams of seed per acre. As soon as the plants have three or four leaves, they hoe them, using a very narrow hoe between and over the rows, leaving the plants 7 to 8 centimetres apart. To produce good quality produce, carrots need to be watered, not abundantly, but often, to keep the soil in a constant state of moisture. Even the growers of Croissy, who cultivate this crop in large fields, water repeatedly; it's only at this price that they obtain the products so renowned on the Paris markets. In the south of France, particularly in the Garonne plains, where this crop is grown on a large scale, half-long carrots are frequently sown in autumn, in September; they survive the winter, and their produce is harvested early in the spring. In the climate of Paris, where, as we have said, the usual sowing ends with the month of July, it is possible, with some advantage, to cultivate late, by entrusting the seed to the soil at the end of August or the beginning of September. In this case, we use the grelot variety, which is sown in beds. The roots only take on a small volume before the cold weather, and by November, when their yellowing leaves detach from the plant, they are the size of a good walnut. At this time, we remove by hand the stalks that don't hold, and cover the carrot bed either with litter or tree leaves, which we place in a layer of Om, 15. Throughout the winter, we can harvest carrots of very appreciable value, which, if they don't have absolutely all the qualities of new carrots, at least have the appearance of them, so much so that, sometimes, they are sold as such on the markets. However, there is no possibility of confusion for buyers, as these roots have no tops, whereas really new carrots always have them. Harvesting. - For current consumption and sales in summer and autumn, carrots are harvested when they have reached about two-thirds of their full development. It would be dangerous to let the roots go beyond this point, because then, as a result of the rapid development brought about by frequently repeated watering, the carrots split lengthwise, which is a cause of depreciation on the market. Carrots can be harvested after about three and a half months, but we never pull them all at once; on the contrary, we start by harvesting only the most developed ones: this thins out the remaining ones, which we don't pull until later. Carrots are sold in bunches of 30 to 40 for the Grelot variety, and 25 to 30 for the half-lengths. Immediately after binding, which is done with wet rye straw, the bunches are immersed in water and washed with a quackgrass brush, without rubbing too vigorously, which, by removing the outer part of the bark, would diminish the intensity of the red color of the roots, and depreciate their value on the markets. Carrots keep very well throughout the winter when buried in sand in a healthy cellar. In this case, we especially recommend the half-long varieties sown in June and July. In November, we pull them up and, after either cutting off the crown or pulling off the leaves by hand, we store them as we have just described. They also winter well, stored in a silo and protected from frost. Half-long carrots yield 5 to 6 hectolitres of roots per acre, or around 300 kilos in weight. Forced cultivation. - Forced cultivation of carrots is generally practiced by Paris market gardeners, who supply the produce for consumption and export during the first four or five months of the year. These early crops have become more widespread each year, due to the foreign trade they generate. Some market gardeners grow their first crops using thermosiphon heating, i.e. by circulating hot water through the soil in the frames. To achieve this, they sow carrots in frames as early as October; then, when the carrots have two or three leaves, they transplant them into the thermosiphon-heated tarpaulins. Using this method, they harvest small bell carrots at the end of December and the beginning of January, which fetch a very high price and are sure to sell well. In agricultural factories with large steam engines, condensation water could be used to grow similar crops. For bedded cultivation, construction begins towards the end of November or the first days of December. New manure is used, half of which is mixed with annealed manure, i.e. manure that has been piled up during the summer. This annealed manure can be replaced by an equal quantity of leaves. This layer should be Om, 50 high, so as to give 20 to 25 degrees of warmth; it is covered with potting soil from old layers that have not yet been used, and placed about On, 15 high. Lightly pack the mould with the back of a rake, or with a board pricked on the tines of a pitchfork, and sow the marbled seed of Baby Carrot at a rate of one and a half grams per frame; then lightly cover the seed with half a centimetre of mould, which you spread by hand. As soon as the plant has a few leaves, we give the frame a little air if the weather is fine and it's not freezing, to prevent the carrots from withering away; on the other hand, we cover the frames with straw mats in the evening. Water lightly when necessary, and start harvesting around four months after sowing. Harvesting should be carried out gradually, first pulling out only the largest roots. Bunches of 40 to 50 carrots are made; each frame provides an average of 5 to 6 bunches. At the beginning of the year, we build new layers, which we sow with Carrots, and at the same time we plant per frame, six rows of gotte lettuce at a rate of seven feet per row. As soon as the lettuce has been harvested, which takes place 40 to 50 days after planting, the carrots are replanted with a little potting soil, spread by hand, lightly basked and the trays closed again. Carrots can be harvested towards the end of March. Finally, a third season is sown on beds built in February. The bed is sown with Carrots, then, instead of frames, the bed is covered with cloches arranged in three rows, and under each cloche is planted either a single plant of romaine, or more usually one romaine and three gotte lettuces. Once the lettuces have been harvested, they are replanted with potting soil, as described above, for cultivation under chassts. This crop yields its produce at the beginning of May, and then the open-air crops will follow these forced cultivation harvests. Seed crops. - Carrots intended for seed production should be sown from May to July, and harvested in November. At harvest time, the best-formed roots are chosen, well-colored and as smooth as possible. They are stored in a cellar, where they are buried in sand, after cutting off the leaves without damaging the central bud. It's wise to keep more carrots than you intend to plant, as it's not uncommon for some to rot over winter. In February or March, we plant the preserved Carrots in the caye, at Om, 50 in all directions, and when the flowering branch appears, we provide it with a stake, against which we tie it. When in bloom, remove all the poor-looking inflorescences, leaving only the best-developed ones. Harvesting takes place by cutting off the umbels whose fruit is ripe, and leaving them to mature in the shelter of a shed. Harvesting takes place successively, as the fruit ripen. The seeds are threshed, then bagged and labeled; as we said, the best seeds are those that are only two years old. Insect pests. - In open-ground seedlings, we often see small, black, extremely agile spiders, called theridia, feeding on the young carrot plant, causing great damage. Frequent watering will keep them at bay, but the best way to destroy them is to wash them down with a decoction of wormwood or water mixed with soot. Carrot moth caterpillars often wreak havoc on seedlings by attacking flowers and fruit, and we have no practical means of destroying them.   Celery (Apium graveolens, Linnaeus) grows wild in all the humid regions of Sweden, Algeria and Egypt, as well as in India and the Caucasus. It has been remarked (1) that Miller tried in vain, by successive sowings, to transform wild Celery into the plant of our crops. This is hardly surprising, given that cultivated Celery, which was already known in the time of Dioscorides and Pliny (2), had no doubt gone through many generations before presenting all the characteristics of a truly edible plant. Moreover, the authors we have just quoted clearly distinguished this plant into wild and cultivated celery, which leads us to presume that in their time there was not yet the difference between these two plants that separates them today, without which they would have indicated them as two distinct plants and not as derived from each other by cultivation. Celery is a biennial plant, with a very short, stubby rhizome bearing compound, pinnate leaves with a highly developed, longitudinally grooved petiole. The inflorescences, which appear in spring (1) P. Joigneaux, Le jardin potager. (2) De Candolle, Orig. des plantes cultivées. in the second year, are compound umbels giving rise to very small, longitudinally ribbed fruits (achenes). The seeds retain their germinative capacity for seven to eight years. Cultivation has led to the development of two different organs in Celery, making this single plant two distinct vegetables. In one category, the leaf stalks have developed considerably and are now full of cellular tissue, instead of remaining hollow, as is the case in the primitive type; this is what makes up the so-called ribbed Celery, or improperly branching Celery. In the second type, on the other hand, where the petioles remain hollow, we look for plants in which the rhizome, joined to the beginning of the roots, has produced a sort of tuberosity rich in different types of food. These are the Celeris raves. These are very unstable and not very old products; the characteristics of the race quickly disappear when the plant is badly cultivated. CELERI A COTES Varieties. - Several varieties of Celery are grown, differing from one another in the size of the plant and the extent of the ribs. In all ribbed varieties, the leaves are upright and close together. Every year, a few new varieties of celery come onto the market. Some disappear almost as soon as they appear. Others, on the other hand, have certain merits, but none yet replace those we have mentioned. 2 9 WHITE CELERY. - This is the variety most cultivated around Paris. Its ribs are very fleshy, and it reaches Om, 50 in height. The ribs are green, no matter what the name says, and only turn white as they ripen (fig. 22). It would be more appropriate to retain the name "white celery" for a very interesting form obtained by M. Chemin, a market gardener in Issy, in which the ribs are naturally white. This celery, which is already very popular at Les Halles, has been adopted by most growers. CÉLERI PLEIN, COURT HATIF. - This variety is a little smaller in size, but the ribs are voluminous and close together. CELERY FOR CUTTING. - A variety similar to the wild type, it has the advantage of growing all year round. Uses. - In this category of celery, the petioles or ribs are eaten blanched by shriveling, which removes their pungency and strong taste. They are used either raw in salads, or cooked and prepared in a variety of ways. Chopped celery is used as a condiment in various soups. In England, Celery fruit is used for the same purpose. Cultivation. - Celery is sown from March on a warm bed. The seed takes quite a long time to germinate, and the plant appears with very small, rounded cotyledonary leaves. When these seedlings have two or three leaves, it's a good idea to transplant them onto a bed, in a nursery, very close to each other; in this way, you'll obtain a much more vigorous plant than if you hadn't transplanted it; however, this method is often neglected, and we're content to transplant Celery directly into the soil, when it is sufficiently developed, i.e. about a month and a half after sowing. The first of the two methods is infinitely preferable. Transplanting is done using a planter, in soil rich in fertilizer. Market gardeners usually do this on old beds from which the frames have been removed. As Celery is slow-growing at first, there is no disadvantage in combining this planting with other crops, so it is often transplanted into the space occupied by half-grown lettuces. The rows in which Celery is transplanted should be Om, 25 apart, and planted at Om, 50 apart on the rows; as this planting is staggered, the plants are equally spaced in all directions. Immediately after planting, water well to promote recovery. Celery plants are very water-hungry, and it's only by watering them often, or planting them in naturally moist soil, that you'll get good results. This first season produces Celery that will be fully developed by September. To succeed this first harvest, Celery should be sown at the end of March and treated as described above. Finally, we sow in the third season, in the last days of April; but whereas the first sowings had to be done on beds, this one is done in the open air and in the ground. Sowing is done very lightly, with further thinning if necessary; for this last season, it is not necessary to send the seedling through the nursery; it can be planted directly in June without any inconvenience. Like the previous ones, this planting is done on land already occupied by salad crops in the process of developing. It is this last sowing that will provide consumption throughout the winter, so it is usually the one that is given the most importance. Once the celery has reached full development, it must be blanched, as it cannot be released for consumption without first undergoing this operation. The processes used to achieve this result are highly varied, depending on the locality and the season. Market gardeners blanch autumn celery simply by covering it in place with old straw mats or long litter. This operation is perfectly successful, because the plants, having been heavily watered, have grown and are touching each other; simply covering them with litter is enough to bleach them completely. In vegetable gardens where they are planted widely apart, they are tied together in autumn with straw, then mounded with soil taken from around them. For winter celery, the procedure is different. Towards the end of October, a trench is opened in the ground, 1.30 m wide and 1.25 m deep, and the soil from this excavation is dumped on either side of the pit. When the pit is ready, we pull out the Celery clods, after binding them with a piece of straw, and plant them in the trench, om 15 from each other, at a rate of eight per line; we plant successive rows until the pit is full. At this point, the plant is watered heavily, to encourage the Celery to recover and to prevent the leaves from wilting. After a few days, you'll see that the Celery has recovered, because it's putting out new leaves; you then bury it, filling in the gaps between the plants with potting soil. To avoid dirtying the leaves, we use two boards, 50 cm long, with a peg nailed to each end. The work is carried out with the help of two men, each of whom is positioned on one side of the pit and holds DYBONSKI. - Vegetable growing. one of the two boards by its end, and, pressing it firmly against one of the rows of Celery, between which he passes it, he fixes it by driving the stakes into the ground. The same is done on the opposite side, so that the gap between two rows is maximized, with the Celery leaves close together on the right and left. This gap is then filled with soil, and the plant is moved on to the next row. Market gardeners often do this in two stages, in order to gradually whiten the Celery; the pit is then left in this state, with litter thrown on top of the leaves to protect them from frost. This process produces white celery for most of the winter. Around Meaux, instead of putting celery in a pit, we blanch it on the spot. To do this, we alternately grow a bed of celery and a bed of lettuce. When it's time to blanch the celery, we bind it in place with straw, and since the lettuce has already been harvested, we use the soil from the beds it occupied to butter the celery. Finally, if you have large, healthy cellars or gallery silos, you can simply remove the Celery clods and store them in these premises, burying the roots in sand. In this way, it can be stored for most of the winter. This procedure is recommended wherever the soil is compact and excessively moist, making it impossible to straddle or ridge. When it comes to selling Celery, it is useful to trim it properly. For first- and second-season Celery, which has simply been blanched by a covering of litter, simply pull it out, rid the roots of the soil they carry, trimming them a little with a pruning knife, then remove the spoiled leaves and even a few of those that are not, on one side, so that on one side you can see the well-bleached heart. Thus trimmed, the Celery is tied in bunches of six, with the side that has been stripped of leaves on the outside of the bundle. For winter Celery, you must also remove the rotten leaves, then wash the feet with plenty of water to rid them of the soil that soils the ribs. Celery for cutting is sown in the field, broadcast in April; the leaves are then harvested by cutting them with a knife, and sold in bunches. This is a minor product that is only grown here and there. Cultivation for seed. - From the Celery plants that reach full development in autumn, we select those that are best formed, i.e. those with long, broad, full ribs and leaves that are upright and close together. They are removed in rootballs and planted at 6m, 50 in all directions, in a separate bed. When the cold weather arrives, they are mounded as you would an artichoke, then covered over winter with leaves or litter. Once the cold has passed, they are uncovered, and in March the mounds of earth are removed. Care should be taken to remove any leaves that are beginning to decay, which could lead to the rotting of those that are still healthy. In May, the Celery plants come into bloom, and the seeds are ready for harvesting in August. This is done by cutting the stalks, drying them on a cloth, and lightly beating them to detach the seeds, which are then sieved and bagged. CELERI-RAVE Varieties. - In these Celery varieties, where selection has focused on the development of the rhizome and root base, there are only slight variations. All have dark green leaves, whose petioles (sides) are hollow and no larger than those of wild celery; they are usually purplish-red. In current production, only two forms are grown, one of which is a perfected version of the other. ORDINARY CELERY - The leaves are semi-erect, the rhizomes are large, rough at the top and covered with roots at the base. PARIS SMOOTH CELERY. - In this variety, bred by Paris market gardeners, the leaves are more spreading and the rhizome is generally a little wider than it is high; the upper part is smoother than in the previous variety. This is the Céleri-Raye type, towards which we should aim as far as possible (fig. 23). Uses. - Céleris-Raves are sometimes eaten raw, as an appetizer, but their use is best recommended cooked. They are a very delicate dish, not yet widely used. Cultivation. - Like its congeners, the Céleri-Raye is a plant that thirsts for fertilizer, and because of this requirement, it is grown only in vegetable gardens with loose, fertile soil, and by market gardeners, who plant it in compost; it is here that it acquires its most beautiful development and, consequently, that its edible part reaches its greatest value. In Celery-Rave, as in most vegetables, organoleptic qualities are directly related to the speed and degree of development. In poor soil, Fig. 23 - Celeri-Rave lisse de Paris. rhizomes are small and hard, as the fibrous tissue then takes over. Market gardeners sow Celeri-Rave in March, on a warm bed. When the plant has two leaves, in addition to the two cotyledons, they transplant it onto a bed, very close together; immediately after transplanting, they water with apples and close the frame to facilitate recovery. Once the Celery has taken root, air is given and the plants are watered frequently. Celery remains in this state until mid-May, when planting begins, and can last until the first days of June. For planting, trim the leaves a little to facilitate recovery by reducing evaporation through the leaves. Plant using a planting frame, either in an open square or between other plants such as lettuces. Throughout the summer, we water often and a lot, which favours the development and increases the quality of this vegetable. It has often been suggested that Celery-Rayes should be thinned out from the bottom, so that all the leaves at the base are removed, to encourage the rhizomes to develop; but this is a bad operation, and market gardeners don't usually do it. Clearly, removing leaves and thus depriving the plant of its means of aerial nutrition does not encourage the development of its underground organs; on the contrary, it weakens the whole plant. Céleris-Rayes cultivated as described above can be harvested as early as September and October, which is when they can already be seen on the markets. However, many market gardeners prefer to preserve this product and only deliver it for consumption in February and March. In autumn, vegetables of all kinds compete fiercely with celery, but in spring, when preserved vegetables begin to rise in price and fall in quality, celery is sold at a much higher price, with an average wholesale price of 2 francs a dozen. To keep them until spring, they simply need to be covered with a good layer of potting soil, either on the spot or in a gauge along a wall. They are trimmed for sale by removing all the rootlets. Growing for seed. - This is done in exactly the same way as described for ribbed celery. Seed-bearing plants should be chosen from those with the most developed and smoothest rhizomes. Leaves should be few and spread out over the ground. Care should be taken to keep these seedbeds away from those of ribbed celery, to avoid cross-breeding. Diseases. - The only disease that Celery suffers from is Rust, which seems to be due to the presence of a parasitic fungus. There is no known way to prevent it, or to treat affected plants. The best way to avoid the inconvenience it causes is therefore to deliver any affected plants for consumption immediately. ...... . . . :. . .  "e 1 : CHERFEUIL Chervil (Scandix cerefolium, Linnaeus) has long been considered a native plant, based on the fact that it is frequently found in village streets or on piles of rubble. It's hardly surprising that this plant has spread to the vicinity of the places where it's cultivated, as its cultivation is very old and dates back to before the beginning of the Christian era. But precisely this constant proximity to cultivated areas raised doubts as to whether it was really indigenous to Europe. Based on precise observations, it seems that this plant should be considered to have had its cradle in temperate western Asia and southeastern Russia, where it was collected as a truly spontaneous plant (1). Chervil, which we grow sometimes as an annual and sometimes as a biennial, has decomposed leaves with very thin blades. Its flower stalks, Om.50 high, end in umbels of small white flowers, followed by long, pointed fruits in pairs. Carrying only one seed each inside (1) De Candolle, Orig. des plantes cultivées. they do not open, and it is for this reason, no doubt, that these fruits are referred to in everyday language as seeds. Varieties. - As we have seen, although this plant has been cultivated for a very long time, it has varied very little, and only two different forms are grown. COMMON CHERVIL. - It corresponds to the type we have just discussed, and is the most commonly cultivated form. CERFETII, CURLY. - This variety, with its frizzy leaves, looks like moss. It has a much greater ornamental effect than the common variety, and its leaves can be used to decorate dishes. They are criticized for being less hardy. Uses. - The leaves of this plant are used to season salads and sometimes other dishes. It should be noted that the use of this plant is virtually unknown in Poland and Russia. It has often been claimed that Chervil should not be confused with Little Hemlock (ÆEthusa cynaptum, Linnaeus). We fail to see how this confusion can be made, since, apart from the difference in appearance, the fetid odor of the hemlock is nothing like the fine, aromatic scent of the Chervil. Cultivation. - Chervil can be sown almost all year round, but it is necessary to repeat sowing frequently if you want fresh, abundant produce. A rich soil, a shady location and frequent watering are all necessary for this plant. The market gardeners of Paris sow chervil over vast areas, because although its price is low on the markets, its rapid growth and abundant produce mean that it is profitable. Sowing is done either in rows spaced Om, 20 apart when the plant is grown on its own, or broadcast when combined with other crops such as cauliflower. About 500 grams of seed are used per acre. Sown in autumn, chervil overwinters and gives several successive harvests. On the other hand, chervil sown in spring and summer can only be harvested once, because it flowers so easily. To ensure a constant supply at this time of year, it is necessary to sow the plants every fortnight or so. Chervil is sold in bunches or handfuls, as large as the hand can hold. Its price, which is only 5 centimes in summer, rises to 25 or 30 centimes in winter. During the summer, one square meter yields ten to twelve. For seed, preference is given to plants which, sown in autumn, have survived the winter, which they also support with ease. Market gardeners, in order to obtain very fine products, transplant Chervil in spring, choosing only plants with ample, abundant leaves in the common variety, and on the contrary, well-curled leaves in the curly variety. Chervil seeds only keep their germinative capacity for two years, and even then, same-year seed is preferable and gives more certain results. TUBEROUS CHERFEUIL This chervil (Chærophyllum bulbosum, Linné) is characterized by decomposed leaves with very fine divisions and covered, as well as the petioles which have a purplish tinge, with rounded hairs which give it a hairy appearance. The root is swollen and fleshy, resembling a half-long carrot in shape, but brown in color (fig. 24). In the spring of the second year, the plant produces a stem about 1.50 m long, swollen above the point of attachment of the leaves, which branches and bears numerous inflorescences in compound umbels. The fruits (achenes) are raised by five longitudinal ribs; they are considered seeds and are sown as such. Fig. 24 - Chervil Use. - The roots are eaten, and their firm, white, tuberous flesh takes on a delicate taste after cooking, making them a much sought-after delicacy. Cultivation. - Tuberous chervil multiplies by seed, but as these lose their germinative capacity very soon after maturity, it is necessary either to sow them immediately, in autumn, in very healthy soil, or better still, to keep them until spring by stratifying them. This involves placing a layer of fine sand in a flowerpot, followed by a layer of seeds, which are then covered again with sand, and so on until the pot is full. It is then covered either with a sheet of glass, or an absolutely flat tile, so that the seal is sufficiently airtight to prevent insects or rodents from entering; for the same reason, the opening at the bottom of the pot is sealed. The pot, thus prepared, is buried close to a wall, to the north, at a depth of Om.30 or so. There is a great advantage in following this practice rather than sowing in autumn, as the soil is not uselessly occupied by gramineae that may be destroyed by insects during the winter. In any case, the soil in which Chervil is sown must be well loosened, rich in decomposed fertilizer and suitably harrowed with a rake. Sowing is broadcast, at a rate of 300 grams per acre. The seed is covered with a rake, then an even layer of mulch is spread over the soil. During the summer, all that's needed is to keep the soil moist with a few waterings and to keep it clean by weeding; this is the only cultural care that needs to be given to this plant. Around July, the leaves turn yellow and dry up, and the plant is ready to be harvested. Once the roots have been pulled out, they are left to dry out for a few hours, then lowered into a cellar or storeroom, where they can be stored until spring with the greatest of ease. The harvest is around 200 kilograms per acre. D. . To harvest seed, the best-formed and most developed roots should be selected in autumn, and carefully stored in the cellar, piled with sand. In the greyhound season, they are planted in planks at Om, 75 in all directions; they go to seed, and it is not usually necessary to stake them, because of the rigidity of the stems. As soon as the stems, which have flowered and set fruit, begin to yellow, they are cut off and the seed is treated as described above. - ." :." . . . . 7 .. "(Agaricus campestris, Linnaeus), grows spontaneously in moderately damp meadows, where it can be found from summer to the first cold days of winter. In vegetable crops, where horse manure is used, it appears on the edges of manure layers or millstones; often, under these conditions, the successive appearance lasts for a very long time. But these are merely accidental occurrences. L'ou has succeeded, through intelligent observation, in determining the conditions under which this vegetation can occur, and in repeating them artificially. Without insisting, in a special way, on the mode of vegetation and reproduction of Mushrooms, which would not fit within the framework we have assigned to this book, we must nevertheless take a quick look at the phases of development of the plant we are dealing with, in order to show what relationship exists between these and cultivation practices. The adult Agaric (fig. 25) is composed, as everyone knows, of two easily distinguishable parts: one called the foot, which represents a kind of cylinder slightly swollen into a rounded mass at its lower end, where it touches the ground; this foot supports a kind of upper disk, to which we give the name of cap. A close look at the hat reveals a spongy mass to which a considerable number of blades adhere at the bottom, radiating out from the point where the hat is attached to the foot. These blades are very unequal in size, some extending from the point where the cap is attached to the foot to its outer edge, while others are only part of this length. These blades, which are not visible when the fungus first appears, because the edges of the cap are folded in on themselves and envelop them completely, are a pale, fleshy pink when they become visible. Gradually, as the cap opens, they take on a darker hue, finally reaching a dark brown color. If, at this point, a mushroom is gently shaken over a sheet of white paper, a sort of blackish dust falls from the surface of the blades. Collecting some of this dust on the paper and examining it under the microscope, we see that each of these grains consists of a small central mass surrounded by an envelope. It is to these very small grains, which make up the black dust on the slides, that we give the name spores, which are the true seeds of the Mushroom. In fact, if these microscopic seeds are placed in a conyenable environment of heat and humidity, they germinate and produce a sort of long filament that can only be seen with magnifying instruments. This filament, at first a single one, soon branches out, and finally forms small white clusters that adhere to the bodies they encounter; in botany, this is called mycelium, and in practice, mushroom spawn. As we follow the development of this spawn, we'll see that it soon forms small clumps or felts in places, known as stroma. On top of this stroma, granulations will soon appear, at first very small, but rapidly growing. As they grow, they differentiate into two distinct parts, the foot and the cap. A new mushroom is born, and the cycle of vegetation is closed. As a result of these considerations, we can consider the spores as the seed, the mycelium or spawn as the organs of vegetation, and finally the Mushroom proper, composed of the foot and cap, as representing the organs of fruiting. We have seen that piles of horse manure often produce Agaries champêtres without intentional cultivation; so it is almost always horse manure that is used as the support, the substratum, on which this cultivation will be carried out. We'll now look at how this manure should be chosen and prepared for this type of production, but first we need to determine the environment in which the crop can be grown. In their spontaneous state, Agarics are always found in the open air, in meadows, or near piles of manure; as a result, they only grow when the state of the atmosphere is suitable for their vegetation, and it is particularly the low, damp heat of autumn that favors their development. In artificial cultivation, mushrooms are obtained either in the open air, with the help of special precautions, or in closed premises such as cellars, quarries, silos, etc., usually from one end of the year to the other. Quarry cultivation. - Of all the premises suitable for growing Agaricum, the ones that are preferred to all others, and which lend themselves best to production from a commercial point of view, are the quarries used for stone extraction. There are a great many of these throughout the Paris basin, which are no longer quarried, but are used for the crops we're interested in here (fig. 26). Rarely do these quarries have mouths, i.e. entrances at ground level, which are therefore easy to access even with cars; more often, on the contrary, they have shafts, fitted with parrot ladders, with disDYBOWSRI rungs. - Vegetable growing. 8 tancés de Om, 50. It is through these wells that the materials needed for cultivation are transported. These mouths, or shafts, communicate with galleries drilled into the ground at widely varying depths. Some are only ten or so meters below ground level, but most are much deeper, and it's not uncommon to see mushroom cultivation taking place 20 or 30 meters below ground. Often there are several levels of galleries, one above the other, separated only by layers of earth a few meters deep. All these varying arrangements have little influence on cultivation. However, the deepest quarries are naturally the warmest, and the most suitable temperature for good cultivation is around 12 to 15 degrees Celsius, so it's best to get as close to this point as possible. The degree of humidity is of little importance, provided that when the quarry is dry, sufficient water is available for timely watering. It's not uncommon to find a spring in the quarry itself, or to be able to divert water from a nearby well. If these resources are lacking, the water has to be brought down from above. In this case, mushroom growers employ a simple but ingenious process, which consists in suspending a string in the shaft, and letting the water flow along this string, to which it adheres by capillary action, thus descending, without deviating from the conductive string, to the bottom of the shaft, where it is collected in a container of some kind. The size of the quarries used around Paris varies greatly; some are no less than several kilometers long, while others are only a few hundred meters long. The height and width of underground galleries can vary greatly within the same quarry, depending on the original use of the site for stone extraction. The height, which in some places is two or three meters, becomes so low in others that it is difficult to stand or even bend in half. These variations in height or width have little effect on cultivation, especially if there is some ventilation, without which the temperature would become uneven and higher in the lower part. Ventilation is necessary in quarries, but it must not be too considerable, so it's useful to be able to regulate it. This is achieved by means of small-diameter shafts, topped by a kind of chimney, usually made of planks, which can be opened or closed at will. Quarries are most often rented for the running mềtre of galleries, regardless of their width or height. The usual price in the Paris basin, at Arcueil, Montrouge, Vanves, Malakoff, etc., is O fr. 15 per running meter, rented by the year. Choice of manure. - The essential point in mushroom cultivation is the judicious choice of the substrate on which they are to live. Horse manure is almost exclusively suitable; however, it seems that donkey, mule or even sheep manure has sometimes been used successfully. But it's best to use only horse manure, and it's not indifferent to use any kind of horse manure. Indeed, manure from luxury horse stables, and in general from all those where straw is lavished too abundantly, is unsuitable. For manure to be good, it must necessarily come from stables where horses are stalled for some time on straw provided relatively sparingly. Experience has shown us that manure can be considered suitable for growing Agaricum, when the manure equals the weight of straw. Manures from vigorous, impatient horses, such as stallions, which break up straw well when trampled, are rightly preferred. If the farmer wishes to use this type of manure, he should leave it under his horses for six to eight days, adding only a little new bedding on top each day to keep the horses clean. Farmers only use manure made from wheat straw. Generally speaking, they are right to do so, as it is with such manure that the produce is most assured; however, we have obtained a good harvest by using manure made from oat straw in an experimental crop at the Ecole d'agriculture de Grignon. Mushroom growers buy manure from car and trucking companies, or from private individuals. Purchases are usually made by the month, at a rate of 4.50 to 6 francs per cheyal per month, bringing the price per cubic meter to around 5 francs. Sometimes manure is bought in bulk, for example in cavalry barracks; it is then sold by the cubic meter, or by the car, taking the above price as the market basis. Manure preparation. - As and when manure is received, it is piled up, until such time as the quantity is deemed suitable, when it is prepared for cultivation. The first step in preparing the manure for cultivation is to build what is known as a floor; this is a sort of rectangular millstone, with sides that vary in size according to the quantity of manure available, but which is always raised to a height of around 1.10 m. To build the floor properly, the manure should be shaken well when picked up, to make it homogeneous, and beaten with the back of the fork, so that the millstone is evenly packed. The millstone is built with a border to determine its width, which varies according to the size of the crop. In industrial production, these floors are rarely less than 4 meters square. Once the edge of the millstone has been made, the manure continues to be deposited, stirring it vigorously with a fork until it reaches the height indicated. This process continues until the entire floor has been raised. Care is taken during assembly to remove any foreign bodies that may have been present. Once the millstone is in place, it is watered to moisten the manure and allow it to ferment. As soon as fermentation begins, the temperature of the mass rises significantly, reaching around 70 degrees after five or six days. About eight days after the floor has been built, it is demolished and a new one built, similar in every respect. To do this, the manure is removed from one side of the old floor and, using a fork to stir it vigorously, the construction of the second floor is begun alongside it. Continue in this way, giving this heap the same height as the previous one and taking care to place all the manure from the periphery in the center of the floor; pack with a fork and water as before. Eight days after this second operation, we demolish again to build a third and final floor. All the precautions indicated earlier are taken here, and it is not uncommon to have to water again, as fermentation raises the temperature of the millstone, resulting in constant evaporation. Through these successive manipulations, the manure has noticeably decreased in volume, and the millstones, which were about 1.10 m high, are finally reduced, if the same surface is kept, to Om, 80 or Om, 85, i.e. by about a quarter in volume. The aim of these successive operations is to rot the manure, so as to obtain a mass that is as homogeneous as possible, moist to the touch, and uniformly brown in color. The temperature of the manure thus prepared by triple manipulation has dropped significantly, to around 30 to 40 degrees. Three or four days after the last floor has been built, the manure is usually ready for use. All these preliminary operations usually take about twenty days; but these rules are not absolute, and must be modified according to circumstances. When the manure is very dry, or when the temperature is low and the weather rainy, preparation can be extended by a few days. Practice, aided by these general principles, is the only way to guide the operator when conditions are no longer normal. Mushroom growers usually build the floors in August, so that they can use the manure in September. Building millstones. - Now that the manure has been prepared using all the operations described above, it's time to start using it. This is done by throwing the manure down through the opening in the shaft, picking it up with wheelbarrows, narrow at the base to allow it to pass easily along the quarry paths, and driving it to the place where it is to be used. It is deposited in successive barrowfuls, forming what are known as chains, whose location corresponds to that which will be permanently occupied by the millstones. These millstones for Agaric cultivation will be about Om, 50 to Om, 60 at the base and as much in height, the top being arranged in a dome, so that one of these millstones represents very roughly in cross-section the figure of a regular ogive. The length of the millstones depends on the size of the crop. Depending on the width of the underground galleries, two or more rows are placed parallel to each other, with only a path of about thirty centimetres between them. Often, along the gallery walls, the millstones are supported by one of the sides, which slightly modifies their shape; this arrangement is adopted in areas of narrow width. For the grinding wheel to be built properly, the worker must take the manure deposited in a chain by hand and place it on the spot where the wheel is to be placed. As the millstone rises, the worker kneels over the manure, tamping it down constantly with his hands and knees, laying it out with absolute regularity. The top and sides are combed with the fingers, then tapped, so as to obtain a surface absolutely free of asperities. To be fit for use, they need to be at around 12 or 14°, which happens seven to fifteen days after construction. If the temperature is higher, because the manure has not been sufficiently consumed, we lift the millstone by passing a wooden stake through it from place to place; in this way, the temperature is rapidly lowered to the desired point. The manure is compacted where it has been lifted. Spawning the millstones. - When the millstone is judged to be in good condition, it should be seeded, or, to put it more accurately, planted with mushrooms. To do this, we use small plates of manure, about Om.05 by Om.07, invaded by the vegetative organs of the Agaric, the mycelium or mushroom spawn. The result is a veritable cuttings operation, known as larding. This mycelium has the property, common to that of most mushrooms, of preserving itself in a dry state almost indefinitely, but resuming its life and growth as soon as it is placed in a warm, humid environment. When dry spawn is available, it's a good idea to put it in the quarry where the millstones are located a few days before use, so that it has time to regain its lost moisture and is ready to grow as soon as it's placed in the millstone. To plant a millstone, we use white plates, or "mises", and, opening a small cavity in the millstone with the hand, we place the "mise" inside. This is placed in two parallel rows, approximately Om, 25 to Om, 30 or Om, 40 apart on each row. Once the stake has been placed, the manure is pressed down by hand to fix it in place. After a few days, eight to twelve, if you look at the bets, you can see that the white has increased, that it has spun and that the grinding stone is beginning to be invaded; the bet has become mnutile, and you remove it to throw it away. Twenty to twenty-five days later, if all has gone well, it's easy to see that the spawn has spread throughout the mass of manure, especially near the surface. At this point, the millstone is lightly beaten to make it smooth and to completely remove any irregularities caused by the larding and removal of the bets. All that's left to do is a final operation, which consists in covering the whole wheel with a uniform layer of specially prepared earth; this work is called goptage. The material used for gopting is usually the debris from the stones quarried. This is not, however, an absolute prerequisite for success, and mushroom growers often use stone waste without soil. We've had good results gopting with falun or shell sand. To be used, the soil to be gopted must be very slightly moistened, to facilitate its adhesion with the manure. Once the soil has been prepared, it is spread on the grinding wheel in an even layer, about 2 centimetres thick, and then beaten lightly with the back of a wooden spade. This operation must be carried out with the utmost regularity, so that the grinding wheel is covered everywhere with the same thickness of soil, and the mushrooms are not hindered in their emergence. All you have to do then is wait for the mushrooms to appear, and maintain sufficient humidity in the galleries by watering the paths. Usually, the first Mushrooms appear twenty to thirty-five days after goptage; however, in exceptional cases, they may take longer to appear. Great care must always be taken during the many operations described above. If you follow the conditions described exactly, you can be sure of harvesting an abundance of mushrooms; on the other hand, deviating from them rarely produces satisfactory results. Cultivation as it is done today has precise rules from which it is dangerous, on pain of setbacks, to deviate even momentarily; but if this production has great demands, on the other hand it presents very serious advantages by the enormous quantity of products it is capable of providing in well-managed operations. Mushroom harvesting. - As we have just seen, the Mushrooms appear about a month after the goptage operation, which means that the time elapsing from receipt of the manure to the moment when harvesting begins is about two and a half months. From then on, the only work consists of picking, which is usually done every day. The approach of the moment so longed for by the grower is heralded by small granulations which, if all has gone well, first appear towards the bottom of the millstones, then gradually invade them completely. These are the very young mushrooms, born on the stroma of the mycelium, which in a few days will grow and acquire sufficient development to be delivered for consumption. The grower must therefore visit his crops frequently, so as not to let the Mushrooms overshoot their time of maturity. Mushrooms can be harvested when the cap, which has become quite distinct from the stem, has not yet half-opened to reveal the blades underneath; open mushrooms suffer a marked depreciation from a sales point of view. Agarics are born on millstones, sometimes isolated, sometimes and most often in small clusters to which we give the name of rocks. Harvesting is done by taking a basket with a handle under the left arm, capable of containing around 5 kilos of mushrooms, and detaching with the right hand, by a slight twisting movement, all the Agarics whose development is judged sufficient. Care is taken to cover with gopter soil all the small cavities made where the Mushrooms have been harvested, especially where a rock has been picked. Once begun, harvesting continues for a variable length of time, depending on how successful the cultivation has been, but usually lasts at least two months, and can last up to four or five, in the best conditions. Yields. - Taking as a basis a cultivation of 1,200 meters of millstones carried out by Mr. Duvillard, a horticulturist in Arcueil, we were able to estimate the yield as being very roughly equal to 3 kilos per running meter of millstone for the entire harvest period. These mushrooms, picked as they develop, are usually sorted into first and second choice. Each portion is carefully packed in baskets lined with paper or linen, and kept away from air and light, which would cause the mushrooms to darken. When sold at the Halles de Paris, the first choice is delivered at around 1 fr. 50 per kilo, and the second at O fr. 75; this represents an average selling price of 1 fr. 10 per kilo, with each quality being roughly equal in weight. Growing costs and profit. - The costs involved in this type of cultivation are easy to estimate, and include the cost of manure, labor and quarry rental. As we have seen, manure is reduced in volume by a quarter in successive manipulations; its initial price being 5 francs, the price of prepared manure will be 6 fr. 25 per cubic metre. With one cubic meter of this manure, we can build 12 to 14 running meters of grinding wheels, which gives each meter an average price of 0 fr. 50. Labor costs, consisting of manure handling, millstone construction, tapping and gopting, performed by day laborers or task workers, come to a total of 1 franc per meter of millstone. As we said, quarry rental is paid at the rate of 0.15 francs per running meter of gallery, in which at least two parallel rows of millstones are set up. All this adds up to 1 running meter of manure: Price of manure....................... Ofr,50 Labor........................ 1, 00 Quarry rental............... O ,07 Total.......... 1fr,57 It is difficult to include in these costs the cost of purchasing the spawn, which, as we shall see later, is usually prepared by the farmer himself.
