The yield is, as we said, 3 kilos of Mushrooms at I fr. 10, i.e. 3 fr. 30; but to this sum must be added the price of the manure from the exhausted millstones, which is sold or used directly for cultivating the fields. This manure, when the harvest is exhausted, is significantly reduced in volume, this reduction being equivalent to two-fifths of the original mass; so that some twenty meters of millstones are needed to make up one cubic meter, which is sold for 5 to 6 francs, or 25 to 30 centimes per running meter of millstone. The product per meter is therefore: Mushroom product.............. 3fr,30 Manure from the demolition of the grinding stone............................. 0 25 Total product......... 3r,55 If we subtract from this sum the cultivation costs amounting to 1 fr. 57, we are left with a sum of 2 francs, representing the net profit that this crop can give per running meter of grinding stone. This type of cultivation is not always a sure-fire success, but it is rare for failures to be frequent when conditions are as indicated. Cellar culture. - In small-scale cultivation, quarries are seldom used; on the contrary, cellars or basements of all kinds, and sometimes silos dug into covered galleries, are particularly popular. Almost all cays are suitable for this purpose; however, the best are those that are deep, where the temperature is more constant. All air vents are usually sealed, to be opened only if the temperature becomes too high. If the cellar is dry, it's a good idea, before using it, to water the soil abundantly, level it and clear it of all foreign matter, so as to make the area perfectly clean. The preparation of the manure is identical to that described above, but the floor is naturally reduced in size. However, there is a limit to this reduction, and no less than 2 to 3 cubic meters of manure can be used, otherwise fermentation will not take place. Once the manure has been prepared according to the rules indicated, the millstones are built. These can be placed anywhere: along the walls or in the middle of the cellar, on the planks or shelves if there are any; on either side of the staircase wall if the latter is wide enough; and sometimes even in flat tubs, so as to make them mobile. Stones should not be larger than the dimensions indicated for quarry cultivation, otherwise the manure could heat up too much and dry out inside the stone, preventing the spawn from growing. Larding, goptage and harvesting are carried out under ordinary conditions, taking care never to let the mushrooms develop too much, even if they are not needed immediately. They can be kept fresh for several days if left in the cellar after harvesting. To ensure constant production, manure is prepared every two months; however, it should not be forgotten that the most successful crops are those started in autumn, so that they can be harvested in winter and spring. Open-air cultivation. - This type of cultivation, much used in the past, has been partly abandoned today. It yields less reliable products, since in addition to the usual causes of failure, there are also climatic influences to contend with. Smoking is selected and prepared exactly as described above. This usually takes place in September. Once the manure is ready, a suitable site for building the millstones is chosen. The site is prepared by simply levelling the ground if it is of a permeable nature, but if it is on the other side of the wall, the millstones are built.

If the soil doesn't retain enough moisture, it's a good idea to cover it with a uniform layer of rubble or clinker to let the water run off, or to dig a gutter in front of the bed where the millstone is to be placed. Some market gardeners build these millstones in full squares, arranged in parallel rather than along a wall, so as to extend the cultivation area. These millstones are built in the same way as those used in quarries, but once completed, they need to be protected from the elements. To do this, they are completely covered either with straw mats or, more usually, with a 5 to 6 centimetre layer of litter. In rainy autumns, it's a good idea to change this cover every time water penetrates it. During the winter, the thickness of the cover is increased if the weather becomes too cold, but care must be taken to ensure that excess litter does not lead to new fermentation in the mass, which would destroy all hope of harvesting. Mushrooms are harvested by removing the protective cover and covering them as soon as they are harvested. All in all, this crop, which in exceptionally mild winters can produce excellent results, must generally be regarded as fraught with difficulties and hazards. It succeeds in the hands of growers who know it well, but it becomes lucky in the hands of the inexperienced. Making the white. - As we have seen, Mushrooms multiply by means of mycelium adhering to a foreign body, most often consumed horse manure. In practice, the word "spawn" is used to designate both the mycelium itself and the manure impregnated with it, which is used to line the layers. In nature, as we said at the beginning of our study of this crop, mushrooms often grow spontaneously, either in meadows or on piles of manure or waste of any kind. The spores were brought there by DYBOWSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 9 the wind, and, finding a suitable environment for their growth, have developed there. We take advantage of this spontaneous development to obtain spawn for use in cultivation. To do this, we remove from the manure pile any part which, on close examination, is covered with the white filaments of the mycelium; we place this manure on racks and dry it in the shade. The dried white mycelium retains its ability to develop when placed in a suitable environment, and this for many years, at least ten. We place it in an attic, so that it remains absolutely dry and does not blacken. This spontaneous white is called virgin white. Mushroom growers harvest it wherever they can find it: on manure, on piles of leaves or shavings exposed to humidity. This mycelium should not, however, be confused with that of other mushrooms; only practice can teach you to make this distinction. Another procedure, commonly used to get rid of spawn, is to demolish part of a millstone just as it begins to be covered with products, and to select all the spawn-laden manure, which can be used immediately or dried for later use. In this practice, care is taken to demolish the part of the layer that shows itself to be the best, both in terms of quantity and product quality. Mushroom growers also produce spawn for three or four successive layers, after which they usually resort to using virgin spawn again, because after this time, the spawn seems exhausted and only produces less beautiful and less certain products. There are several other processes for obtaining white, but they have the double disadvantage of being complicated and not very successful; for these reasons, we feel we must ignore them. Varieties. - Depending on whether virgin spawn of this or that proyenance is used, or spawn of that layer, and also on the various circumstances inherent in the manure, the location, its temperature or the prevailing humidity, the shape and size of the Agaricum often change markedly. Generally speaking, cultivated Agaric is less voluminous and less colorful than that which grows spontaneously; however, very large Agaric is sometimes harvested from layers, particularly those larded with virgin white. Lon wanted to use these different forms to create different varieties. This is how we come to indicate the varieties: petit blanc, gros blanc, blond and gris. But, in our opinion, these determinations have little reason to exist, as they are more a manifestation of variation in external conditions than in the plant itself. Moreover, one of the reasons why there is no point in sticking to them is that, after three or four successive crops, we are obliged to return to virgin white, which will give a product that is not determined in advance, and which may belong indiscriminately to one variety or another. Using the manure from the millstones. - As we have seen, manure from mushroom millstones is usually sold by professional cultivators as fertilizer for large and small-scale farming. 117 It is indeed a fallacy to believe that mushroom cultivation deprives manure of its value as fertilizer, and farmers who buy it for 5 or 6 francs from mushroom growers don't pay too much for it. The analyses we have carried out have given us the following figures: Water and carbonic acid............... 43.6 Dry matter........................ 56.4 Total............... 100.0 The proportion of dry matter is greater than in normal manure, because a certain amount of gopter soil is always carried away. The dry matter of this manure is composed of: Mineral matter...................... 31.05 Organic matter..................... 68.95 Total................. 100.0 The significant proportion of mineral matter is due to the presence of gopter soil. Taking into account this disproportion and evaluating the nitrogen content in relation to 1000 of dry organic matter, we have the following: Nitrogen in one kilo of dry organic matter of ordinary manure....... 3,gr16 Nitrogen in one kilo of dry organic matter of manure after mushroom cultivation....................... 38r, 13 Difference........ Ogr,03 These figures show that the effective nitrogen content has not changed significantly. Animal and insect pests. - In the cellars, quarries or silos where they are built, millstones are subject to the ravages of mice, rats and sometimes moles. It's essential to wage fierce war on these animals, using traps; but it's even wiser to guard against them by having doors that close hermetically, at least at the bottom. These animals, by making their galleries in the millstones, completely devastate them and prevent the arrival of mushrooms. Grey slugs are common in crops, where they feed at the expense of mushrooms. They should be carefully destroyed whenever they are encountered. It's easy to catch a large number of them by placing a few cabbage leaves here and there, on which they are very fond and on which they can be found. Sowbugs, habitual inhabitants of damp places, always accompany the mushroom crops they attack. Most of the time, their ravages are minor; but if the number of these insects becomes too great, they can easily be destroyed by placing large potatoes on the stacks, which Ton cuts in half and hollows out to form two bells, under which the woodlice accumulate and are easily crushed. In the quarries, there is usually a large number of small black flies, which in certain circumstances become so numerous that the light with which we are obliged to descend into the crops is sometimes extinguished by the appalling number of flies which, attracted by its brightness, rush towards it. Mushroom growers attach little importance to this tipulian which, according to M. Maurice Girard's determination, belongs to the Sciara genus. They attach much more importance to a kind of small worm that attacks the young mushrooms it gnaws on; they call it the mushroom moth. As it happens, there is an intimate connection between these two beings, one of which is the perfect insect and the other the larva of a single species. Mushroom growers only cultivate Agaricum once a year in the same quarry, claiming that since the air is stale, cultivation fails if repeated. The real reason for this failure, which is real in the cultures we follow, is the presence of sciara eggs, which fill the cracks in the walls and soon hatch and spread over the millstones they ravage. If the cellar is left to rest for six or eight months, the laryes die and disappear completely. This means of destruction, which growers unconsciously apply under the pretext of letting the cellar rest, as they put it, is therefore quite successful, but has the serious disadvantage of interrupting cultivation. It seems, however, that cultivation could be renewed indefinitely without any danger of failure, provided the cellar is filled with sulfurous acid vapors, burning sulfur from place to place and only returning to the cellar a few days later to avoid any danger of asphyxiation. Carbon sulphide could also be poured into the galleries; but this product is dangerous to handle, because of the ease with which it ignites. It is certain that, by either process, the sciara, whether winged, egg or larva, will be completely destroyed. Plant parasites. - Agarics cultivated in quarries are frequently attacked by parasitic fungi which live at the expense of the plant whose cultivation interests us here. The results of these attacks are so-called rert on the one hand, and mole on the other. There is probably a relationship between these two manifestations, perhaps due to one and the same cause. Mole, which occurs in crops previously affected by green rot, leads to swelling of the Agaricum and later to its decomposition, which is accompanied by a putrid odor, similar to that of rotting fish. The only way to reduce the frequency of these attacks is to methodically remove all Agaries invaded by the disease, as leaving them in the quarry facilitates the spread of its germs. In a note published in 1892, M. Prillieux, who studied the Agaric mole and recognized that it was due to the presence of a parasitic fungus, Mycogone rosea, indicates the removal of all affected Agarics as the only remedy against the spread of the disease. 1 CHICORÉE ENDIVE Chicory, so widely used in its various forms in vegetable growing, is a plant whose origin has been the subject of the most varied hypotheses. The plant, as we know it, has not been found in a spontaneous state, but several botanists, by comparing it with an annual species known as Chicortum pumitum, which grows spontaneously in the Mediterranean region, have thought it could be assimilated to the latter. M. de Candolle, whose competence in such matters cannot be disputed, agrees. As a result, the name Chicortum endivia, by Linnaeus, should be retained for reasons of antiquity for the plant which, as we have just said, is found in the Mediterranean region, in Morocco, Algeria, etc. Chicory endive has produced, through cultivation, two types that are sufficiently different from each other in terms of form, use and cultivation, for them to be studied separately, retaining the names by which they are referred to in practice. SCAROLE CHICORICE Escarole has broad leaves, variously cut along the edges and forming a large rosette on the ground. Its leaves are attached to a very short stem that extends into an abundant branching, bearing blue flowers in sessile heads. The fruits, which follow the flowers, are angular, gray achenes topped by a small, membranous collar. These fruits, which are used as seeds, retain their germinative capacity for five to six years. 2 1 y ~ Fig. 27 - Chicory. Uses. - This plant is widely used for salads. Varieties. - Chicory has produced a number of varieties that differ little from one another. Vegetable gardens and market-gardeners grow almost exclusively green and blond scarole maraîchères, but these types are more or less perfected. To produce good quality products, they must bear abundant, ample leaves, so as to form a clump, often no less than Om.45 in diameter, which must above all be compact and dense (fig. 27). Cultivation. - Scarole is grown in vegetable gardens as an autumn salad, as this is the only time of year when it can develop fully without flowering. Scarole is rarely sown before the beginning of June. However, for some years now, we have been applying the same cultivation methods to Scarole as to Chicory (yoy. page 142), so that we can obtain summer produce. The seed should be sown on a bed and covered with a frame; it is essential that emergence takes place quickly, if we are to have a plant that produces good products. The seed should be spread very lightly, as the plant is not usually transplanted in the nursery, but waited until it has developed sufficiently to be planted directly. The seedling should be watered frequently, to activate the development of the young plant. As soon as the first leaves appear, air should be supplied to accustom the young plants to the action of external agents, and to prevent the leaves from being burned by excessive heat. Three weeks to a month at the latest after sowing, the Scarole plants, which by then have at least three leaves, should be put into position. Some time before this operation, you'll need to completely enley the frames. The seedling is pulled out by lifting the soil with a trowel. Once uprooted, it is placed in handfuls, and a knife is used to remove about a third of the root length, to prevent the plant from pivoting and forcing it to multiply its hair. To compensate for this mutilation, we also shorten the leaves, which reduces the causes of evaporation and promotes recovery. In all cases, it is useful to cover the soil with a good layer of mulch to retain water from watering. Transplanting is carried out with a planter, in rows spaced Om,30 apart, on which the Scarole plants are planted Ou,60 apart, in staggered rows. Care should be taken to press only the hairy part of the root into the soil, as planting too deeply will cause the lettuce to develop poorly. As soon as the plants are planted, they need to be watered frequently. After this first sowing, others can be made, which will now simply be done in the open air and in the ground. They should be treated in the same way as those described above. The last seedlings are sown at the end of July, and put into the ground in the last days of August. After this time, in years with a mild climate, the seedlings can produce for part of the winter; but more often than not, they don't have time to develop and rot during the bad season. When Scarole plants have reached full development, about two months after transplanting, it is necessary to bleach the leaves before releasing them for consumption. This is achieved by lifting the leaves with a strand of rye straw previously soaked in water, and tying them tightly so as to deprive them completely of the action of light (fig. 28). When the plants are well-developed, eight to ten days before tying, the grower lifts the leaves halfway up and holds them in this position with a tie. In this way, the lettuces continue to grow without getting in each other's way. The time needed for Scarole to blanch varies greatly according to the temperature, Fig. 28. - Scarole tied with a piece of straw. It can take from eight days to three weeks, depending on the season and outdoor conditions. When Scarole plants are half-bound in autumn, as described above, they can be lifted into rootballs and planted in trays towards the end of October. They are watered and mats are placed over the windows at night. In this way, you can keep Scarole plants for almost the whole winter. Market-gardeners sell Scarole plants as they have been harvested, with only the binding and spoiled leaves removed. They are sold by the dozen at a price that varies between 1 and 1 fr. 50 for this quantity. Seed production. - To harvest seed, in autumn, from among the late sowings, select the best-looking plants and winter them under a frame. In spring, they are lifted and planted at Om, 60 in all directions. Pinch off the inflorescences to obtain beautiful seeds, which are harvested when the stems begin to yellow. Curly Chicory differs from Scarolea in that its leaves are very deeply and finely cut, often taking on the appearance of a tuft of moss. Their vegetative characteristics are identical to those of Scarole. Their cultivation differs from that of Scarole in that it lends itself better to the production of early crops. Uses. - Chicory, like Scarole, is eaten as a salad, with a slightly more bitter taste. It can also be cooked, making it a very healthy, if not very tasty, food. Varieties. - They differ from one another mainly in the condition of their leaf blades, which are more or less finely cut, and in the ease with which they withstand forced cultivation. ROUEN CHICORY. - A fast-growing variety, with rosettes of leaves often up to Om.40 in diameter. Leaves with relatively wide divisions. It is widely cultivated by Paris market gardeners, who use it for their open-ground plantations. ITALIAN CHICORY. - Very sharply divided, crenulated leaves. Smaller than the previous variety, it is particularly suited to forced cultivation. Not suitable for full-ground 2 2 * * Fig. 29 - Italian chicory. especially for autumn planting, as it rots easily when the heat becomes insufficient (fig. 29). Growing in the ground. - The first Chicory seedlings for outdoor cultivation are sown at the beginning of March. It is necessary to use a warm layer giving at least 20 to 25 degrees to make this first sowing. As soon as the shot is fired, the seed is spread on the potting soil, barely covered, then the frame is closed and covered with a double layer of straw mats. If this is not done, experience shows that plants grown from seeds that have taken a long time to germinate will almost certainly go to seed before they have fully developed. To facilitate germination, it's a good idea to lightly baste the soil. As soon as the young plants have emerged, remove the mats and leave them overnight. Around two weeks after sowing, the plants already have four to five leaves, not including cotyledons. The time has come to transplant them. A new warm layer of potting soil is prepared, and the young Chicory plants are transplanted at around Om, 08 in all directions, equivalent to 250 per frame. Before transplanting, the plant is prepared by trimming the root and leaf tips. Immediately after transplanting, the plant is watered, then the frame is replaced and covered with straw mats for two days, to encourage growth. After this time, the frame is uncovered and, weather permitting, air is given to prevent the plant from wilting. At the beginning of April, the frames are removed to accustom the Chicory to the action of the outside air; about eight days later, the plant can be put in place. After ploughing the soil and covering it with a good layer of mulch, plant the Chicory, watering it beforehand, keeping a distance between rows of Oi, 25 to (1m 40). As with Scarolea, avoid transplanting Chicory too deep into the soil. Frequent watering is necessary throughout the growing season, if many of the Chicory plants are not to flower. About two months after planting, this lettuce has reached full development; then, after a good watering, it can be tied up, taking care, however, not to tie it up until the leaves are no longer moist, otherwise it will rot. To bind this salad, as the leaves are shorter than those of Scarole, it is necessary to make two turns at different heights with the rye straw, so as to retain all the leaves. Bleaching takes place quickly, and the crop can be harvested eight or ten days after binding. It is important not to water bound chicory any more, as this would cause it to rot. These spring crops, started under the precautions we have just described, will continue, but with infinitely greater simplicity, from April to July. May and June seedlings will be sown on a warm bed, from which the glazing will be removed, as soon as the plant has fully emerged; June and July seedlings will be sown in the open air, on an old bed, or even in the ground exposed to full sun. In both cases, seedlings should be sown in the open air, on an old bed, or even in the ground in full sun. Transplant, leaving a spacing of Om, 45 between the plants in all directions, as this is the time when the plants will develop most strongly. Rouen chicory and semi-fine varieties are usually used for this purpose. The last seedling can be used at two different times, by first taking part of the plant from the seedling and leaving the rest, which is light, for another three weeks or a month. In fact, Chicory can be transplanted without suffering, even though it is already well developed and half grown. Chicory requires three to three and a half months of vegetation to reach full development when grown in the open ground, and as this plant is notoriously frost-resistant, the last seedlings should be sown by the beginning of August at the latest. When the plants are fully developed, they must be tied up without delay, otherwise they may start to climb. If, however, you only want to consume them little by little, you should only lift the leaves halfway, then bind them completely a fortnight or so before releasing them for consumption. If frosts are to be feared, when the lettuces have reached full growth, it is useful to throw a little straw over them, which will protect them from the cold; 5 to 6 degrees below zero are enough to damage Chicory. Harvesting can be prolonged by removing the clods and planting them under a frame, where they keep well, provided they are kept a little dry to avoid rotting. Forced cultivation. - For early crops, we start sowing in December and January with Chicory from Italy or Rouen. Layers of new manure are planted in great thickness to ensure a temperature of 30 to 35 degrees Celsius, at least in the first few days. We cover this layer with compost, and sow as soon as the temperature has reached the point we have just indicated. DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 10 1 2 1, The seed is spread evenly over the potting soil; the seed is left on the surface, uncovered, and watered with a fine-headed watering can. Close the frame and line it with straw mats. Germination must take place within thirty hours, otherwise the plant will be worthless. At the end of this time, we check whether germination has taken place, and if not, we cover the germinated seed with about a centimetre of potting soil, which we spread by hand. After a fortnight, the plants, which have taken three or four leaves, are ready to be transplanted on a new layer giving 25 degrees of heat. Transplanting is done in the same way as for seedlings destined for potting. Three weeks later, the young Chicory plants are ready to be planted out, which is done by transplanting them onto a third warm layer covered with Om, 20 of potting soil. Six rows of five Chicory plants each are planted in each frame. Water when necessary, but not too much to avoid rotting. Often, when the chicory is half grown, you transplant a second season of the same lettuce, using seedlings you've prepared for the purpose, following the rules we've just described. In this way, successive crops of Chicory can be grown until the time comes to deliver it to the soil. On average, early-growing chicory requires three months of vegetation, from sowing to harvesting. Growing for seed. - When it comes to obtaining chicory seed, one of the best methods is to keep the plants that best correspond to the type of variety you're growing under a frame over winter, without removing them, and to plant these plants in the spring in Om, 60 in all directions. This procedure gives very good results, but is fraught with difficulties, as Chicory often rots during the winter; so, in the practice of large-scale seed production, we follow a completely different method, which is as follows: The seed is sown on a hot bed in a feyrier; then the plant is transplanted to a nursery under a frame, as described above; finally, in April, it is planted out. It goes without saying that Chicory is not bound, but allowed to set seed freely, and often not even watered, in order to hasten flowering; but this lack of watering has the disadvantage of producing only poorly developed plants. To obtain good seed, it's a good idea to pinch off the tips of flowering shoots; this produces less seed, but it's more beautiful. Harvesting takes place when the plants begin to dry out, which is usually in September. The stems are threshed, and the sifted seeds are put into bags, which are then labeled, so that when sowing in layers, preference should always be given to young seeds, which germinate more quickly, and consequently produce plants less prone to bolting. Harmful insects. - In dry soils, aphids (Aphis sonchi) attack the roots and often cause great damage. It is combated by frequent watering. 6- WILD CHICORY. WILD CHICORY Wild chicory (Chicortum intybus) grows wild in France. The leaves are dark green, often with russet spots, sinuous and sharply lobed. The leaf blade and petiole are covered with abundant hairs. From the middle of the leaves, which form a sort of spreading rosette on the ground, rises a flowering stem with abundant, spreading branches. The inflorescence is a cyme of variously arranged sessile flower heads. The ligulate flowers are a beautiful azure blue. The fruit (achene) is shiny brown. Uses. - Taken up by cultivation, this plant has singularly modified its way of being. As the leaves have expanded, they have become less hairy and less bitter. Thus modified, it lends itself to a variety of culinary uses. Sometimes, in fact, it is eaten as it is, young, in salads; at other times, it is covered with earth to be blanched on the spot. But its main consumption is in the form of etiolate, made in the cellar, to which we give the vulgar name of Capuchin Beard (fig. 30). Varieties. - In addition to the primitive type, several distinct varieties are grown. VARIEGATED WILD CHICORY. - The leaves of this variety are profusely spotted with red. It is used to make Capucin's Beard, and some of the stains persist despite the wilting process. IMPROVED WILD CHICORY. - A variety with very large, wavy, blistered leaves, giving it the appearance of a lettuce. CHICORÉE SAUVAGE A GROSSE RACINE OU CHICORÉE A CArÉ. - This variety, whose taproots are very well developed, is used, as its name suggests, to make Chicory coffee, which is so widely used. The leaves are used to conL2 Fig. 30. - Chicorée Barbe-de-Capucin. fection of the Beard. There are two main types. BRUSSELS ROOT CHICORY OR WITLOOF. - This plant, which resembles Magdeburg chicory, has the particularity that its leaves are grouped together to form a kind of apple resembling a small romaine. The cultivation of this variety is described below. Chicory production. - Wild Chicory is grown almost exclusively near Paris, in the communes of Montreuil-sur-Seine, Rosny and Bobigny. Growers who indulge in this type of production rarely grow Chicory on their own land. The reason for this is that they often don't have the space to sulphurize the soil, that the crop is exhausting, and that it's impossible to grow it successfully two years in a row on the same plot. Land is rented for the duration of a crop, i.e. from spring to late autumn. The land is delivered to the grower in April, smoked, ploughed and harrowed; it is returned in November or December. The price of this rental varies little, as the land used for this crop is always of good quality; it is usually 750 fr. per hectare. Sowing. - Once the land has been suitably prepared, sowing takes place at the end of April or beginning of May. Sowing is done in rows, to facilitate cleaning up during the summer. The rows are spaced Om, 30 apart, and the quantity of seed needed to sow one hectare is around 150 liters, at an average price of I franc per liter. Hand seeding requires no more than ten man-days; in large-scale cultivation, it would be advantageous to use a seeder. Hoeing. - Shortly after emergence of the seedling, which usually takes place eight to ten days after sowing, hoeing should be carried out between the rows with a rake; this work requires twelve man-days. A month or so after this first operation, weeding must be carried out by hand, to remove any weeds that may be found either between the rows, or on the rows themselves, between the feet of the young Chicory. This is a time-consuming operation, requiring around fifty man-days to be carried out properly. Throughout the summer, no care is taken; the leaves, once they have developed strongly, are harvested either by hand and sold as young salad chicory, or with a scythe and fed to livestock. This is an additional product that is sometimes not to be scorned. Harvesting. - Finally comes the time of weeding, which will require constant care throughout. The first job in this order of operations is uprooting. This is done either with a spade or a flat-tined fork; by lifting the plant with the tool, it is easy to pull the roots out of the ground by the few remaining leaves. This removal can be done either as and when required for consumption, or completely, in which case the roots are placed in gauges close to where they are to be used. In any case, it's a time-consuming and consequently expensive job; a man can only pull up an acre of Chicory in a day and a half, as he has to bind the roots with wicker, so as to form bunches of Om, 50 in diameter. This yields around 1,350 bunches per hectare, which are transported to the forcing site as soon as they have been dug up. In Montreuil, farmers who don't have horses have them transported by a contractor, who is paid 12 francs for the transport of 50 bunches. The bunches are then delivered to the grower's home, or to the place where the chicory is to be etiolated. Now it's time to clean the roots, which involves taking the Chicory one by one, by hand, and removing with a fingernail all the leaves, either dead or still green, attached to them. Using a knife would be inconvenient, as it would cut off the heart or central bud along with the useless leaves, thus delaying leaf growth. This peeling, usually done by women, is paid for at the rate of 1 franc per bunch, which, once cleaned, is tied again with wicker so that all the tops of the plants are at the same height. Forcing. - At this point, a layer is laid in a perfectly enclosed caye or cellar, with all openings sealed off, to cover all or part of the surface, depending on the importance of the operation. The layer should be made with fresh stable manure, and be about Om, 30 to Oin, 35 high. When the layer, which has been treaded and watered, has been fired and the temperature has become fixed at around 20 to 25°, all the bunches of Chicory are placed on its surface, side by side and as close together as possible, so as not to lose any space. Some growers even make little "bottillons", which they insert into the spaces left between the bunches. Rarely are the entire layers covered on the same day; on the contrary, we start on one side and continue on the following days, so that the produce doesn't all arrive at the right moment at the same time. When Chicory is layered, doors should be tightly closed, and the smallest exits caulked to prevent light rays from reaching the Chicory, which would turn the leaves green and allow chlorophyll to develop. 2 Fig. 31 - Basket for packaging Capucine Beard. As soon as the Chicory is placed on the bed, it begins to grow so rapidly that you can, so to speak, follow its progress by eye. After ten to twelve days, if the bed is warm enough, the etiolated leaves have reached a length of about Om, 30. During the entire forcing period, water once or twice a day, depending on the warmth of the bed, taking care to use absolutely pure water; the slightest impurity will stain the leaves with rot. Preparation for sale. - When the leaves have reached a length of 0.30 m, forcing is complete. The bunches are then taken out of the cellar with great care, so as not to break the leaves, and the bundle is detached; it is untied, and taking a small handful, it is placed on the table; a pad of moist clay half the size of your fist is placed on the roots, and this is covered with a new handful of Chicory. The bottilton is then formed, tied to the root with a thin piece of wicker, and placed in large baskets that can hold around two hundred (fig. 31). The basket is lined with linen and covered to prevent the leaves from being crumpled and light from reaching them, as they would easily turn green. Baskets prepared in this way are sent to the market. Farmers without horses are obliged to hire contractors, who charge 5 francs to transport four or five baskets in Montreuil. To this price must be added that of a half-day's man-hire to make the sale, i.e. 3 francs, bringing to 8 francs the cost of delivering 800 to 1000 bundles. Product per hectare. - Such are the operations and costs of cultivation, forcing and delivery. It will be easy for us to establish the yield per hectare. In fact, this surface area provides, as we said, in an average year. 1,350 bunches, which, after forcing, yield 50 bunches per bunch, i.e. a total of 67,500 bunches. These are delivered to the Halles de Paris at prices that vary from simple to double, depending on the severity of the winter and the degree of competition from open-ground salads of all kinds. In years when Chicory sales are low, we sell 8 to 12 francs a hundred bottillons. In years when Chicory is in high demand, the price doubles. From these figures we can deduce that a minimum price of 12 francs per 100 bottillons can be established. Expenses to be incurred. - For an area of one hectare, the price of a day's work is 6 francs in Montreuil: Renting one hectare of ploughed and smoked land.............................. 150 liters of seed at I fr. per liter........ Sowing: 10 man-days at 6 fr. per day............................ Hoeing, 12 days at 6 fr............... Weeding, 50 days.................... Weeding, 150 days................. Transport of bundles at 12 fr. per 50 bundles, i.e., for 1350 bundles....... 750 fr. 150 60 72 300 900 324 Cleaning at 1 fr. per bundle................ 1350 16 meters of manure for forcing, at 5 fr. Wicker for binding bundles and boots. 4 cubic meters of clay, 5 fr..... Transport to hall, 1 fr. per 100 bundles 80 40 20 670 Total expenses.......... 4716 fr. Profits from this crop. - Gross production is 1,350 bunches per hectare, each yielding 50 bundles, for a total of 67,500 bundles, which at 12 francs per 100 gives 8,100 francs. If we deduct expenses from this figure, we end up with a net profit per hectare of 3,384 francs. These are the profits that can be made from the production of etiolated wild chicory, details of which were supplied to us by Mr. Clément Guyot, farmer at Montreuil-sous-Bois (Seine). These figures can vary greatly according to whether the work is carried out with more or less somn, so that in other conditions the total production figures, and consequently the profit to be made, can be significantly reduced. Another farmer in the same area yields only 36,000 bottillons per hectare; it's true to say that his cultivation costs are much lower, totalling only 3,270 francs. As a result, under similar conditions, the profit is only 1050 francs, based on the same average price of 12 francs per 100 bunches. In Paris alone, around 80,000 bundles of 50 botillons are consumed every day throughout the winter. In addition to wild chicory, large-root chicory is used for this crop. Growing chicory for cutting. - A fairly common practice in farm vegetable gardens, as well as by growers for sale, is to sow Chicory in March, either in rays spaced from Om, 25, to Om, 30, or broadcast. Weed and hoe during the summer, cutting the leaves for consumption; then, in winter, clean up by removing the dead leaves. In February and March, we cover the Chicory, either with potting soil, or more simply with the soil of the square, if the sowing has been done in rows. The covering should be at least 5 to 8 centimetres thick; after two or three weeks, the chicory will have broken through the ridge of earth, and it's time to harvest. To do this, unshovel the plant, cut off the leaves, taking care not to remove the heart, and cover again. Lon can easily make two or three harvests in the spring, of a salad that's fairly bitter, but generally pleasing. The same operation can be repeated the second year on the same plants, after which they must be pulled out. The same varieties that are used to make Beard can be used for this crop, but the improved Chicory must be added. Growing Witloof chicory. - This vegetable is very popular in Belgium, and is now beginning to be widely grown here too. Its cultivation presents some difficulties, not in the way it is grown, but because of the difficulty of obtaining seed that is absolutely pure and carefully selected. As a result, growing Witloof Chicory with bad seed only produces a product resembling Capuchin Beard, this variety being eminently unstable and tending to revert to the primitive type. So far, the products obtained in France are less beautiful than those from Belgium. Seedlings are sown in the same way as wild chicory; in autumn, the roots are pulled out using the familiar procedure, and all those with a diameter of at least 3 or 4 centimetres at the top are selected; all others are discarded, as are those with several central buds instead of one. These roots are cleaned as for the Beard, and then treated in two different ways. One is to apply the ordinary forcing of the Beard, as described above. In the other procedure, we begin by making all the roots the same length by cutting off the thinned end; they are then Om,20 to Om, 25 in length. Once this has been done, a trench Om, 40 deep is opened, and the roots are placed upright, side by side, filling the gaps with soil; then the trench is filled up again. The plant is thus covered with a layer of soil of about Om, 20. If you're in a hurry to produce, a warm layer of horse manure can be laid over the surface of the pit, the thickness of which will vary according to how quickly you want to get the job done. With a layer Om.50 high, it takes an average of one month of forcing in February to obtain consumable products. Once the roots have been removed from the pit, the leaves are detached with the collar, forming a sort of elongated apple that can be eaten either raw in salads or cooked (fig. 32). Cultivation on beds. - Wild chicory or its variegated variety is sometimes sown on a bed under a frame. Sowing takes place from January to March. A layer of Om, 40 cm thick is laid, filled with compost and covered with a frame. Sow the seed in full panels, without making any rays; water lightly and cover with W lassons mulch. The seed germinates after two or three days, and fifteen days later the young Chicory is already ready to be cut and eaten as a salad. Two or three successive cuts can be made, after which the roots are removed and discarded. After this first crop, you can plant a second on the same layer, rearranging the stoves to restore heat. It's better to sow two plants in succession than to harvest the same plants for too long, because as the plants age, the leaves become increasingly bitter and hard. Wild chicory seed retains its germinative capacity for six to seven years. It is harvested from plants sown in spring and overwintered in the open ground. For improved varieties and W'itloof Chicory, the best plants should be selected and replanted at Om, 30 in the spring of the second year. 1.. ...... The age of cabbage cultivation (Brassica oleracea, Linné), whose use seems to have been known as far back as antiquity, makes it impossible to state with absolute certainty where it originated. However, indications from various documents suggest that the species originated in Europe, where it is still found spontaneously on the coasts of France. The plant is perennial, with a thick, rather unbranched stem, bearing broad, rounded lobed leaves, glabrous on both sides and covered with a glaucous, waxy efflorescence. When the stems become established and bear flowers, the shape of the leaves becomes more moderate; the petiole, originally a few centimetres long, gradually becomes shorter until it becomes non-existent. Leaves are then sessile, embracing and with entire margins. They are cruciform in shape, yellow to whitish in some varieties. The fruits that follow are long siliques, from Om,04 to Om,06, containing round, russet seeds whose volume varies considerably according to variety. The cultivation of wild cabbage since time immemorial has singularly altered its primitive appearance; its uses are therefore extremely varied. In most cabbages, the aim of cultivation has been to develop the terminal bud, which has gradually grown out of all proportion and formed what, in technical language, is known as an apple. This apple is the assembly of leaves into a voluminous bud whose shape, color and size vary infinitely according to variety; there are almost a hundred different types. This first category is known as cabbage. The terminal bud is not the only one that develops into an apple. In Brussels sprouts, for example, all the lateral buds in the leaf axils also develop into apples, which are often very hard and eaten. There are a large number of varieties in which the apples never form; the leaves remain isolated instead of interlocking and pressing together. They then grow very large. These cabbages are known as green cabbages, as opposed to head cabbages, in which the leaves that form the head, removed from the action of light, become absolutely white. In other races of cabbage, cultivation has developed the inflorescence's ramifications, whose cellular tissue, considerably increased, constitutes a sought-after foodstuff, such as broccoli and cauliflower, whose inflorescence is eaten whole. In other cabbages, the cellular tissue of the DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 11 which has hypertrophied, to the point of making the stems grow very large. The beginning of this stem growth occurs in the variety of green cabbage known as Moellier cabbage. But this cellular production of the stem is much more accentuated in Kohlrabi and Turnip cabbage or Rutabagas, between which there is the difference that in the former, the stem bulges above ground, while in the latter, this bulge occurs underground at the expense of the base of the stem and the root. These very different types of cabbage, considered as a whole, correspond to cultivation methods specific to each category. From our purely practical and cultural point of view, we will therefore subdivide cabbages into series corresponding to the uses to which they are put. The first group includes cabbages grown for the use we make of their leaves; we'll distinguish between the head cabbage series, the Brussels sprouts series and the green cabbage series. In the second group, we'll place cabbages whose stems have been developed by cultivation: these are the Savoy cabbages on the one hand, and the Turnip cabbages or Rutabagas on the other. Finally, a third group is made up of cabbages whose inflorescences have been cultivated in order to develop into edible organs, and here we place cauliflowers and broccoli. Admitting this classification, it is useful to draw up a sort of synoptic table, because of the considerable number of forms provided by cultivation of this important vegetable plant: GROUP 1. Cabbage, the leaves of which are consumed. 2nd GROUP. Cabbages. whose 3° GRoupe. in- 1st series. Head cabbage. 2nd series. Brussels sprouts. the stems are consumed. Cabbage 3rd series. Ch. broccoli. ( - - hâtif de Roscoff. Cabbages, taken as a whole, are most successful in temperate climates. In general, they have little resistance to heavy frosts, and high temperatures are detrimental to their development. As a result, in northern Europe, cabbages cannot be expected to survive the winter without shelter. On the other hand, in the South of France, summer cultivation is not very successful, unless there is a lot of irrigation water available; so cabbages are left to vegetate over winter, sown in the autumn. In equatorial climates, cabbages hardly pump at all, despite the humidity of the soil and air. However, spring and summer varieties still provide a bud which, although it is not pomaceous, is nonetheless highly prized by Europeans. For this reason, soils in which clay is a constituent element are well suited to this crop. However, clay acts more through its hygroscopicity than through the element it supplies to the soil, as peaty soils are also very well suited to cabbage cultivation. All cabbages are very fertilizer-hungry, and their success depends largely on the richness of the soil. Nitrogenous materials are particularly suitable for this crop. Slightly decomposed farmyard manure, sludge, fertilizing sewage, guanos, dried blood or flesh, all give very satisfactory results, provided they are supplied in abundance. CABUS Varieties. - Among the many varieties of cabbage grown in gardens and fields, we will only mention those that correspond to different uses or types of cultivation. YORR KALE. - This is one of the earliest-growing cabbages, and for this reason it is widely cultivated, particularly by Paris market gardeners. The head is conical and fairly tightly packed, with as few leaves as possible that do not contribute to its formation. They are smooth, full-edged and slightly glaucous. There are two forms of this variety: the early York cabbage and the large York cabbage, whose size varies with earliness. HEART OF OX. - In all its characteristics, this cabbage resembles the previous one, but its conical head is swollen towards its middle part in the shape of a heart, which earned it its name. As with the previous variety, there is an early, small form and a later, large-headed form (fig. 33). CHOU JOANET or NaNTaIS. - Very early variety, characterized by a flat head and very short stalk. It has the disadvantage of being very hardy in harsh winters, which in the Paris climate means it cannot be grown in the same way as the two preceding varieties. It is widely grown in Brittany and Anjou, whose mild temperatures mean that they can be sown in autumn to get them through the winter. CHOU DE SAINT-DENIS. - This variety produces a flattened head, colored wine-red; the leaves are ample, dark green with full edges. This cabbage is widely grown around Paris during the summer, for consumption in late autumn (fig. 34). Fig. 34 - Saint-Denis cabbage. VAUGIRARD CABBAGE. - This cabbage resembles the previous one in the shape of its head and its wine-red coloring. It is even hardier than the Chou de Saint-Denis and over-winters quite easily, provided the head is not yet fully formed. CHOU ROUGE. - The leaves of this variety are purplish-red, forming a flattened head of varying size. In fact, two different types are grown: the large red cabbage and the small red cabbage, which is less developed but earlier maturing. CABBAGE QUINTALOU CHOUD'ALSACE. - The head of this cabbage is very broad and flattened. The thin leaves are tightly packed together. It is a hardy variety, of very good quality, but late (fig. 35). SCHWEINFURT CABBAGE. - Unlike the previous variety, this one is very early. Its head is very large, reaching or even exceeding the diameter of Fig. 35 - Chou quintal d'Alsace. that of Chou quintal; but its weight is low, as the leaves are widely spaced. The leaves are pale green, cut at the edges and slightly blistered. This variety thus forms the transition between the smooth-leaf cabbages and the Savoy cabbages. CABBAGE MILAN DE PARIS. - Of all the blistered-leaf cabbages, this is almost the earliest. The head is small and moderately tight. CHOU MILAN DES VERTUS. - This variety produces a large head and is one of the largest of the blistered cabbages. It bears a large number of leaves, which are not part of the head. This variety is widely grown around Paris, where it is eaten in autumn and part of winter (fig. 36). MILAN CABBAGE FROM PONTOISE. - This late-growing cabbage is quite similar to the previous one, differing only in its smaller size and later maturity. It is fairly cold-resistant and is eaten in winter. Uses of cabbage. - Cabbages have many culinary uses, which vary slightly from country to country, but are widespread everywhere. E Mete Fig. 36 - Vertus kale. Smooth-leafed cabbages are mainly used to prepare dishes; blistered-leafed cabbages are more particularly used in soups, although there is nothing absolute in this respect. The leaves, cut into strips and then fermented, are used to make sauerkraut, for which smooth-leafed cabbages are almost exclusively used. The red-leaf varieties are often eaten raw, either in salads or preserved in vinegar as a condiment. Growing spring cabbage. - To harvest early in the spring, we mainly use York cabbage, cœur de boeuf, and also Saint-Denis cabbage, which is grown in the same way as the previous two varieties, but yields its produce later. Sowing. - Hâtils cabbages are sown in the last days of August; market gardeners, in their great precision, say at Saint-Fiacre (August 30); in reality, these sowings can extend into the first days of September, but for the climate of the center, they should not be sown later than the 10th of that month. If you sow too early, all the plants, or at least most of them, will flower instead of peeling; if you sow too late, the plants will not stand up well to the cold of winter, and you'll end up with produce that's too late. It is therefore advisable to stick as closely as possible to the indications we have just given. Once you've prepared a patch of good soil in the garden, sow the seeds broadcast. Sowing should be done relatively lightly, to avoid the young plants getting in each other's way, leading to etiolation and overdevelopment of the young cabbage stem, which could then become bent, producing defective plants. We sow about 100 grams per are; given that one gram contains about 320 seeds, and that 50 per 100 of the seeds will give good plants, this will make about 16,000 plants. Once the seed has been spread on the soil, it is lightly harrowed with a rake, then covered with an even, thin layer of decomposed mulch. The soil is then covered with a thin, even layer of decomposed mulch, and the plants are given a few basins to hasten emergence, which takes place after a week or so. Transplanting in the nursery. - As soon as the young plants have two or three leaves, not including the two cotyledons, they should be transplanted. To do this, prepare the ground to be occupied by the planting, and cover it with a layer of Oin, 02 of top soil; draw lines on the surface spaced Om, (0 along which the transplanting will take place, observing the same distance between the lines as between them. The plant is then uprooted, lifting the soil with a spade to avoid breaking the roots. We select all the plants that look good, are sufficiently vigorous and have not aborted their terminal bud. When this bud is missing, the plant is said to be one-eyed, and must be rejected, as it cannot produce apples. Transplanting is done with a planting peg. First, a hole is made in the ground using this instrument; the young plant is placed in it, and the soil is pressed against the roots, leaving another, shallower hole next to the plant, to be filled by watering. Once the plant has been transplanted, the soil is watered a few times to encourage it to take root. Setting out. - Final planting should be done before winter, usually towards the end of November or the beginning of December, in fine weather if possible. The soil for this crop should be south-facing, so as to produce early crops. Cabbages are often planted in "costières", i.e. in beds along a wall, to gain a few days in earliness. Whichever soil you choose, it must be well loosened and contain a significant proportion of fertilizer. After ploughing, rays are traced on the surface, either with a trowel or, as market gardeners do in their loose soils, with a foot. These rays should be about Om, 08 deep, with a distance between them that will vary according to the variety used, but which on average will be 0m, 25 for very early varieties and Om, 35 for those which, being later, should develop more strongly. The purpose of these furrows is to shelter the young cabbage plant. Any snow that falls, blown away by the wind, accumulates in the rays, protecting the plants from the cold. Whatever the soil preparation, the plants are raised in the nursery, lifting the soil with a spade and removing as little as possible. They are planted at Om, 40 or so in the rows, at the bottom of the furrows, with a strong border. In spring, hoe the soil back into the gullies. For all cabbages sown in autumn, which are to overwinter, it is essential to send the plants through the nursery, if you don't want to risk seeing a significant proportion of them flowering instead of pollinating. Repeated transplanting delays vegetation, while multiplying the rootlets, which, injured during replanting, branch out considerably and emit adventitious roots. Harvesting. - Harvesting begins in April for early cabbages planted in costières, and continues in May and June for those planted in beds. For sale, cabbages are harvested as soon as the head is roughly formed, by grasping them with both hands and turning them on themselves, thus detaching the rootlets. The stem and pivot are usually left attached to the head, which is then trimmed by rolling up the useless leaves on one side and holding them in place with a piece of straw. The selling price of these new cabbages varies greatly according to the mildness of the winter, and especially according to the season in which they are sold. Growing summer and autumn cabbages. - Cabbages intended for summer and autumn consumption are sown at the end of winter and the beginning of spring. For example, Joanet and Saint-Denis cabbages are sown in February, under cloche, to produce in June and July. In March and April, we sow a new season of Choux de Saint-Denis, Milans de Paris and des Vertus, and Schweinfurt, to produce in August and September. Whatever the chosen season, which varies, as we've just seen, either with the variety or, for the same variety, with the time of harvest, sowing is always done in the same way as for spring cabbages. Prepare the soil well and broadcast the seeds, covering them with potting soil or mulch. Moisture is maintained with a few drenches. For the first seedlings to be sown under a bell, the procedure is slightly different. Once the soil has been prepared, it is suitably levelled, then a bell is placed on the ground to leave an imprint of the space it will occupy. In this way, you mark out the location of all the bells under which you will sow, and arrange them in staggered rows, following three parallel lines. For this first sowing, choose a warm, sheltered spot. Summer cabbages are not usually transplanted in a nursery; however, a much better seedling can be obtained by transplanting the one sown under a bell. If you want to hasten harvest time, you can even do the transplanting under a bell, planting about thirty cabbages per bell. The soil is ploughed and abundantly fertilized for planting, which takes place about a month and a half after planting. It is not necessary to make furrows; simply mark the lines with a chalk line if planting in the kitchen garden, and simply follow the harrowing lines in the field. Once the plant has been lifted with some care, it is placed in a manna so as not to shake it too much when it is carried to the planting site. The plant is then placed in position, with a strong border. In the vegetable garden, we water to facilitate recovery; in the field, we choose a rainy day if possible, and to facilitate recovery, we soak the root in a mixture of cow dung and soil. The distance varies according to the size of the varieties to be planted. Smaller varieties are planted at Om, 55 in all directions; larger ones, such as Schweinfurt cabbages, are planted at Om, 70 or even Ou, 80 in all directions. These cabbages are harvested by cutting the head off the stalk, but leaving it surrounded by green leaves which do not help to shape it. Prices vary according to season and variety. The most common range is between 12 and 25 franes per cent. Autumn and winter cabbage cultivation. - For this third harvest season, we need to separate naturally late-ripening cabbages from those which, although less so, are nevertheless grown to produce winter produce, because of their resistance to cold. Among those that are really late, we should mention the quintal cabbage, which, sown very early, does not produce until November. The second category includes Vaugirard cabbage and Milan des Vertus and Pontoise blister cabbage, which are sown in May, and produce from November or December onwards throughout the winter, until February and March. As an example of one of these late-growing cabbages, we'll take the Alsace quintal cabbage, which is grown extensively in this region. Growing cabbage for sauerkraut. - Cabbage grown specifically for sauerkraut is widespread in Alsace. There are huge fields of it in all the beautiful Rhine valleys, but it is especially in the vicinity of Iannemarie and Altkirch that this crop occupies the largest area. Cabbage is grown mainly in strong, moist soils; clay and marl soils are particularly suited to it, and it is here that it reaches its most beautiful dimensions. The varieties used to make sauerkraut are particularly those with flat leaves, at the head of which is the Alsace cabbage quintal, and its derivatives such as the Melsbach cabbage. In all cases, whatever the variety used, the better the cabbage, the flatter, tighter and finer the leaves; in other words, not all varieties with embossed leaves are suitable for this purpose. In some parts of Alsace, the gros Chou rouge is used; it is considered hardier than the Chou quintal, but produces much less fine sauerkraut, which is less appreciated and less expensive. Sowing. - To sow cabbage, it's best to prepare a patch of land in the vegetable garden or in a field. The soil must be thoroughly fertilized, then carefully ploughed with a spade and levelled with a rake. Sowing is broadcast from the end of February, and to protect the seed from drought, it's a good idea to cover the soil with a layer of mulch made from decomposed manure. If spring is dry, you can hasten the plant's emergence and growth by giving it a few waterings. At the beginning of April, the plant is lifted from the nursery and planted in the field it is to occupy. At the time of uprooting, all underdeveloped plants are sorted out to ensure that only those showing signs of vigor and good growth are used. Soil preparation. Planting. - Before planting, it is important to prepare the soil in which the cabbages will be grown. This preparation begins with abundant manuring, equal to about 50 cubic meters of manure per hectare, which is ploughed in, followed by vigorous harrowing. When you know in advance what kind of land the plantation will occupy, it's a good idea to plough in the autumn before planting, to rid the soil of any weeds that might require repeated hoeing in the spring. Once the soil has been ploughed and harrowed, rows of Om, 70 to Om, 80 are drawn on the surface, depending on the variety grown. The cabbages are transplanted along these lines, keeping the same distance in all directions. The men in charge of this work plant three to four thousand plants a day. If possible, a misty day or one following a period of rain should be chosen to ensure a good start. During the summer, Fon gives the cabbages two or three hoeings to remove weeds and keep the soil fresh, by creating a thin layer of earth on top of the soil, which insulates the underlying soil and prevents it from drying out. Hoeing can be carried out either by hand, using a hoe, or with a cheyal hoe, which is infinitely cheaper. This is all the maintenance work that needs to be done during the summer. Harvesting and selling. - Harvesting takes place towards the end of October and the beginning of November. Cabbages are harvested by grasping the head with both hands and twisting the stem so as to break off all the small roots. As soon as they are removed from the ground, the cabbages are placed in a heap; then a man armed with a billhook cuts off the head, leaving only the white leaves that make up the head. The removed leaves are sorted, and those that are absolutely green are fed to livestock; on the other hand, those that are half-white are kept separately to make a kind of household sauerkraut (Coumpich) much prized in Alsace. White apples are piled into cars, and taken either directly home to make sauerkraut, or to market. The selling price varies according to whether the year has been more or less favorable to this crop. In years of abundance, one hundred apples are sold for 15 to 20 francs; but this price can rise to 30 francs when the harvest has been poor and cabbages are scarce on the market. Yield per hectare. - Assuming an average price of 18 francs, and that the cabbages have been planted at Om, 80 in all directions, we can estimate the yield that a similar crop can provide. Growing costs consist of. Seed and plant preparation........ 50 fr. Rent for one hectare................... 150 50 cubic meters of fertilizer at 5 fr. each........................... Plowing and harrowing................. Transplanting, 5 days at 3 fr.......... Three hoeing sessions at 25 fr. each......... Uprooting: 10 days at 3 fr......... 250 100 15 75 30 For a total expenditure of........ 670 fr. Given that 15,000 cabbages are harvested at 15 francs per hundred, we obtain a sum of 2,255 francs, representing the price of the harvest. Deducting cultivation and labor costs amounting to 670 francs, a sum of 1,585 francs remains, representing the profit from one hectare of sauerkraut cabbage. DVBOWSKI. - Cultivation 2nd ed. 12 * Sauerkraut production. - In Alsace, every careful housewife makes sauerkraut at home, either because the house has a piece of land where cabbage is grown, or because, less well shared, one has Fig. 37. - Auger for having to buy apples in autumn. In every household, once cabbages have been procured in sufficient quantity, special workers are brought in, who in autumn scour the region and do no more than prepare sauerkraut. They bring with them their own tools, which consist firstly of a kind of auger with a cutting edge, and a blade rolled into a truncated cone, about 6 centimeters in diameter at its widest point, on the edge of which a rod with a handle is inserted (fig. 37). This auger is used to remove the cores from the cabbage, keeping them whole. preparation of sauerkraut cabbage. A second tool, used by the cutters, is a kind of large plane, with four or five blades, fixed transversely in a thick piece of wood, no less than Om,50 wide, 1m,50 long, and Om,08 thick. This piece is made of good quality wood, such as pear, mountain ash or, failing these, beech. The blades are movable, and can be moved closer together or further apart, by means of wedges, depending on how fine the sauerkraut is to be obtained. They are fixed in an opening in the piece of wood, just as an iron blade would be in an ordinary plane. These blades must be kept perfectly sharp by frequent ironing. A bottomless box-shaped frame, the sides of which are equal to the width of the workpiece, slides over the plane. v a Fig. 38 - Shredding sauerkraut cabbage. When it comes to cutting sauerkraut, the first step is to obtain a broken-down barrel (usually a 225-litre Bordeaux piece), and place the planer over its opening. The cutting operation is carried out by two men, one of whom prepares the cabbages by removing the cores with the auger, and the defective leaves with a sharp knife; the other places the prepared cabbages in the mobile frame, pressing them tightly together. When the frame is full, he gives it a brisk translation movement, holding it with both hands and holding the cabbages at the same time. The cabbages grate on the blades and fall in thin strips into the barrel (fig. 38). Cutting is hard work, because you need to work fast if you want well-made sauerkraut. It's paid for at the rate of 1 fr. 25 per 100 cabbages cut, the tools being supplied by the workers. On average, 125 cabbages are needed to produce 100 kilograms of sauerkraut. As far as the business is concerned, sauerkraut is mainly produced in Strasbourg. In good houses, where this product is made with care, its price can reach 60 to 70 francs for 100 kilograms of dry sauerkraut, i.e. from which all the water supplied by the cabbage is expressed at the time of fermentation. When fermentation begins, the cut cabbage is loaded with a few cleanly washed stones. Gradually, the mass decreases, and the water contained in the cabbage floats to the surface; this explains the enormous reduction in weight that the cabbage undergoes when it is transformed into sauerkraut. In the retail trade, sauerkraut sold dry is reconstituted in a certain quantity of water, which doubles its weight and enables it to be retailed at 40 centimes per kilogram. Cabbage preservation. - Smooth-headed cabbages don't take well to the cold of winter, and if they were simply left in the fields, or in the garden squares they occupied during their growth, they would deteriorate rapidly. To prolong their life, we use various methods of preservation, which are generally quite successful. One of the most commonly used methods, and one which usually gives good results, is to uproot cabbages in November. Choosing a northern location, along a wall for example, you begin by digging a small ditch perpendicular to the wall, about half a spade's breadth deep, and throwing back the earth as you go. In this ditch, Ton places the cabbage roots, giving the apple a north-facing mnclination. As this first ditch is occupied by a row of Cabbages placed side by side, it is partly filled with the soil removed from a second similar ditch, which is made immediately behind the first; a new row of Cabbages is then placed in it, and so on. As a result, the soil of each successive gauge will cover the roots of the row of Cabbages placed in front of it, and the ground will thus be uniformly covered with Cabbage apples. As the cold approaches, the apples are covered with a little straw, which is removed as soon as the frost is over; in this way, the Cabbages are easily preserved until February. In very sandy, dry soils, cabbages keep well if you bury the head in the soil and place the root in the air. Growing cabbages for seed. - Cabbages, like most crucifers, hybridize with the greatest ease; they play, as gardeners say, and the seeds no longer faithfully reproduce the variety from which they were harvested. We must not lose sight of this notion when we want to let cabbages go to seed. The only way to prevent these plants from interbreeding is to grow only one variety for seed in your garden, or at least to keep the varieties as far apart as possible. Cultivation methods vary according to variety, and especially according to whether the crop is grown on a large scale or simply in the kitchen garden. In all cases, it is important to take into account the ease with which cabbages not only cross-breed, but also vary greatly, so that, whatever the method used, only plants that correspond exactly to the type you have in mind should ever be chosen as seed-bearers. For example, if you're growing beef cabbage for seed, you'll need to choose plants with apples elongated into a cone and bulging towards the center, those with firm apples and with as many leaves as possible to help form them, in other words, those with few useless leaves around the apple, etc. Growing for seed in gardens. - To obtain good seed, spring cabbages, the process consists in marking the purest plants of the type when the apple is formed. When the apple is fully developed, it is cut for consumption, leaving only the stalk, or core, with three or four leaves. Shortly after removal, a number of buds develop into leafy twigs, which are left to grow freely until the end of August. At this point, they are about Om, 15 in length; they are detached from the plant, by removing a portion of the plant adjacent to the shoot, which is treated as a cutting, i.e. stripped of its leaves at the base, then transplanted with a planter, in a place sheltered from the sun. These cuttings are left in place until spring, when they are lifted with a spade, taking care not to break the roots that have developed, and planted permanently, leaving a distance of Om.75 between each foot. Each plant will be fitted with a stake, against which it will be tied when the branches begin to develop. Fruits begin to ripen in July, recognizable by the yellow tint they take on; they are then harvested before full ripening has caused them to split open, and stored in a dry, shady place, where they finish ripening. When the siliques are dry, the seed is removed by threshing, bagged and stored for five to six years, during which time cabbage seeds retain their germinative capacity. For summer cabbages, we cut the apples as we recommended for spring cabbages; we don't usually take cuttings, but transplant the cores to a wall for the winter, then stake them in the spring. Finally, for winter cabbages, we leave the apples on instead of removing them, and in spring, we take care to incise them with a few strokes of a serpette, to encourage the emergence of flowering shoots. It is essential to remove the leaves from the apples as they turn yellow, to avoid rotting which would compromise the seed harvest. Large-scale cultivation of seed cabbage. - In the Bay of Mont-Saint-Michel, cabbage is grown mainly for seed. According to Mr. Lelasseux, a distinguished farmer in this region, this cultivation is carried out over very large areas. A large number of cabbage varieties are grown for seed production, the main ones being Tourlaville, Saint-Brieuc, coeur de bœuf, etc. There are two different methods for growing cabbage, which correspond to those we have indicated should be used in the kitchen garden. In the first method, cabbage is sown in August, at a rate of 5 kilograms per are; one are of seedling sown under these conditions will suffice to plant one hectare. When the plant has three or four leaves, you can, and it's even a good idea, to transplant it in the nursery; but most of the time, you don't need to do this. If you've transplanted from a nursery, you'll set the plant in walnut, while if you've done without, you'll set it in September. Leave a space of Om.60 between rows, and plant at Om.40 between rows. In spring, two hoeings are necessary, followed by ridging with a horse hoe. It often happens that, instead of flowering, cabbages start to peel; in these conditions, it is useful to cut off the young head during the winter. As a result, the lateral buds develop, and it is from these that the seed is harvested. Harvesting takes place at the end of July, using a f'aucille. In all cases, you must cut while the yellow siliques are not yet open, to allow the seed to escape. Cabbages are placed in bunches, with the help of which hubettes comprising thirty to forty bunches are built. After ten days, they are beaten with a lléau, then winnowed to obtain clean seed, which is then bagged. This is the most commonly used method, but many growers prefer to harvest the apple and then use the lateral buds, which are transplanted as cuttings and are sure to set seed in the spring. These cuttings, known as jetains in the Bay of Mont-Saint-Michel, are transplanted in November, and the cultivation and harvesting procedures are the same as those described above. In Brittany, cultivation costs are as follows for one hectare: Preparing the plant in the nursery........ 60 fr. Etôtage.................................. Harvesting and threshing....................... 60 2 2 40 60 10 80 Total............ 664 fr. As for yield, this varies enormously from year to year, and can be estimated at between 18 and 30 hectolitres per hectare. When you want to produce seed, it's a good idea to make prior arrangements with the company that is to buy it, which will supply the seed needed to make the plant and will usually reserve the right to inspect the plantation and reform any plants that don't seem to correspond to the variety grown. BRUSSELS KALE Two slightly different types of this cabbage breed are grown: the ordinary Brussels sprout and the dwarf Brussels sprout. The first of these two types (fig. 39), which is by far the most widespread in cultivation, is a vigorous plant bearing long-stalked leaves on a stem up to a meter high, with a spoon-shaped blade. These leaves come together at the top of the stem to form a kind of poorly formed apple. In the axils of all stem petioles, buds form small apples the size of walnuts. In this variety, these apples are tightly packed and very well formed. In the dwarf variety, instead of being smooth, the leaves are slightly blistered and very close together on the stem, which acquires smaller proportions than in the previous variety. As a result of the close-knit leaves, the small apples are tightly packed together; they are, moreover, less well formed than in ordinary Brussels sprouts and therefore less prized for sale. Uses. - We eat the lateral buds or small apples, which must be firm and have the average volume of a small walnut. Cultivation. - This cabbage is sown at two different times. The first in February or March, and the second in March or April, to prolong the harvest. Sowing is done in the nursery by broadcasting, taking the same care as indicated above; then, after four to six weeks, transplanting is done directly in situ. For this type of cultivation, in contrast to the other types, it is advisable to choose relatively thin soil, otherwise there is a high risk that the small apples will not form, but that the cabbage will grow all leafy, without producing the expected product. For this reason, this crop is much more successful in open fields than in gardens, where the soil is usually too rich. Brussel sprouts are planted at Om,60, in all directions, Fig. 39 - Ordinary brussel sprouts. and bound tightly. When the stem has reached about Om,50, the terminal bud is pinched off, to encourage the development of the lateral buds. We also hasten the development of the latter by cutting off the base leaves. This cabbage does not fear frost, so harvesting, which can start as early as October for the first sowings, lasts until March for second-season sowings. Yields vary greatly according to the soil in which the crop is grown, but are usually around 350 liters per acre. Seed crops. - As seed-bearing plants, we choose those with well-shaped, hard apples close together on the stem; we mark the plants early and do not harvest small apples from them. In April, we plant them at Om, 75, giving them a stake; we cut off the head of the cabbage if this operation has not been done previously and remove the small apples at the bottom, which are poorly made, as well as those at the top whose development is incomplete, leaving only those in the middle to flower. Harvesting care is the same as above. CHIOUX VERTS Varieties. - Non-pollinating cabbages have given rise to a considerable number of cultivated varieties, most of which we won't go into, since they are part of agricultural crops and are most often grown as fodder. We will only mention those varieties that are suitable for human consumption. CABBAGE WITH LARGE RIBS. - This cabbage is just starting to apple, and could be considered as the transition from cabbage to green cabbage. It is characterized by curly leaves with well-developed veins. It is grown in vegetable gardens because of its resistance to cold. MOEILLIER CABBAGE. - This variety, which reaches a height of 1.50 m when fully developed, has ample leaves which, like those of all its congeners, are sometimes used; but we mention it mainly for the reason that its stem bulges towards the middle, as a result of the development of its cellular tissue. In this way, it is the gateway to the cabbages whose stems we eat.
