Cultivation. - There is nothing special to say about the cultivation of these cabbages, which are grown in the same way as the previous ones. CHIOUX-RAVES Varieties. - Choux-Raves are characterized by the often very voluminous swelling of their stems above ground level. The varieties most commonly grown in vegetable gardens are the following two: WHITE HATIF KOHLRABI. - This variety has a small number of sparsely-developed leaves. The bulge forms quickly (fig. 40). HATIF VIOLET KOHLRABI. - This kohlrabi differs from the previous one in that its stem and leaf petioles are colored light purple. Uses. - The swollen stem is eaten cooked. The cellular tissue contained within has a pleasant taste; it is widely used throughout eastern France, particularly in Alsace. Cultivation. - Kohlrabi is sown in spring, from March to June, in good soil. When the plant has three or four leaves, it is transplanted in place, spaced Om, 25 apart in all directions. Water several times during vegetation, and two months or so after planting, these cabbages can be delivered for consumption. They can be kept for a long time, provided they are pulled up and taken down to the cellar. CHOUX-NAVETS Varieties. - Turnip cabbages, or Rutabagas, whose edible part is formed by the lower part of the stem, and by the root which has gorged itself with cellular tissue, vary from one another in the coloring of the leaves and stems. The most common variety grown in vegetable gardens is the following: WHITE, SMOOTH KOHLRABI. - The fleshy part is shorter than it is wide. The flesh is white (fig. 41). Uses. - Kohlrabi is eaten cooked and prepared in a variety of ways. Cultivation. - Kohlrabi can be sown either in rows Fig. 41 - White, smooth Kohlrabi, or broadcast in spring; after emergence, thin out to leave a space of Om, 40 between plants in all directions. During the summer, we water and hoe. Harvesting takes place in autumn and early winter. CAULIFLOWER Varieties. - Cauliflowers, in which the inflorescence has developed into a globular mass while still young, are classified as tender, semi-hard and hard cauliflowers, according to their greater or lesser hardiness and the time they require to reach full development. LNI9 A Fig. 42. - Lenormand short-stalked cauliflower. CAULIFLOWER SMALL, SALOMON. - This variety, which is still known as Chou-Fleur tendre de Paris, is early and is used particularly in forced cultivation; its head is not very large, but it is quite white and takes little time to form. LARGE CAULIFLOWER, SALOMON. - This demidure variety is more developed than the previous one, without however acquiring a very large volume; its head is tight and of good quality, CHOU-FLEUR LENORMAND A PIED COURT. - This is certainly the most widely grown variety. It is very well suited to growing in pleme terre, and its head develops well (fig. 42). PARIS HARD CAULIFLOWER. - This variety is characterized by the fact that its head, which is slow to form, remains hard and tight for a long time. This variety is mainly used for autumn produce grown in the open ground. Uses. - The inflorescence is cooked and eaten in a variety of ways. Field-grown. - Cauliflower production has become very widespread throughout France, but mainly in the vicinity of large cities. Near Paris, as we'll show later, this large-scale crop is generally very profitable. Cauliflowers like a permeable soil, but one that is not free from a certain coolness, especially when the crop is grown in summer. Soils in which silica is a dominant element are particularly suitable. They require abundant fertilization, using, if possible, fertilizers that have already decomposed and are in a state that allows them to be intimately mixed with the soil. City sludge and manure from the soil, either from early crops or from mushroom quarries, are the preferred fertilizers, which, as we can see, correspond to rapidly assimilable nitrogenous matter. Water is necessary for this crop, but in very variable proportions, depending on the environment in theDYRONSRI. - Vegetable growing. 13 in which it is grown. For example, while irrigation is necessary in the South of France and Algeria, in the climate of Paris this plant can do without watering. However, when watering is possible, it can only produce good results. In the Gennevilliers plain, for example, which is irrigated with sewage water, good results are obtained. In our Parisian climate, we generally grow three crops in the open air during the course of the year, so as to obtain, if not an absolutely consistent product throughout the summer, at least harvests that are very close together. It is therefore necessary to study these three different methods of cultivation, which we classify as spring, summer and autumn crops. Spring cultivation. - To grow cauliflowers in spring, they need to be sown in the autumn of the previous year. Market gardeners usually sow their seeds between September 15 and 20, in the open air on old potting soil, or simply in well loosened soil covered with compost. As soon as the seeds have been sown and covered with a little soil, they are lightly basked and watered repeatedly to keep the soil constantly moist. Seed emergence takes about ten days. If the year is cold, it's a good idea to follow the example of market gardeners, who cover their seedlings with cloches; but this precaution isn't essential and, as we've just said, you can simply sow in the open air. In any case, when the seedlings have grown two leaves in addition to their two cotyledons, it's time to transplant them. There are two ways to do this

One is to use cloches, the other is to transplant under frames; both give satisfactory results, so the choice will depend on the equipment available. To transplant under cloches, the soil is laid out in ados, i.e. in a sort of sloping plank on the south side, so that, starting from ground level, the highest side facing north is about Om, 20. The width of these ados is such that three rows of cloches can be laid out in staggered rows. In this way, a series of ados can be laid out, one behind the other. The soil used to build them must be of very good quality, and must be topped up and covered with potting soil. Once the "ados" have been built, a bell is placed on each of them to indicate where each one should be planted. Nineteen feet are planted per bell. When frames are used instead of bells, they are placed at a slight angle and the inside is lined with potting soil up to Om, 12 of the glass. Transplant at Om, 08 in all directions, equivalent to planting 200 per frame. After transplanting, water. Throughout the winter, care will consist of providing air whenever it doesn't freeze. If, on the other hand, the cold should become intense, not only should doormats be placed over the bells and frames, but the trunks of the latter should also be covered with a bundle of old manure or leaves, so as to prevent frost from penetrating through the boards. If it freezes during the day, the mats should remain on the windows for the duration of the cold, and when the thaw comes, the plants should not be made to pass through sudden transitions, i.e. air should only be uncovered and given gradually. Towards the end of March, we'll uproot the plants kept in trays and plant them out. Care should be taken to reform any plants that are blind, i.e. whose heart has been destroyed. If you want to plant early, choose a bed of earth sheltered by a wall; but you can also plant in a square, provided you get later-ripening plants. As we have said, the soil should be abundantly fertilized and well prepared by ploughing, if the land is free, as transplanting is often carried out in half-planted crops such as carrots or lettuce. Plant at about Om. 70 in all directions and in staggered rows; increase this distance if you have intercrops to manage. If the weather is dry, water again the following day. Watering should be continued throughout the vegetation period, as this crop is mainly grown in the vegetable garden, not in the fields. As soon as the intercrops have risen, we hoe them, cover the soil with mulch and water. Apples start to appear towards the end of April. As soon as they begin to free themselves from the surrounding leaves, they must be protected from the action of air and light, which would make them yellow, hard and unmarketable. They are protected by breaking off the lower leaves and placing them on this apple. Every two or three days, we go back between the rows and cover the apples with new leaves, always taken from the bottom of the plant. The fresh leaves are placed on top of the apple, and the wilted ones on top of these; this constitutes an absolute covering that will leave the apple white and tightly grained. Before the inflorescence branches begin to drop off, i.e. when the cauliflower is at its most valuable, it is harvested by cutting off the head and some of the central leaves that protect it. Harvesting begins towards the end of May for plants planted on a ridge, and continues through June for those grown in a square. Summer cultivation. - For this crop, which is the most difficult to grow, you need to sow seeds of semi-hard cauliflower on a bed under glass at the beginning of April. This sowing must be done fairly lightly, as the young plant will not be transplanted in the nursery. This would cause the apples to appear too early, and result in insufficient volume of produce. Once the seedling has emerged, the frame is lifted and the plant is gradually acclimatized to the outside air. About five weeks after sowing, the plant has taken on four or five leaves, and it's time to put it in place. A fresh, rich soil is absolutely essential; this planting will require frequent watering to give good results, so it should only be done in vegetable gardens where a large quantity of water is available. Transplant at Om, 80 in all directions, taking care to press the plant down to the first leaves. After sowing radishes, the soil is firmly bound and mulched. We can't stress enough that throughout the summer you must water heavily and often: market gardeners water every day; this is the only way to get good apples, which otherwise fall apart and don't produce anything. Care must be taken to ensure that the cauliflowers are well covered when the flower begins to show; the slightest ray of sunlight hitting them for just a few hours will damage them completely. Harvesting begins at the end of July and throughout August. As soon as the apples are good, they must be cut, otherwise they would deteriorate in a few days. In insufficiently watered crops, we have to put up with the ravages of flea beetles, which cause great damage and often destroy the entire crop. Autumn cultivation. - Autumn cultivation of cauliflower is one of the most widespread, for the dual reason that it requires relatively little care and yet gives assured results. Although it is grown by market gardeners and in vegetable gardens, it is mainly a large-scale crop. This type of production is recommended wherever the soil is rich and fresh, and where the expense of abundant manuring is not an obstacle. It is grown extensively in the Paris area, and to give an accurate idea, we can do no better than to describe the details of this cultivation as it is practiced in Chambourcy. The cultivation of this plant, to which other vegetables have gradually been added, has been the source of this locality's wealth. Chambourcy cultivation. - In this locality, where cauliflower production is one of the dominant crops, the soil admirably meets the requirements of the vegetable we are dealing with. Rich, moderately compact and cool even in summer, the soil slopes gently to the north. The plant we use comes from seedlings sown in May. The seeds are sown along a wall to protect them from the midday sun. Sowing is done in the open, as the plant will not be transplanted in the nursery, and care is taken to cover it with mulch so that it can be watered repeatedly without gullying or beating the soil. This daily watering is the best way to prevent flea beetles, which would otherwise wreak havoc. Sowing begins in May and continues until mid-June, to stagger the harvest. This result can be accentuated by using half-hard cauliflowers, then hard cauliflowers at the end of the season. Many growers don't grow the plants themselves, but buy them from market gardeners in Paris at 10 francs a thousand. The land to be planted must be abundantly smoked, as we said. At Chambourcy, slurry is used at a rate of 1 cubic meter per are; the price of this fertilizer delivered to the field is 6 francs per meter. The fertilizer is ploughed in well, followed by two harrows to make the soil perfectly loose. Following ploughing, planting takes place from June 15 to July 25. It is carried out with a planter, spacing the plants by Om, 80 in all directions. The recovery of this plant would be very poor if it were not watered, so growers do not fail to take this precaution. They carry the water on foot, using barrels placed on carts, and watering is done with a watering can without apples. Watering is done very sparingly; one lane of water is enough to water a hundred plants. If the weather's fine, the next day we repeat the operation, and that's all the water these plants will receive for the duration of their vegetation. This watering can be done fairly quickly, at no great expense, whatever the size of the crop. There are growers who plant no less than 60 or 80,000 cauliflowers, sometimes more, and who still do it, and it is estimated that two men, with a horse-drawn cart at their service, can wet a hectare's plantation in a single day. During vegetation, the soil needs to be hoeed twice, to loosen it and prevent it from drying out, while at the same time ridding it of weeds. In the past, these hoeings were done by hand, and were very expensive; today, they are done with a cheyal hoe, and it's easy to get them done for 12 to 15 francs. The hoe is passed between the rows, then a man completes the work using a hand hoe, in the immediate vicinity of the cauliflowers. When the apple appears, it is covered; a woman is employed for this work, who can easily oversee a hectare of cultivation. Harvesting begins in October, for semi-hard cauliflowers sown early, and lasts until late November. The timing and quality of this harvest varies enormously. In general, cauliflowers produce rather poor quality products if the summer is rainy, as they develop as soon as they are planted and without the plant having had time to develop an abundant root system. On the contrary, if dry weather prevails for a month or so after planting and after the seedling has taken root, the seedlings, far from suffering, take advantage of it, as the natural humidity of the soil is more than sufficient to multiply the foliage. If, after this drought, rainfall favors the development of the leaves, and a few days of mild weather in October help the apples to form, the harvest will take place in the best possible conditions, and the produce will fetch a very high price on the markets. As a result of this inevitable subordination of the value of this vegetable to the climatic conditions that govern its development, if its cultivation is remunerative even in average years, it can, in exceptionally favorable conditions, become very profitable. As we are fully aware of all cultivation costs, we can calculate the total as follows, for an area of one hectare: Soil rent............................ Manuring; 100 cubic meters at 6 fr........ Plowing and harrowing...................... Plant purchase at 10 fr. per thousand........... Planting; 10 days at 3 fr........... Watering............................ .... Two hoeing sessions at 15 fr. each............... Covering care: 15 days at 2 fr. 250 fr. 600 100 160 30 20 30 30 Total expenses............ 1220 fr. -1 As for products, they are, as we have said, extremely variable, depending on whether the year has been good or bad. When the season has been totally unsatisfactory, cauliflowers are sold for 15 francs per 100, but rarely less; if, on the other hand, everything has gone well, the price rises to 50 francs. Assuming a price of 30 francs, which is more or less the average, the gross revenue from 15,600 plants per hectare is 4,680 francs. Lowering the price to 25 francs would still yield 3900 francs, giving a net profit of 3460 francs in the first case, and 2680 francs in the second. As you can see, this is still a very respectable figure, which should encourage the grower to further extend this production, all work on which can be carried out using the tools at his disposal. Forced cultivation of cauliflower. - Only the earliest, most tender varieties of cauliflower are usually used for early crops. In this case, we use seedlings obtained by exactly the same procedure as that described above for spring cauliflowers, i.e. the seedling sown in September is transplanted into a nursery under a bell, or in a frame, where it is left until the time comes to plant it on a bed. Around New Year's Eve, we start building the beds that will receive the first cauliflower season. This layer is built with a mixture of annealed manure and new manure, to which leaves can also be added in varying proportions. This layer should be about Om, 50 high, to obtain 20 degrees of heat. Once the layer is built up, place the chests in which you will load Om 18 with compost. When the shot is fired, the cauliflower plant is carefully removed and planted by hand in the potting soil of the bed. Plant in two rows, two or three to a frame, depending on whether you're dealing with a variety with stronger or weaker development. As cauliflowers develop quite slowly in the early stages, the space left between the plants is usually used to grow either radishes or lettuce, or sometimes both at the same time. This is not a great disadvantage, as they will be harvested before the cauliflowers are fully developed. Cauliflower requires some watering as it grows. This crop should be given air whenever the weather permits. When the leaves have developed sufficiently to touch the glass, the boxes should be lifted with screw jacks, and the base secured with manure. Apples begin to form in April, and as soon as they appear, the same care as that described above for outdoor cultivation should be applied. Harvesting takes place towards the end of the same month. This harvest, provided by cultivation under a frame, can be prolonged by renewing the plantations, spacing them fortnightly, until they can be planted in the open air; in this way, sustained produce can be obtained throughout the spring. Market gardeners use the beds they build in November and December for growing lettuces, reworking them at the end of January or in February, and mixing a certain proportion of new manure with the old manure to bring back fermentation and produce renewed heat. In April, the carrots are harvested, and as the cauliflowers begin to touch the glass, the frames are removed, vegetation is completed in the open air, and the harvest takes place in May or June, depending on the variety and the time of planting. Growing for seed. - Producing cauliflower seed is not always a successful operation, which explains why it fetches such a high price on the market. Whatever the variety, cauliflowers for seed should always come from seedlings sown in September and kept in a greenhouse. When the apples appear in spring, choose the plants that will give the best results, i.e. those with a firm, tight texture. Avoid choosing apples that are mossy or have insufficient development. Once the selection has been made, only one leaf should be left on each apple to prevent it from hardening under the action of the sun; this leaf should be removed once the inflorescence has developed. Once flowering has taken place, it's a good idea to pinch off the tips of flowering shoots, to ensure the formation of the siliques placed at the base of the inflorescence. As soon as the fruits turn yellow, harvest them and leave them to mature under cover. Insects and parasites affecting cauliflowers are the same as those affecting cabbages and broccoli. BROCOLLI cabbages Broccolis are very similar to cauliflowers, but differ in a number of vegetative characteristics that make them easy to distinguish. They are grown as biennial plants, meaning that, sown in the course of one year, they only produce in the spring of the following Fig. 43 - Early white broccoli. year. Broccoli leaves are narrower and more numerous than those of Cauliflower; the petioles bearing them are usually bare for a long length. The stems are long, so that the apple is often more than 50 centimetres above the ground. The apples are fine-grained, white and of very good quality in the advanced varieties. Uses. - Same as for cauliflower. Varieties. - Broccoli varieties are relatively few in number; a few with colored apples are known, but the most sought-after are those with fine grain and which are as close as possible to our cauliflowers proper, whose ancestors, according to some authors, they are. WHITE BROCCOLI HATIF. - This variety vegetates quickly, with abundant leaves and very wavy edges. The apples are white and of good quality (fig. 43). ROSCOFF BROCCOLI. - Not very distinctive variety, grown mainly throughout Brittany, where it performs well in winter, supplying markets throughout the season. MAMMOTH BROCCOLI. - A late variety producing very good quality apples. Introduced to the market by English growers, it quickly spread to crops around Paris. Cultivation. - Broccoli cultivation has expanded considerably in recent years. Indeed, it has the great advantage of being able to supply produce when cauliflower is in short supply; it supplies the big city markets throughout the winter and part of the spring. Considerable quantities are sold at the Paris market halls; Aveyron, Poitou and a large part of Brittany supply this consumption in the big cities. Produce from the Paris region only arrives later, succeeding that from the regions just mentioned. Cultivation in the Bay of Mont Saint-Michel. - The following information, which we owe to Mr. Lelasseux, a farmer in Saint-Broladre, relates to procedures followed throughout the temperate region of France, which provides winter supplies. Starting in April, an area of variable size is prepared by deep ploughing and harrowing with a rake, depending on the size of the crop, and on the basis that one hectare needs to be sown in order to plant one hectare. Once the soil has been prepared, the seed is broadcast at a rate of 5 kilograms per are. Immediately afterwards, the seed is raked in and, if possible, covered with a light mulch. Watering is usually unnecessary, as germination is easy thanks to the natural humidity of the soil at this time of year. By May, plants with three to four leaves are ready for planting. The soil for this crop must be rich and loose, but it is useful if the richness does not come from fresh fertilizers, but from manuring done the previous year. Fresh fertilizers, especially those coming from cowsheds, would have the serious disadvantage of making Broccoli grow in leaves, while the apple would only take on a small volume. The soil is made loose by double ploughing followed by harrowing. Broccoli is planted in rows, using a planter, with a strong border. It is advisable to leave one meter between the rows on which the Broccolis are planted at Om, 60, thus obtaining a plantation of about 15,000 plants per hectare. During the vegetation period, two or three hoeing operations are necessary; these can be carried out using a horse hoe, which simplifies the operation and reduces its cost. In autumn, it's a good idea to use the spade to bring the soil down to the base of the Broccoli, to protect it from frost. In November, the apples start to appear. To keep them very white, we cover them with leaves taken from the base, as we said when talking about Cauliflower; but here this operation is less essential, for the reason that Broccoli, being amply covered with leaves, and the sun rarely appearing at the time when the apples are forming, they run little risk of yellowing. However, by covering them, we obtain more beautiful and therefore more saleable products. Harvesting begins as early as December, in any case before the seeds have spread. The apples are snipped, leaving a sufficient number of leaves to wrap them, which facilitates packing; this is done by placing the Broccolis in a sort of large open-worked cages, about Im, 20 square, which are covered with straw held in place by strings. Selling prices vary according to the beauty of the produce, and also according to the year; they range from 10 to 25 francs per hundred. Growing around Paris. - In this region, seedlings are sown in the second half of April. To hasten vegetation, it's a good idea to sow the seeds either on an old bed, or in a northern exposure, in garden soil, which is covered with compost. The seeds are buried with a rake, and the soil mulched after being lightly trodden. As soon as the plant has produced three leaves, not including cotyledons, it is transplanted into the nursery, in troughs spaced Om, 15 cm apart. Care must be taken to press the plant down to the first leaves, and to water after transplanting. Planting out, which usually takes place in June, is done by removing the rootball plant with a spade and replanting it in a square at a distance of Om, 80 in all directions. The soil should be rich in decomposed fertilizers such as old topsoil. During the autumn, the soil is hoeed several times, taking care to bring the soil back onto the plant, to make a light ridge. Broccoli plantings are often intercropped. To protect the plants, not from the cold, to which they are not very sensitive, when these do not exceed about 8 degrees below zero, but rather to avoid the sudden alternations of freezing and thawing, it is customary in November to spade a hole next to each plant, on the north side. Pressing down on the plant, we then incline it in this sort of small pit, and if very heavy frosts occur, we throw a layer of long litter over the plant, enough to shelter it from the frosts. This is how growers in the Oise region do it. We have grown Broccoli in the Paris climate, in DYBOWSRI. - Vegetable growing. 14 by planting them in troughs to a depth of about Om, 15. A little leaf sprinkling in the troughs goes a long way towards protecting the plants from frost. The buds appear in March and April. It is a good idea to cover them and harvest them as soon as they have reached full development, otherwise they will quickly lose their value. Insect pests and parasites. - All cabbages have numerous enemies, which attack the various organs of the plant. From a young age, and often as soon as the two cotyledons appear, cabbages and cauliflowers are often attacked, especially in dry, south-facing soils, by a beetle called Altica brassicer, known in gardens as the tiquet or earth flea. This insect often wreaks such havoc that part or even all of the young plant may perish. Preventive treatment consists in sowing in summer, especially with a northern exposure, and keeping the soil moist by frequent watering, which benefits the cabbage. Curative treatment, on the other hand, involves spreading fresh horse manure, or soot, wood ash or sawdust, soaked in heavy oil. Later, the cutworm or larye of the harvest moth (Agrostis segetum) gnaws at the roots of young cabbages; there's no other way to get rid of it than to look for it and crush it. The Cabbage White (Pieris brassteæ) and the Cabbage Moth (Hadina brassicæ) lay eggs on cabbages, giving rise to larvae whose ravages are very fearsome. In some years, the entire leaf parenchyma is completely devoured by these caterpillars, which also attack cauliflower apples, sometimes so badly damaged that they cannot be eaten. If the number of caterpillars is small, the best thing to do is to collect them by hand; this is what the market gardeners and growers of Chambourcy do. If, on the other hand, their numbers are very large, a good method is to syringe them with water, in which carbon sulfide is emulsified at a rate of around 10%. Cabbages, particularly cauliflower and broccoli, are attacked by a parasitic fungus that manifests itself as a sort of white efflorescence, which practitioners call white, mennter or plaster. This fungus, which goes by the name of Cystopus candidus, is unfortunately impossible to combat, because it lives inside the plant's tissues. It manifests itself externally when cabbages and cauliflowers are going to seed; it also wreaks havoc on seedbeds, often absolutely destroying inflorescences. Plants affected by this disease must be burned to destroy the germs. This plant, to which Linnaeus gave the Latin name Cucumis sativus, seems, as de Candolle predicted and as herbal botanists have confirmed, to have originated in north-west India. It grows abundantly and spontaneously at the foot of the Himalayas. It does not appear there with all the vegetative characteristics we know, but the age of its cultivation, which was already known to the Greeks and Romans, may well have modified them. There's every reason to believe that the cucumbers found in the Himalayas, although referred to by some botanists under different names, are closely related to our own Cucumis satieus, whose ancestors they are. The Cucumber is an annual plant whose recumbent stems bear alternate, large, palmate leaves, cordate at the base and forming five rounded lobes, sometimes toothed. These stems bear tendrils, which are the terminations of aborted axes. The inflorescence is monoecious. Male flowers appear first. After fertilization, the ovary develops into a fruit of variable length, size and color. These fruits are sometimes smooth, sometimes covered with a kind of small protuberance ending in prickles, which are found on all plant organs. The seeds are inside the fruit, embedded in a kind of pulp that is the result of the disintegrated placentas. These seeds look a little like a pear seed; they are white. They retain their germinative capacity for seven to eight years. Uses. - Cucumbers are used in a wide variety of culinary preparations. They are eaten cooked and stuffed, or raw in salads when fully developed. They are often eaten when only half-developed. They can be marinated either in vinegar, which is what gherkins are made of, or in water with aromatic herbs, as is common practice in northern Europe. Varieties. - Cucumbers have been cultivated for a very long time, so their fruit shapes and sizes have varied greatly. In practice, they are divided into two classes, some with white fruit, others that remain green. BONNEUIL WHITE CUCUMBER. - Large fruit, swollen in the middle. The plant that bears them is hardy and very well suited to outdoor cultivation (fig. 44). HATIF WHITE CUCUMBER. - Differs from the preceding cucumber only in that its fruit is long and cylindrical rather than bulging. Green when young, turning white as it develops. GREEN GHERKIN CUCUMBER. - Fruit covered with warts, green, becoming yellowish when ripe. This highly fertile variety is specially cultivated for harvesting as soon as the fruit reaches finger size and length. ROLLINSON GREEN CUCUMBER. - Very long, stalkless green fruit with longitudinal grooves. Clearly distinguishable from previous varieties in that it is strangled near the stalk into a 6 Fig. 44. - White cucumber Fig. 45. - Green cucumber. de Bonneuil. Rollinson. sort of narrow neck (fig. 45). It is particularly suited to forced cultivation, for which it is almost exclusively used. Open-ground cultivation. - Cucumbers multiply by means of the seed, which always germinates with the greatest ease. The varieties most commonly used for outdoor cultivation are the green gherkin cucumber and the early white cucumber. Although hardy, cucumbers are very sensitive to frost, and a drop in temperature to zero is enough to kill them. For this reason, seedlings can only be sown late, or shelters must be used to protect them until the time when white frosts are no longer feared, which in our climate is the second half of May. Given the need to avoid a drop in temperature, there are two possible methods: one involves sowing in situ and sheltering the young plant under cloches, the other involves sowing in a frame and replanting in situ. On-site sowing is done by opening trenches in the soil, Om, 10 deep and Om, 50 wide, which Ton fills either with manure from old layers, or with leaves, and then covers with the soil removed from the trench. The length of these trenches varies according to the size of the crop. Between each trench, leave about 1.20 m. Once the soil has been prepared, sow cucumber seedpots in the middle of the trench, Om.60 apart, place five or six seeds and cover them with a bell. When emergence has occurred, leave two or three plants under each bell, and pull out the others, which can be used to make a new plantation. As soon as the seedling takes its first leaves, it should be given some air to gradually get used to its action, so that the cloches can be removed at the beginning of June. If sowing under a frame, this should be done in the first few days of May on a warm bed. When the plant has two cotyledons, it is transplanted one by one into cups of Om, 10, and placed in a terrau prepared as above, towards the end of May. In the Midi region, seed is simply sown in a square, without the use of trenches or bells. Whichever method is used, once the plant has two leaves, it is pruned to encourage rapid branching. After this initial pruning, the plant is usually left to its own devices. Fruits appear at the end of June; we leave whatever the plant is willing to produce if these fruits are destined for making gherkins, in which case they are harvested as soon as they are the size of a finger. Harvesting should take place every two days. If, on the other hand, you want large cucumbers, you should leave only about ten per plant. In dry soils, cucumbers should be watered frequently; in cool soils, on the other hand, water should only be given if the drought becomes persistent. Gherkins, which are produced in large quantities around Massy, Palaiseau and Verrières, are sold by weight at the Halles. The price is usually O fr. 30 to O fr. 50 per kilogram. As for cucumbers, they are sold by the dozen, and their price varies greatly according to the season and the development they have acquired. In Paris, white cucumbers are sold by the dozen for between 2 fr. 50 and 3 francs during the summer. For seed, we choose the best-formed fruit and let it mature on the vine. When the fruit is judged to be completely ripe, which can be recognized by its lighter color, we open it and remove the seeds inside. Wash with plenty of water, then dry in the shade. Cucumber cultivation is very successful throughout the intertropical zone, making it a valuable resource for vegetable growing in our colonies in this region. Here, seedlings are sown in situ, without the use of soil or compost. Cultivation on a bed. - Paris market gardeners frequently grow cucumbers as early crops. They have succeeded in perfecting their cultivation methods to such an extent that their produce appears at the halles in the early months of the year. We owe the following information to M. Curé, one of the most expert market gardeners in this type of cultivation. Sowing begins in the first fortnight of December. The seeds are sown on a very hot bed equipped with heaters; one by one, they are pushed into the soil with the tip of the finger. Emergence takes place after a few days, and as soon as the two cotyledons are fully developed and the first leaf begins to appear, the plant is transplanted. For this purpose, Om10 diameter cups are placed on a new bed, at a rate of 100 per frame. In the middle of each of these cups, placed on the layer and filled with potting soil, we transplant a cucumber seedling using a finger or a planter, taking care to press it in up to the cotyledons. Water lightly, then close the trays and cover with straw mats overnight. If it freezes very hard, it's often necessary to leave the mats on for part or all of the day. But it's important to remember that this occlusion is detrimental to young plants, which quickly wither, so avoid prolonging it. As soon as the young plant has two well-developed leaves, the stem above them is cut. As a result, the buds in the axils of the two leaves develop into two shoots, and the plant is ready for planting. Before this happens, if winter is severe, it is often useful during the vegetation period either to reshuffle the layer's warmers, or even to build a new layer on which the seedlings or cups are transported. A month and a half after sowing, the seedling is ready for planting. A new, warm layer is prepared and filled with potting soil, half-mixed with garden soil. As the layer warms up, a longitudinal line is drawn across the potting soil, dividing its height in two. The young cucumber plants are planted along this line. Two or three are placed per panel; this is done by removing the cups and pushing the seedlings down to the cotyledons. When recovery is assured and the two branches - the result of pruning - begin to elongate, cover the soil with a light mulch and maintain adequate humidity with watering. As the two branches lengthen rapidly, they are directed so that one moves towards the top of the frame, the other towards the bottom. These two branches are not allowed to elongate indefinitely, and are pruned above the fourth or fifth leaf. The result of this operation is the production of as many new branches as leaves have been left. Each of these new productions will bear, in the axil of each leaf, flowers, some of which will be male, others female, and it is not uncommon for there to be several female flowers in the axil of each leaf, giving rise to as many young cucumbers. From then on, pruning consists of pinching off each extension as soon as it has a single leaf. Pruning in this early crop can therefore be summarized as follows: first pruning to two leaves, then pruning to four leaves, and finally repeated pruning to one leaf. As soon as the fruits appear, they are, as we've just said, often grouped in pairs in the leaf axils. Immediately after flowering, the best one is chosen to cut off the second. This leaves three or four, then a few more, so that each plant produces a total of eight or ten fruits throughout its life. Fertilization of the female flowers is not usually a concern, as it takes care of itself; however, in early cultivation, it is sometimes useful to transport the pollen with a brush. When the fruits begin to grow, we help them to develop by placing each one in a lamp glass, which has the advantage of making them grow straight and giving them uniform coloring. The 1 green varieties are the only ones used for this early crop. For very early production, the Rollinson variety is generally used, but the market gardeners of Paris prefer to use it in their bedding crops for a breed they have bred themselves, which is similar to the Green Gherkin Cucumber. Each time the fruit is harvested, which takes place a fortnight or so after the flower that produced it has bloomed, all the leaves that are beginning to turn a little yellow are removed, to make room for the new leaves, which are carefully not crumpled or foxed. The old leaves are set aside and used for packaging. These cucumbers are sold by the dozen: they are wrapped six at a time, first in a few leaves, then in white paper, so that only the ends are visible. At the beginning of the season, these fruits sell for up to 20 and 25 francs a dozen, but the price drops rapidly to just 3 to 6 francs in May. There is a large export trade in the first fruits to the capitals of northern Europe, particularly St. Petersburg. Enemies. - Cucumbers are often attacked by aphids. This can be seen when the plants stop growing and the leaves wrinkle irregularly. This parasite can be eliminated with a few syringes of water in which dried tobacco leaves have been cooked. Cucumbers are also attacked by a parasite to which practitioners give the name "gray" because of its color, and which invades the leaves and stems with such intensity that the plant quickly perishes. No effective remedy is yet known, so in certain regions where this disease occurs abundantly, it is impossible to grow cucumbers in the open air, while those grown in frames are usually spared. Squashes, which for many years have been plunged into deep chaos, have now been neatly and precisely classified by M. Naudin. According to this author's remarkable work, all the diverse forms of squash found in gardens can be related to three main species: Cueurbita maxima Michaux, Cucurbita pepo Linné and Cucurbita moschata Michaux. It follows that all the countless so-called species that have been created over and over again should be considered as nothing more than varieties of the types we have just listed. Uses. - Despite being of different types, all gourds are used for the same purposes, which consist in their use in the preparation of either special dishes or various soups. However, their qualities differ greatly from one variety to another. The fleshy part of the pericarp is very poor in fibrous tissue, so the flesh disintegrates completely when cooked. These fruits contain mucilage, starch and sugar in their tissues, in highly variable proportions; the predominance of one or other of these substances results in different properties and uses. The sweetest of these fruits can be used to make a variety of jams. They are also used to make resins. Some of them, reduced to marmalades and flavored, make excellent jams whose color is reminiscent of that of apricots. Squash fruit is frequently used to adulterate commercial apricot pastes. In general, all gourds are not very delicate plants; they are grown in the same way, whatever their species. It is worth indicating the distinctive characteristics of each of the three species that have provided the varieties for our crops. I. - Cucurbita maxima. This species includes all varieties with very large fruits, and in particular all those commonly known as pumpkins. The vegetative characteristics of this species consist in the shape of the leaves, which are always large, kidney-shaped and with shallow margins; in the numerous hairs that cover all the organs of the plant; these hairs are not very hard and never take on the consistency of prickles. Fruit stalks are smooth and often swollen. The shape of the fruit varies markedly from one race to another, but in general the fruit is raised with more or less pronounced ribs, and more often than not its diameter exceeds its length. According to research carried out, M. de Candolle considers this species to have originated in the warmer parts of the Old World, from where Europeans brought it to America. In support of this opinion, we should mention the fact that the species was discovered in a spontaneous state in Guinea, on the banks of the Niger. Varieties. - There are a considerable number of varieties of this species, differing in color, shape and size. The most widely cultivated are POTIRON GRIS DE BOULOGNE. - A vigorous variety whose very large fruit often reaches almost a meter in size ( Fig. 46. - Bright red pumpkin from Étampes. diameter. Its name comes from the fact that the rind is covered with embroidery, giving it a gray appearance. The flesh is mealy, yellow and very thick. It keeps well. POTIRON ROUGE VIF D'ÉTAMPES. - Fruit with large ribs and smooth, bright orange-red skin. Average size, usually not reaching that of the previous variety (fig. 46). GREEN SPANISH PUMPKIN. - The fruit of this variety is smaller, scarcely more than Om,50 to Om, 60 in diamè̂tre. Its flesh, which keeps for a long time without spoiling, is bright yellow. This variety is highly prized at the market. GIRAUMON SQUASH. - This variety, whose fruit is relatively small, has a very distinctive shape due to Fig. 47 - Giraumon squash. the hypertrphia of the fruit's eye. The flesh is of good quality (fig. 47). II. - Cucurbita Pepo. This species, which seems to have originated in America, is distinguished from the previous one by a number of characteristics derived from the organs of vegetation, which are found in the innumerable varieties found in cultivation. The leaves are lobed, sometimes even fidate, and bear numerous indentations; they are covered with epidermal productions that often become hard and form true prickles found on petioles and stems. DYBOWSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 15 Fruits are usually longer than they are wide, with the exception of a few irregular-fruited varieties in which the diameter exceeds the height of the fruit in length. Fruit rind is generally hard, which increases shelf life. Varieties. - These are very numerous, but only a small number are commonly grown; among the main ones, we should mention: COURGE DES PATAGONS. - A vigorous, hardy variety, producing cylindrical fruit up to Om.50 long by Om.20 in diameter, with five longitudinal ribs, forming rounded, protruding cords on the surface. Very dark green in color. The yellow flesh is of average quality and keeps for a long timeFig. 48. - Pa- temps squash (fig. 48). tagons. TOURAINE PUMPKIN SQUASH. - A variety with very large fruit, generally considered to be a forage crop. ITALIAN LONG SQUASH. - Non-running plant, whose very short stem forms a large tuft of leaves on the spot. Fruits long Om, 60 by Om, 10 in diameter. Flesh not very abundant. This variety is mainly cultivated to harvest the young fruit, the size of a small cucumber, which is eaten stuffed like eggplant. MARROW GOURD. - Cultivated for the same uses as the previous variety, particularly in England. The fruit is similar in shape to the Patagonian squash, but much smaller in size. Two sub-varieties are widely cultivated in England, one with a white rind, the other with green mottling. III. - Cucurbita moschata. Although not absolutely certain, this species is thought to originate in the Far East. The reason for this is that, of the many varieties of squash whose seeds we received from Japan, all those representing races formerly cultivated in the country were related to this species. The stems of this species are usually elongated. The rounded-lobed leaves have white spots in many varieties. The fruits, with very good-quality flesh, have a thin rind and poor keeping qualities. MELON-SHAPED SQUASH. - This variety, whose seeds first came to us from Japan in 1877, has fruits that absolutely resemble a strong, dark-green cantaloupe melon. The flesh is of excellent quality and is ideal for jams and pastes. COURGE PLEINE DE NAPLES. - A vigorous variety with vigorously developing stems. Fruits about 50 cm long, with the distinctive feature that the entire upper part, narrower than the tip, is completely full; the seed-bearing placentas are found only in the swollen part. This is a good variety, which unfortunately has the disadvantage of not always ripening well in the Paris climate, due to its late flowering. CARABACETTE SQUASH. - A new variety of excellent quality. Characterized by very long fruits with a diameter of Om.10 to Om.15. The fruits are full for most of their length. Flesh is orange-red, sweet and suitable for dishes and jams. Cultivation. - Squashes multiply exelusively by means of the seed, and, although these plants easily produce adventitious roots, this property is not used in practice to propagate by marcotting or cutting. Seed germination is always very easy, and we sometimes take advantage of this to sow seeds on the spot, but this is a defective procedure which has the double disadvantage of occupying the soil for too long and producing products which often mature only imperfectly. For these reasons, preference should be given to seedlings sown on a bed or under a frame. Until recently, squash was part of market garden production, but since pumpkins have fallen significantly in price as a result of the expansion of this crop, these plants have been abandoned by market gardeners, and today can only be found on the plains around large cities. Despite having passed through different hands, cultivation has remained as it was in the past. Seedlings must, as we have said, be sown under a frame; for this purpose, a warm layer is built up at the beginning of April, or an old layer that has already been used to grow other vegetables is used. Finger-sow the seeds in the bed, i.e. make a hole with your finger and place a single seed in it. These seeds are pricked out at about Om, 07 in all directions, sowing around 300 seeds per frame. Lightly basked, then covered with a frame, these seeds germinate rapidly. As soon as the young plants, in addition to two cotyledons, have shown a leaf, they should be transplanted. Transplanting must be done by hand, and the squash plant is replanted again under a frame, at a rate of one hundred plants per panel. Care must be taken when planting to press the young plants down to the level of the two cotyledons, which has the advantage of producing adventitious roots in the entire buried part, thus increasing the vigor of the plant. In garden cultivation, seedlings are usually planted in Om-10 diameter pots, which makes them easier to transplant. Sometimes, we even go so far as to sow the seeds directly into the cups and leave them there until they are ready for planting. This is a faulty procedure, as squash tolerate transplanting very well, which has the undeniable advantage of producing stocky, vigorous plants by pressing the seedlings down to the cotyledons, which is not the case with direct sowing. For the reasons given above, direct sowing is not to be recommended. However, if you do wish to sow in the field, it would be useful to arrange the soil in a sort of trough, into which the seeds are sown; this procedure provides a sort of ridging, while leaving a small basin around the plant for watering. With plants prepared under a frame, planting usually takes place in the second half of May. If you plant earlier, you will need to use cloches to protect the plant from spring frosts, to which it is very sensitive. Before planting, the soil must be prepared. This preparation consists in drawing lines on the field to be used for this crop, leaving a distance between them which varies according to the breed being grown, but which in general should not be less than 2 meters and, in the case of pumpkins, should be as much as 3 meters. On the rows thus laid out, small pits of Om, 40 on a side and of roughly equal depth are made every Im, 50. These pits are filled with decomposed manure, leaves or any other material capable of providing the nitrogen fertilizer needed for the rapid development of squash, along with a little heat from fermentation. Once these materials have been deposited in the pits, they are trodden down and watered, then covered with soil from the excavation to form a conical mound in the shape of a vast molehill, ending at the top in a sort of small basin in which to plant a squash plant. When setting up, use flat baskets or trays to transport the plants to the site, whether they have been grown in a full frame and raised into a clod, or with the cups into which they have been transplanted and from which they will be extracted. When planting, care should be taken to spread out the roots of the young plant, which should be buried up to the cotyledons. Immediately after planting, give the seedlings copious watering; it is useful to shelter them for the first few days, by covering them with a bell, an upturned pot or simply a cabbage leaf, which is removed as soon as, after a few days, recovery is judged to have taken place. Young plants are quick to grow; it is useful, though not always done, to cut the stem above the first two leaves, not including the cotyledons. This pruning results in the development of two branches, which can be stopped when they have produced five or six leaves. Flowers appear on the third-generation branches, the first of which are male; later, female flowers are produced, recognizable by the round, inferior ovary beneath the flower. We must be careful not to remove the male flowers, as we have sometimes seen done, on the pretext that they never bear fruit, and not to forget that their presence is essential for fertilization, as the female flowers usually bear no trace of fertile stamens. As soon as the young fruits are about the size of an apple, it's time to decide which ones to leave. In large-fruited varieties, such as Pumpkins, only one fruit is left per plant, and all the others are removed as they appear; this is the only way to obtain a well-developed Pumpkin. In small-fruited varieties, the number of fruits left can vary from three to five. Finally, for varieties where these fruits are eaten young, leave as many as the plant is willing to produce, removing them only when their volume is deemed sufficient. To increase fruit size, growers take care to bury the stems at certain points along their course; in this way, as adventitious roots develop in the buried areas, fruit nutrition is increased accordingly, and there is a noticeable increase in volume. Once you've decided which fruits to keep, you prune the branches carrying them, two or three leaves above their point of attachment. At the same time, cut off the tips of all branches not bearing fruit. Squashes are greedy for water and fertilizer; so, although watering can be dispensed with, their development can be significantly exaggerated by giving them a few sprinklings of water, either simple or with a solution of some kind of fertilizer, while they are growing. We sometimes use piles of manure or compost, which must remain unused during the summer, by planting a few squash vines on them, whose fruits then develop considerably. Fruit should be harvested at the latest as soon as the white frosts begin to set in. Cut the fruit off, removing with it a fragment of the branch that bore it. They should be stored in a shed to protect them from rain, which could cause them to rot, and from frost, which could damage them. The sale takes place in autumn. The Pottrons are then taken to the market, where they are sold by the piece; the price varies according to size. Prices vary according to size, from 1.50 francs for small fruits to 5 francs for large ones. Given that they are planted 3 meters apart in one direction and Im, 50 in the other, and that only one is left per plant for the large-fruited varieties, one hectare can produce around 2, 200 fruits. Seeds should always be harvested from the largest, best-formed fruits that correspond as closely as possible to the type of variety, as these plants are very prone to degeneration as a result of hybridization between varieties grown in close proximity to one another. These seeds retain their germination capacity for five to six years. 2 . 4 Crambe or Sea cabbage (Crambe maritima Linné) is native to temperate western Europe. The plant is characterized by broad, thick leaves with variously cut margins, glaucous on both sides; the thick, fleshy petioles are purplish-brown at their base; this color is evenly distributed on the leaf blades of young leaves. The inflorescence is a compound cluster, bearing white cruciform flowers followed by siliques containing a single seed. Fertilization does not always occur, and the flowers are very often sterile. The seeds retain their germinative powers for only a short time. Uses. - Crambé is widely eaten in England. In France, it has been little cultivated to date, so we are dependent on England, which sends us fairly large quantities every year. We eat the petioles, which have been blanched by the deprivation of light. They are eaten in the same way as asparagus or cardoons, and make a very fine, highly sought-after vegetable. Sowing. - Crambé is difficult to propagate by sowing, due to the short germination period of the seeds. To give it some chance of success, it's best to obtain seed as soon as it ripens, in the course of the summer, and place it in stratification in fine sand. For best results, the best procedure is to sow the stratified seeds on a bed in spring, and then transplant them into the ground. Sowing in the open air has little chance of success, unless done in May, sheltered by a north-facing wall. Flea beetles are fond of young plants, and must be removed by spraying in the morning, before the dew disappears. Multiplication by cuttings. - If you already have Crambé plants, it's a good idea to use a cutting procedure described by Mr. Berthaut in the Bulletin de la Société centrale d'horticulture de France. Here's how it should be done: Towards the end of April, the old plants are pulled out, and all the roots cut into sections of Om, 10 to Om, 12 in length. These sections are divided into two categories, one consisting of all roots at least Om, 008 in diameter, the other of all the smaller rootlets. The small roots of the second batch are nursed at Om, 10 in all directions, to be used only the following year. The larger roots, on the other hand, are used directly. Whether you have at your disposal seedlings raised by sowing, sections of large roots or small roots that have spent a year in the nursery, you will use them indiscriminately, when it's time to put them in place; however, sections of roots are infinitely better than seedlings raised by sowing; the product they will provide will be more abundant than that of plants raised from seed. It is important to know that only roots are suitable for making cuttings; underground stem fragments, which we are often tempted to use, do not give any good results. This is because roots easily produce adventitious buds, whereas stems only produce roots with difficulty. Planting. - To grow Crambé, you need well-smoked, loose soil. It is prepared by deep ploughing; then the soil is divided into 1.20 m planks, leaving paths of Om.50 between each one. On each plank, five rays are traced with the hoe, giving them a depth of about Om.10, and the Crambés are transplanted into the rays at Om.20 intervals. Watering is necessary to help the cuttings take root. Their recovery is assured, and by June, the square is covered with thick greenery. The water from the sprinklers washes away the soil, filling in the furrows and burying the Crambés plants a few centimetres deep. Shrinking. - Things are left in this state until March, when the plants are hoeed, the soil is brought back to the Crambés rows, and each plant is covered with a large upside-down pot. The leaves soon start to grow, forming one to three rosettes on each plant, which are removed with a knife when the petioles have reached a length of about Om, 12 (fig. 49). Forced cultivation. - Instead of thinning at the time of natural growth, it is usually done by forced cultivation, using two different methods: either forcing the Crambés on the spot, or putting them on a bed. In the second of these two methods, we start by building a layer in December or January with horse manure, giving it a thickness of Om, 60, with well-established stoves. The Om, 20 layer is filled with potting soil inside the trunks, and 50 plants per frame are planted. Panels, either glazed or simply planked, are placed over the whole; these panels are covered with a double layer of doormats or simply a thick layer of leaves. Harvesting can be done about three weeks after forcing begins. Forcing on site produces more beautiful products, but the operation takes a little longer. It consists of placing boxes fitted with plank panels over the Crambés crops. Then, digging out the paths, a layer of hot manure is laid on top of Fig. 49 - Crambé maritime. the boards, which is then reworked if the temperature drops below the frames. The panels are covered with mats or manure, and forcing is similar in every way to that used for Asparagus (fig. 13). After a month of forcing, the crop can be harvested. Yield. - Treated as described above, Crambé yields around 2,500 kilos per frame. This English vegetable sells in Paris for around 4 francs a kilogram in December and January, and for 1.50 in March. Although these prices will undoubtedly fall rapidly, Crambé cultivation will nonetheless remain worthwhile, either for sale or for direct consumption. There's a long way to go from the methods used in the past to the one recommended by Mr. Berthaut; we've tried it out and obtained the best results. In general, therefore, we should abandon the seedling system, which is not very advantageous, in that it is very uncertain, especially as it is easy to obtain as many root cuttings as you want, since all the plants that have been forced once are now cut after heating in April, to make new cuttings, which in turn will produce the following year. This system therefore requires only one year, whereas previous planting methods required two or three. Watercress (Nasturtium officinale, R. Bronn) grows wild in all streams and ponds in temperate Europe and Asia. For a long time, it was simply harvested where it grew spontaneously, but the ever-increasing need for it soon led to its regulated cultivation, which is now as widespread as it is lucrative. Cress (fig. 50) is characterized by succulent, fleshy twigs bearing alternate pinnate leaves with rounded segments, the terminal one always larger than those on the sides. These branches terminate in a bractless, clustered inflorescence. The white, cruciform flowers are small and give rise to long fruits (siliques) containing small, flattened, orange-yellow seeds. Uses. - In addition to the medicinal uses of watercress, due to the high proportion of iodine (1) in its tissue, watercress makes very tasty and widely used salads and seasonings. Its reputation as a powerful depurative has earned it the nickname Santé du corps (1) Chatin, le Cresson. Varieties. - Strictly speaking, these don't exist, and it's more or less the wild watercress that's grown on every farm. However, a few skilled growers have, through well-managed selection, formed breeds for cultivation that are more advantageous than the standard plant, in that the leaves become wider and form more compact rosettes with -c-f..-...-.AA... Fig. 50. - Watercress. the tips of the branches, give a higher yield. At the same time, the dark green to reddish-brown color of its leaves and stems gives it a vigorous appearance that makes it highly sought-after. On the other hand, there are types degenerated by poor cultivation, in which the leaves become narrow and frizzy, giving low yields. Cultivation. - As we have said, watercress grows spontaneously on the banks of small streams of running water, and sometimes also on the edges of ponds. But there is a considerable difference in the products obtained in either case, and watercress from flowing water is infinitely preferable to that which grows in stagnant water, for the reason that the latter usually takes on a muddy taste which robs it of much of its quality. Choice of water. - When growing cress, it's important to choose the best conditions. A watercress bed can therefore only be established if a certain quality of running water is available. But it doesn't matter what kind of water you use. Water from a small river or open stream, while sufficient for watercress cultivation, would be totally unsuitable for industrial production. As we shall see, for a watercress farm to become truly lucrative, it is first and foremost necessary to be able to harvest watercress during the winter. Water from a stream would have the disadvantage of being at an inconsistent temperature and subject to variations in ambient air temperature. For these reasons, the only water that can be used is that supplied by springs and reaching the watercress bed in a covered canal. It is also necessary for this water to come from deep strata, which, by regulating its temperature and making it constant, will have the added advantage of providing a flow rate that will not be influenced by external conditions, so that it will remain uniform during the hiyer as well as during the summer. DVBONSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 16 The composition of the water is also important. Water loaded with gypsum or carbonate of lime has the disadvantage of forming deposits on the leaves, making them dirty and reducing their value. Water from peaty soils, which is laden with organic matter, gives watercress a very unpleasant muddy odor. The best waters, therefore, are those that can be used for human consumption, in other words, those that are drinkable. Watercress pits. - When you have access to water that meets the conditions we have just described, you can think about setting up watercress pits, but you mustn't lose sight of the fact that under different conditions, it would be risky to engage in this type of production, at least for commercial purposes. In order to regulate the water supply and the quantity of water to be supplied to the plants, pits are dug in the ground of varying dimensions, depending on the importance of the crop and the quantity of water available. In industrial cultivation, the most common dimensions are 60 to 80 meters in length. It would be a disadvantage to exceed this length, as the end of the pit receiving water that has been in contact with the air for a long time would behave badly, for the reasons we have already set out. It is important not to give these pits too great a width, as we must not lose sight of the fact that the water supplied by the inlet canals will lose all the more of its speed the greater the surface area, so that by exaggerating this width the current could become almost nil, or in any case insufluously sensitive; the water would then become croupy, and the Cress would take on a bad taste. The most suitable width is around three meters. However, this width will vary according to the strength of the water flowing into the pit. A water flow of 25 liters per minute is required for a width of one metre, i.e. 75 liters for the same time and a pit three metres wide. This is the only way to obtain sufficient current. A single pit is not sufficient for continuous watering. You need to set up several so that the produce from one alternates with that from the other. There are watercress cultivations in the départements around Paris, with no less than a hundred pits, and sometimes more. It doesn't matter how these pits are arranged in the field. Understandably, it would be unwise to use the water from one pit to feed a second, as the water would then be in temperature equilibrium with the outside air, and would freeze during the winter. Pits should be placed parallel to each other, with a 1-meter-wide bank between them. In the past, the banks or embankments were given over to vegetable cultivation, and for this purpose they were given a greater width, but this practice is more or less abandoned nowadays. It follows from the layout of the pits that the water must flow in the direction of their length, then divide into a transverse channel with as many arms as there are pits to be served (fig. 51). Each arm is controlled by a valve that regulates the water flow to each pit. It doesn't matter if the pits are set up in any type of soil. Siliceous soils would have the disadvantage of producing insufficiently watertight pits8 N 3 -3 4 2 21 4 F. 8 18 E 4 al 2 ~~~ ~ a CS 6 6~ 3. R * 4 2 E Mna Fig. 51 - Plan of a three-pit cressonnière. ches. Clay or clay-limestone soils are best suited to their construction. The depth of the pits is not in itself very important, but they must be at least Om, 40 in this sense, although it is understandable that this height should be increased at one end of the pit if it is built on sloping ground. In fact, the bottom of the pit should only have a very slight slope equal to about 2 millimeters per meter, i.e. Om, 16 for a pit 80 meters long. The bottom of the ditch can be ploughed and smoked if it is of very poor quality, but more often than not it is simply flooded a few days before planting. As the bottom is softened by the water, it is left to drain before planting. Multiplying watercress. - Cress produces seeds that germinate easily, but this method of propagation is rarely used. The reason for this is that we grow plants whose leaves are as broad as possible; by using the seed we would often be exposing ourselves to degeneration, or at the very least we wouldn't obtain as uniform a product as by planting, which is done using twigs that, as soon as they come into contact with the soil, rapidly emit adventitious roots. If, however, it is necessary to sow seeds, as may be the case when establishing new watercress beds, the water should be removed from the pits by closing the sluice valves in spring or summer, and the seeds sown on the mud. Once the seedlings have grown well, the water is gradually released. Planting, which, as we've just said, is the most common method of propagation, is usually done in August. To do this, we take branches of watercress from a pit in full development, simply by pulling on them. The planting starts at the head of the pit, i.e. on the side from which the water comes, and usually the watercress is deposited in small clumps on the mud of the pit. Keep Om, 10 between the feet on and between the rows, and lay the watercress in such a way that the leafy end of its branches faces the head of the pit. It would be advantageous not just to throw the watercress on the ground in this way, but to transplant it, so that it picks up better and the harvest is a few days earlier. Four to five days after planting, the watercress has taken possession of the soil and the ends of its branches have straightened out. At this point, let enough water run off to cover the soil with 3 to 4 centimetres of water. It's now time to give the watercress its first fertilizer application. This consists of half-decomposed stable manure, which is thrown in handfuls over the watercress to cover it partially. To prevent the manure from being carried away by the water, it is applied to the soil with a light bat consisting of a board attached to a long handle. A few days after applying the manure, the watercress reappears vigorously above the layer of fertilizer. From then on, the pit is given all the water it needs, and a few days later the harvest can begin. Replanting should be done every year; after the pit has been cleaned and the silt removed, replant as described above. Harvesting watercress. - As we have just seen, harvesting begins soon after planting and can last all year, provided the necessary precautions are taken. In regions too far from Paris, watercress is only harvested during the winter, when the produce reaches its maximum value. In the Oise region, for example, harvesting only takes place from November to May, as the summer produce is too low in value to bear the cost of transport. For a watercress farm to produce abundant and remunerative products, it is therefore very important, as we have said, that the water does not get cold in winter, and that it does not freeze in the pits. It is also essential, in order to obtain good harvests, to smoke after each cut, following the procedure indicated above. To make the cut, a board is placed across the pits, on which the cutter stands. He kneels down and bends forward; with his left hand he grasps the watercress, lifts it up and cuts it with a serpette held in his right hand. As soon as enough cress has been harvested to make a handful, he binds it with wicker, a small bundle of which he wears on his belt. The lower end of the bundle is smoothed with a serpette, then the handle is thrown into the water on the shady side. When the worker is skilled, he cuts a hundred handles an hour. Each one weighs around 300 grams. In the evening, the bunches are packed into large conical baskets, in which they are placed so that the leaves are in the center, with the other end resting against the wall of the basket. As a result, a central cavity remains unoccupied in the pamer. The bundles are consolidated by covering them with a little straw and tying them with string. Each basket contains around 18 to 20 dozen bundles. The whole lot is then shipped by high-speed train or transported by car to the market. Winter prices generally range from 1 franc to 1.50 francs per dozen. In summer, the price is reduced by three-quarters. The yield from a watercress farm varies greatly according to the situation, the quantity of water used and the care taken. In any case, growers admit that the benefits are often considerable. Although watercress does not produce products similar to those we harvest in France, it does provide good harvests in our equatorial colonies, where we recommend its cultivation. Cress is attacked by a number of insects, notably the flea beetle (Altica sisymbrii), which can be removed by temporarily submerging the pits. It is also important to prevent the pits from being invaded by aquatic plants, which would hinder the watercress's development. CESSON ALÉNOIS Cress alénois (Lepidium sativum, Linné) is a small cruciferous plant that seems to have originated in Persia. This annual plant produces a rosette of deeply dissected leaves, from the center of which a branched stem rises after a few months of vegetation, giving rise to clusters of small, white, cruciform flowers followed by fruits (siliques) containing a small number of oblong, orange seeds. They remain germinative for five years. Uses. - The leaves are used as a condiment or to make salads. Varieties. - In addition to the primitive type, which is by far the most widespread, we also grow varieties with broad leaves and curly leaves. Cultivation. - To prevent the plant from going to seed too quickly, it's a good idea to sow in the shade during the summer, so as to obtain a longer harvest. Cresson alénois can be useful for large-scale cultivation. In a short space of time, it produces seeds that can be sold commercially. In this circumstance, sowing is broadcast in April at a rate of 5 kilograms per hectare. For watercress to grow well, the soil must be fine, i.e. made uniform by harrowing and rolling. This crop requires no care until harvest time. This takes place in August and September. The watercress is mown, then left to dry in javelins. Finally, it is flailed and winnowed. The yield is four to five hundred francs per hectare. Crosnes see Épiaire. . ... .. 1 4 - SHALLOT Shallot, which Linnaeus named botanically Allium ascalonicum, based on Pliny's belief that the plant originated in Ascalon in Judea, is not considered a species by the most authoritative modern botanists. On the contrary, they tend (1) to regard it as a simple variety of the common onion. Indeed, while the characteristics derived from taste are different, all those derived from the vegetative organs and the flower are unmistakably similar. What's more, although the regions where it has been claimed to grow spontaneously have been explored, it has not been found in the wild. Finally, it very rarely produces seeds, which again indicates a kind of degeneration due to long cultivation and a profound change in its primitive state. The plant resembles the onion in its fistulous leaves and swollen, hollow stem; it differs in that its bulb branches early, forming a variable number of cloves that spread apart. - The bulbs are used as a condiment, either cooked or preserved in vinegar. (1) De Candolle, Orig, des plantes cultivées. Varieties. - There are two slightly different types, one the ordinary shallot (fig. 52), the other with more rounded, less branched bulbs, known as the Jersey shallot; plus a few local sub-varieties. Cultivation. - Shallots are grown in multiple stages, using bulbs that are planted in the same way as garlic. The soil must be deeply tilled. Avoid using freshly smoked soil, which could cause all or part of the plants to rot. Lines are drawn Om, 20 apart, along which the cloves are transplanted, keeping the same distance between plants. This planting is done in February and March, but for the Jersey Shallot it is preferable to do it in autumn, as this plant does not fear frosts. It takes early possession of the soil and provides bulbs which, reaching full maturity very early, keep much better than if planted in spring. Harvesting takes place when the leaves begin to turn yellow. Leaving bulbs too long in the ground can cause them to rot. This serious disadvantage, which frequently occurs in shallot plantations, can be remedied by removing any plants that seem to be affected by this rot. In Brittany, where this crop is grown on a large scale, the leaves are braided after they have dried, to form a sort of string of bulbs that young Bretons sell in the streets of Paris, along with garlic and bay leaves. The insects that attack shallots are the same as those that cause damage to leek and garlic crops. This newly-introduced vegetable is now widespread everywhere, and deserves to be. Rosary is native to Japan, where it has long been cultivated as a food plant. It is also used in China. It is a perennial plant with numerous underground, tuberous rhizomes formed by a succession of nodules resembling the swollen root collars of rosary oats. The simple or stalked stem, usually erect, bears opposite, rough, cordate-based leaves that are toothed and accuminate. The sessile flowers form false whorls or contracted cymes in the leaf axils. The flower, which is that of a labiate, bears a purplish corolla. Uses. - The swollen roots, no bigger than a finger and 5 to 6 centimeters long, are eaten cooked in water, then prepared in the same way as salsify, but with more finesse and a less firm consistency. They can also be made into pickles or used in vegetable salads. This new vegetable has been introduced and spread in France by the devoted care of a good man, Mr. Paillieux, who for many years has devoted his time to researching new vegetables, which he then spreads among the public. It is remarkable how quickly this vegetable spread, for it was only in 1882 that Mr. Paillieux received fresh rhizomes of the plant for the first time, enabling him to start cultivating it. No effort was spared to make it known, and today it is an almost popular vegetable. It would have been normal to give it the name Rosary, which is its real name. Instead, it was given the name Crosnes, the name of the village inhabited by Mr. Paillieux. Let's accept it as a tribute to the researcher's dedication. The following information is based on his observations, which we summarize below. Cultivation. - Epiaire grows in all types of soil, but compact clay soils make uprooting difficult if not impossible. It is therefore preferable to choose sandy or very loose soils. Plant in February or March in holes (mm 15 to Om, 20 deep) spaced Om, 40 apart in all directions, with three or four tubers per hole. The only cultivation care required is to keep the soil surface clean and loose by hoeing. Harvesting begins around December 1st in the Paris climate. Before this time, the tubers have not finished growing. As they don't start growing again until March, they can be left in the ground until then, and only pulled as and when they are needed for consumption, as frosts are not to be feared. Avoid leaving tubers exposed to the air for several days, as they will wilt and lose their value. Yields can be around 12,000 kilograms per hectare, but this is mainly a small-crop plant. One kilogram contains around 600 tubers. Its only enemy is the white grub.
