In cultivations with a large number of varieties, there are two types of Spinach, which some authors have claimed to be two species, based on the fact that one bears fruit with spines formed by the enlarged calyx, while the other has smooth fruit. This separation does not seem justified, and everything leads us to believe that round-fruited spinach is a product of cultivation (1) where, for a long time, only the spiny varieties were known. The cultivated species has not been found in a spontaneous state; however, following the indications given by ancient authors, there is every reason to believe that the species originated in Persia. Without making any distinction between the various cultivated types, they should be linked to the species designated by Linné as Spinacia oleracea. Spinach has long-stalked leaves with sagittate blades often over 2 decimeters long. The plant is grown as an annual or biennial. When Spinach flowers, it's easy to see that some plants have male flowers, while others have (1) De Candolle, Orig, des plantes cultivées. female ones. Spinach is therefore dioecious. The flowers, which are arranged in spikes of sessile cymes, are followed by the fruits (achenes), covered by the enlarged pieces of the calyx; these end, as we have said, in a point, in the varieties that are close to the type, while all those that are highly perfected by cultivation have smooth fruits. These seeds retain their germinative capacity for five years. Uses. - The cooked leaves of this widely used vegetable are eaten, with the undeniable advantage of providing green food even in the depths of winter. Varieties. - In addition to ordinary spinach, which is fairly close to the supposed primitive type of this plant, and whose cultivation is no longer practiced today, there are several varieties whose distinction is based on the size of the leaves, and the earliness or lateness of the plants of which they are composed. ANGLETERRE SPINACH. - This variety is widely grown by market gardeners, as it doesn't flower until very late in the spring. Its leaves are broad and erect. The seed is thorny. SPINACH SLOW TO BOLT. - Although it has a round seed, this variety is similar to the previous one in that it doesn't flower until very late in the spring. The ample leaves are sagittate, but blistered instead of noticeably smooth. HOLLAND SPINACH. - This is a good, cold-resistant variety, which makes it particularly sought-after for winter cultivation, as opposed to its predecessor, which is mainly reserved for spring. DYBONSRI. - Vegetable crop. 17 A fairly widespread sub-variety is the Spinach of Viroflay (fig. 53), whose leaves are very ample and provide an abundant harvest. Cultivation. - Spinach is grown at two times of the year, corresponding to two slightly different forms of production. On the one hand, there are autumn seedlings, which produce in autumn, winter and early spring; on the other hand, there are spring seedlings, which produce in spring and summer. Fig. 53 - Viroflay spinach. Autumn sowing begins on August 15th, and is repeated every fortnight until mid-October. The first sowings produce a first harvest towards the end of September. Those sown in September can be harvested during the winter; and finally, those sown in October do not produce until after the heavy rains. Spinach likes a fertile soil, which it is useful to fertilize especially, and which must be, above all, healthy and free from too much humidity during the winter, which would lead to rotting at the base and destruction of the seedling. The soil on which the seedlings are to be sown should be ploughed, then trodden and raked. Spinach is usually broadcast, spreading about 250 grams of seed per acre. The seed is then harrowed with a rake, after which a light layer of loam or straw is spread. Spinach emerges after about ten days, bearing two elongated cotyledonar leaves. For spinach sown in August, it's a good idea to harvest at the end of September or the beginning of October, otherwise the leaves will develop too much and risk spoiling in winter. Harvesting is done by cutting the leaves one by one with the fingernail; avoid harvesting by cutting the leaves with a knife, which would compromise subsequent harvests. This first harvest is used as an opportunity for thinning, if necessary, to leave an interval of at least Om, 10 to Om, 15 between plants. After this initial harvest, harvesting continues throughout the winter, until around May. In order to be able to harvest during very cold spells, it is advisable to place straw mats on top of the spinach beds, which are removed as soon as the frost has dropped to two or three degrees. These mats can be replaced by straw and long bedding. When May comes, spinach tends to come up, and a final harvest is made by cutting off the whole plant. To succeed this winter crop, which is by far the most advisable, spinach is sown in spring, from February to May. Spinach is sown either broadcast or in rows, but as it sets seed quickly at this time of year, it should be sown more tightly to obtain a higher yield, which is all the more justified as the plants only occupy the soil for a relatively short time. At this time of year, we sow at a rate of 400 grams per are, and most of the time only two harvests are made, sometimes even just one, when the plants tend to develop their stems and flower. It is essential to water this spring crop abundantly, if you want to obtain high quality products. Large-scale cultivation. - Spinach is grown in open fields in the suburbs of Paris, particularly around the towns of Versailles and Saint-Germain, exclusively in autumn. Spinach is broadcast in August, in the proportions we have indicated. Don't forget that this plant is greedy for fertilizer, and that it's useful to give the soil a fertilizer that can be buried by ploughing before sowing. One harrow stroke after sowing is enough to bury the seed. A similar crop yields three harvests: one in autumn, a second in December or January, and a third by cutting off the entire plant around April or May. The first and last harvests produce an abundant crop, with each cut yielding around 100 kilograms of leaves. For the winter harvest, the weight of the leaves is about half that. These leaves are sold in large quantities to commission agents, who then cook them and sell them in this state to fruit growers. The price of spinach leaves varies greatly according to the season. It is 15 to 20 francs for 100 kilograms of leaves in autumn, rising to 30 or even 40 francs in winter, before falling back to 10 francs in spring. This gives a total average price of 20 francs per 100 kilograms, and for the three harvests, a net product of 50 francs per acre. When cooked, Spinach diminishes significantly in weight, so that one kilogram of green leaves yields only 670 grams of cooked Spinach. The price of cooked spinach averages 40 centimes per kilogramme, which means that growers who are far from the centers of consumption would be well advised to cook and send them in this form. This would have the double advantage of reducing the volume and weight of shipments, while increasing their value. For the same quantity of sheets, T'are would obtain a sum of 66 francs, which would only be burdened by the low cost of baking. Seed carriers. - When it comes to harvesting seeds, spinach should be treated as a biennial plant, i.e. only those sown in August or September that have survived the winter should be harvested. Market gardeners harvest the leaves of the plants that will supply them with seed in the spring. It's best not to do this, but to leave the seed-bearing plants untouched, simply thinning them out and removing plants whose leaves don't correspond to the type you have in mind. Spinach flowers in June. As soon as flowering is over, we pull out all the male plants, which are no longer needed, and wait for the moment of maturity, which occurs at the end of July, to cut the seed-bearing plants. Let them finish

They are then threshed. The seeds are bagged and can be used for sowing for up to five years. The origins of broad bean cultivation (Vicia Faba, Linné) are lost in the mists of time, dating back to prehistoric times. The plant has never been found in a spontaneous state, and it is highly probable that it would have disappeared a long time ago due to the size of its seeds, which prevents any natural dissemination and makes it a target for animals of all kinds, if cultivation had not taken hold of it at a very early date and enabled it to reach us. The plant's vegetative organs are characterized by a robust stem with a few branches at the base. These stems are raised by four protruding angles, giving them a square appearance. The leaves are compound, pinnate and not odd. Papilionaceous flowers are white with a black spot in the center of the petals. They are borne in pauciflorous clusters in the leaf axils. The fruits are pods, sometimes erect, sometimes curving towards the ground. They contain a variable number of seeds, rarely more than eight. The seeds are large, flattened, kidney-shaped and have an easily detachable aril. They retain their germinative capacity for six years. Uses. - Broad beans are eaten as grains, either fresh or dried. The latter have the disadvantage of being covered with very thick husks that must be removed, as they cannot be eaten. Varieties. - Although it has been cultivated for a very long time, the broad bean has generally not varied much, and the number of different types of cultivation is relatively small. Here are two of the most commonly grown varieties. MARSH BEAN. - Plant about Om 80 high, pods in twos or threes, containing two to four very large seeds. WINDSOR BEAN. - Plant often taller than the previous one. Numerous pods, often solitary, containing one to three well-developed seeds. Grown in the Paris climate. - Fèves are hardy plants, but fear heavy frosts. In our climate, they are sown as soon as the temperature eases after the winter. Sowing takes place in February or March at the latest. They can be sown in any soil, but rich, moderately compact soils are best suited to broad beans. Seeds are planted in rows Om, 30 apart, spaced Om, 15 apart along the rows, and buried Om, 05 apart. To hasten plant development, it's a good idea to water when the weather turns dry. Fava beans are fast-growing, so they are fully developed by May. As soon as flowering begins, we pinch off the ends of the stems above about the tenth inflorescence. This has the dual effect of hastening the development of the preserved pods, and preventing the black aphid that attacks the herbaceous parts of the plant from wreaking havoc. Harvesting takes place either when the beans are two-thirds developed, or when dry. Cultivation in the Midi. - In the Midi region, seedlings should be sown in autumn, starting in September for the orange region and in October for the olive region. Sowing is carried out exactly as described above. These autumn sowings produce early-flowering Fava beans, whose pods can be harvested in March, April and May. Under these conditions, Fèves are capable of producing an advantageous product, as the pods sell in the spring for up to 30 francs per 100 kilogr. at the Paris halles. They belong to several botanical species, some indigenous, others, on the contrary, native to the temperate regions of South America. Sowing and selection on the one hand, and hybridization on the other, have produced a very considerable number of varieties and hybrids, which are widespread in gardens today. In practice, all these different types form two categories corresponding to different cultivation methods and requirements; on the one hand, they form the group of small-fruited strawberries or strawberries of the four satsons; on the other, large-fruited strawberries. All strawberry plants are perennials, their rhizomes producing two types of rameanx, some sterile and of indefinite elongation, bearing scale-like leaves from whose axils buds produce normal leaves, which are trifoliolate and stipular. These twigs root at the level of the buds they produce, and are known as runners or stolons (lig. 54). These rooted stolons can be used to propagate strawberry plants. The plant also produces other self-determining branches. The flowers are arranged in uniparous cymes, bearing a calicule, a calyx and a corolla, which is white and composed of five parts. After bearing numerous stamens, the flower's receptacle elongates into a kind of elevation, bearing an indefinite number of carpels that transform into small, indehiscent dried fruits (achenes). The edible part of the Strawberry is therefore not the fruit, as is often claimed, but the hypertrophied receptacle, whose tissue is gorged with adibles; it is nevertheless true that, in practice, U Fig. 54. - Stolon or strawberry runner. This is the part of the fruit that has retained its name, and we will refer to it as such. The fruit is surrounded by the calyx and calicule, which are persistent and form an induvie. Fruits vary infinitely in size, shape and color, but all contain sweet, mucilaginous substances mixed with essences that make them a pleasant-tasting food. The fact that they ripen in spring, when few other fruits are available, also contributes to their popularity. Finally, Strawberries have the advantage of being easy to force-feed. Uses. - Strawberries are eaten either raw or cooked. In the latter case, they are used to make highly prized jams and syrups. PETITES FRAISES. Small Strawberries seem to recognize a single botanical species as their ancestor: Linnaeus' Fragaria vesca, which grows wild in all the woods of the northern hemisphere. In practical terms, it differs 2 29 from all other strawberry plants in that both the type and the cultivated varieties are generally elongated, with white flesh and protruding seeds. All cultivated varieties have the advantage of sustained flowering throughout the fine season, when given the necessary care, which is why Fig. 55. -Fraisiers des quatre saisons (fig. 55). Varieties. - Varieties are generally less important than for large-fruited strawberries, for the reason that, as we shall see, the most common method of propagation is by sowing, and characteristics do not always reproduce with great fixity. However, there are certain types that we should aim for, because they correspond either to genuine qualities or to commercial requirements. At present, the most sought-after varieties are those whose fruits are bright red and whose elongated shape gives them the appearance of a small gherkin. IMPROVED DURU STRAWBERRY. - The fruit is long and bright red. This is one of the most widely grown varieties for the Paris market. It is very similar to an older variety with dark red fruit called Fratse Janus. STRAWBERRY WITHOUT NET. - This is an old variety whose cultivation has been rather neglected, as it does not produce sufficiently beautiful fruit. It has the particularity of not needing a net, which makes it ideal for edging vegetable gardens. Two different forms are grown, one with red fruit and the other with white. Sowing. - Sowing is a method of propagation that gives very good results, provided special care is taken and, above all, great care is taken in selecting the fruit to be sown. Four Seasons Strawberries are sown in March and April. They should be sown on beds with an average temperature of 15 to 18 degrees. The soil placed on the bed consists of a mixture of equal parts of potting soil and sandy soil or heather. It is placed in the trunk so that it comes to within 6 to 7 centimetres of the frame glass. After levelling the soil with a rake, it is lightly beaten with a planchette to make the surface absolutely even. The seeds are then scattered to form a tight seedbed, and covered with a thin layer of sandy soil. Water lightly with a fine-headed watering can, then replace the frame and cover with a mat until the seedling appears. Emergence takes place after about ten days, when the young plants appear with two very small cotyledonary leaves. From this point onwards, air should be supplied during the day, taking care to lift the frame on the side facing away from the wind to avoid drying out, and humidity maintained by frequent watering. In this way, you obtain healthy seedlings, and take advantage of a foggy day to remove the frames and leave them exposed to the direct action of the air. Seedlings can also be sown in the open air during May and June. In this case, it's best to choose a sunny location and keep the soil moist with constant watering. Seedlings sown in the open air are less attractive than those sown in beds or trays. As soon as the plants have four or five leaves, they can be transplanted. After watering the seedling, remove all the best seedlings, and after preparing the soil by ploughing and raking, draw lines Om, 15 apart, along which the strawberry plants will be transplanted. Two plants are often placed together, to produce larger clumps. When transplanting, which is done with a large planting tool, care must be taken not to bend the roots, but to press them in perpendicularly. Water immediately after transplanting, and repeat the watering frequently. If you want to obtain beautiful plants, it's a good idea to replant in August, which will multiply the plant's hair and make it more vigorous. Replanting is carried out using a trowel, removing the rootballs and keeping a distance of about Om.25 between the rows. If these plants produce either sterile or fruit-bearing shoots, they are removed and vigorous plants are obtained which can be used for planting. Another procedure consists, when the plants are strong enough to undergo the first transplanting, in planting them in rows Om, 50 apart, and covering the soil with a good layer of straw. Leave any nets that the plants are willing to produce. As soon as they are sufficiently rooted, they are removed and transplanted into beds in a nursery, to be used as seedlings the following year. This is the best method for obtaining strawberry plants that produce good quality fruit; however, it is the least followed, because of the time and labor required to do it properly. In either case, planting can be done early in the autumn, or in March. Strawberry plants planted in autumn will produce a full crop the following spring, and a smaller additional crop in autumn; they should be left to occupy the soil for three years, i.e. they should be uprooted when they have produced their third spring crop. On the other hand, Traisiers planted in spring will produce a full crop in autumn. They will remain in the ground for three years, which means that they will only be uprooted at the onset of winter in the third year of planting. This procedure will produce a steady crop, provided that new plantings are made every year at the times indicated. The soil chosen for planting should be fresh and substantial, if possible. Strawberry plants Fig. 56 - Basket used to pack small strawberries. also do well in sandy soil, provided they do not lack moisture, though this should not be too great. The plants are planted in four rows spaced Om, 35 apart, staggered Om, 45 apart. Once planted, the soil is covered with a layer of straw and watered. Frequent watering will be necessary throughout the fruiting period, and it will be useful to remove all netting as it occurs. Harvesting. - Harvesting begins around June 15th and continues until the end of September, provided that we take care to baste morning and evening. Production slows noticeably in July and early August, especially when watering is lacking. Harvesting takes place in the morning or evening; only the reddest strawberries are picked, and the stalks are removed by cutting them with a fingernail. The 4 Fig. 57. - Packing strawberries in Provence. Harvested strawberries are placed in pots or small baskets lined with vine leaves or strawberry plants. For sale, they are packed in lidded baskets (Fig. 56), containing one kilog. of fruit, as is done around Paris; these baskets are lined with a few leaves. In Provence, small stoneware pots (fig. 57) are used, in which the small strawberries are placed, then placed on top and held in place with a paper cone. DYBOWSKI. - Vegetable cultivation. 18 Harvesting the seed. - It's important to choose carefully the fruit that will provide the seed. Only well-colored fruit from fertile plants should be taken, from which all netting and poorly-formed fruit have been removed. Fruits from late August or early September are taken as seed, because they are the most ripe. When they are fully ripe and have taken on a beautiful vermilion-red color, we crush them in water, then pass the pulp through a fine cloth to separate the seed, which we dry in the shade and then bag. These seeds can only be used for two or three years, after they have lost their ability to germinate. LARGE-FRUITED STRAWBERRIES Most large-fruited strawberry plants are the result of hybridization between various botanical species. These include the Virginia strawberry (Fragarta rtrginiana, Ehrahrt), native to Canada, and the Chilean strawberry (Fragaria Chiloensis, Duchesne), which grows wild in southern Chile. To these should be added the Capron strawberry (Fragarta elattor, Ehrarht), of which only one or two varieties are grown, and these are not very widespread because of their distinctive, slightly musky taste. Varieties. - The number of varieties or hybrids cultivated today is extremely considerable, and many of these are of little value. The most widely cultivated varieties are STRAWBERRY MARGUERITE LEBRETON. - Robust and relatively fertile variety, with large, conical fruit, 3 Fig. 58. - Strawberry Marguerite Lebreton. Fig. 59. - Héricart de Thury strawberry. It is widely used in forced cultivation, to which it lends itself remarkably well because of its earliness (fig. 58). STRAWBERRY VICOMTESSE HÉRICART DE THURY. - Plant widely cultivated around Paris. Medium-sized, unevenly rounded fruit with juicy, fragrant flesh. An early variety suitable for forced cultivation (fig. 59). FRAISE Dr MORÈRE. - A vigorous variety with very large fruit and pink, sweet flesh; highly sought-after in the trade. Later than the two preceding varieties, it succeeds them in the order of maturity (fig. 60). JUCUNDA STRAWBERRY. - Late variety with large, dark-red fruit. Still a widely cultivated plant. Grown in the open air. - Seedlings do not faithfully reproduce the various large-fruited strawberry varieties. Fig. 60. - Strawberry Dr Morère. fruit, so this method of propagation is only used in the search for new varieties. When, on the other hand, the aim is to reproduce existing varieties, we use either nets or splinters that can be detached from the mother plants. If you want to propagate a variety of which you have only a limited number, you need to take special care of the plants that will provide the nets. Having obtained a few seedlings of the variety to be propagated during the summer, they are planted at a good exposure in the nursery, and when the cold comes, they are covered either with cloches or frames. Around March, the clodded plants are lifted and transplanted to a ploughed and smoked bed, spaced at Om, 40 apart in all directions. Mulch the soil, and if inflorescences appear, cut them out. It doesn't take long for the vines to produce numerous, vigorous threads, which are left to hang out on the ground. In July, we remove all the rooted nets, rid them of any yellow leaves they may bear, and plant them in a nursery at Om, 15 in each direction. Mulching and watering are necessary to obtain vigorous plants. Often, to obtain well-formed clumps, they are planted in- Fig. 61. - Strawberry Jucunda. seems to be two plants of equal strength; this procedure is always followed when a very large quantity of nets is available. If the aim is to propagate a common variety of which there are already many plants, there is no need to plant them in special beds beforehand; simply detach the most vigorous nets in the growing beds. In all cases, care must be taken to ensure that nursery plants do not produce new nets, which would be carefully removed if they did. Around mid-September, the strawberry plants should be planted. To do this, we need to prepare a plot of land that has not been used for strawberry planting for several years, as experience has shown that new plantings do not do well under these conditions. Strawberry rows should be spaced at Om, 35 apart, with plants spaced at Om, 50 to Om, 60 apart, depending on the vigor of the variety. Early-bearing varieties should be planted with a southern exposure, while late-bearing varieties should be planted with a northern exposure, so as to extend harvesting time as much as possible. Strawberries should be transplanted in rootballs and watered immediately after planting. The following spring, hoe the plants, taking care not to bury them, and cover the soil with mulch. Flowering takes place in May, and the fruits of the early varieties begin to ripen at the end of May. With the late varieties, harvesting lasts until July. Harvesting is carried out with the same equipment as for the Four Seasons Strawberry. According to M. Baltet, one hectare of strawberries in Brittany yields an average of 15,750 kilograms of fruit, at a price of O fr. 20 per kilogram, or 3,150 francs. Forced cultivation. - The most popular varieties for this type of cultivation are Marguerite Lebreton, Héricart de Thury and Dr. Morère; Fraisiers des quatre saisons are also used, but less frequently. Strawberry plants can be forced, either on the spot, or by transporting them on a bed; both methods give good results, but the second is the one most generally used. To do this, we use plants that we have nursed in July, and in September we put them in pots of Om, 16 to Om, 20 in diameter. In each of these pots, we place a clump made up, as we said, of two plants planted at the same time in the nursery. Potting should be done in pre-prepared soil. It should be made up of one-third free-range soil, or soil from lawns previously piled up and suitably rotted; to this should be added one-third potting soil which has not yet been used, and an equal proportion of sandy heather soil. After repotting, we bury the pots in the soil, water them and, when October comes, cover them with boxes and frames to which we give air until it freezes outside. At night, they are covered with straw mats. In January, or later if you like, build up a layer of new manure, half of which is mixed with annealed manure or leaves. Refill the layer with sandy soil or light potting compost, in which to bury the pots, at a rate of twenty-four per frame, as soon as the layer reaches a temperature of 12°. It's important not to overheat and not to exceed the temperature indicated above, so a thermometer should be placed in the bed. In February and March, when the strawberry plants are in full bloom, the temperature can be allowed to rise to around 16°. Water when the soil dries out a little, but avoid giving too much water, especially during flowering, as this could cause the flowers to rot or leak. Before flowering, the soil is covered with mulch to prevent the fruit from getting dirty; often, inflorescences are even supported with small wooden forks. It's very important in this crop not to set the temperature too high, and to reduce watering to what's strictly necessary to avoid excess humidity; with this dual aim in mind, air is given whenever the outside temperature permits. Harvesting usually begins after two and a half months of forcing. Harvesting can continue for around three weeks. Another procedure, still using the seedling prepared in the nursery, consists in planting in September in plots Imn, 30 wide, in which four rows of strawberry plants are placed at Om, 30 on the rows. A path of Om 50 is left between each bed. In October, the boxes and frames are placed, and when January comes, the paths are dug and stoves are set up to the height of the windows. The same care is taken with maintenance, and the harvest takes about two and a half months. Finally, we can simply cover strawberry beds, specially planted as we have just indicated, with boxes and frames, without establishing layers in the paths. This method produces fruit in great abundance from the beginning of May. As in the previous case, care must be taken to provide air in order to avoid too great a rise in temperature. With this cultivation method, yields are more or less equal to those obtained with open-ground cultivation. Animal and insect pests. - Strawberries are very fond of ferrets and rals. They often wreak considerable havoc in forced cultivation. Traps should be set for them. Slugs of all kinds frequently attack strawberries. The only way to destroy them is to place a layer of rancid butter on each slab. If you visit these boards early in the morning, you'll find the slugs attracted by the smell of butter, which are easy to destroy. The strawberry plant's most fearsome enemy is undoubtedly the larva of the chafer (Melalonta vulgaris) or white grub, which cuts all the roots, destroying the plant at fruiting time. The only way to destroy them is to pull up the strawberry plants as soon as you see them wilting, and look for the beetle larva, which is usually easy to find because of its size. Finally, the larvae of the Julius fragarium tipula attack the fruit, devouring its entire interior. There is no practical way to defend against the ravages of this insect. Taken as a whole, beans, grown all over the world, refer to several different botanical species. On the contrary, all the countless varieties grown in our climate originate from a single species, the common bean (Phaseolus vulgarts, Linnaeus). Beans are cultivated in our climate as annuals, but they can last longer if the temperature conditions are right. Its stems are long and voluble, and all varieties with this character are said to have oars, because of the need to provide them with supports; often its stems remain short and not voluble, which constitutes the so-called dwarf varieties. It seems that these two categories have existed for as long as beans have been cultivated; however, these characteristics are not always absolutely fixed, and it is not uncommon to see dwarf varieties producing here and there a few plants of beans with voluble stems. Beans have pinnate leaves, composed of three slightly cordiform, acuminate leaflets; the leaves are arranged in alternating order. The flowers, generally white, are arranged in single clusters, averaging three to ten flowers. They have a papilionaceous corolla with a spiral-shaped carina. The single-located ovary is followed by a fruit (pod), usually called a husk, which contains seeds of different shapes, colors and numbers, depending on the variety. Bean fruits have an endocarp which is sometimes hard and parchment-like; sometimes, on the contrary, this part of the fruit becomes fleshy. This makes up, on the one hand, Beans with parchment or for shelling, and on the other, Beans without parchment or mange-tout, so called because the whole pod is eaten. Bean seeds are either white or colored, and the husks that cover them are more or less thick, giving them very different organoleptic qualities. In all cases, these seeds are a first-rate foodstuff, as the seed coats contain a significant proportion of a nitrogenous substance, known as tegumine, associated with the starch. Numerous hypotheses have been put forward as to the origin of the bean, but nothing to date allows us to settle the question definitively, for the reason that nowhere has the plant been found in a spontaneous state. It is nevertheless true that the presence of its cultivation could not be authentically established, as having been made at a time prior to the discovery of America, that, in any case, from that time, this culture took a great extension, and that the varieties multiplied from that day; that finally, among the seeds found in Peruvian tombs, it was possible to link some of them to the Beans proper. All these clues together allow us to assume, if not establish definitively, that this plant originated in South America. - Beans are eaten as young pods, when they are known as green Hartcots. The mange-tout are used for the same purpose, with the advantage that they can still be used while the beans are still formed. The seeds are used either green or dry. Varieties. - There are innumerable varieties of bean; we will limit ourselves to indicating those most commonly used in both small and large-scale farming. But all these varieties should be separated into two groups, depending on whether they are dwarf or climbing, as they correspond to two different growing methods. Dwarf beans are most commonly grown in gardens, and are the only ones used for early production. They have the great advantage over those in the second category of being able to develop rapidly and not requiring stakes, which are often costly. I. - DWARF STRING BEANS. A. - Varieties with white seeds. HOLLAND FLAGEOLET BEAN. - Flageolets are the most widely known and esteemed of the shelling beans. The bean is small, depressed, and has a greenish tint which gives it a pleasant appearance and makes it sought after for consumption as a green or dry bean. FLAGEOLET BEAN WITH EMBOSSED LEAVES. - A small variety which, for this very reason, is much sought-after in forced cultivation. It is also sown in the open, where it produces abundant, early yields. It is characterized by its unevenly blistered leaves. It is grown for green consumption (fig. 62). Haricor rlageoler D'ÉTaMpEs. - Ample, abundant foliage; very early flowering, giving rise to Fig. 62 - Flageolet bean with embossed leaves. numerous pods. Like its predecessor, this variety is mainly grown for green consumption, either of the pods or the beans. Haricor FlageolEr CHEVRiER. - In recent years, a number of varieties have been sown whose beans remain almost green after drying. The Chevrier bean is one of the first varieties of this type. Apart from the advantage of coloring, this variety has the disadvantage of being delicate in many circumstances. The green color of the bean is partly due to the variety and partly to the fact that it is harvested before full maturity. B. - Varieties with colored beans. VAVIN CHOCOLATE BEAN. - Variety similar to Flageolets in shape and size of beans, Fig. 63. - Haricot de Bagnolet. but these are of a brown color which has earned them their name. It is especially used for the large-scale cultivation of early vegetables in southern France and Algeria. BELGIAN BLACK BEAN. - Small variety, well suited to forced cultivation. Green pods, sometimes variegated with purple, containing small, black seeds when ripe. Grown exclusively for green beans. HARICOT DE BAGNOLET. - This variety is larger than the previous one. The pod is straight and fruiting is abundant. The beans are purple, blackish. This is one of the most widely grown varieties around Paris for the production of green beans (fig. 63). 11. - DWARF BEANS WITHOUT PARCHMENT. HARICOT NOIR D'ALGER. - This variety, with its black beans, is widely cultivated, as its pods retain the property of being able to be eaten green for a long time. III. - STRING BEANS WITH PARCHMENT. A. - White-grain varieties. SOISSONS BEAN. - This is the most commonly used variety in this category. The voluble branches can grow to a height of around 2 meters; its flowering is prolonged and produces an abundant crop. The pods are long, curved and contain the largest seeds of the genus, highly sought-after for everyday consumption (fig. 64). B. - Varieties with colored beans. HARICOT ROUGE DE CHARTRES. - This bean belongs almost exclusively to large-scale cultivation; it is eaten only as beans. These are dark wine-red. It is a productive variety. IV. - STRING BEANS WITHOUT PARCHMENT. WHITE PRAGUE BEAN. - Productive variety but 8 ~ 2 * Fig. 64. - Late Soissons bean. Used mainly in large-scale cultivation. White, almost rounded grain. Grown in the open air, in the Paris climate. - Beans thrive in loose, deeply tilled soil containing decomposed fertilizers. Sloping, south-facing soils are ideal for early sowing. The earliest you can start sowing is towards the end of April, if you don't want to use shelters, as beans are very sensitive to cold. You can bring them forward by about a fortnight, taking care to cover them every evening with straw mats laid on top of the bean beds. Sowing can continue until the end of June, if the beans are to be harvested as beans, and until the end of August if they are to be harvested green. Seedlings are sown either in bunches or in rows. The first of these two systems is used almost exclusively for dwarf varieties. In rows spaced Om, 40 to Om, 50 apart, pocks are opened at equal distances, either with a hoe or a spade, and five or six seeds are placed in them, spaced slightly apart, and covered with about Qu 05 of soil. In large-scale cultivation, we often sow in rays, on which the beans are spaced 09, 10 apart, but it is always preferable to sow in bunches - these sowings are generally done with a seed drill - this is how they are practiced around Paris, When we grow beans, it is necessary, whether we adopt rays or bunches, to leave a space between the rowsDYBONSKI. - Vegetable growing. 19 of about Om, 80. One and a half liters per are are sown in rays, and 2 liters in bunches. Beans germinate after ten or fifteen days. When they have emerged from the soil and are bearing their first leaves, the first hoeing is carried out to fill in the hollows left by sowing and to remove weeds. After hoeing, it's time to give the varieties that need it the support it requires. This can be done either by hoeing, or by cutting branches from the coppice. If beans are planted in bunches, place a single twig in the center of the clump. If the seedlings are planted in rays, the reams are inserted every two or three feet, taking care to incline them so that the ends of the reams of two neighboring rays meet at their apexes. This arrangement, by leaving the passage free every two rows, facilitates harvesting. After the reams have been laid, or in any case when the beans begin to show their first flowers, the ground is hoeed a second time, and the soil is raked over the bean stalks, making them slightly ridged. Harvesting begins around July for early beans eaten green, and in August for beans. Beans continue to produce until frost. Beans for green consumption should be harvested as soon as the pod has reached at most half of its full development. E4 EST Beans intended to provide dry grains should be harvested in fine weather, when the pods, having turned yellow, begin to dry out completely. Flageolet beans whose green color is to be preserved should be harvested before the pods have completely dried out. Once harvested, the beans are tied in bunches, left to dry out on the ground if the weather is fine, then taken into a shed or attic, where they are hung to dry out completely. Only when drying is complete can the seeds in the pods be extracted by flail threshing, but they can be kept fresher by extracting them from the pods only as and when needed. Bean yields vary greatly from year to year, and are generally higher the hotter and wetter the summer. In years of drought, however, yield is very low if Ton has not been able to water the crop. If the same beans are harvested green, then in fresh beans and finally dry, a maximum of 45 kilograms of green beans, 8 liters of fresh beans and 4 liters of dry beans can be obtained per acre. If harvested exclusively dry, they can yield over 20 liters per acre. Green beans can be preserved very easily, provided they are stored in stoneware jars, in which a bed of beans and a layer of gray salt are placed alternately. They should be washed with plenty of water before cooking. - Beans are grown in Algeria, Spain and, to a lesser extent, in the Mediterranean region, with a view to producing early products that are shipped to Paris and England. In recent years, this type of production has become so important that forced cultivation in the North of France has been greatly reduced. Mr. José Congiu provided us with the following information on the very important crops grown in the province of Malaga. They have the advantage of relating exactly to the procedures followed in Algeria. In the Malaga area alone, around 300 hectares of beans are grown annually, with the produce destined exclusively to supply the Paris markets during the winter. Bean growers bring in the seeds they need from Paris, usually the variety known as Vayin chocolate. Growers are well advised to bring their seeds from more northerly countries than their own, as experience has shown that this produces earlier yields than seeds harvested locally. For export crops, sowing takes place at two different times: in August on the one hand, and in December on the other. In both cases, seed is sown in bunches in well-prepared soil, with four or five seeds per bunch. The lines are drawn at Om, 45, and the clumps are spaced Om, 25 apart. This arrangement facilitates the hoeing essential to this crop. If the weather is dry, it is urgent to water, which is done by irrigating every four or five days. Ilaricots begin to produce about forty days after sowing, so that those sown in December produce from January onwards, and the harvest continues until April. Those sown in August produce their pods from October to December; this double sowing makes it easy to obtain a continuous product throughout the winter months. Yields. - Harvesting is entirely green, so we pick everything the plants produce. The yield varies from year to year, and also according to the land planted. It reaches its peak when the beans are sown in good exposure and frequently irrigated. Under these conditions, between 3,000 and 3,500 kilograms of green beans are produced per hectare. Sales prices are extremely variable, depending on when the crop is harvested: from December to February, beans sell for no less than 1.50 to 1.80 francs per kilogram. But this price falls rapidly, to O fr. 30 and even O fr. 20 per kilo in April and May, the prices obtained for sale on the spot. Forced cultivation. - Early bean production has greatly diminished since the produce is imported during the winter from southern regions. As a result, the cultivation of beans on beds, which used to be practiced by market gardeners, has now been completely abandoned by these growers. However, it is still a useful crop for vegetable gardens in rural areas, where communications with major centers are difficult. We cannot recommend this type of production from a commercial point of view. Beans are grown as early as possible, either with the help of heat produced by thermosiphons, or on layers. Only the second of these two methods needs to be considered here, as thermosiphon cultivation is beyond the scope of this book. Beans are sown on hot beds, starting in January, using early varieties of flageolet. The seeds are broadcast and covered with a small layer of potting soil. As soon as the plants have sprouted, which takes about three days, they are transplanted either to a nursery under a different frame, to be planted ten days later, or directly into the ground, which is a less efficient procedure, as the plants are less stocky. In all cases, the plants must be sunk right down to the cotyledons, and to do this, the layer is covered with about Om, 20 of potting soil. The plants are placed in three rows per frame, and transplanted, two by two, leaving a space of Om, 12 between each clump thus formed. Do not over-water, as this will rot the plants. Harvesting takes place two and a half months after sowing, and should be repeated every two or three days. To obtain a continuous yield, two or three seedlings are sown on a bed, and from April onwards, they are sown in the open ground covered with a frame. Finally, to achieve the same yield as in the open ground, you can sow in April under a frame and transplant at the beginning of May, provided you cover the plants overnight with straw mats. Harvesting seeds. - Beans destined for seed can simply be harvested from plants sown in beds, taking care not to harvest green. This procedure is sufficient for all common varieties, but for very advanced and early breeds, better results are obtained by harvesting the seeds only from plants that have been transplanted. Although theoretically beans retain their germinative capacity almost indefinitely, since seeds extracted from Tournefort's herbarium have germinated after more than eighty years, in practice preference should be given to seeds from the previous year, which germinate faster and give more robust plants than old seeds. This plant, of which many species and varieties are known today, is represented in our cultivation only by the Chinese yam (Dioscorea batatas, Decaisne), which was introduced to France in 1848 by M. de Montigny, French consul in Chang-Haï. Yam is a perennial plant with an underground rhizome. Its stems, which can reach 3 to 4 meters in length and are voluble, bear alternate leaves that frequently become opposed by training; these are sagittate, cordiform at the base and with pinnate-pedal venation; at their axils are born bulbils whose size varies from that of a pea to that of a hazelnut. The flowers are dioecious and arranged in variously composed clusters. The most interesting organ, the one for which the plant is cultivated, is that peculiar kind of fleshy rhizome, which in reality is no more than a disproportionately enlarged bud or bulblet. This is an annual organ; the previous year's rhizome is replaced the following year by a larger one. In its second year, it reaches a length of no less than Om, 60 to Om, 90. Its shape is very special: very small near the surface of the ground, it gradually increases in diameter, only to decrease again and end in a mossy point (fig. 65). At its widest, its diameter is often equal to Om, 06 or Om, 10. A gray envelope covered with small rootlets conceals a very white flesh which, when broken, exudes a slightly viscous substance. The rhizome of the yam is devoid of any special flavor, which is good for spreading its use, and is very rich in a starch vaguely reminiscent in shape of the potato, but with grains of a much more even size. Uses. - Rhizomes are eaten in the same way as potatoes, which they resemble; but their taste is much finer and lacks the unpleasant flavor found in many poor-quality potatoes. Cultivation. - Sowing is not used for multipli- Fig. 65. - Chinese yam. cation, as it would produce results too slowly. Moreover, yams do not ripen in our climate, and it is only in the Midi or in Algeria that we can hope to obtain fertile seeds. It would be in our interest to plant seedlings which, through intelligent selection, would perhaps lead to an RF type of yam whose rhizomes, being shorter and stubbier, would be easier to pull up. Were it not for the serious drawback of extracting yams from the soil, it is certain that the plant would long ago have taken its place in vegetable gardens and even in large-scale farming. There are various ways of propagating yams. The one that gives the quickest results is to use the upper, tapering part of the rhizomes removed for consumption, as well as the entire rhizome, when its length is only about Olil, 15. This method of propagation has only one drawback, which is the difficulty of obtaining a sufficient number of organs for planting each year. So, if we were to grow this plant on a large scale, we would have to resort to one of the following two methods. The first, and simplest, is to collect all the little bulblets that have developed in the leaf axils during the summer, in the autumn, when the half-dried plants have let them fall to the ground. These bulblets are stored over winter in a cellar, where they are stratified in sand. In March, they are pricked out at Om, 10 in each direction, in a bed in the vegetable garden, and in autumn, they form small rhizomes, Om, 15 in length, to be used for planting the following year. Another method is to take cuttings in May or June, and give them a length of Om, 10 or so. These cuttings are transplanted close together, under cloches, along a north-facing wall. When they have taken root, the cloches are removed. They are watered several times during the summer, and at the end of autumn as many small bulbils are produced as cuttings. Kept over winter, these bulblets can be used in spring to form plants exactly like those that have developed spontaneously in the axils of aerial leaves. Any soil is suitable for this crop, provided it is rich in fertilizer and sufficiently moist; in dry soils, harvesting is always poor. The soil is ploughed deeply, then levelled and the prepared seedlings, stored in the cellar, are transplanted using a planter sunk deep enough so that the rhizome ending at the top is Om 05 below ground level. A spacing of Om, 30 to Om, 40 is maintained in all directions between the plants. M. de Villepin, director of the Pilletière farm-school, who has been involved with yam since its introduction to France, cultivates it in a slightly different way, with the best results, it seems. Around February, he builds a bed covered with his frames, under which flower pots are arranged close together. In each of these pots, filled with potting soil, he plants either a fragment of a large rhizome or an aerial bulb. It doesn't take long for the yams to develop well. At the beginning of May, when frosts are no longer to be feared, he digs a deep hole in the soil intended for them. Then, from Om, 50 to Om, 50 in all directions, a hole is dug in the ground with a stake, which is driven in as far as the hole will allow. This hole is filled with potting soil, and one of the pots prepared under the frames is planted in it. As soon as the plant has risen to Om.40, it is given a stake against which it wraps, then irrigated during the summer and given one or two hoeings. Harvesting takes place in autumn, and M. de Villepin obtains 40,000 kilograms of yams per hectare using this method. This method has the advantage of yielding an abundant product, thanks to the use of beds in spring; it only has the disadvantage of requiring a lot of labor and raw material, consisting of potting soil and bedding manure. In ordinary cultivation, the crop is harvested either in autumn of the first year, when only rhizomes 0u30 to Om, 40 long are obtained, or only in the second year, so as to produce twice the size, and therefore twice the yield, without the need for additional expense. This biennial cultivation is very successful, as Yams have little fear of the cold and can withstand a frost of 15 degrees if a few leaves or large litter are thrown on the ground. However, the great drawback that makes the cultivation of this excellent plant so infrequent is the difficulty of uprooting it. The rhizomes sink vertically into the soil, and to extract them, it is necessary to open a gauge Om, 80 deep at one end of the cultivated field, and then gradually break up the soil in front of you with a hook, taking great care not to damage the extremely fragile yams. This method is costly, and has the added disadvantage of mixing in poor subsoil when the soil is not deep. A patient experimenter, Mr. Chapellier, has tried to breed a variety with shorter roots, but his results have not yet been completely successful. To prevent the roots from sinking, he advises planting them in pots, but the result is a twisted, poorly formed root. Yams uprooted in November and placed in cays keep very well until March or April. They are not attacked by any insect, nor are they known to suffer from any parasitic disease. .-. .. ".. HTAF.P. The very many varieties of cultivated lettuce found in vegetable gardens all seem to recognize as their origin the plant designated by Linnaeus as Lactuca scartola, found in a spontaneous state in almost all of the ancient world, and particularly throughout temperate Europe and northern Africa. The Greeks and Romans already cullivated it, but varieties were few and far between at the time. Today, the number has increased considerably. Lettuces are characterized by leaves that are more or less ample and elongated, depending on the variety; together, they form a large terminal bud, sometimes globular and pressed against the ground, which constitutes the lettuce proper, or head lettuce. At other times, the bud elongates into a rounded cylinder at the top, which characterizes the category of Roman lettuces. Lettuces are sometimes annuals, sometimes biennials; when the time comes to flower, a numerous-branched stem rises from the center of the leaves, ending in flower-head cymes. These bear only a small number of light yellow flowers, followed by flattened elliptical fruits (achenes), sometimes white, sometimes black, which are improperly called seeds; they retain their germinative powers for four to five years. APPLE LETTUCE Varieties. - These lettuces, all of which form more or less globular apples, have leaves of infinitely varying size, color and degree of division of the leaf blades. They constitute varieties whose hardiness and consequently cultivation requirements differ greatly. They are subdivided into spring, summer and winter lettuces, but this classification is by no means absolute, and almost all varieties can be moved from one category to another, provided they are cultivated appropriately. The most commonly used varieties are as follows: Spring varieties. These varieties have the characteristic of developing rapidly, making them particularly suitable for either forced cultivation or cultivation in the open ground, in a sheltered situation. Because of the short time they take to develop, they are unsuitable for summer cultivation, as they quickly flower under the influence of high heat. GOTTE LETTUCE WITH WHITE SEEDS. - Low-growth variety. Leaves are soft green, turning yellowish in parts exposed to direct sunlight. The plant is stocky, forming a small apple on the ground. Despite its small size, this variety is widely used, especially in forced cultivation, where it is particularly useful for its rapid development. It is hardy and, in a sheltered location, can withstand the cold of winter. GoTte A GRaINE NOIRE. - Differs from Y - 4 Fig. 66. - Black-seeded gotte lettuce. by the color of its seeds and its even greater earliness. It is widely used in market gardening, although its head never becomes hard (fig. 66). CREPE LETTUCE WITH BLACK SEEDS. - Light foliage, slightly more developed than in the preceding varieties; widely used for early vegetables. GEORGE LUEE. - This variety, which is still more or less confined to market gardens around Paris, is taking on a greater growth habit than all those mentioned above; despite this, it lends itself very well to forced second-season cultivation. It is the most suitable lettuce for the first spring planting. Summer and autumn varieties. All the varieties included in this second category are characterized by the fact that they flower less quickly than the preceding ones, and are therefore more resistant to the heat of the summer season. LAZY BROWN LETTUCE. - A vigorous plant with ample, dark-green foliage marked with reddish-brown, forming a hard head. Its name comes from the fact that it is slow to bolt; its seeds are black. PALATINE LETTUCE. - This variety, also known as L. rousse, forms a head with numerous leaves colored reddish-brown on the outside. Its seeds are black. These two varieties are the most widely grown around Paris, and are easy to sell at market stalls. LAITTE BATAVIA BLONDE. - Batavia lettuce forms a bulky, medium-hard head. Its leaves are embossed and toothed at the edges. The Batavia breed of lettuce has given rise to numerous variations, some of which are very similar to Romaine; they therefore represent a sort of transition between the two types, Lettuce and Romaine. Winter varieties. This group is made up of hardy plants that can easily overwinter in the open air in the Paris climate. The product they produce is of a less delicate quality than that produced by the two preceding categories, but their ease of cultivation, and the early spring produce they produce, make them much sought-after, particularly in rural potasers. PASSION LETTUCE. - This plant has dark green leaves with red spots. The head is medium-sized and rounded. The seed is white. All DYBOWSRI. - Vegetable cultivation. 20 varieties, this is certainly the hardiest. It is only suitable for winter cultivation (fig. 67.). LArue MoriNe. - Variety with weaker development than the preceding ones, bearing very ample green leaves. Hard, full apple. White seed. Uses. - Lettuces are all used in salads: they are all the more sought-after as their leaves are softer; for this reason, those supplied by forced cultivation are generally more highly esteemed than those grown in the open air. Fig. 67. - Passion lettuce. They are also eaten cooked and variously seasoned. Growing in the open air. - To be able to plant lettuces early in the spring, they should be sown in the autumn and overwintered under cover. Pancake, gottes and Georges lettuces, which are usually used in this way, should be sown in the second half of October. Sowing is done broadcast, on top of an old layer of potting soil; lightly baste, and emergence takes place after four to five days. As soon as the cotyledons are well developed and the first leaves begin to appear, it's time to transplant. This can be done under cloches or under a frame. In the former case, the soil is laid out in "ados", covered with a few centimetres of potting soil. Each bed receives three rows of cloches (fig. 68), under each of which 24 to 30 young lettuce plants are transplanted. When transplanting is to be done under a frame, the soil should be placed in the trunk so that it reaches approximately Om, 08 of the glass, and a distance of Om, 06 should be maintained between the plants. After transplanting, lightly baste the plants, then, when they have recovered, give them air until the day when the cold begins to set in, it becomes necessary not only to keep the cloches and frames closed, but even to surround them with litter or leaves and cover them with straw mats at night. Once the frosts have passed, air is gradually introduced to accustom the young plants to its action. Finally, in February, later or later depending on the severity of the year, we completely remove the shelters, leaving the young plant, which by then has three or four leaves, exposed to the air for a week or so, after which it can be definitively planted out in the open air. The first plantings are made in a well-sheltered hillside; then, from the first days of March, you can plant in a square. The soil should be well loosened and rich in potting soil. Transplanting is done in staggered rows Om, 20 or Om, 25 apart, depending on the variety. A few radish seeds are often sown before transplanting. A few waterings are useful; they are given in the morning so that the excess water has time to evaporate before the cold weather that is to be feared during the night sets in. The plant thus preserved under glass can be used for successive plantings until April. This method of cultivation produces lettuces that develop rapidly and give hard, well-shaped apples. To follow on from this first season, Gcorges lettuces are sown either in the open air or under cloches at the beginning of March. The resulting seedlings are planted directly in a full square. Finally, in March, we start sowing Palatine and Lazy Lettuces, which we renew every two or three weeks to ensure sustained production. The last seedlings are sown in July and August, when other lettuces take over from the lettuces. Throughout the summer months, lettuces are easy to grow: they are sown in good soil and watered frequently, then transplanted into ploughed, smoked beds. At this time of year, however, lettuce plants need to be watered frequently if they are to give good results. For this reason, it is useful to cover the soil with a mulch to retain water and prevent it from drying out too quickly. In market gardens, lettuces are watered abundantly every day. Some lettuces, as we have said, can withstand the cold of winter, such as the Passion variety. This variety should be sown in the second half of September, in the open ground, with enough light to prevent the plant from withering. As soon as the plant has four or five leaves, it is transplanted to a good exposure, leaving a distance of about Om, 40 between the plants. After transplanting, watering is carried out to help the plant take root. In normal winters, there's no need to protect the lettuce plant, which will get through the bad season without a hitch; but if the thermometer falls to more than 8 or 10 degrees below zero, and if the plant is not protected by snow, it's a good idea to cover it with a bit of litter, which can be removed as soon as the cold has passed. At the end of winter, lettuce develops rapidly and can be harvested towards the end of March or in April. Sometimes, young lettuces are delivered for consumption when they have five or six leaves, either by using the remains of unused plants for this purpose, or by sowing them specially for this particular purpose. In these conditions, we use early varieties whose seeds are scattered either singly in a bed specially set aside for this purpose, or between other vegetables such as cabbages, beans, etc. This process produces what is known as "cutting lettuce", because it is harvested by cutting above the root; no special variety is assigned to this crop, but all can be used, provided they are fast-growing. Forced cultivation. - Lettuces, and in particular all fast-growing varieties, do very well with forced cultivation. As a result, this method of production has been very popular for many years now. Paris market gardeners produce large quantities of lettuces throughout the winter, some of which are sold at market halls, and a large proportion of which are exported abroad, where they command premium prices at this time of year. The varieties most frequently used are Laitues gottes, crêpes and Georges, the latter being reserved for the last crops grown under chassis. Market gardeners in Paris build the first beds for their lettuces around the middle of October. These layers are made by mixing equal parts of dry manure, preserved in millstones and known as annealed manure, with fresh manure. These piles are given a height of about Qu 40 and covered with potting soil to a thickness of Qm. !5. When the layer is ready for use, seven rows of gotte lettuces are planted, with seven plants per row, making a total of forty-nine lettuces per frame. The plant used for this purpose was sown at the end of August or the beginning of September, then transplanted under cloche as soon as the first leaf appeared. The same plant can be used for subsequent crops. These lettuces are harvested at the beginning of December, when the central leaves have begun to form a small head. As soon as the lettuce has been delivered for consumption, the frames and boxes are removed, the layer of potting soil is removed, and the manure is reworked by adding about half the original quantity of new manure. Care must be taken in this operation to mix the new manure thoroughly with the old to allow the mass to heat up again. When the layer is ready for use, forty-nine plants of lettuce from the autumn sowing are planted in the same way as before. At the beginning of February, or by the middle of the month at the latest, the lettuces are harvested, and the soil in the frame becomes free again, so a third generation of lettuce is planted, without the need to reshuffle the bed this time. Lettuces are usually sold by the hundred. The packaging consists of large conical handles, which market gardeners use to transport most of their vegetables. Many early lettuces are shipped abroad, usually by commission agents who come to the growers' premises to buy the lettuces on the spot. Selling prices are extremely variable, depending on the season. During the winter, lettuces are sold for 10 to 12 francs per cent, but this price is halved in the spring. ROMAN LETTUCE Although some botanists have thought it useful to separate the Romaine variety from the Lactuca sativa variety, making it the Lactuca romana variety, we see no reason for this separation. In the latter, the head, instead of remaining short and often almost globular as in the Lettuces, elongates into a kind of cylinder of varying height; but this characteristic alone is insufficient to make the Romaine a separate species, especially as it is not, as we have said, absolutely fixed. Uses. - Like head lettuces, Romaines are used to make salads. They differ in that the leaves are firmer and crunchier. Varieties. - The Romaines that are widely grown are not very different in appearance; however, each variety corresponds to a distinct cultivation method, so it's worth mentioning them here. They are distinguished less by their shape than by the coloring of the leaves. ROMAINE VERTE MARAICHERE. - A variety with dark green leaves. Used especially for forced cultivation. It has produced a form used by market gardeners, known as Romaine plate, because during the first period of vegetation the leaves form a sort of flat cup, then gradually rise to form a very large head. ROMAINE BLONDE MARAICHÈRE. - Light green leaves. This variety is used for outdoor cultivation (fig. 69). ROMAINE ROUGE D'HIVER. - Dark red leaves. This variety is used for outdoor winter cultivation because of its resistance to cold. Open-air cultivation. - As with lettuces, Romaine must be sown in autumn towards the end of September or beginning of October, so that it can be planted early in the spring. As soon as the plant takes its first leaf, it is transplanted, preferably under cloches, as we have indicated for Lettuces. Green Romaine and sometimes blond Romaine are used for sowing. Whenever the weather permits, i.e. when it is not freezing, it is advisable to give the plants some air by sowing Fig. 69. - Romaine blonde maraîchère. vant les cloches sur des crémaillères, sinon le plantait étioler. In mild winters, despite all precautions, the plant may grow too vigorously. In such cases, it is necessary to delay the growth of the plant by uprooting it and replanting it in the same place; this replanting causes a salutary delay in vegetation. Frames are rarely used to protect Romaine plants over winter; the use of cloches is infinitely preferable. In February, Romaine plants sown in autumn can already be used for costiere planting. In March, they can be planted in the open air in a square. For these plantings, the soil should be prepared by ploughing and covered either with a layer of compost or decomposed mulch. Romaines are planted at Om, 35 in all directions. Red Romaines are planted in autumn, like those just mentioned; but as they are hardy and resist cold well, as long as it doesn't exceed 12 to 14 degrees, they are planted directly in autumn. It's a good idea to plant them not in a square, but in a sheltered hillside. As soon as the days become warm and sunny in March, it's a good idea to water the plants, but this should be done in the morning, so that the excess water has time to evaporate during the day, and any frosts that may still occur at this time of year are not to be feared. When the Romaine apple is well formed, it is tied with a strand of rye straw, soaked in water beforehand, and pieced to make it completely supple. This binding must be done in dry weather, as it has the effect of hastening and increasing the whitening of the leaves making up the apple. Romames sown in autumn can be harvested towards the end of April or in May. To succeed this first season, Romaine blonde seedlings should be sown at the beginning of March. It's a good idea to shelter the seedling with a frame, and even use a bed if you're in a hurry to harvest. As soon as the plant has acquired four or five leaves, it can be transplanted directly into a well-smoked, mulched square. Watering must be as frequent as it is copious, if you want to obtain well-developed Romaines that don't flower until they have formed their buds. Sowing can continue throughout the summer months, without the need for a bed or even a frame from April onwards; but Romaine crops grown during the summer months do not do very well, so Romaine is usually not sown until the end of May. Later crops will only succeed if they are watered copiously. Watering should only be done in the evening or morning, and never during the day, as this could cause the Romaine leaves to stain. Forced cultivation. - Romaines do well in forced cultivation, provided they are placed under a bell and not under a frame, as is the case for lettuces. As a result of this requirement, this early crop cannot be grown in the dead of winter, as cloches provide less protection against frost than frames. The seedling to be used for bedding is sown in the first days of October, under a bell or under a lightly shaded frame, if the weather is fine. A fortnight or so after sowing, the seedlings are transplanted under a bell as described above. The seedlings are then kept on the bedding until the time is right to plant them out on the bed. Layers for Romaine cultivation are built up from the end of December to the end of February; they are given a thickness of about Om, 45 for the first ones and Om, 35 for the last ones. Equal parts of annealed and new manure are used. The layer is topped up with about Om, 18 of potting soil, then bells are placed on top of the layer, staggered in three rows. On these layers we plant either a single Romaine plate, or a Romaine verte and three Laitues gottes; but for some years now this second arrangement has been abandoned, as experience has shown that these lettuces harm each other. When planting, take care to make a sort of hole in the soil with the planting tool, so as to form an opening beneath the Romaine plant. This procedure ensures that the roots are not burned by excessive heat. In addition, at the edge of each bell, we push in a little of the compost to form a small opening in the soil, allowing the air trapped under the bell to communicate with the outside atmosphere. These aeration conditions are essential to obtain fully developed lettuces. For Romaine grown on beds in February, we plant one plant under each bell and another between the bells, exposed to the open air. As soon as the Romaine planted under a bell has been harvested, it is replaced on the feet planted in the open air. This process can produce two or even three successive seasons of Romaine in a single layer. Romaines are sold in bunches of four. At the height of the season, they are sold in bundles of thirty-two. Romaines de primeur usually fetch 2 francs to 2 fr. 50 per four in February and early March. But this price drops rapidly, and in spring the thirty-two plants are sold for only 6 to 7 francs, sometimes even less. Growing for seed. - To obtain good lettuce or Romaine seed, it's best to use either plants sown in autumn and planted in spring, or those sown early on the bed. In all cases, when the Lettuce or Romaine plants have reached full head, choose those with the best apples; remove them in rootballs and replant them at Om, 50 in all directions. As soon as the flowering stem rises from the center of the leaves, it should be staked, and it is often useful to remove the leaves at the base, which could rot and destroy the whole stem. When these lettuces have flowered, the seeds are protected by placing a bell (fig. 70) at the top of the stile supporting the plant, to protect them from rain and from birds, which are fond of them. These seeds retain their germination capacity for four to five years. Diseases and pests. - Apart from slugs, which are very fond of young lettuce plants, these lettuces also have to contend with aphids, some of which (Aphis Sonchi) attack the roots, while others attack the inflorescences, causing great damage. The former are controlled by frequent watering, the latter by spraying with tobacco water... Fig. 70 - Lettuce plant in seed, sheltered by a bell. Lettuce also suffers from another plant pest, which in recent years has wreaked considerable havoc on lettuce crops. This parasite is known as the Lettuce whitefly (Peronospera gangliïformis). Mr. Max. Cornu (I), in a comprehensive study he made of this parasite, found that the only way to preserve oneself from this parasitic fungus was to carefully avoid the spread of its spores. To do this, firstly, all lettuces in which the disease manifests itself intensely should be dried out and then burned, and secondly, the same soil should not be used for growing lettuces for several years in succession, as the germs may remain in the soil and produce a blight on the lettuces which (1) M. Max. Cornu, Etude des Péronosporées (Mémoures de l'Acad. des sciences, 1882). more or less accentuated spots of disease. No treatment applied to the lettuces themselves would be successful, for the reason that the fungus grows inside the plant, and the parasite cannot be destroyed without killing the lettuce carrying it. The symptoms of this disease are white spots on the underside of the leaves. Affected plants develop poorly and, in all cases, all diseased parts end up rotting. These ravages are most noticeable when lettuces have to be packed for a few days, as is the case when they are shipped abroad. It is not uncommon for seemingly healthy lettuces to rot completely after two or three days' packing. Gardeners can't afford to take too much care to get rid of this pest. Lamb's lettuce (Valertanella olitoria, Linnaeus) is a biennial plant, meaning that when sown in autumn, it doesn't bear fruit until the following year. This plant, generally indicated as indigenous, seems to be widespread in France only in the subspontaneous state. It has escaped from cultivation and become naturalized in our fields, particularly in clay and clay-limestone soils. According to de Candolle (1), its true home is Sicily and Sardinia, where it is found in pastures. From there, it would have been imported into our crops. In any case, as the same author points out, its use seems to be absolutely modern, as there is no mention of it in cultivation works published in the seventeenth century. Lamb's lettuce has opposite, spatulate leaves. Pairs of leaves are close together on the stem, forming a more or less compact rosette on the ground, depending on the variety. In the spring of the second year, the stems lengthen and branch dichotomously to form inflo(1) Origins of cultivated plants. 4 , FE. tas *- resences in biparous cymes. The purplish, cmq-parted flowers give rise to fruits which, containing only one seed, do not open. It is these achenes that are commonly referred to as seeds; they are gray and small in volume, to the point that one gram contains around a thousand of them. They retain their ability to germinate for five years. Uses. - The whole plant is eaten as a salad. It is a very useful crop in the countryside, providing produce throughout the winter. Varieties. - Lamb's lettuce has formed a small number of varieties, of which one in particular is widely cultivated and deserves to be so: it is the one used by market gardeners. ROUND MOSHE. - The leaves of this variety are shorter than those of the type; the spatula shape is more accentuated. It is often referred to as M. double, which is due to the fact that the buds in the leaf axils all develop early, forming a rosette that becomes more and more branched, resulting in a fuller plant (fig. 71). This variety is the most productive, due to the volume it acquires, and should therefore be sown clear in the field. We have produced a few breeds with thick, blistered leaves, which have the advantage of wilting less easily and therefore lend themselves to long-distance transport. These qualities make them particularly suitable for market sales. Cultivation. - Lamb's lettuce is always sown directly on site. The soil in which this DYBONSKI. - Cultivation, 2nd ed. 21 is prepared by ploughing, then harrowed and suitably levelled with a rake. The first seedlings are sown around the middle of August. The seed is broadcast, avoiding sowing too tightly; the most suitable proportion is around 100 grams per are. The seed is buried with a light rake, and the soil is covered either with a highly decomposed mulch, or with compost. Fig. 71 - Round lamb's lettuce. If the weather is dry, germination should be hastened by a few not too violent basinings, to avoid ravining the soil and dragging the seed along, which would concentrate in places and give uneven emergence. Eight to ten days after sowing, Lamb's lettuce appears with two small, rounded, glaucous-green cotyledonary leaves. About two months after sowing, in October, the first Lamb's lettuces are harvested. Harvesting is done by cutting lettuce stalks flush with the ground with a knife, taking care to remove the stalks where the sowing is too tight, so as to thin it out. All Lamb's lettuce sown in August must be delivered for consumption before winter, which they would otherwise be unable to withstand. To succeed this first season of lamb's lettuce, we have taken care to sow other crops. These, begun as we have just said, around the middle of August, can be continued until around October, by renewing them several times during this period to obtain successive products. In a pinch, three sowings can be enough to provide for consumption throughout the entire Lamb's lettuce season. The first, sown in August, will produce in October; the second, sown in September, will produce throughout the winter; and the Lamb's lettuce whose seeds are left in the ground at the beginning of October can only be cut at the end of the winter and part of the spring. We must be very careful not to sow the lamb's lettuce seeds too close together, otherwise they will rot, and this rot will spread from one plant to the next, creating patches of lack in the square. For the same reason, seedlings must be kept free of weeds by weeding. Chickweed is particularly harmful, as it quickly invades the whole crop. Lamb's lettuce doesn't fear frost, so it can spend the winter unprotected. However, to be able to harvest it, even in snowy weather and heavy frosts, it is useful to cover the beds either with straw or litter, or, preferably, with straw mats supported by small stakes fitted with transversely placed wooden slats. The great advantage of using straw mats is that they can easily be removed and replaced when the weather turns bad again. The result is an abundance of lettuce throughout the winter, so it's a good idea to sow a lot of it to make sure you don't run out. The market gardeners of Paris sèment la Mâche très en grand dans leurs terres, car c'est un des rares légumes parmi ceux qu'ils cultivent, qui supporte les atteintes des gelées. Seeds. - To harvest the seed of this plant, seedlings sown in September are allowed to overwinter, taking care to remove any plants that are insufficiently doubled due to the weak development of the lateral buds into a rosette of leaves, as well as any whose leaves are too long and not very fleshy. Lamb's lettuce starts to flower in May. Once flowering is over, you need to keep an eye on when the fruit begins to ripen, as it falls off with the greatest of ease. When the plant starts to turn yellow, pull it up and lay it on a tarpaulin or canvas of some kind, so that the fruit can ripen and be easily removed by light beating.
