It's a good idea to label the bags with the date of harvest, as experience shows that while a two- or three-year-old seed will germinate in ten or fifteen days, a one-year-old seed will often take nearly a month to germinate. ... ..." ... ... . .. .. . . . .... . . MELON In gardens, Melons present themselves in such a multiplicity of forms that many botanists, on the basis of the fact that the plant had been found in the wild in various forms, felt authorized to classify all cultivated Melons into a number of different species. This question was taken up again in 1859 by M. Naudin, who after studying it experimentally and cultivating no less than a thousand Melons from all parts of the world where this crop is widespread, published a remarkable memoir (1) in which he links all cultivated Melons to Linné's Cucumis Afelo, whose innumerable forms should consequently only be considered as varieties, or races that can be perpetuated by sowing. The reason why melons, which grow wild in different parts of the world, have been made into several species is that this plant, which has the property of infinite variation, can take on very different forms. It is this very property, once well studied and understood by Mr. Naudin, that enabled him to link all melons to a single primitive type. (1) Ann. des se. nat. S. 4, vol. XI. All melons have this property of extreme variation, so much so that it is often very difficult to preserve pure varieties in cultivation. Soil, climate and cultivation methods are all causes whose influences are immediately felt not only in our cultivated melons, but even in those that seem to grow spontaneously. What's more, this plant hybridizes very easily, and it is rare for several varieties grown in close proximity to each other not to exert reciprocal influences due to pollen transport by insects. Melons have been found in a truly wild state in two distinct regions: in Africa, on the banks of the Niger, and in Asia, in English India. Melons are annual plants that can, in special circumstances, become perennial. Their long, sarmentose stems sometimes crawl along the ground, and sometimes climb up whatever supports are within reach; they hold themselves in this position by means of tendrils, which are stipules or the result of transformed axes. The leaves of Melons, arranged in alternating order, are webbed and covered with abundant hairs, as are the branches. The leaf blade is divided into five rounded lobes, more or less deeply incised depending on the variety. Melons are monoecious plants. Male flowers appear first, and are yellow with five divisions. Female flowers have an infertile ovary covered with long hairs when young. The fruit to which the female flowers give rise behaves very differently, depending on the variety under consideration, and also, for the same variety, on the conditions in which the plant is placed. In all cases, it's a voluminous berry containing a considerable number of seeds arranged in its early stages on three placentas, from which they detach when the fruit is ripe, and which are then found swimming in a kind of liquid thickened by the debris of the placental part, which has become deliquescent. As we have said, the general shape of the fruit varies greatly from one variety to another, as does the state of the surface. M. Naudin, taking these characteristics into account, has classified all melons into ten different categories. For those of us who are concerned here only with varieties of general cultivation, recognized by all as being the best, we will content ourselves with indicating two of these categories, which include all the melons usually grown in gardens. I. - CANTALOUPE MELONS. Varieties. - Cantaloupe melons produce fruit that is usually large in size. These fruits are marked by deep grooves which divide them on their surface into more or less pronounced segments, or ribs. The epicarp or rind of the fruit bears wart-like asperities, varying in number and size according to variety. The flesh is red or orange-red, with a pleasant, delicate fragrance. MELON NOIR DES CARMES. - A productive and remarkably early variety, particularly suited to early fruit production. The fruit has a diameter of about Om, 15 to Om, 18; it bears few warts, its flesh is dark red and of very good quality. MELON PRESCOTT A FOND BLANC. - Vigorous plant producing fruits from Om, 25 to Om, 30 in diameter. Prominent ribs covered with large warts (fig. 72). Flesh orange-red, sweet and very Fig. 72. - Prescott cantaloupe melon with white base. fine. Unquestionably one of the best melons, if not the best of all. It is suitable for market gardening, where it is commonly used. It has a sub-variety with smaller fruit, less prominent ribs and dark green mottling. This is the prescott petit hâtif melon, which is very well suited to early cultivation. There are a great many other sub-varieties of prescott melon, which need not be described because they are local. Since melons are essentially plants subject to variation, any gardener who cultivates them over a certain number of years can create a special variety for himself through selection, once he has recognized its real qualities. II. - EMBROIDERED MELONS. Varieties. - Embroidered melons differ from the previous varieties in that the ribs on the fruit Fig. 73. - Melon sucrin de Tours. are much less prominent. Their surface has no warts, but is covered with a network of sinuous, protruding lines that give the skin a mottled, often grayish appearance. The flesh, although not as thick overall, nevertheless provides a roughly equal quantity of edible material for the diet, because the bark is not very thick. The flesh is less orange-red than in cantaloupes; it is also often white or greenish. Although sweeter than melons in the first category, it lacks the finesse of cantaloupes. These melons are less prized than those described above. They are grown mainly in the south of France, where their hardiness makes them ideal for open-air production. MARKET MELON. - Medium-sized, yellowish-green fruit with shallow ribs. Musky flesh, quite pleasant to the taste, hardy variety. SUCRIN DE TOURS MELON. - Fruit with shallow ribs, covered with conspicuous embroidery. Fruit diameter usually around Om, 15. Firm red flesh of very good quality (fig. 73). CAVAILLON MELON. - Vigorous plant producing oblong fruit covered with sparse embroidery, particularly in the grooves of the ribs, which are not very pronounced. Very sweet, fragrant green flesh. This variety is especially suited to cultivation in the south of France and Algeria, as its fruit is rarely of good quality when summer heat is low. There are several different forms of this variety, some with green flesh, others with red flesh. All are suited to southern cultivation. Cultivation. - Melons are plants that only vegetate and bear fruit if they are kept at a constantly high temperature; the slightest chill is enough to damage the vegetative organs and compromise any fruiting. For these reasons, it is only in the climate of the South of France that these plants can be grown in the open air, and even then it is necessary to let the season of white frosts pass before entrusting them to the action of the open air. In the climate of Paris, cultivation in the open air is rarely practiced, as the yield is too random. We are obliged to compensate for the lack of atmospheric heat by supplying it artificially. At any time of year, this type of cultivation can only be carried out on beds, but these will vary greatly in their construction according to the time of year at which you wish to operate. Melons lend themselves very well to forced cultivation, so they can be grown very early in the season, provided you know how to vary the circumstances according to the needs of the moment. In all cases, melons thrive in rich soil, provided it is not too light. Soil alone is therefore unsuitable, and you'll get more vigorous plants and more beautiful fruit by mixing it in equal parts with good, free-flowing soil. Bedding culture. - Although, as we have said, provided the melons are given the warmth they need to thrive, they can be cultivated at almost any time of the year, in practice the first sowings on beds are only done in the second half of January. If you sow earlier, not only would you have to provide a lot of heat to combat the rigors of the outside air, but you'd also have to worry about insufficient light, which would be detrimental to the plant's development. In January, we build a layer to a height of Om, 60. The materials used for this construction consist of fresh horse manure mixed with about a third of annealed manure, i.e. preserved in millstones since the summer. When the layer has heated up and become uniform in temperature, the melon seeds are sown into the compost, which, as we have recommended, has been mixed with clean earth. These are either placed in troughs or pricked out one by one with a finger. It is advisable to sow seeds of very early ripening varieties at the same time as those of slightly later ripening varieties. For example, sow early prescott melons and white prescott at the same time. This will extend the harvesting season. As soon as the seed has been sown, cover the frame with thick straw mats to prevent heat loss. Five or six days later, the melons emerge from the soil, and the mats are removed during the day and replaced only at night. The young plant develops rapidly if the heat is around 25 degrees, and you can see its tigelle and the two cotyledons it carries, rapidly gaining strength. When the cotyledons are well-developed and the center

starts to emerge a young leaf, the time has come to transplant them. For this purpose, a new layer similar to the first is prepared. The seedlings are then transplanted either into the ground, at a distance of Om, 15 in all directions, or into Om, 10 diameter cups previously placed in the soil covering the layer. This second method is preferable, as it allows the young plants to be transported later without the slightest delay caused by transplanting. In all cases, the seedlings should be transplanted one by one, and pushed down to the cotyledons, so that adventitious roots can be formed over the entire buried portion. After transplanting, water lightly and close the trays, which can be left covered with their mats for the first day, to help the plants take root. After a few days, the young melon plants produce their first leaves. When there are three of them, it's time for the first pruning. This is done about 1 cm above the second leaf. This operation is called "ététage", and should be carried out with a sharp penknife to obtain a clean, easy-to-heal section, and as soon as possible, since Fig. 74 - Melon plant having undergone first pruning. the section of an already large branch would heal badly (fig. 74). As soon as a new bud begins to develop in the axil of each of the two retained leaves, it's time to proceed with pruning. All these preliminary operations take between three weeks and a month at the most. Sown in mid-January, it's not until mid-February that the melons can be set. Setting out. - To proceed with planting, we build a new layer in the conditions we have indicated above. If only a small crop is to be grown, for example ten to fifteen frames, it is essential to build large stoves around the trunks, measuring around Om, 50. When, on the other hand, as in the case of Paris market gardeners, the crop is grown over a very large area, these stoves become unnecessary, as in these conditions there is no heat loss to the sides, and only the frames at the edge of the bed need to be surrounded by stoves. In all cases, the paths between the boxes should be filled with manure up to the height of the frames. Inside the chests, soil is placed, which, as we have said, must be mixed with potting soil to a height of about Om.20, so that there is only Om.15 of this soil on the surface of the glazing. Once the layer has warmed up to a temperature of 25 to 30 degrees, the plants are planted at a rate of two feet per frame. Some gardeners plant three plants, but this has its disadvantages, especially when growing vigorous, fast-growing varieties. If two plants are planted, the melons are placed along a line that divides the bed into two equal parts. If, on the other hand, three plants are planted, two are placed along a line drawn towards the upper third of the frame, and one along a line drawn towards the lower third. 1 - 4 Plants that have been raised in pots are removed from their pots and planted out, sinking them down to the cotyledons. The soil should be covered with a light layer of mulch. The buds in the axils of the two retained leaves soon elongate, forming two branches, one pointing upwards and the other downwards on the frame. Each of these arms, when sufficiently elongated, is pruned above the third or, better still, the fifth leaf. The result of this pruning is a new generation of buds; all those located in the axils of the retained leaves will deyelope, which means that the branches of this third generation will number six or ten. These branches often bear both male and female flowers. It's a good idea to fertilize these flowers, because if the fruits knot, if they develop, we'll gain time, but often they abort, so we remove them and prune again with two leaves in order to obtain fourth-generation branches which, in turn, will give abundant fertile flowers. We know that insects visiting flowers carry pollen from male flowers to the organs of female flowers, unconsciously fertilizing them. When forced cultivation takes place in the cold season, it is rare that the plants can be given air and that these insects can play their useful role. Artificial fertilization is therefore necessary. Taking male flowers from which the corolla has been removed, the pollen is shaken into the female flower (which can be recognized by the swelling below the flower, which is the ovary that will become the young melon). To proceed more methodically, you can take the pollen with a brush and place it on the stigma of the female flower. Fruit selection. - As soon as the young fruits, known as "maille" after flowering, start to swell, it's time to choose which ones to keep and remove all the others. Gardeners who want to obtain the best possible results leave only one fruit per plant; often, however, and particularly when the plants are vigorous, it is more advantageous to leave two fruits. In all cases, all others are removed at the latest when they are the size of a hen's egg. They can be pickled in vinegar, making a pleasant condiment. Preserved fruit must be chosen with the greatest care. They should be well formed and not have an overdeveloped eye, i.e. the point of insertion of the flower, which would later give them a defective shape. Fruit-bearing shoots should be pruned to one leaf above the point of fruit insertion. Later on, it will be necessary to ensure that no new fruit is produced, which can be removed as soon as they appear. For this reason, you should frequently visit the melon frames and remove any new branches that may develop. Melons like frequent watering, but this should be avoided until the meshes have been tied, as this could cause all the flowers to run off. Once the fruit is well formed, DYBONSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 22 the whole plant should be bathed frequently, avoiding pouring too much water over the base of the plant, which could cause it to rot. The water used should be at a temperature of 12 to 15 degrees. An additional harvest can sometimes be obtained by preserving a new, ripe mesh when the first fruit is almost ripe. This produces what is known as a "regain", which is more often than not a fruit of inferior quality. If, instead of keeping just one fruit, you prefer to leave two, you'll need to select them from two branches far apart to ensure that they come to the right place. Harvesting. - It takes an average of three months of cultivation before the fruit can be harvested. This may come sooner or later, depending on the warmth of the soil and the state of the season. You can tell that a fruit is approaching maturity when its color becomes lighter, and small cracks appear near the insertion of the stalk. The time has come to cut the fruit, which is done by removing a fragment of the stem that produced it. Melons should never be cut until they are absolutely yellow in color and have a strong odor, as they would then be of poor quality. Ripening generally takes place thirty to forty days after the knot is tied. Second-season cultivation. - To ensure continuous production, new melon seedlings are sown in February. These seedlings can be renewed and continued at three-week intervals until mid-June. This crop is grown under the same conditions as those described above. Melons should be given the same general care and pruning, but it should be borne in mind that as the season progresses, the temperature becomes higher, and that consequently, to provide the plants with the same degree of warmth, it is no longer necessary to make such thick layers. In April and May, a trench is dug in the ground, Om 25 to Om 30 deep and 1 m wide. The soil is thrown back to the right and left, and the trench is filled with manure. The soil is then placed back on top of the layer, where boxes and frames are placed. Finally, in May, with a similar layout, you can place bells 1.20 m apart on the trench, under each of which you plant a melon plant. As soon as the pruned branches begin to grow too long to fit inside the bell, it is lifted onto three racks and left above the plant until summer. Cultivation care is the same, i.e. straw the soil and baste frequently after fruit formation. Pruning is always the same. Growing in the Midi. - In the south of France and in Algeria, melons are easy to grow if you want to obtain fruit in the normal season. Seedlings are sown on a bed in April and prepared by transplanting into pots. From mid-May onwards, the melons are planted in the open ground on I-meter-wide tees, in the middle of which manure pots are made every 1.5 meters, on which the melon vines are planted. It is advisable to follow the same pruning methods as those described above, but in most cases only prune twice, after which the plants are left to their own devices. Under the influence of high temperatures, the vigor of the plant is much greater than in our artificial cultivations, and it is possible to leave two or three fruits on each plant without damaging the quality of the product. When growing varieties with embroidered fruit, this number is often exceeded, but it must be remembered that the size of the fruit is always inversely proportional to the number left on each plant. Choosing the seed carrier. - For seed, we choose the best-formed melon, with thick, well-colored flesh and a delicate fragrance. But if all these characteristics are of value, there is another which is perhaps of even greater importance: that the melon from which you wish to obtain seed should come from a garden where only one variety is grown. Melons are highly capable of hybridizing with each other, so if you're not careful, you may harvest seeds of perfect quality, but the following year you'll end up with nothing but degenerate plants. The influence of hybridization is of no importance when it comes to harvesting the fruit, but it is crucial when it comes to obtaining good seeds capable of perpetuating the shape and quality of the fruit that produced them. These seeds retain their germination capacity for five to seven years. Animal and plant parasites. - Melons are frequently attacked by parasitic animals that wreak havoc. On the one hand, there's the mite (Acarus cueumeris), which in practice is given the name "gray" because of the color it turns the attacked organs. It's difficult to get rid of, but the disease is alleviated by frequent drenching of the underside of the leaves with a decoction of labac or a light emulsion of black sayon and petroleum. - The same treatment can be applied against aphids, which attack young melon shoots. This plant still suffers from other diseases, the causes of which are still poorly defined and for which no effective treatment is known. These include canker, which eats away at the branches and sometimes the fruit; nail (I), or tissue rot; and powdery mildew, due to the presence of a parasitic fungus which is not very dangerous and which can be partially destroyed by applications of sulfur flower. (1) Recent work by Messrs. Prillieux and Delacroix has shown that this disease, which often causes great devastation, is due to the presence of a fungus, Scolecotrichum metophthorum. - . ... . . . .. = 1. ... tivated in Europe. It seems that the two forms that Linnaeus considered to be Brassica napus and B. campestris are simply derivatives of each other. These wild forms, with their spindly roots, would become the primitive type of turnip, whose roots have developed to a greater or lesser extent, thus constituting the numerous varieties we know today. But these are only probabilities; it would be interesting to undertake cultivation starting from types with spindly roots, in order to see whether it would be possible, by applying intelligent selection, to modify the state of this taproot. In any case, it would appear from various documents that Turnips proper, i.e. plants with fleshy roots, can be considered to have originated in temperate Europe. (1) (1) Since the publication of the first edition of this book, a very important note has appeared (Revue horticole, 1891, n° 21) concerning Observalions sur l'origine et les variations. du Navet. The author, Mr. Blanchard, head gardener at the Hôpital de la Marine in Brest, describes his patient experiments. The edible part of the plant is the fleshy taproot, which often extends several centimeters above the ground, in a sort of cone of various colors, to which, in practice, we give the name of collet. This is surmounted by a set of simple leaves, more or less abundant depending on the variety, and which are sometimes whole or lobed, sometimes, or on the contrary, fides or parts. From the center of these leaves rises, in the same year if the plant is sown in spring, or only in the second if sown in autumn, a stem bearing clusters of cruciform flowers, colored more or less intense yellow. These inflorescences have no bracts. The fruits that follow the flowers are long, irregular siliques containing spherical, reddish-brown seeds that remain germinative for five years. Uses. - The fleshy part of turnips is eaten cooked and prepared in a variety of ways. In Alsace, large turnips are cut into thin strips, then fermented to make a product similar to sauerkraut. Varieties. - Like all highly cultivated plants, he harvested Brassica napus seeds growing wild on Ouessant, where neither turnip nor Navette had been cultivated in living memory, and sowed them successively, always selecting plants for fleshy root formation. After fourteen years, these plants were already fleshy and finger-sized. These experiments are absolutely conclusive, and the fact must be taken for granted that our cultivated Turnips have their origins in the spindly-rooted plants growing spontaneously in temperate Europe. Turnips have produced a considerable number of varieties, varying in shape, size and color, as well as in quality. Among the horticultural varieties, we will limit ourselves to indicating the most important, dividing them into long-rooted turnips, and those whose root is more or less flattened. Turnip flesh does not always have the same consistency; there are soft-fleshed varieties, which are usually the most prized, and those with more or less hard flesh, which have the advantage of being easier and longer to preserve. As we have said, the color of the root varies greatly in turnips. This color has little influence on quality, and there are some very good yellow or gray turnips, but in general, especially in Paris, white-rooted turnips are preferred. When producing this vegetable for sale, this preference should be taken into account. LONG TURNIP VIRTUES. - This is one of the most widely grown varieties in the Paris region. The root is pure white and ends in a more or less sharp point. The flesh is meltingly sweet and of excellent quality. Navet Marteau is simply a form of the previous variety, in which the root bulges towards the lower part. This is the type of turnip used for vegetable and market gardening. By selection, market gardeners have created a form with few leaves and a smooth, well-shaped root, which they use especially for forced cultivation (fig. 75). MEAUX TURNIP. - This is a variety with dry flesh and very large development. Its white root, greenish at the collar, often reaches Om, 40 in length by Om, 07 in diameter. It is mainly used to supply markets in late winter, as it keeps very well (fig. 76). LONG TURNIP FROM ALSACE. - Approximately the same size as the previous turnip. But its flesh is Fig. 75. - Navet des Vertus, Fig. 76. - Navet de Meaux. Marteau race. more tender. This is the variety used for fermenting, cabbage-style. NAVET DE FRENEUSE. - This undemanding variety does well in poor soils. Its spindle-shaped, gray-white root is about Om.15 long. NAVET ROND DES VERTUS OR DE CROISSY. - Widely grown around Paris. Its root is white, its flesh fine and sweet; its diameter, equal to its length, is Om,07; this root is entirely buried (fig. 77). FLAT, WHITE TURNIP. - Its root is only Om,04 thick and Om,10 in diameter. Its flesh is not very sweet, but of good quality. All the edible partFig. 77. - Navet rond des Vertus. tible lies on the ground, or is barely buried at the base. Full-ground cultivation. - Turnips are widely grown around Paris. In some places, it is grown throughout the summer. Around Croissy, it is grown over vast areas. Turnips are never in short supply at the halles, where they are available all year round. Growers start sowing as early as March. The soil used must be rich, well prepared by deep ploughing, then trodden on the surface. Sowing is done in rows, using mainly the Marteau variety, of which 40 grams are sown per acre. Immediately after sowing, the soil is watered and kept constantly moist by repeated watering. This produces vigorous plants that don't bolt and are resistant to flea beetles, which almost inevitably attack them. As soon as the plant has two leaves in addition to the cotyledons, it should be thinned out. To do this, growers use a very small hoe, no more than Om, 05 wide, which they use to pass easily between the rows, which are Om, 15 apart, as well as over the rows, so as to leave a space of Om, 10 or so between the plants. Immediately after hoeing, the main aim of which is to thin out the plant while removing weeds, we water heavily. These spring crops require a lot of water; the farmers who practice them, less well-equipped than the market gardeners of Paris, draw water from wells, using a barrel attached bucket-style to the end of a rope that winds around a plank drum set in motion by a horse. When the barrel is mounted, a man, or even a child, tips it into a tank, which is done with the greatest of ease, its handle being fixed near the middle of the barrel, i.e. close to the center of gravity. Two to two and a half months after sowing, Turnips can be harvested; so growers sèment this plant every fortnight, to always have some to harvest. When it comes to producing Turnips for winter storage, they should only be sown from August onwards; all Turnips sown before this date will infallibly go to seed if they are not harvested as soon as their roots are more or less developed. On the other hand, turnips sown in August will keep well until late autumn, when they should be brought in from the cold. For winter storage, varieties such as Navets de Meaux, des Vertus and de Freneuse are mainly chosen; they are usually sown broadcast, on ploughed ground, then compacted by rolling. If sown at the beginning of August, watering is essential to ensure good growth of the young plants. Everything depends on rapid growth in the early stages of vegetation. Turnips that suffer from drought during this period become hard and stringy, even if they develop rapidly later on, due to exaggerated development of the fibrous tissue. For the last seedlings sown at the end of August and beginning of September, watering can be dispensed with, as the nights become cool and damp, and vegetation will proceed rapidly. When the plant has three or four leaves, we light it, leaving a distance of Om, 15 to Om, 20 between the plants. In Alsace, when turnips are half-developed, they are lightly ridged, so that the root remains white throughout. In rich soils, Navets can be obtained simply by sowing them in fields, on top of wheat stubble removed with a scarifier, or in areas where beans have just been harvested. Flat turnips are preferred for this type of cultivation. For turnips that are to be delivered for sale, as we have said, we pull them up as soon as the roots have formed. Turnips with long roots are pulled out by hand, using a hook. The turnips are placed in bunches of 12 to 15, depending on their size, tied together by the leaves, from which all yellow ones have been removed. The roots are then washed with plenty of water, before being taken to market. Avoid using quackgrass brushes, which would scratch the roots. Approximately 400 to 450 bunches are obtained per are; it is impossible to fix the price, as it varies from simple to double depending on the year, and especially on the season. In summer, for example, bunches are worth O fr. 50 to 0 fr.40, while in autumn the price is halved. If you want to keep turnips over the winter, only pull them up towards the end of October, and cut the leaves off at the neck. The roots should not be washed, but simply removed from the soil by hand, then lowered into a cellar or storeroom; they keep very well over winter. Another preservation method, no less good, is to put them in silos. Turnips are also sold by the bunch in winter, but as Fig. 78. - Navet de Meaux put in bunches. they have no leaves, they are pierced at the top with an auger, and one or two strands of rye straw are passed through the hole (fig. 78). Forced cultivation. - For a very long time, turnips were forced to be grown only at the end of winter, camamnni tutielu on warm beds, on which turnips of various varieties were sown, often mixed with radishes or carrots. In recent years, this crop has made great strides; market gardeners devote themselves to it in a special way, and we know of one grower who sold, in the space of a year, the very respectable sum of 12,000 fr. This crop is also very lucrative; but we do not recommend indulging in it without being absolutely sure of the quality of the seeds to be used, on pain of exposing oneself to serious setbacks. Market gardeners exclusively use Nayet Marteau, or rather a special form of this variety, in which they have succeeded, through selection, in having the lowest possible number of leaves, enabling them to be sown fairly tightly in frames, without however getting in the way and withering away. It is essential to have this shape to achieve the excellent results obtained by market gardeners. The first Turnip layers are built at the beginning of February, rarely before. Layers should be thick enough to give 20 to 24 degrees of warmth. They are loaded with Om, 15 to 18 of potting soil, and the frames are placed and covered with straw mats. Once the heat has died down and the temperature has become more or less constant, it's time to sow the seeds. This is done by pushing your finger into the soil to a depth of about Om, 03 and dropping one or two Turnip seeds into the soil. Sow at Om, 12 in all directions, giving around 90 turnips per frame. Immediately after sowing, the frames and mats are put in place, and the plant emerges in four or five days. As soon as the plant has taken its first leaf, it's a good idea to inspect the crop, in order to remove any duplicates, i.e. those that may have been two in the same hole. Vegetation develops rapidly, and is encouraged by watering as and when needed. A month and a half after sowing, the frames are removed and placed on new layers, which are made thinner because of the higher outside air temperature. These second layers are often made in trenches, in the form of muted layers, and when the turnips are harvested, melons are planted in them. Finally, some market gardeners use the trays placed over the second-season turnips to sow a third season, and when the turnips are well developed, the trays are transferred to the boxes where the second-season turnips were, which have already been harvested and which, as we have just said, will be replaced by melons. So, in one spring, these frames are used to produce three seasons' worth of turnips, and then to house melons. Market gardeners sometimes sow turnips under cloches, ten to a cloche; the cultivation procedures are the same as those described above. Harvesting takes place after about two months of cultivation. The roots, which by then have reached Om 10 in length and Om 03 in diameter, are harvested and placed in bunches of 12, laid flat in two rows. Most of these Navets are bought by commission agents, who export them to Germany and especially Russia. A small proportion is consumed in Paris. At the beginning of the season, the price per bunch varies between 1 and 2 francs; then it drops to 0 fr. 75. We harvest 8 bunches per frame. te 1. NAVET. Seed crops. - To harvest seed from Nayets grown in the open ground, the best-formed roots are selected in autumn and placed in gauges along a wall, taking care to cover them with litter to prevent them from freezing. In February, plant them at Om, 40 in well-prepared soil. If several varieties are grown for seed, care should be taken to keep them as far apart as possible, to avoid the cross-breeding that would inevitably occur if several varieties were grown together. When the plants have flowered and the first siliques have formed, the tips of the inflorescences are pinched off to obtain more beautiful seeds. When the siliques start to turn yellow, the stems are cut off and moved into the shade, to be threshed when dry. One plant can yield around 100 grams of good seed. For turnips grown in beds, select the best ones and replant them. Harvest in autumn of the same year. Insect pests. - Spring turnip crops are vigorously attacked by various flea beetles (Altica oleracea, A. nigripes, A. nemorum), also known as ground fleas or ticks. They can be controlled by spreading soot, lime, wood ash or horse dung on young plants, but the best way is to water abundantly whenever possible. The roots are attacked by the Anthomye larva, which burrows into them, making the turnip scabby as a result of the resulting blisters on the surface. Attacks by these larvae only occur in dry or insufficiently watered soils. The onion (Allium cepa, Linnaeus) is one of the oldest cultivated vegetable plants, making the search for its origin as difficult as it is imprecise. However, according to linguistic documents and the herborizations of exploratory botanists, its country of origin seems to be central Asia. Onion is a perennial plant, but cultivated as a biennial. Its vegetative organs consist of a bulb with a low-volume tray, bearing adventitious, white, unbranched roots; it is surrounded by fleshy tunics that represent the base of the leaves, i.e. their sheaths. The outermost sheaths terminate in an aerial, listular blade, elongated to a mossy point. These leaves are few in number; in the spring of the second year, a vigorous stem rises from their center, hollow throughout and strongly swollen towards the lower third of its length. The stem ends in a voluminous inflorescence of umbels of uniparous cymes, surrounded in their youth by a membranous spathe. The flowers are greenish, liliaceous, and their ovaries give rise, after fertilization, to trigonal capsules containing angular black seeds. He DYBONSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 23 not infrequently, the stems bear buds instead of flowers, which develop into bulbils; this transformation, accidental in most varieties, becomes the rule in the Rocambole onion. Uses. - Onion has been used since antiquity. Pliny reports that one variety was grown in Egypt, and was so highly esteemed that it was paid homage to as if it were a deity, much to the amusement of the Romans. The onion is considered above all as a condiment; it is so widely used that it is combined in most dishes. Varieties. - This plant, like all those widely cultivated, has given rise to a considerable number of varieties, most of which are landraces, reproducing faithfully by sowing. Variations have occurred in bulb color, shape and size, so that onions can be divided into two categories, one characterized by white bulbs, the other by colored bulbs, and which in common practice correspond to two different eras and cultivation methods, although there is nothing absolute in this respect. It is certain that most varieties would lend themselves to both combinations; the cultivation of Niort onions, as we shall see later, supports our assertion. OGNON BLANC HATIF DE PARIS. - Characterized by a silvery-white, depressed bulb with a diameter of Om, 05 to Om, 07 (fig. 79). This is the variety most commonly grown by Paris market gardeners, who produce it fresh and half-developed for consumption. This variety, which is very similar to those grown in southern Italy, seems to be a modification adapted to our climate. LARGE WHITE ONION. - This variety is characterized by the greater development of its bulbs, which are less flattened and less regular in shape. Fig. 79. - Early white onion from Paris. Fig. 80 - Yellow onion from Vertus. grown concurrently with the preceding variety, but less early. OGNON JAUNE DES VERTUS. - The bulb of this Onion reaches about Om, 08 in diameter. It is covered with dry, coppery-yellow husks. This variety is the most widely grown around Paris. Huge quantities are produced, supplying a significant part of Europe. It is certainly one of the varieties that lends the most support to the export trade (fig. 80). MULHOUSE ONION. - This variety resembles the previous one, but is slightly redder in color. It is the subject of a special trade. OGNON ROUGE PALE DE NIORT. - Widely grown in the Vendée and Poitou regions, the Niort onion is pale pink in color with a slight purplish hue; its diameter is about Om 08. Growing white onions. - White onions should be grown in all vegetable gardens, as they produce new produce in spring, whereas preserved onions begin to decline significantly in quality. Market gardeners in Paris cultivate white onions extensively; it's a crop that often occupies the most land in their marshes during the winter. Market gardeners who have exceptionally good, rich soil, which is healthy during the winter, sow white onions very early, from the beginning of August. On the contrary, when grown in the compact soil of country kitchen gardens or fields, they are only sown in the course of the same month. Experience has shown that onions sown early, and which therefore develop strongly before winter, are infinitely less resistant to the cold than those which, at this time of year, are still small. Whatever the sowing time, which will vary, as we have just said, according to the quality of the soil, sowing should be done broadcast and in a nursery. For sowing, the soil must be well prepared by ploughing and raking to level the surface and remove any stones. Once the seed has been entrusted to the soil, we lightly bury it with a rake, then spread a thin layer of highly decomposed mulch on the surface. The seedling sown in the nursery is intended for transplanting. In healthy soils, particularly in the marshes of Paris, transplanting is always done in autumn, from the end of September, to give the plant time to recover before the onset of heavy frosts. In damp, compact soils, it is preferable to wait until the end of February, or even the beginning of March in prolonged winters. Whatever the chosen time of year, the land to be planted with Onions should be well loosened by ploughing in the fertilizer needed for the crop. Fresh manure should not be used, as it will rot the onions. Sludge and manure from old layers are very suitable for this crop. The smoked and ploughed soil is trodden, either by foot or with a roller in large-scale cultivation, then harrowed with a rake to obtain a surface absolutely free of unevenness. On the prepared soil, lines are drawn at intervals of Om, 08 to Om, 10, along which the Ognon is transplanted with the planter, keeping the same distance between each line. Before transplanting, the plant must be prepared; this involves carefully pulling it out of the nursery, eliminating any plants that are too small. The uprooted plant is placed in the hand so that all the roots are on the same side; then, using a serpette, the roots are cut off about a third of their length, and the leaves are removed in the same way. The purpose and effect of this operation is to make the roots multiply and, by removing part of the leaves, to prevent too much evaporation, thus facilitating the plant's recovery. After transplanting, there is no need to give the plant any care, apart from a few waterings if the season is dry. Harvesting the produce. - White onions are rarely allowed to reach full development. More often than not, it is eaten while the bulbs are still young, with all their leaves on; it is sold under the name of Ognon nouveau. In this form, harvesting begins at the end of April for market gardeners who plant early in the autumn, and continues until June. Market gardeners gather the young onions into bunches or handfuls of six or seven, depending on size; three handfuls make up a bunch, the price of which, at the start of the season, is O fr. 35 to O fr. 50. In the vegetable garden or in the field, everything that is not harvested green is left in the soil until it is fully ripe, which happens towards the end of June. Once the leaves have just about dried out, the onions are torn off, left to dry out in the sun for a day or two, and then stored in a dry place with little light, to prevent them from turning green. These onions are easy to store for most of the summer, and make it possible to wait patiently for the harvest of colored onions. Growing colored onions. - We have said that this separation into white and colored onions corresponds to two different growing methods in the Paris climate. There are many exceptions to this delimitation, especially when, instead of looking at a given locality, we consider the cultivation of onions from the most general point of view. For example, the pale red Niort onion, widely grown in Poitou, Vendée and Brittany, is sown in autumn. It's true that the privileged climate of the regions where this crop is grown lends itself very well to this type of cultivation and that, thanks to the mildness of the winters, there's no need to fear the destruction of the plant by the cold. This type of cultivation can therefore be compared to that of the Ognon Blanc in the Paris climate, with the difference that transplanting only takes place in spring, around March. This is the same way to obtain the large onions produced in the Midi region. In the climate of Paris and throughout northern France, colored onions are grown in a different way, which needs to be described. These onions are harvested at the end of winter. Once the hard frosts have passed, and as soon as work in the fields begins again, the land for this crop is prepared. In the vegetable garden, plowing is done with a spade; in the fields, with a plow. This ploughing is used to bury a manure of completely decomposed fertilizer; it's a mistake to believe that beautiful onions can be obtained from unsmoked soil; on the other hand, the use of fresh manure should be avoided, as it could cause the bulbs to rot. Once the soil has been ploughed, it must be harrowed and rolled several times, to ensure that it is very crumbly and free of all kinds of roughness. The soil must therefore be absolutely well prepared, whether you're working in the vegetable garden or in the fields. Colored onions are usually sown directly into the soil, using a broadcast sowing technique. Transplanting is rarely used for this crop, especially when it is grown in large quantities in the field. It is essential to sow as evenly as possible, so as to obtain plants that are evenly spaced on the ground, and bulbs of uniform size. Around 100 grams are sown per are, followed by light harrowing; the soil is then rolled, or in the kitchen garden, packed evenly, either by trampling with hooves, or by beating with a board pricked on the end of the tines of a fork (fig. 81). l'el is the procedure generally followed, particularly in the Epône and Mézières areas; but it would be to the grower's advantage to modify it in the following way. The seed should be sown in rows with a seed drill, on perfectly prepared ground; then harrowed in the same direction as the seed drill. The result will be that the harrow teeth, passing through the radii of the seeder, will spread the seed while leaving it more or less in line. In this way, the Ognon will be able to occupy small strips on which it will be fairly evenly spaced. The great advantage of this method is that it's easy to weed. The biggest problem in growing large onions is weeding, which in the usual method has to be done entirely by hand, whereas when sowing in rows spaced by harrowing, weeding can be done with a hoe, provided that Om.25 is left between the rows; the work is thus considerably reduced. Ognon rises after fifteen to eighteen days, appearing above the ground with its single cotyledonary leaf curved in the shape of a small whip. It is left to grow freely for about a month, with only two or three waterings in the vegetable garden, if the soil is dry. After this time, in foggy weather if possible, the plants are thinned out with the utmost care, leaving a distance of at least Om,06 to Ou,08 between each plant in all directions. At the same time, Ton performs an initial weeding operation to remove any weeds that may have emerged along with the Onion. If emergence has been very uneven, it's a good idea to take advantage of the seedlings uprooted in the tightest areas to replant the empty spaces. Throughout the summer, the plantation will be kept in a good state of cleanliness by weeding, which in field cultivation is done by women by day. When, towards the end of the summer, the weather turns rainy, Ognon's vegetation becomes excessively prolonged: it is to be feared that the bulbs will not ripen properly and that conservation will therefore become difficult. Harvesting can be hastened by raking back any leaves that are still green, either with the back of a rake in a small crop, or with an excessively light roller, or even better, an empty barrel rolled across the field; these are broken at the point of insertion on the bulb and dry out quickly. From this point on, the crop can be harvested. Harvesting and storage. - Towards the end of August or in the course of September, the Ognon leaves dry out and the bulbs are ready to be harvested. If the soil is hard, you can pull them out with a rake. As they are pulled up, the onions are laid on the ground in long trails. If the weather is fine - and if possible, we should choose it for the harvest - we leave the bulbs to dry out on the ground for two or three days, after which they should be brought in. In many localities, particularly in the Midi and Centre regions, dry leaves are gathered together to form bundles, or by braiding the leaves together, into long rosaries, which are then hung in attics. Around Paris, on the other hand, we cut off the adherent leaves, and put the onions in a dry place, a room or an attic, to spread them out in thin layers on the threshing floor. On condition that the room chosen 2 auai Fig. 82. - Storage of onions in wheels. is dry, storage can last until May. Onions have little fear of the cold, and bulbs kept at a temperature of 3 or 4° below zero lose none of their quality. It is important not to remove the dry skins that cover the bulbs, as their presence contributes to long conservation. In the Paris area, where onions are widely grown, both to supply the city's markets and for export to neighboring countries, notably England, they are preserved so that they can be sold until spring. Ognons are preserved by placing them in millstones. These are established by laying a floor on the ground, supported by crossbeams. The sides are made of wattle and daub. These millstones are 1.20 m wide and 50 m high. To prevent the ognons from overheating, bunches of rapeseed stalks are placed here and there, forming a sort of ventilation chimney; in addition, the sides are louvered, giving free access to the air. The millstone ends in a thatched roof (fig. 82). Yield and cultivation costs. - In the large-scale cultivation around Aubervilliers and Noisy-le-Sec, production costs are generally quite low, for the reason that, as we have seen, once sown, onion is left to its own devices, so to speak; the soil is simply weeded twice to remove weeds. Here are the production costs in these areas: Soil rent....................... ..... 350 fr. Plowing and harrowing..................... 100 Manure: 25,000 kilos at 8 fr. per 1,000 kilos. 200 Seeds, 20 kilos at 6 fr.................. Two hoeings at 45 fr..................... Harvest: 20 days at 3 fr.............. 120 90 60 Total................... 920 fr. In Normandy, in the vicinity of Caen and more specifically around Luc, the cultivation of ()gnon is very extensive. The soil is simply smoked with kelp, and the Ognons pâles des Vertus grown there reach a very considerable volume under these conditions. Weeding costs are low, as there is very little weed growth on the beaches where the onion is sown. Yields vary greatly from year to year, fluctuating between 15,000 and 25,000 kilograms per hectare; but in years of great abundance, such as 1884, yields have been as high as 35,000 kilograms on good-quality land. They are sold either by the hundred kilog. or by the bag, which contains 25 kilog. and Fig. 83. - Onion bag. which has a particular shape; it is long and narrow, so that with a diameter of Om, 35 it has a length of 1m, 20 (fig. 83). The price per 100 kilog. varies, depending on the year, between 12 and 18 fr. for Ognons tout venants. Assuming a yield of 20,000 kilog. and an average price of 15 francs, we obtain a gross sum equal to 3,000 francs. There is a completely different method of cultivation which gives excellent results in many circumstances. In April or May, onions are sown very close together, on a broadcast basis. As a result, the bulbs hardly develop at all, and most remain the size of a hazelnut. As soon as they are dry, we pull them out and preserve them using the same procedures as those described above. In the spring of the following year, these onions are used as seedlings. They are planted one by one from Om to 10 in all directions. They produce very large crops in autumn. In this form, the bulbs are called Ognons grelot. They are sifted to separate the smaller ones, which are preferred. They are sold at 110 to 120 francs per 100 kilograms, which represents around 200 liters. Cultivation for seed. - If you want to grow (gnons) for seed, whatever their variety, they should be kept in a well-ventilated attic to prevent them from growing too early. In February or March, they are planted at about Om, 25 in all directions, taking care to use only the best made and best preserved; when, after flowering, the capsules begin to open, the inflorescences are cut off with a portion of the stem that bears them, and put into bunches to hang in an attic. Don't harvest them all at once, but only as they ripen. With Onion, the youngest seed is the best, but in any case their germination capacity does not exceed two years. If you have to use seeds whose age you don't know exactly, it's a good idea to try them out beforehand, by counting a certain number of seeds and germinating them in a flowerpot placed on a bed. This will reveal the proportion of seeds that will germinate, and consequently the quantity that should be sown on a given surface. Diseases and pests. - Onions are prone to rot, perhaps caused by a hitherto unknown parasitic disease. This rot, known in practice as grease rot, occurs especially when this crop is frequently grown on the same terram. It also occurs in rainy summers, when the affected plants are not scattered here and there, but are usually found in groups, forming spots in the crop. This plant is attacked by the larvae of the small chafer (Anisoplia horticola) and by those of the moth (Entomya caeparum), which attack the bulbs; there's nothing you can do to protect yourself from these insects. gi Sorrel (Rumex acetosa, Linnaeus) is a plant native to Europe and northern Asia, where it is very commonly found in woods. It is perennial with a short rhizome and taproots. Its sagittate leaves have pinnate venation and form a rosette of leaves on the ground, from the center of which rise flowering branches. Flowers are sometimes hermaphroditic, sometimes unisexual. They give rise to triangular pyramid-shaped fruits, surrounded by a persistent calyx. These fruits (achenes), commonly known as seeds, are black and shiny. The seeds retain their germinative capacity for three years. Uses. - Sorrel leaves, which contain oxalic acid in combination with lime, have a sour flavor that makes them highly prized in a variety of dishes. Varieties. - Through cultivation, we have succeeded in obtaining very broad leaves, providing a high yield. The two most recommended varieties are the following: BELLEVILLE SORREL. - Characterized by very broad leaves with a blistered surface, this is the variety most commonly grown around Paris (fig. 84). VIRGIN SORREL. - This variety is so named because of its ability to flower very little, resulting in very high yields. Some authors elevate this variety to the rank of a species, making it Rumex artfolius. Cultivation. - Sorrel can be propagated either from seed, or by dividing clumps; the latter method is usually used only to propagate virgin sorrel. To grow well, it requires deep, substantial soil; cool, shady sites produce abundant harvests in summer, but growers around Paris, who are especially keen to get a high yield in winter, when this commodity is at its most expensive, sow it on warm, south-facing slopes. In this way, they are able to supply the markets throughout the bad season, without the help of any shelter. DYBONSKI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 24 In vegetable gardens, sorrel is often sown or planted along the edges; this is not a very advisable practice, as it is impossible to give it the watering it requires during the summer; moreover, the leaves that come to rest on the sand of the paths always carry with them a few grains, which are very difficult to get rid of during cooking: growers, on the other hand, prefer to sow in rows, which facilitates the hoeing that needs to be done during vegetation. Sowing is usually done in spring, in March and April, but can be extended throughout the summer in the vegetable garden, with watering to facilitate emergence. If sowing in rows, leave a distance of Om.25 between rows, and a rake or harrow is all that's needed to bury the seed. After emergence, hoe to remove weeds. The first harvest takes place only two and a half to three months after sowing; but sorrel grows fast and harvests follow one another at short intervals. (Mn can be harvested by cutting all the leaves at once with a knife; but market gardeners rightly prefer to pick the leaves one by one, so as to spare those in the middle and obtain a higher yield. In large-scale cultivation, harvesting is done with a knife during the summer, and leaf by leaf during the winter, when this vegetable sells at a high price. In summer, one acre yields 250 kilograms of leaves per cutting; in winter, this yield is reduced by two-thirds, often even more. In summer, these leaves are sold wholesale for 10 francs per 100 kilog. At this time, they can be cooked and stored in this state in stoneware vases for most of the winter. By cooking, one kilog. of sorrel leaves is reduced to Okg, 360. Forced cultivation. - Until a few years ago, market gardeners used to grow a lot of early sorrel. This crop is now much less important, since, as we have just said, sorrel grown outdoors can be harvested during the winter in sheltered locations. In the countryside and in farm kitchen gardens, it may be worthwhile to practice this early production method, which combines great simplicity of operation with high yield. To get sorrel through the winter, simply pull up the old clumps and plant them in a warm bed. They produce two or three harvests of leaves, after which they need to be replaced by new roots, as the first ones are exhausted. Another method, which gives less rapid results but a more prolonged harvest, consists of sowing sorrel in summer in plots Im, 30 wide, and leaving paths of Om, 50 between these plots that can be occupied by other crops. When December comes, we place boxes on the sorrel planks and cover them with frames; we then dig the paths to Om, 30 deep and lay down a layer of f'umier that we reshuffle from time to time, in order to maintain a temperature of around 15 degrees in the boxes. With this forcing system, a continuous harvest can be obtained throughout the winter. Seed carriers. - Choose the plants with the thickest leaves for seed. It is harvested when the fruit husks begin to dry out, which takes place in July. Ripening is completed by spreading the inflorescences out on a cloth; when these are completely dry, the seeds are separated by gentle threshing. Insect pests. - The leaves of sorrel plants are attacked by the larvae of the sorrel fly (Pegomya acetosæ) and a leaf beetle (Gastrophysa raphani), which wreak havoc. Syringing with water containing one percent dissolved potassium sulfocarbonate completely rid sorrel of its enemies. Parsnip (Pastinaca sativa, Linnaeus) is a plant native to middle and southern Europe, where it is commonly found in the wild. The edible part is a taproot, long and slender in the wild, shortened and swollen in cultivated varieties. The leaves are sheathing and compound-pinnate. Around May, a sturdy branch rises from their center, fluted at the surface and bearing yellow, greenish flowers in compound umbels. These are followed by fruits that are winged achenes, due to the exaggerated development of the marginal ribs. They are used as seeds, but only retain their germinative capacity for two years. Uses. - The roots are eaten either as a condiment in soups, or as a dish when still young. Varieties. - Without mentioning the long parsnip, which is only a slight modification of the wild type, and which is hardly ever used in vegetable growing, we shall mention two varieties that are worth cultivating, as follows: GUERNSEY LONG PARSNIP. - Root about three times longer than large, smooth and slightly swollen. A high-yielding variety, but of inferior quality to the following (fig. 85). PAnais RoND. - This is the most common variety grown in vegetable gardens, and is earlier than the previous one. Fig. 85. - Guernsey long parsnip. Fig. 86. - Round parsnip. Its root is wider than it is long, with longitudinal furrows (Fig. 86). Cultivation. - Parsnips are undemanding when it comes to choice of soil; they do well in any soil that has been suitably cleared and abundantly supplied with decomposed fertilizer. Sowing is done either broadcast or in rows, which is more convenient for covering the seed properly. Rows are spaced by Om, 30 and sown at a rate of 30 grams per acre. Broadcast sowing requires about 50 grams for the same surface area. When the seed has been sown with good quality seed, it is useful to thin out to leave a distance of at least Om, 15 between the plants. Sowing can be done from February to July, the first ones having to provide their products before winter, the last ones on the contrary only coming to fruition the following spring. In the south of France, we sow in September and October to harvest the following year. Cultivation care is minimal, consisting of weeding and watering when the summer is too dry; when the season is rainy, parsnips do not need to be watered while they are growing. Parsnips do not fear frost, so they can be left in the ground over winter. However, digging them up is difficult when the ground is frozen, so it's a good idea to dig up at least part of them and put them in the cellar, where they keep very well. Harvesting is done with a flat-tined fork, and yields around 350 kilograms per acre. Very large quantities are produced in the Plame des Vertus, and sold at the Halles de Paris. To obtain seeds, in March we select the best-formed parsnips, either from among those kept in the cellar, or from among those left in place and belonging to the last sowing. In all cases, replant, keeping a distance of om 40 between them in all directions. As soon as the fruit begins to ripen, which happens around August, the stalks carrying them are cut off and stored in a shed, where they mature. c -. 4 The Patate or Batate (Batates edulis, Choisy) is a perennial plant that has been cultivated since ancient times throughout the intertropical zone, particularly in South America, where it is thought to originate, although it is not known for certain. Some authors have assumed an Asian origin, based on the fact that its cultivation has been practised by the Chinese and Japanese since ancient times; but, according to Dr Bretschneider, the first Chinese description dates only from the third century of the Christian era. Its cultivation was known in Spain as early as the middle of the sixteenth century; however, for a very long time it remained virtually unknown in France, where it only began to spread at the beginning of this century. The potato has creeping stems and alternate, cordate, acuminate leaves. The flowers are campanulate, but are never seen in the open air in the Paris climate. The plant produces fleshy roots, varying in size and color from one variety to another. These false tubers contain, in association with starch, non-crystallizable sugar which gives the flesh a sweet taste, more or less pronounced depending on the variety. Here, according to M. Poileux, is an analysis of the tubers of the Malaga Pink Potato: Water.................................... Starch................................. . Sugar.......................... ..... . .. .. Albuminoids.................... Fat.......................... Cellulose................................. Organic matter, pectics, salts and sllice....... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ..." 68.60 14.60 9.90 1.85 0.35 0.40- 4.30 100.00 - From the figures provided by this analysis, we can see that the potato is a vegetable of undeniable nutritional value. Uses. - The potato is eaten in the same way as potatoes, but its sweet, chestnut-like flesh makes it very popular with some people, while disliking many others. In the tropics and the hot zone, the potato is an integral part of the common diet. It is also widely used in confectionery. Varieties. - A very large number of varieties are grown in hot climates; each region can be said to have its own varieties, so we won't even attempt to describe them, contenting ourselves with indicating the main ones among those grown here. MALAGA PINK POTATO. - The fleshy roots of this variety are elongated, the skin is grayish pink and the yellow flesh is of very good quality (fig. 87). MALAGA YELLOW POTATO. - The roots are long and small in diameter, but the flesh is very fine. Unfortunately, this is a late variety and therefore does not adapt well to cultivation in the Paris climate. PATATE IGnaMe. - A variety remarkable for the strong development of its edible parts, which Fig. 87. - Malaga pink potato. weighing up to 4 kg. The skin is dirty white. Flesh of average quality. MINIME POTATO. - Whitish variety, with elongated tuberous roots, small in volume, but very numerous on the same plant, and therefore very high yielding. Flesh is sweet and slightly watery. Grown in the Paris climate. - In practice, the potato is propagated almost exclusively by cuttings. When tubers are available, the most common method is as follows. During the month of March, a warm bed is built, giving about 20 degrees of heat. The layer is covered with chests and frames, and when the heat has passed, the potato tubers are buried in potting soil, about Om, 15 high, placed on top of the manure, and covered with Om, 05 of potting soil. A few days after this operation, the tubers emit young shoots which soon break through the mould and fill the space left free under the frame. At this point, i.e. around the middle of April, we build another layer, on which we place cups of Om, 10 in diameter; we fill them with potting soil, and in each cup we transplant a cutting taken from the potato shoots. These cuttings are Om, 10 to Om, 12 long shoots from which we remove the leaves near the point of section, leaving only two or three at the top. Cuttings transplanted in this way take root after a few days, provided they are watered and the frames are hermetically sealed and covered overnight with straw mats. When new leaves appear, you can be sure that the shoots have taken root. Then, gradually, you can accustom the young plants to withstanding the action of the outside air, by raising the frames on racks during the hottest hours of the day. It has often been suggested that, instead of simply cutting twigs to make cuttings, cuttings should be taken from the tuber to which they are attached. This procedure offers no advantage, as the cuttings always root with the greatest ease, and it has the serious drawback of exhausting the tubers, which will then provide a much smaller number of cuttings, which is a disadvantage, as they continue to be cut and rooted until the middle of May. In the course of May, and up to the beginning of June, depending on whether the cuttings were taken earlier or later, we prepare the site in the vegetable garden that is to be occupied by this crop. This preparation consists in opening trenches in the ground, 1.50 m apart, which are Om.40 deep and Om.50 wide. The soil that is removed is discarded on both sides. The trench is filled with either old horse manure or new manure mixed with leaves. After treading and watering, the trench is covered with the soil removed from the trench, to which topsoil is added. When, after a few days, the dull layer thus constructed begins to warm up, a row of potato cuttings raised in cups and accustomed to the air, as we have said, is planted in its middle. These cuttings are planted at Om, 75 one from the other, then after watering them, they are covered with a bell, which is lifted a little during the day to remove it completely when frosts are no longer to be feared. This type of planting can be continued into June, a period which should not be exceeded, as otherwise only an unintegrated product will be obtained. As you can see, the time of planting can vary, but the most important factor is the condition of the cuttings: don't leave them in the cups for too long, as the tuberous roots will then run around the sides of the flowerpot, and the resulting tubers will be defective. If you want to grow early potatoes, you do the same thing in every respect, with the only difference being that you start earlier, and instead of planting on a dull bed, you plant on a warm bed under a frame. In any case, maintenance care is minimal, consisting of weeding if weeds develop, and sparing but abundant watering if the summer is dry. In all cases, watering should cease from August onwards, to give the tubers time to ripen. The first plantings can be harvested in August for direct consumption, but the entire crop should not be harvested until the first white frosts have reached the leaves. The tubers are then carefully removed to avoid injury, which would lead to rotting, and left to dry out for a while in a sheltered spot. After this, they should be placed in a room where the temperature does not fall above 5 degrees Celsius, and which is free from humidity. One of the best methods is to bury them in well-dried sand, ashes or sawdust. In all cases, preservation is difficult, and it's a good idea, especially when the tubers are not quite ripe, to hurry up and deliver them for consumption, if you don't want to see a certain number of them rot. Only as much as is needed for planting should be kept. To avoid having to preserve tubers for planting, which often presents difficulties, we use another method, which has given us the best results. At the time of uprooting, we take a certain number of old Potato plants; we prune all the branches, keeping only a length of about Om, 10 and we do the same to the roots; after which, we repot each plant in a pot of Om, 14 in diameter, we wet them, and we put them in a temperate greenhouse. The plants begin to grow again; as soon as they have put out new leaves, and consequently new roots, they can be placed in a greenhouse at a lower temperature, or even simply under a frame. In spring, the branches are cut to make cuttings, and the old plants can also be used as seedlings. Cultivation in Spain and Algeria. - In southern Spain, Algeria and Tunisia, potatoes are grown on a large scale, with excellent results. We are indebted to Mr. Poileux for the following information on several hectares of production in Spain, in the province of Malaga, where potatoes are grown in the same way as in southern Spain and Algeria. In all these regions, which benefit from high temperatures, the potato can survive the winter in the open ground; so, when the produce is uprooted at the start of winter, the old plants are kept and replanted after the branches have been shortened, in a sheltered spot along a wall, if possible. These plants recover well, and when the time comes, it will be easy to cut the cuttings needed for planting. If these old plants are lacking, or if a harsh winter has destroyed them, we can resort to tubers, which we put in the ground in January, in order to cut the shoots as cuttings. Finally, if these two resources are lacking, we are reduced to buying cuttings in the spring from a more fortunate neighbor, or having them brought in from a more southerly region. These cuttings are sold per 109 kilograms at 30 francs per kilogram. One kilogram of cuttings yields an average of 80 to 100. From March or April onwards, the ground is prepared for planting the potatoes. The soil must be heavily fertilized, using around 25,000 kilograms of farmyard manure. The manure is ploughed in, then the soil is laid out in ridges, which are made either with a plough or, better and more simply, with a ridging machine; they are given a width of around Om, 80 and kept very rounded. As soon as the soil is prepared, planting can begin, but can continue until July without any noticeable reduction in yield. For planting, cuttings are given a length of Om, 20 or so; they are placed in the ground without needing to undergo any preparation. The cuttings are planted at Om, 80 and a line is drawn across the top of each ridge. As soon as planting is complete, the cuttings are irrigated heavily; after a few days, they start to grow again. Throughout the summer, we irrigate twice a month; this operation is carried out by a man who can treat two hectares in a day. Under the influence of the watering, the potato branches soon develop and cover the soil completely. When they are uprooted, they can be used as fodder for cattle, which readily eat them. Harvesting takes place in November and December, using a two-pronged hook in the same way as for potatoes. Yields vary according to the variety used. The most productive is the Patate minime, which can often yield 20,000 kilograms per hectare. Unfortunately, its tubers are not very floury, so they are not very popular. Pink Malaga or white Castilian potatoes, which are widely used for large-scale cultivation, yield 12 to 13,000 kilograms per hectare. Throughout the southern region, as well as in Algeria, the potato is a staple food. In Spain, its regular selling price is 13 to 18 franes per 100 kilograms. However, they are beginning to be consumed in central France, particularly in Paris, and there may be some advantage in importing them. In Paris, the average selling price is 50 francs per 100 kilograms. Assuming a maximum price of 18 francs, and adding the cost of transport, which is 8 francs from Malaga to Paris, we can have potatoes that cost 26 francs per 100 kilograms when delivered. Here is the estimated cost of cultivation for one hectare: Rent for irrigable land............... 25,000 kilograms of manure at 18 francs per 1,000 kilograms. Harvesting............................... 150 fr. 250 100 46 50 20 12 50 Total expenses........... 628 fr. 50 As for yield, if we sell the produce on site, it is equal to 12,000 kilogr. at an average price of 15, or 1,800 francs. This yield would double if, instead of having the products consumed locally, they were shipped to Paris or any other major city. Potato cultivation provides real services in all our colonies in the warm region, with even less care than in Spain; without irrigation, it provides high yields and to some extent replaces the potato, which does not do well there. ...: 1 :". 216; 11 . D 1. ... 11L " Ma:. 25 " pl-opine pigtaia .. DYBOWSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. .  D. F.. 1 . . . . . : "2 25 .3. Parsley (Petroselinum sativum Monch.) grows wild throughout southern Europe. Its cultivation is relatively recent, and in ancient times parsley was known only as an officinal plant. Parsley has several compound leaves with deeply indented blades. From the center of these leaves, which form a rosette on the ground, a branch grows in the spring of the second year, bearing numerous compound umbels of greenish-yellow flowers, followed by small fruits (diakenes), flattened on one side and raised by five longitudinal ribs. They contain a single seed, which retains its germinative capacity for only two to three years. Parsley roots are taprooted, and cultivation has created varieties in which this organ develops strongly and becomes fleshy. Uses. - Parsley leaves are used as a condiment, either raw or cooked. The raw leaves of curly varieties are used to garnish dishes. The large-rooted varieties are used to flavor soups, as a substitute for parsnips, which are preferred in many places, particularly in northern Europe. Varieties. - In addition to the primitive type, which is still widely grown in gardens, we cultivate varieties with curly leaves that are much more ornamental than common parsley. It has been said that Parsley can easily be confused with a poisonous plant, Little Hemlock (Æthusa cynapium L.), but here no more than with Chervil, with which it has also been claimed that confusion is possible, we do not believe that the error is feasible, provided that we take the U * amo FV 3 N Fig. 88. - Common parsley. the slightest attention, or by invoking the sense of smell, which will be unpleasantly impressed by the nauseating odor of hemlock. COMMON PARSLEY. - Common parsley, grown in gardens, is very similar to the type, but cultivation has only increased the size of the leaves, which retain the same appearance as in their primitive state (fig. 88). CURLED PARSLEY. - The leaves of this variety are divided and the blades are curly, giving this plant a very special and elegant appearance, which makes it resemble certain cultivated ferns. Several sub-varieties have been formed, depending on the degree of cleavage of the leaf blade; all are equally recommendable and should be given preference over the common variety (fig. 89). PARSLEY WITH LARGE ROOTS. - The leaves of this variety resemble those of common parsley, but the Fig. 89. - Curly parsley. roots develop sufficiently to resemble small parsnips. They can reach Om, 04 in diameter by Om, 18 in length. Cultivation. - Parsley is sown either broadcast as market gardeners do, or in rows following the method used in large-scale cultivation. When sown in rows, leave a distance of about Om, 30 between rows. Sowing begins in February and continues through June and July, but the earliest sowing gives the best results. Parsley takes a very long time to germinate, so when you grow it in the kitchen garden, it's a good idea to hasten the germination of the seeds by frequent watering; despite this care, it's not uncommon for nothing to appear for a fortnight or more. In the vicinity of Chambourcy, where there are over fifty hectares of Persia in the commune alone, curly varieties are grown exclusively. Harvesting takes place three or four times a year in summer, but for the last few harvests in September and October, only the oldest leaves are removed, leaving all the best ones, so that they can be removed in winter, when they fetch a high price. This harvest must always be carried out by detaching the leaves one by one, so as not to damage the heart and thus increase the quantity of the product. Chambourcy's growers find the practice of this cultivation a great advantage, in that it enables them to make a good profit from the soil and keep their gardener boys busy, who would otherwise be idle for part of the winter. Parsley is sold in handfuls, made up of leaves arranged in bunches and tied with a sprig of rye straw. In summer, the price of these handfuls is very low, often no more than 0.75 francs a dozen; in winter, on the other hand, the price rises sharply, reaching up to 3 francs for the same quantity. Parsley seedlings must be renewed every year, otherwise the plant will flower in the second year and produce nothing. Parsley with a large root is grown in the same way as the one just mentioned, but the rows should be thinned out to leave a space of Om, 15 between the plants. If you want to harvest good seed, it is useful to transplant in February all the plants that seem to have the best qualities. When the fruit ripens, the stalks are cut off and placed in a shed for threshing. 1 . . . 1. ... . : . . ." 1 1 1. .. 1 D . . .. " . .. A . . . . . . 7 ?: AFTI D. ? . . . 1 .7. .. .. ?. ". G ie mo u de Chet. is related to Linné's Capsicum annuum, and appears to have originated in South America. Its cultivation is modern; ancient authors make no mention of it. This plant, perennial in its country of origin, is cultivated here as an annual. It is a small sub-shrub reaching Om, 50 to Om, 80 in height, with upright stems that are semi-woody at the base. It bears lanceolate, oblong leaves arranged in alternating order. The flowers have a white, rotate corolla; they are solitary and placed in the leaf axils. The fruits that follow them are berries of very different shapes, green when young and turning bright red or golden yellow when ripe. At this point, the fruit is hollow, and its flavor is sometimes strong and burning, sometimes sweet and aromatic. The seeds are discoid, yellowish and retain their germinative powers for four years. Uses. - Pimento fruits are used as seasoning; they can be used either green or ripe. It is customary to add a few to pickles in vinegar. Ground to a powder after drying, they make up red pepper or cayenne pepper, which is widely used in England and the French colonies. The sweet-fruited varieties are eaten raw or cooked, mainly in Spain.
