Varieties. - These are very numerous and can be divided into two categories: one comprising hot-flavored peppers, the other sweet-fruited. We will limit ourselves to mentioning one variety of chaFig. 90. - Long red pepper. Fig. 91. - Square sweet chilli pepper. cation of the two categories, all the others being not very far removed from these two types. LONG RED PEPPER. - This is the most common variety, with hanging fruits about Om 10 long, of a bright red color (fig. 90). SQUARE SWEET PEPPER. - A robust plant bearing large, obtuse fruits marked with four deep grooves. There are red-fruited and yellow-fruited sub-varieties (fig. 91). Grown in the Paris climate. - To bear fruit abundantly, Piments must be sown early, in the soil, otherwise they won't have time to ripen their fruit before the onset of autumn frosts, to which they are very sensitive. Seedlings are sown in February and March on a warm bed and under a frame; as soon as the plant has two or three leaves, it is transplanted into a nursery at Om, 10 in all directions, also under a frame. Leave it in the nursery until the beginning of May, when it should be planted out. If you want to harvest early, the best method is to build up a layer of old manure and leaves at this time, which will give off little heat. The plants are planted on this layer and under a frame, spaced out by Om, 60. The frames are only removed when frosts are no longer an issue, and when the temperature has risen so that the chillies can withstand the full force of the air; in our climate, this doesn't happen until the beginning of June. It goes without saying that, before completely removing the frames, the plants should have been accustomed to the action of the outside air, through progressive daily aeration. As soon as the plants have branched out, they should be staked to prevent the wind from blowing them over. To grow well, chillies require repeated watering, which should become frequent once the frames have been removed. This method of cultivation, which is sometimes applied to small-fruited chillies, is the only one that can be used to produce sweet chillies, which, without all these precautions, would not ripen in the Paris climate. For these varieties, it is often useful, if you want to obtain a large volume of fruit, to leave only a dozen per plant, and to prune off anything beyond this number. Small-fruited chillies can be grown more simply if you're not in a hurry to harvest them. In fact, plants that have been raised in trays can be planted in a sheltered spot in the open ground. If planted in rich soil and watered regularly, they will ripen in autumn. Growing in the Midi. - In the south of France, in Algeria and especially in the south of Spain, chillies are grown in abundance, as their fruit is widely used as a condiment or, in the case of sweet-fruited chillies, as a popular vegetable. Sown in trays, then transplanted in nurseries, chillies are planted in May in soils that can be frequently irrigated during the summer. Under these conditions, they produce abundant crops. Harvesting the seeds. - If you want to obtain good seeds, as soon as fruiting begins, select the plants bearing the best fruit, leaving them only four or five of the best, and removing all the others. When the fruit is brightly colored and ripe, open it and extract the seed, wash it and dry it in the shade before bagging. Dandelion, which grows spontaneously in most fresh meadows throughout France, has been cultivated into an improved form, with leaves that are larger and more numerous than in wild plants, forming voluminous clumps. Dandelions growing spontaneously belong to several botanical species, of which the one that appears to have provided the cultivated plant is Taraxacum officinale, Will. This plant is characterized by long, toothed, stalked leaves that form a small rosette on the ground, with several buds giving rise in spring to yellow flower heads borne on a hollow stem. The seeds (achenes) are oblong and flattened; they retain their germinative powers for only two years. Uses. - Dandelions are used in salads, either green or, more generally, after having been subjected to shrivelling. Varieties. - The wild plant has been cultivated to produce forms which differ from the primitive type in that the broader leaves, together with the more numerous buds, form a much fuller rosette than in the plant growing spontaneously. There are two main types, one known as hátif Improved Dandelion, the other as full-hearted Improved Dandelion (fig. 92); it is the latter that is most recommended for cultivation. Cultivation. - Dandelion is always propagated by sowing, but this can be done in two different ways, either in situ, or by transplanting the seedling. Both methods give good results. PS d-i5 taFig. 92. - Improved full-hearted dandelion. Sow in situ from March to the end of May. The soil must be well smoked if vigorous, high-yielding plants are to be obtained. The type of soil is of little importance, as Dandelions thrive everywhere, but moisture is necessary for them to thrive, which is why they thrive in soil containing clay. After ploughing, the soil is laid out in furrows Om.25 apart and Om.10 deep, at the bottom of which the seed is sown and covered with a little soil. Water frequently, which helps the seed to emerge after about ten days. If emergence has been good and the seedlings are too tightly packed, light them so that they are at Om 07 from each other. If transplanting is desired, seed is broadcast in the nursery, then replanted when the plant has four or five leaves. Planting is done in rows on which the plants are transplanted at Om, 10. From the onset of winter in the first year, in November-December, Dandelions can be covered to protect them from the action of light when they grow, which would cause them to turn green. When sowing in situ, simply fill in the furrows. If, on the other hand, transplanting is practised, the soil taken from the square or potting soil is used to fill in the furrows. In both cases, the dandelions need to be covered with Om, 10 to Qu, 12 of soil. Harvesting. - Dandelions can be harvested as early as the end of December, if the soil is covered early and the winter is mild. Harvesting lasts until March. You can either cut off the leaves alone, which ensures the plant's survival for two or three years, as it is a perennial, or, as is generally done, cut off the entire rosette of leaves at the plant's collar. In the latter case, it is necessary to sow seedlings every year. Dandelion can be treated in the same way as wild chicory. It then becomes a highly prized kind of capuchin beard. Harvesting seeds. - For seeds, choose the fullest, widest plants. Leave them in place, and take care to harvest the seeds as soon as they are ripe, otherwise they will be blown away by the wind. The Poireau or Porreau, which has long been considered a botanical species, is, says M. A. de Candolle, according to J. Gay's monograph, and the suspicion already conceived by earlier authors, only a form of a species growing spontaneously in the East and throughout the Mediterranean region. It should therefore be considered botanically as Linnaeus' Allium Ampeloprasum, variety Porrum. Leeks are grown as biennials. It is characterized by a bulb that is generally not very bulbous, extended in a sort of cylinder by the leaf sheaths arranged in distichous order. It is the bulb and the base of these seeds, whitened by lack of light, that are consumed in various forms. In the second year, a simple spike emerges from the center of the leaves, ending in an umbel of cymes, which is initially surrounded by a membranous spathe. The flowers are followed by three-celled capsule-shaped fruits containing flattened, black seeds. Uses. - Leeks are most often used as a condiment, in soups; however, in some areas, notably Alsace, they are eaten as variously seasoned dishes. Varieties. - There are quite a number of cultivated varieties; however, in current commercial cultivation, two in particular are used: POIREAU LONG DE PARIS. - This variety, the most widely used by market gardeners and growers around Paris, has the great advantage of having a foot, i.e. the part formed by the leaf sheaths, that is very long, about Om, 30, but with a diameter that is little more than Om, 02. The leaves are long and very glaucous (fig. 93). POIREAU COURT DE ROUEN. - The stalk is only Om, 15 to Om, 18 in length, but its size is Om, 05 to Om, 06 in diameter; it is not uncommon to see this diameter us even exceed this Fig. 93. - Poireau long de Paris. measure, notably for the sub-variety of P. monstrueux de Carentan (fig. 94). Sowing. - In localities where leeks are grown in large numbers, they are sown in nurseries from the end of February to the end of March. Care should be taken to loosen the soil by deep ploughing, and the seed should be broadcast fairly tightly, then covered with a light harrowing with a rake, and a mulch of decomposed manure. If spring is dry, water to facilitate emergence, which takes place after ten to twelve days. The plant, thus raised, Fig. 94. - Poireau court de Rouen. can be used as early as April; it must be the size of an ordinary pen pipe. Installation. Choice of soil. - Leeks like moderately compact, fresh soil, if possible, and in any case, plenty of decomposed fertilizer. It is therefore useful to fertilize the soil before planting. In the Paris region, around Epône, Falaise and Mézières, where this crop is grown on several hundred hectares, it is fertilized with either slurry or farmyard manure. In large-scale cultivation, the fertilizer is buried by ploughing; in gardens, this work is done with a spade. In all cases, the soil is then loosened by careful harrowing. Before transplanting, the plant must be prepared. This operation consists of uprooting it in the nursery, then sorting it, eliminating any plants whose dimensions are too small; on the other hand, those that are judged to be good are gathered by handfuls, and the roots are shortened by removing about a third of their length with a serpette. The leaves are cut in the same way. As the plant is prepared, it is placed in a heap and covered with a mat to prevent it drying out. For transplanting, in the crops grown at Epône, lines are drawn Om, 40 apart; then, on a foggy day, if possible, the Leeks are planted with a planter at Om, 08 to Om, 10 on the lines. Transplanting is a long and rather arduous task; it takes no less than forty man-days to plant one hectare. Transplanting begins in April and continues until June. During the summer, hoeing is necessary to keep the soil free of weeds, and also to loosen the soil and bring it back onto the leek rows, so as to create a sort of light ridge. This process produces leeks that are white over a long length. During the summer, two or three hoeing operations are carried out, depending on how clean the soil is. Some growers sow part of their leeks on a bed at the beginning of February, in order to produce early crops. This results in an earlier plant which, when transplanted in March, produces crops that can be harvested as early as September. Harvesting. - Harvesting begins as early as September for bed-raised plants; but for those that are DVBONSRI. - Cultivation, 2nd ed. 26 have been sown in the ground, it is not advisable to uproot them before October. Once uprooting has begun, it can continue throughout the winter, as and when sales require it, since Paris leeks are very hardy and frost-resistant. If the sale goes well, i.e. if prices are high, all the leeks are sold before March, at which time the rest of the leeks destined for sale are uprooted and placed in gauges. To do this, a slanting trench is opened with a spade, as far as possible along a north-facing wall or shelter, and the Leeks are placed pressed against each other; each row is covered with a small layer of earth, against which a new row of Leeks is placed, and so on, alternately, until everything is in place.

placed. This work could be done before winter, so as to clear the ground and have the leeks ready for sale. But growers don't practice this system, because if the vegetable doesn't sell well, they prefer to let it spend the hiyer on the spot, with a view to obtaining seed the following summer. Yield and cultivation costs. - Leeks are harvested and put into bunches. It is usually sold in bundles of thirteen bundles of three or four leeks, i.e. around fifty per bundle. The trend is to replace this sales method with one by the hundred, which has the advantage of being more precise. Prices vary widely, depending on size, season and year. The minimum price for Paris is 1.50 francs per hundred, but in good years this price can rise to 5 francs. In all cases, a price of 2 francs per cent for medium-sized leeks can easily be accepted as the current average. Assuming this price, it is easy to establish, on the one hand, the cultivation and selling costs, and on the other, the gross product per hectare, and consequently to deduce the net profit that such a crop is capable of producing in the Paris area. For one hectare, cultivation costs can be calculated as follows: Purchase of seed and production of plant. Land rent,......................... Manuring: 50 cubic meters at 5 fr...... . Plowing and harrowing...................... Transplanting: 40 days at 3 fr. 50......... Three hoeing sessions at 35 fr. each................ Uprooting and bundling: 20 days at 3 fr. 50....................... . ... ... Transport to the hall...................... 150 fr. 250 250 100 140 105 70 125 Selling expenses at 5%................. 310 - - - - - - 1500 francs. The gross product is equal to the sale of Leeks, which are transplanted at Om, 40 in one direction and at Om, 08 in the other, amounting to 312,000 for one hectare. At a price of 2 francs per cent, this gives a sum of 6,240 francs. If we subtract from this the sum of the expenses incurred, a net profit remains, which in these circumstances may be equal to 4,740 francs. Growing short leeks. - A distinguished farmer, Mr. Rabourdin, from Villacoublay, has been cultivating the Rouen short leek on a large scale for many years. By constantly choosing seed-bearing plants of great beauty, he has succeeded in obtaining plants of truly surprising size. Every year, at exhibitions, we can see the very fine products he brings, which frequently reach a diameter of Om, 08. Good cultivation and the richness of the soil in which they are grown certainly play a large part in this considerable development; but it's certain that the purity of the seed used and the choice of rootstocks also play an important role. To achieve this result, Leeks are sown on beds in February; when they have fully emerged, the frames are lifted to accustom them to the action of the outside air, to which they will be completely subjected by removing the frames as soon as, towards the end of March, their development is deemed sufficient. Towards the beginning of April, it is transplanted into heavily smoked, deeply tilled soil. Lon traces lines on the ground, using a spike drill, at intervals of Qu, 30 from each other, and it is in the bottom of the furrow thus formed, that the Leeks are transplanted at Om, 20 from each other. During the summer, hoeing is carried out to remove weeds and bring the soil over the leeks to fill in the furrows, and then, with the second hoeing, to make a slight ridge, which will lengthen the naturally short stalk of this breed of leek. The leeks grown by Mr. Rabourdin are very frost-resistant, so they can be delivered for consumption from autumn onwards, and harvested for almost the whole winter. Their selling price, because of the very considerable volume they are capable of acquiring, is always high and varies very little; it is 4 to 5 francs per cent. A hectare containing 166,000 of them can provide, by counting the cent at 4 francs, a gross product equal to 6,640 francs, from which it is necessary to subtract the cultivation costs, which are roughly equal to those of the system previously employed. Cultivation on the Vertus plain. - Leeks are grown in a very special way throughout the immense plain north of Paris, stretching from Pantin and Aubervilliers to beyond Noisyle-Sec. The process could not be simpler, and is worth mentioning, not because it produces high quality products, but because it is capable of producing a hidden harvest, the benefits of which are not to be scorned. Throughout this region, leeks are sown in spring as a broadcast crop in onion fields. The onion seed is mixed with a proportion varying from one-tenth to one-sixth of Paris long leek seeds. It benefits throughout the summer from the care given to the onion, and at the end of the summer, once the onions have been harvested, it remains the sole owner of the soil, which it continues to occupy throughout the autumn and a good part of the winter. Products obtained in this way are not of good quality, so their price is low, usually not exceeding 1 franc per cent. Cultivation on beds. - Market gardeners in Paris cultivate short leeks as early crops. They sow them in January, on beds, at a rate of 35 grams per frame. As soon as the weather permits, it is given air to prevent it from withering. At the beginning of March, it is transplanted, either on beds used to grow lettuces, or in pots along a wall; transplanting is done in potting soil, so we don't use a planter to go faster, but simply transplant with a finger. The plants are planted at Om, 04 in all directions, and care is taken to press them in deeply to give them a little more length. Immediately after planting, they are watered, and this watering should be repeated frequently during the vegetation period. If you have transplanted on a bed, the panels should be removed as soon as the plant is well established. Cultivated in this way, leeks can be harvested in June. Quite often, market gardeners grow this vegetable for a second season, sowing it in February and treating it in the same way. The produce is always of excellent quality, and is sold at a bargain price on the market, both for its beauty and for the time of year at which it is grown. Seed cultivation. - As we said when talking about the cultivation of Leeks in the Epône area, seed production is obtained by simply leaving in place all the plants that have not been uprooted for consumption. The result is seeds that can be harvested in August. This is the most common way to produce commercial seed, unless you leave an unused seedling sown in February in the nursery and plant it in July and August, when you have free land; this seedling also goes to seed in the spring of the following year. We do not recommend using these methods if you only want to obtain a small amount of seed for your own use. In this case, it is infinitely preferable to uproot the leeks in the spring, then to select and replant only those plants, leaving a space of Om, 40 between them in all directions, which show the highest level of the qualities sought after in the cultivated variety; it is only by this procedure that we will succeed in perfecting the breeds we cultivate. Harvesting is carried out successively, cutting only the inflorescences that bear ripe fruit, which can be recognized not by their color, which does not change appreciably, but by the fact that the first capsules to be formed open up, revealing the black seeds they contain. It's best to keep the seeds in the fruit, simply by gathering the inflorescences into bunches and hanging them in a dry place. It's a good idea to label them, as these seeds only retain their germinative powers for three years, and even then the youngest seeds are always the best. Insect pests. - The leek's most feared enemy is the alder moth, commonly known as the Guillot. It attacks the leaves, gnawing away and completely damaging them as it penetrates the seeds. There's only one way to get rid of it, and that's to cut the leeks flush with the ground and burn the leaves, which carry the moth larvae with them. If the Leeks are soaked with earth and then watered, they quickly regrow, and after a short time all traces of the damage have completely disappeared. "7 . C. .. POIRÉE Poirée, still known as Swiss chard, has the same origin as beet (Beta vulgaris, Linné). It grows wild in sands throughout the Mediterranean region. It differs from beet in that, whereas beetroot cultivation has produced voluminous, fleshy roots, the variation in beetroot has only been in the growth of the petioles, which have become very large. Their width often equals that of the open hand. Apart from this characteristic, all the others are identical to those we have indicated as belonging to Beets. Uses. - The petioles are eaten mainly, and when well prepared, make a pleasant dish. The whole leaves are also used in mixtures with sorrel to soften its acidity. Poirée leaves are still eaten like spinach. Varieties. - They differ in the color of the leaves, which are more or less tender green, and in the width of the petioles. COMMON PEAR. - This variety has abundant, yellowish-green foliage. The petioles are narrow, so this plant is used for its whole leaves, which are eaten like spinach. WHITE CHARD PEAR. - In this plant, the petioles or chards are very wide, and it is these that are eaten, with the exception of the leaf blade (fig. 95). Cultivation. - Pear cultivation is similar. Fig. 95. - Poirée à cardes blanches. much like beets, but with the difference that transplanting is not a problem here, since its influence is mainly felt only on root development, which is of little importance. Poirées can be sown as early as April or May, in the open ground, in rows spaced Om, 40 apart. When the plant is well established, light it with a hoe to leave the same distance between plants as between rows. To ensure good development, water during the summer. You can also sow this plant on a warm bed in April, then when the plant has four or five leaves, transplant it directly into place at the distance we've indicated. Under these conditions, repeated watering and mulching of the soil are essential for the plants to thrive. Poirées can be delivered for consumption as early as August and September, and the harvest lasts well into winter. The common variety is not very sensitive to cold, so it is usually not sheltered; the carded variety, being more sensitive, must be protected either by ridging and covering with leaves, or by transplanting the plants under a frame. Harvesting is carried out in two different ways, depending on whether the produce is to be consumed or sold. In the first case, the outermost leaves are removed successively, so that a single plant can be harvested for a very long time. If, on the other hand, the Poirées are to be sent to market, the whole plant is cut off and tied with a piece of straw. In some localities, however, the leaves are detached and sold in bunches. Seed production. - For seeds, we choose the plant that best presents itself in terms of purity of character, and store it under a frame over winter, to be ready for planting in spring. It will be useful to stake the plant when it flowers. - The Garden Pea (Pisum sativum, Linnaeus) has never been found in its natural state. Only ancient documents indicate its country of origin, which was probably western Asia, from where the plant gradually spread throughout the world through cultivation. But it must be admitted that these are more hypotheses than definite indications, as the Pea has nowhere been found in the wild. Peas are annual plants whose hollow, weak stems need support to keep them upright. They bear leaves arranged in an alternating order. The leaves are compound, pinnate, but only the first two or three pairs of leaflets have a normal blade; the others, on the contrary, as well as the terminal one, are reduced to the single main vein, which has the property of rolling up into a tendril. This feature enables it to bind itself to foreign bodies placed within its reach and to maintain the stem in a vertical position. The flowers, which in horticultural varieties are white, have a papilionaceous corolla and are arranged in a uniparous cyme reduced to two flowers. These are followed by green, succulent pods when young, which are dry and parchment-like when mature. They contain a variable number of seeds, usually between six and twelve, depending on the variety. These seeds are covered by envelopes, which sometimes lie tightly on the two cotyledons they cover, and sometimes, on the contrary, are larger than the surface of the cotyledons, wrinkling as the grain dries. Their shape, which is normally spherical, becomes more or less flattened on two sides when the seeds are numerous in the pod, as a result of their compression. Germination lasts for three years. Uses. - Peas are eaten in three different forms: as dried beans, stripped of their husks and known in the trade as split peas, they are used to make soups or purées. Fresh peas are used in a variety of dishes and are known as petits Pois. Lastly, certain varieties are used by employing both the seeds and the pod that contains them, which is tender and devoid of parchment; these are the mange-tout peas. Because of the starch and nitrogen they contain, Peas are a very nutritious food, especially when used dry. Varieties. - The very many varieties that this plant has provided for cultivation can be subdivided into two categories: shelling peas and snow peas. In each of these subdivisions, it is necessary to separate the dwarf varieties from those which, becoming very tall, must be supported and which for this reason are called oar varieties. i u i I. - peas to be seeded. A. - Rowed varieties. PRINCE-ALBERT PEA. - A variety reaching Om, 80, with few branches and spindly stems. It is not very productive, each inflorescence often giving only one pod containing six to seven seeds. Despite its low fertility, this variety is widely cultivated, as it produces very early crops. It is used as an early crop in southern France (fig. 96). Pois Michaux. - Several sub-varieties of this pea are grown, all of which are characterized by the vigor of the plant Fig. 96. - Prince-Albert pea. and its great hardiness. The plant's numerous pods contain seven to eight seeds. CLAMART pea. - A vigorous, fast-growing variety. It bears abundant pods containing eight to nine seeds. It is widely grown around Paris for green consumption (fig. 97). KNIGHT RIDED PEA. - This moderately productive pea is nevertheless an advantageous crop, because the seeds are very sweet and can be eaten even when they are already large. At maturity, the seeds become wrinkled. B. - Dwarf varieties. HATIF DWARF PEA. - This Pea needs stakes, as its stem is about Om, 50 high and does not support itself well. It is an early variety, good for spring cultivation in vegetable gardens. HOLLAND DWARF PEA. - It grows to no more than Om, 25, so can do without a stake; it is extremely early and therefore suitable for spring cultivation. Its yield is low, as each plant bears only about ten inflorescences. II. - SNOW PEAS. A. - Row varieties. RAM'S HORN PEA. - Fig. 97. - Clamart pea. This variety grows up to 50 cm high and produces abundantly. The pod is Om, 12 long, flattened and rounded at the tip. There are several other more or less distinct varieties, including the Giant Pea, which is characterized by pods up to Om, 15 long and containing eight to ten seeds. B. - Dwarf varieties. HATIF PEAS. - Plant of Om, 25 bearing numerous pods whose seeds are visible on the outside. The other varieties in this category are of little importance. Cultivation. - Peas thrive in all types of soil, provided they are both moist and rich in readily-assimilable fertilizers. Moisture is absolutely essential for them to thrive, so while they can be grown throughout the spring and most of the summer in northern countries, they can only be grown during the cool season in the south. We shall see that the pea's need for soil moisture leads to different cultivation methods in different regions. It's an exhausting crop, so the soil must be abundantly fertilized with farmyard manure or city sludge, if you don't want it to become impoverished. In practice, peas are only grown every three or four years on the same plot. Peas multiply exclusively by means of the seed, which germinates rapidly when entrusted to the soil. The cotyledons remain underground, and the young plant grows actively from the start. If you're growing taller varieties, it's a good idea to fit them with stakes as soon as the young plants are Om, 10 high, i.e. ten to fifteen days after emergence. The stakes most commonly used are branches, varying in height depending on the variety, with their own branches, which are pushed into the vicinity of the peas so that they can cling to them. Dwarf varieties need no support. Growing in the Paris climate. - Peas can be sown in different ways, depending on the season and the variety grown. Dwarf peas can be broadcast, but this method only has disadvantages: under these conditions, the seeds are difficult to cover, and if hoeing is necessary, it is difficult. For these reasons, this plant is usually sown in rows, sometimes in bunches. Sowing in rows offers the greatest advantage in terms of ease of cultivation, and is adopted almost everywhere. The distance to be kept between rows differs according to the period and the variety. For dwarf varieties, a spacing of Om, 30 is sufficient, but this distance must be doubled for high-growth varieties. Growers say that seedlings intended to produce the first crops in spring are sown in autumn towards the end of November, at Saite-Catherine (November 25). Pois Michaux is used for this crop. The soil should be well exposed to allow the plants to survive the winter without suffering, and to develop rapidly in the spring. Sloping ground or land along a wall with a south or southeast exposure is best. Peas are sown at a rate of about 2.5 liters per acre. Snow peas can be harvested before winter, and can easily withstand the winter without suffering. In February, they are hoeed and fitted with oars that are inserted obliquely so that the two rows closest to each other touch at the top, allowing you to pass easily through the spaces left free and harvest at your leisure. To speed up pod development, pinch off the ends of the stems as soon as they have produced eight or ten inflorescences. This operation needs to be carried out on two separate occasions, to ensure that both the main stem and any subsequent offshoots are treated. In large-scale cultivation, where it is often difficult to use stakes, this operation, to which we give the name of topping, becomes a rule, because by reducing the height of the plants, it enables them to be damaged less than if they were left to grow indefinitely. Peas sown in autumn produce early in the spring, but at a time that is difficult to specify, as it varies according to the severity of the season and the exposure of the sown land. Spring sowings, which will produce after those of plants sown in autumn, can be started early, as peas do not fear light spring frosts. They can be sown as early as the end of February and continued at regular intervals until the end of May or the beginning of June; later, under normal conditions, only a very small yield would be obtained. In naturally damp, cool soils, and in sheltered situations, sowing can be continued throughout the summer, only to be uprooted in the first days of August. Where the situation and the soil allow such late crops to be grown, serious advantages can be gained, as peas always fetch a high price in the autumn. In these successive sowings, vaDYBONSRI. - Cultivation, 2nd ed. 27 early and dwarf varieties are used first, followed by the ever-higher-yielding row varieties. Finally, for autumn cultivation, dwarf and fast-growing varieties are preferred. Around Paris, peas are widely grown for green harvesting. In the Saint-Denis plain, near Epône and Chartres, peas are grown over considerable areas. In all these large-scale crops, the peas are not harvested, but simply pollarded to stop them elongating. When dry peas are to be harvested, they are sown at the beginning of April, and harvested as soon as the stems and leaves turn yellow, which happens at the end of July. Yield is around 25 liters per acre. However, when grown in the vicinity of large cities, peas are most often harvested fresh, which gives a more advantageous yield. Harvesting takes place as soon as the pods contain half-formed seeds; it must be done successively, every eight days or so. When it's time to harvest, we pass between the rows, taking great care not to trample on any branches lying on the ground, and we pick only those pods that seem sufficiently advanced; we mustn't pull on them, as this would break the stem carrying them, but detach them with our fingers. Successive harvests of green peas yield 45 to 50 kilograms per acre, or 9 to 10 decalitres of pods, each decalitre yielding one and a half to two liters of fresh beans. Southern cultivation. - In the south of France and in Algeria, peas are increasingly being grown for green harvesting. In these regions, where winter frosts are too mild to affect pea vegetation, peas are sown in autumn around September, using early varieties. Harvesting takes place from February to April, precisely when the peas grown in our climate are not yet producing. At this time, the green pods are sold by the kilogram at a very high price, varying between 30 and 60 fr. per 100 kilograms during the first months of the year. These Midi crops have completely wiped out the under-frame production that used to be widespread. Seed crops. - For large-scale seed production, peas are sown at the beginning of April, not harvested green and pruned. For early dwarf varieties, it is preferable to sow in nurseries, then transplant as soon as the plants have two or three leaves. Enemies. - Peas are attacked by two parasitic fungi which fortunately do not cause them much damage. These are rust, which occurs in rainy years, and powdery mildew, which appears during severe drought. Both diseases are untreatable. The seeds are frequently attacked by bruchus (Bruchus pisi), which devours the cotyledons and embryos of the pea to the point of rendering them unsuitable for sowing. is native to South America; authentic documents and samples of plants growing spontaneously that have been collected there seem to prove this beyond doubt. Chile appears to be the plant's motherland, from where it spread first to neighboring countries and then to North America. The Spaniards (1) first brought it to Europe around 1580, but although the plant had already been widely cultivated in America, and despite the extreme ease of its propagation, it remained a pure curiosity for a very long time, and twenty years later, de l'Ecluse was still citing it as a rare plant. It took no less than a century for this excellent plant, which we would be hard-pressed to do without today, to gradually spread into cultivation, where it was still considered only as a fodder plant. It's difficult to pinpoint the exact date of its introduction to France, but it's certain that it was already widely grown in Belgium when it was introduced to France. That's probably where it came from, as it was first con(1) De Candolle, Or. des pl. cult. nue in the northern provinces, from where it spread, gaining ground from near and far. Olivier de Serres, in his Théâtre de l'Agriculture, and de Combes in l'École du Jardin Potager (1749), recommend its cultivation. Parmentier was instrumental in gaining its acceptance in the agricultural world. The potato is a perennial plant. If we follow the evolutionary phases of a young seedling, we see that as it produces aerial branches, it also gives rise to underground branches. These, after having lengthened by a variable amount, swell either towards their tip, which is the most common case, or sometimes also at an indeterminate point along their course. This bulge grows rapidly, becomes filled with starch and becomes what in practice is known as the potato tuber. This tuber is therefore a modified branch; indeed, everything points to it, for it bears leaves that are reduced, it's true, to a sort of scale, but which nonetheless have buds in their axils. It is these tubers that give the plant its perennial character, and serve to propagate it. The potato's aerial stems are angular and more or less robust and upright, depending on the variety. They bear alternate leaves that are so deeply indented that they resemble pinnate compound leaves. The flowers, of which many early varieties are devoid, are large, more or less purplish or pinkish, with rotate corollas; they are arranged in cymes simulating umbels. The fruits that follow these flowers are rounded, green, yellowish berries, induced by the calyx. They contain flattened, kidney-shaped seeds, which are used to produce new varieties. Uses. - Potatoes are so widely used that it's hardly necessary to insist on this point. Everyone knows that these tubers are eaten cooked, and there are countless dishes in which they can be used alone or in combination with other products. Varieties. - When the potato was first introduced into Europe, two types of tuber were already known, one yellow, the other reddish. A considerable number of varieties have since been sown. Their differentiation is based on shape, color, size, earliness and so on. M. Henry de Vilmorin (1) bases their determination on the shape and color of the sprouts produced by the tubers, because, according to him, this is one of the most stable characteristics. We won't go into the description of even the most common varieties, as such a study would be beyond the scope of this book. We are all the more authorized to do so as most of these varieties belong to the field and cannot, therefore, be considered as truly vegetable. Even among these, we have to make a choice, and we will only mention those that are most commonly grown. MARJOLIN POTATO. - Tubers of medium size, elongated, tapering slightly towards one end. Smooth, purplish sprouts. This is the variety most commonly grown in vegetable gardens. It is well suited to forced (1) Test of a meth. cat. of potato varieties. cultivation, as it is early and its tubers are very close to the base of the plant. TETARD MARJOLIN POTATO. - Tubers larger than the previous variety, with lightly colored sprouts; less early than the previous variety, but also recommendable. KIDNEY OR NETTLE-LEAVED POTATO. - Elongated tubers, slightly pointed at one end. Pink sprouts covered with white hairs, making them easily recognizable. Early variety used for early crops. ROSCOFF FLAT POTATO. - Medium-sized, elongated, flattened tubers with inconspicuous eyes. A very late variety with a remarkable shelf life, which can be eaten until June. Widely grown in Roscoff. Cultivation. - This plant is undemanding in terms of soil, and can be grown in almost any type of soil. However, there is a notable difference between the products obtained in good, rich, sandy soil and those that can be obtained in compact, clay soil. This difference concerns both the quantity and quality of the produce, and is to the detriment of cold, clayey soils, which produce only small yields of watery, unpalatable tubers. Sandy, rich, moderately humid soils with warm exposure are best suited to potato production. The fertilizers the plant craves must be supplied in a decomposed state. Farmers in the Paris region mainly use city sludge, which has previously rotted in heaps and been put on racks, or manure from mushroom mills. On farms, platform manure may be suitable, but it is preferable to use manure from vegetable garden beds. On the shores of the ocean, the fertilizer used consists of stable manure layered with kelp and seaweed. Manure can be applied in two different ways. When fertilizer is plentiful, the soil is fertilized at a rate of around 30,000 kilograms per hectare. When fertilizer is not plentiful, a small amount of manure can be applied to each potato spot. In early crops grown around Paris, both methods are used simultaneously. In the cultivation of table varieties, the only one which really depends on vegetable production and which we are concerned with here, tubers should never be planted as they are taken out of the cellars or silos in which they are kept for current needs. It is absolutely essential to prepare them, which will both hasten and increase production. At the end of summer or the beginning of autumn, when potatoes are uprooted, all medium-sized tubers should be selected which show the best characteristics of the cultivated variety. They should come from clumps that have produced abundant, early, high-quality produce. Since planting requires around 800 tubers per acre, we use this figure as a basis for keeping the quantity deemed necessary. After digging, the potatoes are left in the air for a few hours, to give them time to dry on the surface, and to remove any excess soil that may have stuck to them. Once the tubers have dried out, they are placed in flat baskets or, better still, in racks specially built for this purpose (fig. 98). These are boxes of varying dimensions, but in practice they are Om.60 long by Om.40 wide. They have a slatted bottom, made from Om,02-wide rods with Fig. 98. - Potato tray. free spaces of equal size. The perimeter of the box is made of boards Om,08 high. At the four corners are small wooden pillars, projecting a few centimeters below and forming a foot. All the racks must be built exactly to the same pattern, so that they can be stacked one on top of the other. The tubers are placed in these trays, side by side, resting on their tips, so that the larger end is in the air. As the boxes become full, they are arranged in stacks (fig. 99), either under a shed or in a well-ventilated room. The potatoes should remain in this state until the cold weather arrives, at which time the windows of the room or storeroom should be carefully closed whenever frost is expected. If the weather turns mild, take advantage of the opportunity to let in some fresh air. Taken care of in this way, the tubers will begin to produce a vigorous sprout during the first months of winter. This sprout, which Fig. 99. - Stack of apple trays Fig. 100. - Potato sprouted in a tray. remains the only one in most tubers, will gradually increase in size, and often even sprout a few roots on its surface. The tubers are now ready for planting (fig. 100). Growing in the Paris climate. - Planting with tubers prepared as described above should be done early, in order to benefit as much as possible from the advance already obtained through prior germination, and to obtain early products. It should not be forgotten that potato tuber shoots only emerge from the ground a fortnight to three weeks after they have been planted, and that consequently, although these shoots are sensitive to cold, planting can be done early, because as long as they are underground, they are safe. For our part, we plant the tubers in the course of February, at the beginning or at the end, depending on whether the cold ceases sooner or later, and we have never had to complain about this haste in planting; on the contrary, we obtain very satisfactory results in this way. Sometimes, however, the young leaves are affected by white frost, but this accident has no serious consequences, and in all cases can be easily avoided by spreading a little straw on the young shoots when the temperature drops. Planting begun at the time we have just mentioned can be continued until May and June, to obtain new products throughout the summer. Whatever the season, planting practice is always the same. Horticultural varieties produce little foliage, as most of them do not flower; they can therefore be planted at short distances. The distance kept between rows is Om, 50, and planting is done every Om, 25 to Om, 35. When it comes to placing tubers, deep pockets of Om, 15 are opened in the soil with a spade or hoe, and a little decomposed manure is deposited at the bottom. A tuberule is placed in each pot; it is laid down by hand and buried, keeping it upright. The pot is then filled in, though a sort of small basin is left at each planted spot. When the shoots are about Om, 15, or gives a hoeing intended to remove weeds and bring back a little earth to the foot of each plant; it's not a mound that needs to be made, but simply bring back the earth to a height of 5 to 6 centimeters. The disadvantage of high ridging is that it removes the tubers from the direct action of the air, preventing them from growing. However, it would be unwise not to ridge at all, as a certain number of tubers would emerge above ground level and become green and inedible. Harvesting begins in June and can continue throughout the summer. Harvesting can be carried out in two different ways: often, as soon as it is presumed that some of the tubers can be eaten, a dig is made at the base of the potato, and only the largest are removed, sparing the smaller ones, which are left in place for longer. The other method is to harvest the whole crop when most of the tubers are fully developed. When harvesting in order to preserve the tubers, either for winter consumption or to make the plant, wait until the tops have completely dried out before proceeding with the uprooting. Roscoff cultivation. - In Roscoff, Jersey and a few other places with especially favorable winter temperatures, planting is done at the beginning of January. The procedures followed are those indicated above. Harvesting begins in April, when the produce is sold at a very high price. The late variety named after Roscoff is widely grown there. It is planted in April, using a special method: the land is smoked, then ploughed and harrowed, and laid out on a flat surface, with one potato at a time. As these are late-ripening products, there's no need to germinate the tubers. The rows are spaced alternately by Om, 30, then by Om, 50, which makes like a path every two rows; on the rows a distance of Om, 30 is kept. Once the tubers have been placed on the ground in this way, they are covered with 1m, 10 of earth; later they will be buttered again. Harvesting takes place when the tops have dried out. The potato variety grown in this way has the advantage of retaining all its qualities until around June; for this reason, it is highly sought-after, and its selling price is always high. Large quantities are exported to England. To preserve its quality, it is stored in granaries and covered with a tarpaulin to prevent it from turning green. The average harvest is 75 hectoliters, each weighing 65 kilograms per hectare. We borrow the following information from a note by Mr. N. Schneider published in the Revue horticole (1891, no. 8). "On the best exposed slopes, harvesting began on May 6, and prices were quoted at 500 francs per tonne on the London market. By the end of May, the price had dropped to 433 francs per tonne. In the first days of June it was 276 francs, in mid-June 138, and at the end of June it was 85 francs." "In 1886, the export of potatoes from the island of Jersey produced a figure of 10 million francs; in 1889, it only reached 6,600,000 francs. Southern cultivation. - In the south of France and in Algeria, potatoes are planted much earlier than in France, as frosts are not to be feared and the potatoes benefit from the winter rains to develop well. Planting takes place in August, September and October, and the produce is ready for harvesting in December, January and February. They are rarely eaten locally, but are usually shipped to more northerly regions. Bedding culture. - This type of production, which used to be very advantageous, has become much less important since the railroads began transporting produce from the south of France to us. In rural vegetable gardens, where there is no communication, this type of cultivation still has its raison d'être, especially as it has to be admitted that its produce is infinitely superior to that obtained in the south of France. The first layers are built around New Year's Day. They are made of annealed manure mixed with new manure. This layer should give about 15 degrees. Twenty feet per frame is planted; when the tops touch the glass, the boxes are lifted and the height of the stoves increased. Harvesting takes place after two and a half months of cultivation. Each frame yields around 5 kilograms of tubers. To obtain a sustained product, successive plantings are made every month until March. Diseases. - The most formidable of all potato diseases is undoubtedly that produced by a parasitic fungus known as Phytophthora infestans. It invades all the plant's organs, which blacken and eventually rot. Affected tubers soon emit a strong, putrid odour. The many preventive treatments available can be summed up as follows: 1° grow early varieties that have been made precocious by prior germination, as the disease develops mainly in autumn; 2° reject as unfit for planting any tuber stained with the disease; 3° carefully burn dry tops and never put them in manure, as the fungus spores can be preserved there; 4° only return this crop to the same land every two or three years, to give the spores time to be destroyed. Fortunately, it has been found that treatment with copper salts produces the best results, and timely applications help to keep the disease under control. As soon as the leaves are covered with the characteristic spots, the plants are sprayed with the copper salt preparations used to treat vine diseases, such as Bordeaux mixture or celestial water; the parasitic fungus is thus destroyed and the tubers remain healthy. The indication for this treatment is due to the learned professor at the Institut Agronomique, Mr. E. Prillieux (1). (1) Compte rendu de l'Académie des sciences (t. CVII, 1888, p. 447). It is not clear whether the cultivated radish formed a separate species, as Linnaeus assumed and named it Raphanus sativus, or whether the plant in our gardens was simply derived from a species that grows spontaneously in Europe and is known as Raphanus Raphanistrum. Very convincing experiments, in our opinion, were carried out by M. Carrière, head gardener of the nurseries at the Paris Natural History Museum (1). He collected seeds of R. Raphanistrum, sowed them, and after the fourth generation he was fortunate enough to see a large proportion of the plants he had grown transform their spindly roots into the fleshy roots that are the hallmark of edible Radishes. Even better. As Mr. Carrière continued with his seedlings, it wasn't long before his plants began to differentiate and give rise to the various forms we know from cultivation. It has been objected (2), while recognizing the value of these experiments, that R. Raphanistrum was a European plant, and that Radish has been cultivated in China and Japan since (1) Carrière, Orig. des pl. domest. demonstrée par la culture du Radis sauvage, 1869. (2) De Candolle, Orig, des pl. cultivées. a high antiquity. But the plant could perhaps have been transported when, in the second century BC, the Chinese endowed their country with a large number of plants from our crops. What's more, couldn't the plant have once grown in Asia and now disappeared from the wild? It seems to us that theoretical considerations must give way to evidence drawn from direct experience. It has also been objected (1) that there are differences between wild and cultivated radishes, based on hardiness, flower coloring and the degree of partitioning of the fruit. But all these characteristics do not constitute specific differences, because they are essentially variable, and even more marked differences have been observed in other vegetable plants, when the plant was transferred from the wild to the cultivated state. The same differences exist between carrots, beet, cultivated cabbage and cabbage growing in the wild, yet no one has thought of making these two different states into separate species. Cultivated Radishes are characterized by fleshy taproots, covered in early life by a coleorhyze. The leaves are pinnate, with more or less serrated margins, depending on the variety. From their center, either in the first year if the plant was sown in spring, or only in the second if sown in autumn, grows a robust branching ending in clusters of cruciliform flowers. These flowers are followed by siliques which do not (1) Vilmorin, Les pl. potagères. DYBOWSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 28 vrent pas et qui contiennent un nombre variable de graines arrondies, roussâtres. Uses. - Radishes are eaten raw. Varieties. - The very marked differences between cultivated varieties are based on the color, shape and size of the roots. For practical purposes, Radishes should be separated into Fig. 101. - Round Radish Fig. 102. half-long Radish scarlet. with white tip. two classes, one comprising all varieties that can be grown at any season of the year, and which are therefore called all-month Radishes; the other, those eaten in autumn and winter. I. - ALL-MONTH RADISHES. We shall confine ourselves to mentioning the varieties that are most widely grown. ROUND SCARLET RADISH. - Round, slightly flattened, bright red root. Foliage not very abundant. This variety is used in forced cultivation (fig. 101). RADiS DeMi-LoNG A BOUT BLANC. - Elongated, cylindrical root, often even a little swollen towards the base. Good variety for spring and summer cultivation (fig. 102). LONG PINK RADISH. - Root long and tapering towards the base fig. 103. Long pink radish. Fig. 104. - Long black winter radish. Cultivated during spring and summer (fig. 103). II. - WINTER RADISH. black winter radish. - Black-brown, rough root, Om, 20 to Om, 30 long. White, pungent flesh. Abundant foliage: hardy plant (fig. 104). This category includes all the Japanese radishes known as Daï-Kon, which have spread to our crops in recent years. Most varieties have white roots, often very long. Growing Radishes every month. - Radishes in this category are very simple to grow. The seeds are broadcast, sometimes alone, but usually in association with other crops. About 500 grams of seed are sown per acre. It is essential to cover the seed with potting soil or mulch, so that the frequent watering required by this crop can be carried out without fear of gullying the soil and dragging the seed along with it. Radishes are fast-growing; all can be harvested when they are two-thirds developed, which is 22 to 30 days after sowing for the white-tipped pink and round scarlet varieties, when they have been sown in loose, very rich soil and watered sparingly. Harvesting too late is a disadvantage, as it causes the roots to dig in and become unfit for consumption. Radishes can be sown all year round; during winter, they are sown in beds in lettuce or carrot crops; from February onwards, they can be sown in the open ground in good exposure. Throughout spring and summer, sow every fortnight or so to obtain a steady crop. If you sow after September, it's a good idea to use straw mats that are laid out overnight over the crops, supporting them on gaulettes; in this way, you can harvest right up until the coldest weather. Growing winter radishes. - These radishes are broadcast from May to July. Market gardeners in Paris prefer to sow with their fingers, i.e. to make a small hole every Om, 15 with the finger, into which they place one or two seeds. Once the seeds have sprouted, they are thinned out if they have been broadcast, and doubled in finger sowing if there are two plants in the same hole. Some market gardeners grow black radishes on a bed in spring, producing very tender roots. In the open ground, harvesting takes place after two or three months. Radishes are pulled out for winter in October, the crown cut off, and the roots buried in sand in a cellar. Growing for seed. - Seed production is very big in the Bay of Mont Saint-Michel. One grower alone, M. Touzard, sowed up to 52 hectares of Radishes for seed in the same year. Radishes are sown around mid-April in nurseries, and three weeks to a month later are transplanted to well-disturbed soil. Rows are spaced Om 40 apart, with Om 30 between plants. We hoe in summer. When the fruit turns yellow, cut with a sickle and place in bunches, which are then arranged in "moyettes". When the stalks are dry, they are stored in the attic and the threshing is reserved for winter. The result is 8 to 10 hectoliters, priced at 90 to 100 francs. Enemies. - Radishes, when young, are, like all crucifers, ravaged by flea beetles, which can be fought by frequent watering. Horseradish (Cochlearia Armoracia, Linné) is a plant indigenous to temperate Eastern Europe, from where, in the opinion of M. A. de Candolle, it must have been introduced and spread to Germany, France and neighboring countries, where it is now cultivated. The plant's vegetative organs are characterized by very long, cylindrical roots that penetrate deep into the soil; their yellowish bark is rough and covers a white, fleshy tissue. The cellular tissue contains allyl sulfide, which gives it a strong, burning mustard-like taste. The leaves of this plant are large, measuring no less than Om, 40 long by Om, 15 wide; they are wavy and glossy green (fig. 105). From their centers, flowering branches of about Om, 60 rise each year, ending in small clusters of small, white, cruciform flowers, followed by silicate fruits, which are usually sterile; the seeds of this plant are therefore relatively rare. Uses. - The root, once stripped of its bark, is used to prepare a condiment that resembles mustard in taste, and is widely used throughout Eastern and Northern Europe. The grated root is sometimes eaten directly, with vinegar, and sometimes used in the preparation of various sauces. Propagation. - Horseradish rarely produces fertile seeds, so this is not the way to propagate it. It is propagated using Fig. 105. - Cultivated horseradish. root fragments are cut to a length of about Om.05 and transplanted into a nursery in spring. Each of these root pieces will produce adventitious buds and numerous roots. Transplanting is done at Om, 15 in all directions, during March and April. The seedling remains in the nursery for a whole year, and it's not until the following spring that it's used to put it in place for good, where it will complete its development. Choosing the site and planting. - For planting, choose loose, fresh soil; soil from land reclamation or clearing is particularly suitable; it's also a plant that thrives on fertilizer, and to help it develop rapidly, it's a good idea to fertilize the soil before planting. Before planting, the soil must be specially prepared. The seedling is pulled out of the nursery in the first few days of spring; this must be done with care, so as not to break the numerous roots that have developed on the planted section. The most convenient instrument for this is the flat-tined fork. Once removed from the ground, the plants are prepared by pruning the roots, which consists in selecting the largest and longest root and removing all the others, leaving only one pivot, which is shortened to Om, 25 or Om, 30. The small roots removed will be used to make plants for the following year. Having undergone this preparation, the roots are transplanted using a large planter, taking care to push them in as vertically as possible. Each plant is planted at a distance of Om.40 to Om.50 between the rows and between the rows. Cultivation consists simply of hoeing, which is carried out whenever the soil is invaded by weeds. Uprooting. - Roots remain in place for two years; they are usually pulled out with a flat-tine fork throughout October, or even November, weather permitting. Once the roots have been removed from the ground, the leaves are removed with a rake, along with any adventitious roots that have developed on the main pivot. If it has been growing under favorable conditions, the main pivot has developed very strongly; it usually measures from Om.05 to Om.07 in diameter at the middle part (fig. 106). Locations where this crop is grown. - Horseradish is grown extensively in eastern France. In Alsace, horseradish is grown on a large scale, particularly in the Haut-Rhin region around Colmar. Large-scale production is also found in Saxony and Baden. Moreover, it is grown in all the kitchen gardens of Germany, Austria, Poland and Russia, where it is cultivated in exactly the same Fig. 106. way as in large-scale farming. - Horseradish root. However, in some gardens, a kind of stone paving is placed under the roots in the square intended for this crop, to prevent the roots from rotating beyond a certain depth. By carefully preparing the plant as we have indicated, i.e. by cutting the main root to the required length, this paving, which is costly to set up, is rendered unnecessary. Yield. - Once the product has been removed from the soil and the leaves and lateral roots removed, it is sold either directly to the market, as is generally the case in Alsace, or exported to various consumer centers. The wholesale price varies according to the product; the minimum is 15 francs per cent, taken on the spot, while the maximum often exceeds 30 francs for large premium roots. Assuming an average price of 22 francs, it's easy to see what the yield per hectare is. Indeed, with planting at Om, 50 in all directions, we obtain 40,000 roots per hectare, which at 22 francs per cent comes to 9680 francs. But this crop occupies the soil for two years, so the gross yield is only 4840 francs per hectare per year. The costs of cultivation, fertilization, weeding and ground rent must be subtracted, adding up to a total annual expense of around 600 francs per hectare. Conservation. - Once all the rootlets have been removed, horseradish is taken down to the cellar, where it is layered in fine sand. It can be kept there for about a year, without losing many of its qualities, provided the shoots are removed as they appear. 2 1 E *: biennial plant, native to southern and temperate Europe. Its spatulate leaves are similar to those of lamb's lettuce. The root is white, fusiform and crunchy. From the center of the rosette formed by the leaves on the ground, a branched stem rises in the spring of the second year, bearing light-blue campanulate flowers that give rise to capsules induced by the calyx. These fruits have three chambers containing a large number of extremely fine seeds. Uses. - This plant (fig. 107) is eaten in the same way as lamb's lettuce, in salads, with the difference, however, that the leaves are supplemented by roots, which have a pleasant flavor. Cultivation. - Rapunzel grows best in fresh, substantial soil. The soil must be well prepared and levelled with a few strokes of the rake. However, plants sown at this time of year run a high risk of flowering in autumn, and therefore of not producing any product. This too-rapid bolting is particularly likely to occur when the soil lacks freshness, so it is wiser to sow only in June and July. Sowing is done by broadcasting, but as the seed is extremely fine, it is useful to mix it with a few handfuls of fine sand, sifted and dried beforehand; this procedure spreads the seed much more evenly. The seed is buried with a simple rake, after which the soil is compacted Fig. 107 - Cultivated Rapunzel. by pricking the tines of a fork on one end of a board, with which the soil is evenly struck and covered with a little long litter. Germination takes place after about ten days, if the soil is given a few light drenches with a fine-headed watering can. When the plants have produced a few leaves, if necessary, they are lighted and weeded to remove weeds. A few radish seeds are usually sown at the same time as the Rapunzel, to shelter the young plant in its early stages, and are removed before their foliage gets in the way. This lettuce can be harvested as early as October and November, but is usually left to spend part of the winter in the ground, and is not eaten until late January and February. It's at this time of year that large numbers arrive at the Paris market halls. Seeds. - Do not harvest seeds from plants sown in May, which will come up in the same year. These seeds would give rise to plants with the same tendency to bolt too early, so the seed should only be harvested from plants that have overwintered and flowered in spring. The seeds are extracted from their capsules by gentle threshing. They can be used for up to five years, during which time they retain their ability to germinate. -- Scorsonera (Scorzonera hispantca, Linné) grows wild in southern France, Spain and many other regions. Its cultivation dates back no more than a century, since all documents prior to that date refer to it only as a plant found in the wild. Scorsonera is perennial by its rhizome; it bears broad, Om, 04, long lanceolate leaves. From the center of these leaves, branches arise from the first year, bearing heads of yellow, ligulate flowers, giving rise to long achenes, pointed at the base and obtuse at the apex. These fruits are white; in practice, they are known as seeds. They retain their germinative capacity for only two years. The rhizome extends into a pivot which follows it; this is the edible part of the plant, with rough, black bark, and is usually unbranched throughout its upper part. Uses. - Scorsonera pivots are eaten after they have been stripped of their bark and cooked. Cultivation. - Scorsonera are grown in abundance, especially around Paris, near Pantin, Aubervilliers, Noisy-le-Sec, Epône and other towns. In the south of France, their cultivation is less widespread, but nevertheless yields very rapid results. To develop fully and quickly, Scorsonera needs soil rich in decomposed fertilizer; fresh manure has the disadvantage of making its roots branch out. Fresh siliceous soils are particularly suitable, provided they are rich in organic matter. Around Paris, this crop is smoked almost exclusively with city sludge, which seems to suit it very well. The soil must be ploughed deeply, so that the roots can grow vertically in the soil without being disturbed. Scorsonera is sown in rows, which are traced on the ground using a spike drill, which could be very advantageously replaced by a seeder. These lines should be Om, 20 apart, and the seeds sown in them should be overlapped by only Ou,02 or so. These seedlings are sown in March and April, at a rate of 100 grams per acre. When the young plants, which appear with two very elongated cotyledonary leaves, are well ley, and in addition to these they have taken two or three leaves, we give a hoeing between the lines, and if we want to obtain beautiful products, it is useful to take advantage of this work to thin out the plants on the lines so as to leave between them a space of Om, 06 approximately. A second hoeing is often necessary in summer. During the summer, a good proportion of Scorsonera plants come into flower; to prevent the roots from hardening, and at the same time increase their development, it is useful to mow these inflorescences. Some growers prefer to let these plants flower, in order to harvest the seed; but the product they obtain Fig. 108. - Scorsonera boot. from the sale of the seed does not compensate for the damage caused to the crop in this way. Harvesting begins in November, and continues until the end of the year. Scorsonera do not fear the cold, but as it would be difficult, if not impossible, to extract them from the ground when the soil is frozen, it is useful to pull them all up towards the end of November, to put them in gauges close to the house, and to cover them in case of cold. When the soil is unsuitable for this type of production, we sometimes do things a little differently, and instead of sowing in spring, we sow in August, to harvest in autumn of the following year. This procedure is not very advantageous, as the plants tend to flower in spring, and the roots become tough and difficult to eat. What's more, seedlings sown in August only grow well if watered, so this crop can only be grown in kitchen gardens, where it occupies the land for more than a year. For sale, Scorsonera are packed in bunches using a mold similar to the one used to pack asparagus, which is Om, 12 in diameter (fig. 108). All the leaves are left after these vegetables, and the roots are bound with a wicker tie, which is placed halfway down the length of the roots. Growers reckon that, on average, one hectare produces 7,000 bunches, the usual price of which at the Paris halles is 30 francs per hundred bunches, giving a gross product per hectare of 2,100 francs. As for cultivation costs, they are as follows in the areas surrounding the capital: Rent for one hectare....................... Manure: 25,000 kilogr. at 8 fr. per 1,000 kilogr........................... 300 francs. 200 To be carried forward...... 500 francs. DYBONSEI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 29 To be carried forward........... Plowing and harrowing..................... Seeding and sowing......................... Two hoeings at 35 fr...................... Uprooting and bundling.............. 500 francs. 100 50 70 100 - Total expenses........ 820 francs. The result is that, although the gross production figure is not very high, this crop is still advantageous, for the reason that cultivation costs are relatively low. Seed production. - We have already mentioned that seed can be harvested from plants that go to seed in the first year. This procedure, which is generally followed in large-scale cultivation, is not to be recommended in the kitchen garden. In this case, it is infinitely preferable to select the best and most voluminous roots in autumn, overwinter them and plant them in the spring; in this way, you can be sure of harvesting only seeds that will give birth to good plants. The seed is harvested when the flowerheads can be seen to open, so it can only be done gradually and successively. .... .. . . ... ". .1. -.4 ★24 SALSIFIS Salsify (Tragopogon porrifolium, Linnaeus) is a plant that was much more widely cultivated a century or two ago than it is today. The Scorsonera, which combines a great similarity of taste with numerous cultural and culinary advantages, seems to have largely replaced the Salsify. Individuals of the latter species are found in the wild in Greece and Algeria. In all cases, it is a southern species, which explains its sensitivity to cold. From a purely botanical point of view, they are very similar to scorsonera, differing only in the number of involucral bracts. Their leaves are narrow, somewhat resembling those of leeks in shape, but stalked and pinnately venationed. The flowers, arranged in flower heads, are purplish and give rise to elongated achenes. Seeds only retain their germinative capacity for a short time, so it's wise to use only those harvested the previous year. The edible part is the rhizome, with the main root arranged in a fleshy pivot (fig. 109). Uses. - The roots are scraped free of bark 1 and then cooked. Young leaves can be eaten in salads. Cultivation. - To grow well, this plant requires Fig. 109. - Cultivated salsify. a deep, rich and sufficiently loosened soil. It is sown in rows Om, 20 apart in March, using around 120 grams per acre. It is useful to baste the soil to hasten germination. When emergence has occurred, hoeing is carried out to remove weeds, as well as to thin out the plants on the rows and leave a few centimetres of space between them. During the summer, it's worth hoeing again to keep the soil clean. Any plants that come into flower should be removed, as their roots are hard and inedible. Harvesting takes place in autumn. All roots are pulled out with a flat-tine fork; those that are not eaten immediately should be placed in gauges. It's a good idea to cover them with straw or litter, as frost could damage them. For seeds, keep the straightest and least branched roots, which should be placed in gauges and sheltered. In spring, transplant Om, 20 in all directions. The seed must be harvested as soon as it is ripe, as many birds are very fond of it, and leaving it standing could lead to it being eaten. The leaves are attacked by a parasitic fungus (Ofdium), which can be preserved by repeated sulphur treatments. ... i PR."... plant that everything suggests is native to Peru. In fact, the tomato as we know it, with its ribbed fruits, has never been found there growing spontaneously; but one whose small, rounded fruits resemble cherries has been found there in a truly spontaneous state. Observation of the facts shows that the mamelonning of the fruit, due to the multiplication of the ovary's lodges, is an effect of cultivation, and that we are therefore entitled to consider the tomatoes of our gardens as stemming from plants whose fruit was primitively smooth and had a specific number of lodges. This fact is supported by the fact that, after three generations, the tomatoes we have grown, which originally had only two lobes, have given us fruit with multiple lobes and mamelon-shaped fruit on plants where the majority of the fruit was still normal. In France, tomato cultivation does not go back very far, and we know market gardeners who well remember that their grandfathers cultivated tomatoes as a plant that was still quite uncommon. The tomato is a sarmentous plant whose stems and leaves are covered with glandular hairs that give off a strong, worm-like odour at the slightest rustle. The inflorescences, which are clusters of cymes, are abnormally placed as a result of entrainment. Flowers are followed by fruits, which are berries colored either yellow or red in most horticultural varieties. A cross-section shows that the number of lodges varies from one fruit to another. Seeds are yellow, flattened and very numerous in each lodge; they retain their germinative powers for four years. Uses. - The berries are eaten raw in salads, or cooked, in which case they are used in the preparation of various dishes. Varieties. - This plant has a great tendency to variation, and although, as we have said, tomatoes have not been cultivated for very long, we already know of a large number of forms which differ from one another in the way the whole plant grows, in its greater or lesser precocity, and in the shape, color or size of its fruits. Preference should always be given to early varieties, as in outdoor cultivation vegetation is always halted by late frosts, which catch the last fruits before they are fully ripe. We recommend the following varieties: HATIVE RED TOMATO. - The fruits of this variety develop strongly, often weighing up to 400 grams. They are criss-crossed with deep ribs. This variety is best suited to outdoor cultivation (fig. 110). HATIVE DWARF TOMATO. - This variety resembles the previous one, but is smaller in size, making it particularly suitable for cultivation under glass. Fig. 110. - Early red tomato. LARGE SMOOTH TOMATO. - Fruits are slightly transparent red, with a smooth Fig. 111. surface. Their development is very considerable, but they have the serious disadvantage of remaining almost always green near the stalk, and moreover, of splitting transversely, which facilitates their rotting and depreciates them for sale (fig. 111). Cultivation. - Because of its southern origin, the tomato cannot develop fully in the Paris climate if grown in the open air. It is therefore necessary to hasten its growth by sheltering it under a frame while it is still young. Tomatoes for outdoor cultivation should be sown from mid-February to the end of February, or even the beginning of March. I prepare a warm bed and scatter the seeds over the soil, barely covering them. They germinate quickly, and the young plants they give birth to are endowed with active growth. Three weeks after sowing, the plants are already Om, 10 in height and have four to five leaves. At this point, they are uprooted and transplanted either onto the same bed, or onto a similar bed built from scratch. Transplanting should be done at Om, 10 in all directions, i.e. at a rate of around 200 per frame. Care should be taken when planting to press the young plant down to the cotyledons, which shortens its height a little and increases its vigor by making it take on adventitious roots along the entire length of the buried part. As soon as the tomatoes have recovered, which can be after five or six days, if you have taken care to water them and cover the frame with a mat on the first day, you should take advantage of every day of good weather to give them some air by lifting the frames. It often happens that, either because the frames have not been lifted often enough, or because the bed has been too warm, the tomato vines over-extend themselves and, after a short time, come to rest against the glass, before the time has come to expose them to the open air. This inconvenience can be avoided a " Fig. 112. - Young tomato plant. by uprooting them all, and replanting them in the same place, sinking them deeper. In this way, as well as being shortened, the plant is delayed in its growth, and will now be able to wait without inconvenience for the moment when it is time to set out. Tomatoes (fig. 112) can be planted in the open air, before the spring frosts have passed, as they are very sensitive to the slightest drop in temperature below zero. In the Paris climate, this planting is done around May 15th. It's best to choose a site with warm exposure; a hillside sheltered by a wall or building is particularly suitable. For large-scale production, tomatoes are planted in full squares, but always in a south-facing position. Tomatoes thrive in soil rich in fresh fertilizer; planting is often done in potting soil, and plants grown in this way develop rapidly. Tomatoes are planted in rows 80 cm apart, with rows spaced 50 cm apart. Tomatoes should be planted right up to the first leaves, to increase the production of adventitious roots, and a small basin should be left around the base to receive water for watering. These plants are very water-hungry, and it's a good idea to line the small basin at the base with mulch and water it as soon as the plants are planted. Pruning. - If tomatoes were left to their own devices, the branches would branch excessively, and most of the flowers produced would drop off without bearing fruit. These would not become abundant until very late in the autumn, and would not have time to ripen before the frosts. From these considerations it follows that it is absolutely necessary to prune these plants, which, by reducing their vigor and concentrating vegetation on the first inflorescences, will force them to develop well and bear fruit early. Pruning is therefore essential, as experience has shown time and again. There are several systems for carrying out this operation; we will indicate two of the most commonly used: Shortly before planting, often 77 7 2 5 2 2 2 - M FO Fig. 113. - Tomato plant fitted with a stake. before this operation, a bunch of flowers appears on young tomato plants, which are about Om.25 high at the time, at the end of the main stem. In the axils of the leaves below this inflorescence are buds which, if left to develop, will soon grow so large that they will absorb all the plant's sap, and this first inflorescence will either abort completely, or in any case give only one or two fruits. The most common pruning method consists in cutting off this first inflorescence, then selecting four of the most vigorous lateral branches, and allowing each to produce two or three bunches of flowers.
