Some growers prefer to keep the first inflorescence at the end of the stem; but in this case, they only use two side branches, and obtain a slightly earlier, but less abundant product. Whichever system is used, care must be taken to ensure that each branch does not branch out, and to achieve this it becomes necessary to remove all lateral branches as they develop; this is the only way to obtain fruit from preserved inflorescences. Since tomatoes are sarmentous plants, it's impossible to leave them to their own devices, otherwise the branches would drag along the ground and the fruit wouldn't ripen. Market gardeners provide each plant with a stile, against which they tie all the branches (fig. 113). This method of support can be replaced by stakes which are driven in along the lines, four meters apart, and to which two lines of wire are attached by any method. The branches are then attached to the stakes with strands of straw. When the fruit begins to turn red, which happens at the beginning of July, we remove the leaves that conceal them, so that, exposed to direct sunlight, they ripen more quickly. At the end of the season, we remove all the leaves, which hastens the ripening of the last fruit. If the first frosts arrive before all the tomatoes are ripe, we pick them as they are, and put them under a frame, on a bed of straw, so that they finish ripening. Harvesting usually begins around July and lasts until the cold season. Around Montlhéry and Lieusaint, there are very large tomato crops, all of which are shipped to Paris. On average, one plant yields three kilograms of fruit, or 750 kilograms per acre. Wholesale prices range from 15 francs to 25 francs per 100 kilograms, when these products are sold at the end of summer and in autumn; prices for early produce are, as we shall see, much higher. Cultivation in southern France. - In the south of France, and more particularly in Algeria and southern Spain, tomatoes are grown on a very large scale, to be shipped as early produce to Paris, England and the northern European states. We are indebted to Mr. Congiu, an agricultural engineer, for the following information on crops grown on an area he estimates at over 500 hectares in the province of Malaga. These data are of great interest, as tomato production in southern Spain is similar to that in Algeria. The fertile plains of Tunisia would be an advantage. In Spain, tomatoes are sown in nurseries at the beginning of September. A month or so after sowing, they are planted on land facing south, with no need for irrigation. Tomato rows are spaced Om, 80 apart, with Om, 50 between rows, equivalent to planting 25,000 tomatoes per hectare. If the weather is wet, we don't water; if, on the contrary, it's very dry, we give each plant a little water with the watering can, when planting, to encourage recovery. We don't prune these tomatoes, and more often than not we don't even give them stakes, leaving them to produce long branches that drag on the ground. We believe it would be worthwhile to change this state of affairs and adopt the cultivation practices used in the Paris area. The result would be earlier yields, and the very high price this commodity fetches would more than compensate for the additional costs involved. The reason for these reflections is that yields Fig. 114. - Tomato packaging. in these regions, which are so favorable to tomato cultivation, are only 25 to 30,000 kilograms per hectare, or about one kilogram per plant, whereas in France this yield is double or even triple. Harvesting begins towards the end of December, and continues until April and May. Prices vary greatly from year to year and from period to period; at the start of the season, for example, they can reach Ifr 80 per kilogram, but this is a maximum that is not always reached and, in any case, does not last long. Prices soon fall to Ofr, 50 per kilogram, and finally to 0fr, 20. These figures clearly show that it would be in your interest to hasten the harvest of the product by careful pruning. Nevertheless, as we can see, this crop is extremely profitable. Most of the tomatoes produced in Spain are shipped to France, while the rest are consumed in Madrid and other major cities in the country of production. These fruits are shipped in white wicker baskets lined with paper (fig. 114). Forced cultivation. - The cultivation of early tomatoes is becoming less important every year, due to competition from southern countries. However, in the countryside, where communications with major centers are difficult, it can be useful to engage in this early production, if only for personal use. Tomatoes can be sown as early as January, on a warm layer; when the plant has developed sufficiently, it is transplanted to a second layer. In March, they can be planted out permanently on a bed under a frame, at a rate of nine plants per panel. It is important to use only dwarf varieties for this production, and to raise the boxes as the plant grows and touches the glass. Pruning consists in keeping only two branches with two bunches each and removing all the rest. The retained branches are fixed to gaulettes, which are set up parallel to the glass, supported by stakes driven into the soil of the bed. Care must be taken to provide air whenever the temperature permits, especially at flowering time, as failure to do so could result in the flowers remaining infertile. A system has been proposed (1) that gives faster results and higher yields. After sowing and transplanting the tomatoes, as described above, they are planted at Ou, 25 in all directions, i.e. at a rate of thirty per frame. Pruning is carried out by removing all the lateral branches; the plant is thus reduced to a stem bearing a small number of leaves and ending in an inflorescence which, as a result of the removal of all the branches, increases so much that it is often necessary to prune it itself, to hasten the ripening of the first fruits formed. You can expect a harvest of six to eight tomatoes per plant, ripening in May and June. Seed carriers. - For seeds, always choose the largest, healthiest and best-formed fruits. They should be left to ripen completely. To extract the seed, crush the tomatoes in water, then throw the whole thing on a sieve to separate the seeds, which should be left to dry in the shade. Parasitic disease. - Tomatoes are attacked by a parasitic fungus which, towards the end of summer in wet years, wreaks considerable havoc. This disease is the same as that of the potato; it is due to Phytophthora infestans, (1) M. Berthault, Bul. de la Soc. Cent. d'Hort., A. 1884. DYBOWSRI. - Culture, 2nd ed. 30 24. As in the case of potatoes, it is now easy to get rid of this disease by applying the copper salt treatment used against vine diseases. It is to Mr. Prillieux, the learned professor at the Institut Agronomique, that we owe the propagation of this method, indicated by Mr. Jouet, which gives absolutely reliable results.
